<meta>This page is the preface to A. Wolf's translation and edition of Spinoza's "Short Treatise on God, Man, and His Well-Being," originally published in 1910. It follows the front matter and library markers from the previous page.</meta>

# PREFACE

This volume is primarily intended to be an introduction to the philosophy of Spinoza <note>Baruch Spinoza (1632–1677) was a Dutch-Jewish philosopher and a primary architect of the Enlightenment.</note>. The *Short Treatise*, though not entirely free from difficulties, is well-suited for this purpose. It contains the essential elements of Spinoza's philosophy in a form that is less demanding than the *Ethics* <note>Spinoza's most famous work, the *Ethics*, was written in a complex style modeled after mathematical proofs.</note> with its rigid geometric method. The *Short Treatise* cannot, of course, take the place of the *Ethics*, but it prepares the way for a much easier and more productive study of it than is otherwise possible. The *Introduction* and the *Commentary* provide all the help that the reader is likely to require.

At the same time, the *Short Treatise* holds a special interest for more advanced students of Spinoza as the most important aid to the study of the origin and development of his philosophy. Their needs have not been overlooked. Every care has been taken to provide a faithful version of the *Treatise*; notice is taken of all variations in the text and notes which are likely to be important. Even the unusual punctuation and the frequent use of capital letters have been mostly reproduced here from the Dutch manuscripts. While the *Introduction* and the *Commentary* may be largely unnecessary for the advanced student, I hope they will also be found to contain something interesting and helpful even to him.

The *Translation* was, in the first instance, based on the Dutch text contained in Van Vloten and Land's <note>Johannes van Vloten and Jan Pieter Nicolaas Land were scholars who produced the standard 19th-century edition of Spinoza's collected works.</note> second edition of Spinoza's works. Subsequently, however, I spent a very considerable amount of time and effort in examining the manuscripts themselves, with the result that the present version may, I think, claim to be more complete than any of the published editions or translations.

The *Life of Spinoza*, which forms the greater part of the *Introduction*, is based on an independent study of all the available material. This material has been considerably increased in recent years by the research of the late Prof. Freudenthal, Dr. K. O. Meinsma, and Dr. W. Meyer, so that the older biographies of Spinoza require correction in some respects. I have also utilized to a greater extent than has been done before all that is known of contemporary...

<summary>This preface introduces a new translation of Spinoza's "Short Treatise," designed both as a beginner's guide to his philosophy and a rigorous resource for advanced scholars based on original Dutch manuscripts. The author emphasizes that this edition incorporates updated biographical research that corrects earlier accounts of Spinoza's life.</summary>
<keywords>Spinoza, Short Treatise, Ethics, philosophy, Van Vloten, Land, Freudenthal, manuscript study, biography, 17th century rationalism</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues A. Wolf's preface to his 1910 edition of Spinoza's "Short Treatise on God, Man, and His Well-Being." It details the scholarly sources and acknowledgments involved in the book's production.</meta>

# PREFACE

I have focused on Jewish history and Jewish life, and have devoted more attention to Manasseh ben Israel <note>Manasseh ben Israel (1604–1657) was a prominent Portuguese rabbi and teacher in Amsterdam who may have been one of Spinoza’s instructors.</note> than he has typically received in this connection. I have not found it necessary to provide detailed references to every authority because the earliest biographies and all available documents relating to Spinoza have been edited by Professor Freudenthal in a single volume titled *The Life History of Spinoza* <note>original German: *Die Lebensgeschichte Spinozas*</note>, and the evidence can easily be found there. For the general history of the period, I consulted the works of Motley, Blok, and the *Cambridge Modern History*; for the history of the Jews, I consulted Graetz <note>Heinrich Graetz (1817–1891) was a pioneering Jewish historian famous for his eleven-volume history of the Jewish people.</note>.

In the second part of the *Introduction*, I limited myself to a general statement of the history and context of the *Short Treatise* that can be understood without any prior knowledge of the *Treatise* itself, leaving specific details for the *Commentary*, where they are addressed as they arise. With the help of facsimiles <note>A facsimile is an exact copy or reproduction of an old manuscript or book.</note>, the reader is able to judge for themselves on various matters that would otherwise have to be taken on faith. In the preparation of this part and the rest of the volume, I found the writings of Professor Freudenthal, Dr. W. Meyer, and C. Sigwart very helpful, and I am also more or less indebted to the other writers mentioned on pages 127 and following <note>original: "cxxvii f." Wolf uses Roman numerals for his introductory page numbering.</note>, or in other parts of the volume.

In conclusion, I wish to acknowledge my obligations to everyone who helped me in any way. Dr. Byvanck (Librarian of the Royal Library at The Hague) and Mr. Chambers (Librarian of University College, London) enabled me to consult the manuscripts with as little inconvenience as possible. The Royal Society has given me permission to reproduce the facsimile on page 60. Professor S. Alexander of the University of Manchester has read the *Introduction* in proof and made valuable suggestions. I wish to thank them all very cordially, and I hope that the usefulness of the result may, in some measure, compensate for all the trouble given and taken in the preparation of this volume.

->A. WOLF<-

->HARROW, *November* 1909<-

It was originally written in Dutch and was lost to the public for nearly two centuries until its rediscovery in the mid-19th century.</note>

<meta>This page serves as the opening epigraph for the introductory section on the life of Spinoza, following the outline provided on the previous page.</meta>

> "Thus it stands before us, this life of a thinker, entirely dedicated to the truth, and in that very fact lies the sublime nature of its quiet greatness. For to die for the truth, it is said, is difficult—but it is even more difficult to live for it." <note>original: "So steht es vor uns, dies Denkerleben, ganz der Wahrheit geweiht, und darin eben beruht die Erhabenheit seiner stillen Grösse. Denn zu sterben für die Wahrheit, sagt man, sei schwer—schwerer ist es für sie zu leben."</note>— W. WINDELBAND, *In Memory of Spinoza*. <note>Wilhelm Windelband (1848–1915) was a distinguished German philosopher and historian of philosophy. This quote is taken from his address "Zum Gedächtnis Spinozas," delivered in 1877 to commemorate the 200th anniversary of Spinoza's death.</note>

<summary>This page features an epigraph by German philosopher Wilhelm Windelband that honors Spinoza's lifelong dedication to truth, suggesting that living for one's principles is a greater challenge than dying for them.</summary>
<keywords>Wilhelm Windelband, Spinoza, truth, philosophical life, quiet greatness, ethics</keywords>

<meta>This page begins the biographical account of the philosopher Baruch Spinoza, following the introductory epigraph by Wilhelm Windelband.</meta>

# THE LIFE OF SPINOZA

## § 1. HISTORICAL ANTECEDENTS

Baruch or Benedict <note>original: "Benedictus is simply the Latin equivalent of the Hebrew Baruch."</note> Spinoza was born to Jewish parents on November 24, 1632, in Amsterdam. At that time, the Jews of Amsterdam consisted almost entirely of refugees, or the children of refugees, who had escaped from Spain and Portugal. In those countries, they had lived as <term>crypto-Jews</term> <note>Also known as Marranos; these were individuals who secretly practiced Judaism while publicly maintaining a facade of Christianity to escape persecution.</note> in constant dread of the Inquisition.

Spain had been the home of Jewish people long before the introduction of Christianity. Under non-Christian rule, they enjoyed considerable power and prosperity. With the introduction of Christianity, however, came the desire to convert the Jews. Because the Church was not particularly ethical or scrupulous about the methods it employed, a series of intermittent atrocities began, which stained the history of medieval Christianity for many centuries. 

Fortunately for the Jews, these persecutions were neither universal nor constant. Hostility broke out here and there, but there were usually also safe locations and healthy members of society who were immune from this deadly disease of prejudice. While the fanaticism of the mob was often provoked by envy, the fanaticism of princes was, as a rule, overcome by their own personal interests. For the Jews of Spain included some of the bravest soldiers, some of the most capable Ministers of State, and, above all, some of the most resourceful financiers. The Kings of Spain and Portugal, accordingly, took the Jews under their protection, though they could not always prevent the outbreaks of violence which involved the loss of thousands of Jewish lives. During periods of peace, the Jews competed with their neighbors in

<summary>This page introduces the biography of Baruch Spinoza, detailing his birth in Amsterdam to a family of Jewish refugees and providing a historical overview of the Jewish experience in Spain and Portugal under Christian rule.</summary>
<keywords>Baruch Spinoza, Amsterdam, crypto-Jews, Inquisition, Spanish Jews, religious persecution, medieval history, Jewish refugees</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the historical overview of the Jewish community in Spain, focusing on their intellectual achievements and the subsequent persecution that led to their expulsion and the rise of the "crypto-Jewish" community.</meta>

# INTRODUCTION

...lives because of their devotion to literature, science, and philosophy. They produced eminent poets, celebrated doctors and astronomers, and highly influential philosophers. Indeed, the 10th, 11th, and 12th centuries have come to be regarded as the "golden age" of Jewish history since the dispersion <note>The "dispersion" or Diaspora refers to the scattering of the Jewish people from their ancestral homeland.</note>, primarily due to the prestige achieved by the Jews of Spain. 

But religious fanaticism never truly rested. The situation reached a breaking point in 1492 when, under the harmful influence of the Grand Inquisitor Torquemada, the Jews were expelled from Spain. This occurred despite the grand promises made by King Ferdinand and Queen Isabella while they still required Jewish assistance in their wars against the Moors. 

The Jews were offered a harsh choice: baptism or banishment. Those who preferred to go into exile rather than convert were prohibited from taking any gold or silver with them. More than 200,000 Jews paid the price for their religious loyalty and left their native land—the home of their ancestors for centuries. Many thousands of them met an early death due to the extreme hardships of their travels. 

Approximately 50,000, however, chose baptism and remained in Spain. Many of them remained Jews at heart, resisting the influence of the Jesuits with their own methods of secrecy until an opportunity arose to escape with their worldly possessions. Some of these <term>crypto-Jews</term> (also known as <term>Maranos</term> <note>The origin of the name *Marano* is uncertain. It seems to have been applied to the "New Christians" to mean "the damned," possibly referring to 1 Corinthians 16:22: "If anyone does not love the Lord, let him be accursed. Our Lord, come!" <note>original: "anathema maranatha"</note></note>), as well as many of the original exiles of 1492, found a temporary refuge in Portugal. 

But this safety lasted only a short time. Soon, the agents of the Inquisition were hunting for any sign of Jewish practice among the "New Christians" in Portugal, just as they had in Spain. Even the most trivial excuse was used as sufficient evidence for prosecution. Countless converts, or their descendants...

<summary>This page describes the end of the Jewish "golden age" in Spain, culminating in the 1492 expulsion and the rise of "Maranos"—Jews who lived as secret practitioners while publicly appearing Christian to escape the Inquisition.</summary>
<keywords>Spanish Inquisition, 1492 Expulsion, Maranos, crypto-Jews, Torquemada, Ferdinand and Isabella, New Christians, Jewish Diaspora</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the history of the Jewish "converts" in Spain and Portugal, describing their persecution by the Inquisition and the expansion of these repressive policies into the Netherlands under the Spanish crown.</meta>

xiii
THE LIFE OF SPINOZA

...of converts were condemned to the dungeon, the rack, and the stake without mercy, while princes and priests shared the spoils without hesitation. It is no wonder that the eyes of Spanish and Portuguese <term>Maranos</term> <note>original: Maranos; a term for Jews who converted to Christianity under pressure but often practiced Judaism in secret.</note> were always searching for cities of refuge. About a century after the expulsion from Spain, good news arrived from the rebelling Netherlands.

Not content with the wholesale expulsion and slaughter of Jews and Moors, the Spanish <term>Inquisition</term> <note>The tribunal established by the Catholic Church to find and punish heresy.</note> turned its attention to all Christians who were suspected in any way of the slightest disloyalty to Roman Catholicism. The work of this "Holy Office" was vastly expanded in scope when the religious policy of Ferdinand and Isabella was adopted by their grandson, the Emperor Charles V. He desired nothing less than the entire extermination of all heresies and heretics, so that the world and everything in it might be reserved exclusively for Roman Catholics, with the Emperor at their head. In accordance with his policy, he issued various edicts for the elimination of sects and heresies and introduced the <term>Inquisition</term> into the Netherlands, which is our only concern here. 

Upon the abdication of Charles in 1555, his son, King Philip II, continued his father's religious policy, but with far greater zeal. Within a month of ascending the throne, he re-enacted his father's edicts against heresy. Four years later, he obtained a <term>Bull</term> <note>original: Bulla; an official papal decree named after the lead seal, or *bulla*, used to authenticate it.</note> from Pope Paul IV for an ominous strengthening of the Church in the Netherlands. Instead of the four existing bishoprics, there were to be three archbishoprics with fifteen bishoprics under them. Each bishop was to appoint nine additional <term>prebendaries</term> <note>Clergymen who held a specific administrative position within a cathedral or collegiate church.</note> to assist him in matters regarding the <term>Inquisition</term>, two of whom were to be inquisitors themselves. Four thousand Spanish troops were stationed in the Netherlands, and the government was more or less in the hands of Anthony Perrenot, the Archbishop of Mechlin (better known as Cardinal Granvelle). He was a man after Philip's own heart—a kind of Torquemada—along with his subordinate, the

<summary>This page describes the brutal reach of the Spanish Inquisition under Charles V and Philip II, specifically focusing on how these repressive religious policies and expanded church hierarchies were forced upon the Netherlands.</summary>
<keywords>Maranos, Spanish Inquisition, Charles V, Philip II, Pope Paul IV, Netherlands, Cardinal Granvelle, religious persecution, Bishoprics</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the historical background of the Dutch revolt against Spanish rule and the religious persecution of the Inquisition, setting the stage for the tolerant environment of the Netherlands where Spinoza's family would eventually seek refuge.</meta>

xiv
INTRODUCTION

The inquisitor Peter Titelmann swept through the country like a tempest, dragging away whole families to their destruction without being held accountable by anyone. Fortunately for the Netherlands, William of Orange—the <term>Stadtholder</term> <note>A title for the chief magistrate or provincial governor in the Dutch Republic.</note> of Holland, Zeeland, and Utrecht—had discovered from King Henry II of France the full scale of King Philip II’s bloody schemes for the total elimination of religious dissenters. Although William was still a Catholic himself at that time, he was repulsed by such heartless inhumanity committed under the guise of religion and determined to watch and wait. 

In the meantime, the "holy" inquisitors had many opportunities to satisfy their unholy thirst for violence. Situated between France and Germany, the Netherlands were naturally influenced by the Calvinism of the former and the Lutheranism of the latter. Under these circumstances, giving unlimited power to the Inquisition effectively meant sentencing an entire nation to death. 

The people were furious. Various leagues and alliances were formed. For a time, the situation appeared so threatening that the Regent, Margaret of Parma—a devoted follower of the Jesuit founder Ignatius of <term>Loyola</term>—granted a decree known as the *Accord* in 1566, which abolished the Inquisition. However, this was only a tactic to gain time by tricking the poorly-coordinated rebels. 

The following year, 1567, the Duke of Alva appeared on the scene; he was the most bloodthirsty and unscrupulous villain of his entire generation. He brought ten thousand veteran troops with him to "purge" the Netherlands of heretics. This began a grim struggle for existence that would last for eighty long years (1567–1647). <note>The Eighty Years' War officially ended with the Peace of Münster in 1648.</note> 

After various successes and failures, the seven northern provinces were largely abandoned by the ten southern provinces. In 1579, these northern provinces joined together in the Union of Utrecht to defend one another “with life, goods, and blood” against the forces of the King of Spain. At the same time, they decreed that “every citizen shall remain free in his religion, and that no man shall be molested or questioned on the subject of divine worship.” The United Provinces managed to hold their own under the leadership of “Father [William]...” <note>A reference to William the Silent, known as the "Father of the Fatherland."</note>

<summary>This page describes the brutal persecution by the Inquisition in the Netherlands and the subsequent rise of the Dutch Revolt, culminating in the Union of Utrecht and its landmark decree of religious freedom.</summary>
<keywords>Peter Titelmann, William of Orange, Duke of Alva, Margaret of Parma, Union of Utrecht, religious freedom, Inquisition, Dutch Revolt, Eighty Years' War</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the historical background of the Dutch Republic's rise and the arrival of the first Sephardic Jewish refugees (Maranos) in Amsterdam, highlighting the connection between the Dutch revolt and the search for religious freedom.</meta>

xv
# THE LIFE OF SPINOZA

"William," <note>Referring to William the Silent, Prince of Orange, who was a central leader of the Dutch revolt.</note> the silent but sleepless guardian of his country's fortunes. Commerce also soon revived, for Dutch sailors were more than a match for the Spaniards, whom the English also helped to cripple, notably by the destruction of the Great Armada in 1588.

The Netherland revolt against Spain and the Inquisition was, we may be sure, followed with keen interest by the Spanish and Portuguese <term>Maranos</term>, <note>original: "Maranos"; a term for Sephardic Jews in Spain and Portugal who were forced to convert to Christianity but often practiced Judaism in secret.</note> who had their relatives and agents in all the European centers of commerce. The decree of toleration included in the Union of Utrecht <note>A 1579 treaty that unified the northern provinces of the Netherlands and laid the foundation for religious freedom in the new Dutch Republic.</note> seemed to hold out some promise to them; and the lot of the Maranos was not likely to improve—indeed, their needs only became more urgent—when Portugal was conquered by Spain in 1579 <note>Philip II of Spain claimed the Portuguese throne in 1580 after a succession crisis beginning in 1579.</note>.

About the year 1591, there arrived in Amsterdam a new consul from the King of Morocco. The consul's name was Samuel Pallache, and he was a Jew. He commenced negotiations with the magistrates of Middelburg, in Zeeland, for the settlement of Portuguese Maranos there. The religious temper of the clergy made the negotiations fruitless. But the Portuguese Maranos were in such desperate straits that some of them resolved to seek refuge in Holland without any preliminary arrangements, relying simply on the natural sympathy of the Dutch for all fellow victims of Philip II and the Inquisition.

Accordingly, in 1593, the first group of Marano fugitives arrived in Amsterdam. They had sailed from Oporto and had an adventurous voyage. They were captured by English <term>buccaneers</term> <note>Pirates or privateers who specifically targeted Spanish interests in the Atlantic.</note> and taken to London. They owed their release chiefly to the bewitching beauty of one of their number, the fair Maria Nunes, who had an audience with Queen Elizabeth and actually drove with her in an open carriage through the streets of London. An English Duke offered her his hand in marriage, but the beautiful Marano declined the honor, being determined to return to the religion of her ancestors.

Such was the spirit of these fugitive Maranos who settled in Amsterdam and secretly returned to Judaism. The secret leaked out in 1596. They

header: INTRODUCTION

...they were celebrating the Day of Atonement at the house of the above-mentioned Pallache when their mysterious gathering aroused the suspicion of their neighbors. Armed men then arrived on the scene and arrested the surprised worshippers, who were suspected of being Catholics <note>The original text uses "Papists," a term for Roman Catholics that was often used disparagingly.</note>. But when it was explained that they had fled from the Inquisition, that they had brought significant wealth with them, and that they would do their utmost to promote the commercial prosperity of Amsterdam, they were set free and left in peace. Two years later, in 1598, they were allowed to acquire their first place of worship, though formal permission was not given to the Jews to hold public worship until 1619, nor were they recognized as citizens until 1657. In any case, the first Jews settled in Amsterdam in 1593, and others soon followed from Spain, Portugal, France, and elsewhere. What interests us here is that among these early arrivals were Abraham Michael d'Espinoza and his son Michael, who was to be the father of our philosopher, Benedict Spinoza.

### § 2. THE HOME OF SPINOZA

The name Spinoza (also written variously as *Espinosa*, *d'Espinoza*, *Despinoza*, and *De Spinoza*) was most probably derived from *Espinosa*, a town in Leon. The Spinozas lived originally in Spain. During the persecutions there, some of them seem to have outwardly embraced Christianity. (As late as 1721, eight of their descendants, living in or near Granada, were condemned to life imprisonment as "Judaizing heretics" <note>A term for individuals who secretly practiced Jewish rituals while outwardly identifying as Christians.</note>.) Some fled to Portugal, others to France, but they met again in Amsterdam as soon as it became known that Jews were tolerated there. Benedict's grandfather is twice described in the synagogue archives as Abraham Espinosa of Nantes, from which it

header: THE LIFE OF SPINOZA

It would appear that he lived there for some time. On the other hand, it seems that Michael (his son and the father of Benedict) stayed at one time in Figueras, near Coimbra, and that his third wife came from Lisbon. As tradition unanimously describes Spinoza as being of Portuguese descent, it seems reasonable to suppose that his father and grandfather came from Spain or Portugal, and that their stay in France was only brief.

Very little is known of Spinoza’s father and grandfather. They were merchants and were evidently held in high esteem. For as early as 1622, we find Abraham Espinosa filling an important honorary office in the Amsterdam Jewish community, of which he seems to have been the recognized head in 1628. His son, Michael Espinosa, held office even more frequently. He was Warden of his Synagogue in 1633, 1637–38, 1642–43, and again in 1649–50, when he was also one of the Wardens of the Amsterdam Jewish School and presided over the charity for granting interest-free loans. If not rich, he was probably well-to-do. In 1641, it is true, we still find him living in the *Vloyenburgh*, but at that time this was probably not the poor quarter it later became. Soon afterward, however, he moved into the *Houtgragt* (now the *Waterlooplein*), and the house where he lived during the final years of his life still looks substantial today. It is numbered 41 and can also be identified by a stone tablet (placed there in 1743) which bears the inscription “’t Oprechte Tapijthuis” (The Upright Tapestry House). But, whatever his worldly fortune may have been, Michael had more than his share of domestic sorrow. His first wife died in 1627. His second wife, Hannah Deborah, the mother of Benedict, died in 1638. He married again in 1641, but his third wife, a lady from Lisbon, also died before him in 1652. The year before, in 1651, his daughter, Miriam, died at the age of 22, and only a little more than a year after her marriage to

Samuel de Casseres. Michael had also lost three other children, and only two of his six children—namely, Benedict and a daughter, Rebekah (born of the first marriage)—survived him when he died shortly afterward, in 1654.

Spinoza’s childhood was no doubt happy enough. Until he was five, he would have been entirely under his mother’s care, as was the Jewish custom. Then his school life would begin, with its charming introductory ceremony. The ceremony connected with the young boy's entrance into school life was probably one of the last and happiest of his poor mother’s experiences. It was performed partly in school and partly in the synagogue, of which his father was Warden at the time. According to traditional custom, a young girl baked three cakes of fine flour and honey for the boy, and fruit was provided in abundance. One of his father's learned friends would carry him in his arms to the synagogue, where he would be placed on the reading dais while the Ten Commandments were read from the Scroll of the Law. Then he would be taken to school to receive his first lesson in Hebrew. Some simple Hebrew verses would be smeared on a slate with honey, and little Baruch would repeat the Hebrew letters and eat the honey and other treats, so that the words of the Law might be sweet to his lips. And then back into his mother's arms!

Unfortunately, his mother died when Baruch was barely six years old, and for the next three years or so, he was left in the care of his half-sister, Rebekah, who may not have been more than twelve years old herself. Judging by subsequent events, there was probably not much love lost between Rebekah and Baruch. For when their father died in 1654, she did her utmost to prevent Benedict from receiving his share of the inheritance; he went to court, though he let her keep nearly everything after he had won the lawsuit. At his death, her conduct was also not

exemplary; she hastened to the Hague to claim her inheritance, but left again as soon as she learned that the property left was hardly enough to cover his debts and funeral expenses. All this, however, still belonged to the future. In the meantime, one may well imagine the moving picture of the child standing by his mother's grave and lisping the mourner's prayer in Hebrew, which he had only just begun to learn. For nearly a whole year afterward, he could be seen twice or three times each day in the neighboring synagogue, reciting aloud that same mourner's prayer with a mysterious feeling of awe and solemnity, yet glad nonetheless to be doing something for his poor mother. Each anniversary of her death would be commemorated by a special light that was kept burning at home for twenty-four hours in memory of a light that had failed, but was believed to still be shedding its rays in another sphere. And the solemn days of the Jewish calendar were only made more solemn for him by tender memories of "the touch of a vanished hand, and the sound of a voice that was still."

We must not, however, exaggerate the sad side of young Spinoza's life—though it certainly had its sad side. When he was in his ninth year, he received a stepmother. Being only a recent Marrano refugee from Lisbon, she may not have been exactly the kind of woman to inspire young Spinoza with any especially warm attachment to Judaism. Like so many Marranos, she may have been half Catholic in her training, due to the necessity of outward conformity to Roman Catholicism. Still, she was probably kind to the children, and the home would resume its normal tone. The Jewish calendar, moreover, has its joyous festivals, even its frivolous carnival; and a good Jew like Michael Espinosa was not likely to neglect his religious duty to be merry and to make others merry on these occasions. First, there was the weekly Sabbath and Sabbath eve (Friday evening), so often and so justly celebrated in verse—even by Heine, in his

Then there were the three Pilgrim Festivals, Passover, Pentecost, and Tabernacles, all of them essentially joyous in character. On the first two evenings of Passover especially, children play an important *role*. One can easily imagine the important air with which little Baruch opened the domestic celebrations on these occasions by asking the meaning of such strange dishes as bitter herbs, a yellow-looking mixture of almonds, cinnamon and apples, etc. By way of answer, his father would then relate to the assembled household the old, yet ever new story of the bitter lives which the Israelites had lived in Egypt, of the bricks and mortar with which they had to build Pithom and Ramses under cruel taskmasters, until God delivered them from

their oppressors. And the familiar story of ancient Egypt and its tyrants would soon lead up to the more recent barbarities in Spain and Portugal. Possibly, indeed most probably, there were strangers as guests at the table—for hospitality had become not a luxury, but a necessity among the wandering Jews. Perhaps some recent arrival, fresh from the hell-fires of the Inquisition, would relate the latest story of martyrdom. On such an occasion, it may have been that Spinoza heard of the martyrdom of “a certain Judah, called the Faithful, who in the midst of the flames, and when he was already believed to be dead, began to chant the psalm *To you, O God, I commit my soul*, and died singing it.”* But the fundamental themes of the Passover evening celebrations were those of courage and faith that the Guardian of Israel neither slumbered nor slept.

There were also other celebrations of Israel’s deliverance in the past. There was the Feast of Lights, or of the Rededication of the Temple (*Chanukah*), in memory of the brave Maccabees. An entire week was more or less spent as a half-holiday, devoted to games and merriment. The spirit of the holiday is well expressed in a cheerful table hymn composed by Ibn Ezra, the poet and commentator whom Spinoza regarded so highly. The following are the opening stanzas:

> “Eat dainty foods and fine,
> And bread baked well and white,
> With pigeons and red wine,
> On this Sabbath *Chanukah* night.

> “Your belongings and your lands
> Go and pledge, go and sell!
> Put money in your hands,
> To feast *Chanukah* well!” †

<page-num>xxii</page-num>
<header>INTRODUCTION</header>

Then there was the Feast of Lots (*Purim*) in celebration of Israel’s escape from the evil designs of Haman, as told in the *Book of Esther*. As the life of the Jew would become intolerably solemn if all his persecutors were taken seriously, Haman was treated more like a clown than a villain, and the half-holiday associated with his name was celebrated as a kind of carnival, when it was considered wrong to be serious, and when witty people readily indulged in parodying even Rabbis and prayers, and had full license to make fools of themselves and of others. Above all, it was the occasion for plays, *Purim* plays, as they were called. At that time, these were not yet formal plays, but informal buffooneries linked to the story of Ahasuerus and Haman, or, by way of variety, focusing on the story of the Sale of Joseph, or David’s encounter with Goliath, and the like. On one such occasion, Spinoza may have witnessed a play written by one of his senior schoolmates, Moses Zacuto, whose *L’Inferno Figurato* (written in Hebrew) expressed the writer’s contempt for the Inquisition. The hero of the story was Abraham, whose steadfastness against Nimrod and legendary escape from the fiery furnace were intended to represent the Jewish fortunes in Spain.

Lastly, mention may also be made of what may roughly be described as a kind of Confirmation ceremony when Spinoza completed his thirteenth year. On that Sabbath, he would chant aloud in the Synagogue a portion of the Law, or Pentateuch, and possibly also the portion from the Prophets appointed to be read on that day. After the service in the Synagogue, his father would entertain all his friends at home in honor of the occasion, and young Baruch would, according to custom, make a speech at the table. This speech would, of course, have been carefully prepared by him for the occasion, not without the assistance of his teacher; and filial gratitude for the past and lavish promises for the future would begin and end a more

<header>THE LIFE OF SPINOZA</header>

...or a less learned discourse. One would like to know what he actually said, and what he thought of it all afterward!

In the meantime, his time must have been fully occupied. He was at school from 8:00 until 11:00 each morning, and from 2:00 until 5:00 in the afternoon on weekdays. Some of the hours when he was not at school were spent on school preparation, and also in the study of secular subjects under a private teacher or teachers. He most likely continued to study at the Jewish school or academy until he was eighteen, providing him the opportunity to develop the uncommon ability he had already clearly demonstrated at the age of fifteen through the challenging questions he asked Rabbi Morteira. At eighteen, it was high time to consider a means of making a living. His brother (or half-brother) Isaac died around that time. His father may have thought of bringing him into the family business. However, Spinoza’s interests did not lie in business. He preferred to seek a means of support in an occupation that would keep him in touch with science and scholarship. This likely led him to learn the art of polishing lenses, a craft pursued by many learned men of his generation. By that time, he may have already shown some of his heretical tendencies, and these may have caused some friction at home. This was possibly the reason why his half-sister Rebekah and his brother-in-law de Casseres tried soon afterward to exclude him from his share of the inheritance his father left when he died. Spinoza, however, could hardly have been so inconsiderate as to cause his father unnecessary pain; he likely kept most of his doubts to himself and remained in his father’s house as long as his father lived—that is to say, until March 1654, when he was in his twenty-second year.

<summary>Spinoza balanced a rigorous education in religious and secular subjects with a burgeoning interest in science, eventually choosing lens polishing over the family business to support his scholarship. Despite his developing heretical views and family tensions, he lived in his father's home until the latter's death in 1654.</summary>

### § 3. THE EDUCATION OF SPINOZA

It is not difficult to describe the general features of Spinoza’s early education. The Amsterdam Jewish community had its own boys' school, which was founded around 1638, and which all Jewish boys would attend as a matter of course. We know the general curriculum of this school from contemporary accounts. We also know the names and characters of some of its most important teachers during Spinoza's time. There were seven classes in the school. In the lowest class, little boys were taught to read their prayers in Hebrew. In the second class, they learned to read and chant the Pentateuch in Hebrew. In the next class, they were taught to translate parts of the Pentateuch from Hebrew into Spanish (which continued to be the mother tongue for many Amsterdam Jews for a long time, despite the harsh treatment that had been dealt to them and their fathers in Spain). Here they also began to study Rashi’s Hebrew Commentary on the Pentateuch—a commentary written in the eleventh century, but remarkably rational for its time. The boys in the fourth class studied the *Prophets* and the *Hagiographa*. In the remaining higher classes, they studied Hebrew Grammar, portions of the Talmud and of the later Hebrew Codes, and the works of Ibn Ezra, Maimonides, and others, according to the discretion of the Rabbi who instructed and advised them. The school hours were from 8:00 to 11:00 A.M. and from 2:00 to 5:00 P.M. (or earlier during the winter months). We are explicitly informed that during the hours the boys were at home, they would receive private tuition in secular subjects, even in poetry. The school also possessed a good lending library.

Of the teachers under whose influence Spinoza must have come during his school days, the most important

<summary>This section describes the structured seven-class curriculum of the Amsterdam Jewish boys' school attended by Spinoza, which balanced traditional Hebrew studies with secular private tutoring. <meta>This page continues the discussion of Spinoza's teachers, focusing on the lives and characters of Saul Morteira and Manasseh ben Israel.</meta>

<page-num>xxv</page-num>
<header>THE LIFE OF SPINOZA</header>

undoubtedly were Rabbi Saul Morteira and Rabbi Manasseh ben Israel. Saul Morteira was the senior Rabbi of Amsterdam. Born in Venice about 1596, he studied medicine under Montalto, the Marano Court physician of Maria de' Medici. Montalto died suddenly while accompanying Louis XIII to Tours in 1616, and it was the desire to bury Montalto in a Jewish cemetery that brought Saul Morteira to Amsterdam, where the Jews had only recently (1614) acquired a cemetery in Ouwerkerk (also called Ouderkerk), not very far from the city. While in Amsterdam, Morteira accepted a call to the Rabbinate of the older of the two synagogues there (the *House of Jacob*). A third synagogue came into existence two years later, but in 1638 the three synagogues were amalgamated, and Morteira acted as the senior or presiding Rabbi until his death in 1660. Morteira had had a taste of court life and was not altogether lacking in philosophical appreciation; but he was essentially medieval, strait-laced, prosy, and uninspiring. It is related that when Spinoza was only fifteen years old, Morteira marveled at the boy's acumen. By an irony of fate, he also presided over the court of Rabbis who issued the ban against Spinoza in 1656.

In Manasseh ben Israel we have a different type of character altogether. He was born in 1604 and had a tragic infancy. His father, Joseph ben Israel, was one of a hundred and fifty Jews whom the Inquisition in Lisbon was about to consign to the flames in 1605, when Mammon was successfully enlisted against the priests of Moloch. A million gold florins, eight hundred thousand ducats, and five hundred thousand crusados were paid to King Philip III, and a hundred thousand crusados to the saintly ecclesiastics; they then became reconciled to spare their victims the flames of hell on earth, even if it should entail their loss of heaven hereafter. At the *auto-da-fé* in January 1605, the unhappy Jews were paraded in penitential garb and

<meta>This page continues the account of Manasseh ben Israel’s family fleeing the Inquisition. After paying a massive bribe to King Philip III and the Church, the prisoners were spared from being burned at the stake.</meta>

<page-num>xxvi</page-num>
<header>INTRODUCTION</header>

...made a formal confession of their secret and most sinful loyalty to the religion of Moses <note>The OCR text reads "Jesus," but contextually and historically, this refers to the religion of Moses (Judaism), which the Inquisition prosecuted as a "sinful" crime.</note> and of the Prophets. The King graciously obtained papal absolution for their "heinous crime," and they were dismissed—alive, it is true, but wrecked in health by torture, and robbed of their possessions by a Catholic king and holy priests. Joseph ben Israel naturally fled, at the very first opportunity, with his wife and their infant son Manasseh. They went to Amsterdam, where Manasseh lived nearly all his life. He succeeded his teacher, Rabbi Uzziel, as Rabbi of the second Amsterdam Synagogue (the *Habitation of Peace*) in 1622, when he was barely eighteen years old; he started a Hebrew printing house about the year 1627; and in 1640 he was about to emigrate to Brazil when he received an important appointment in the senior department of the Amsterdam Jewish School, where Spinoza must have come under his influence. 

Manasseh was not a great thinker, but he was a great reader, and he made up in breadth of outlook for what he lacked in depth of insight. Like so many contemporary theologians, he was inclined toward mysticism, it is true; but there was a touch of romance in his character, and, urged by an irresistible yearning to help his suffering brothers, his very mysticism with all its trivialities played a useful part: it prompted him to schemes which may indeed appear quixotic, and which certainly brought his life to an untimely end, but which bore fruit nevertheless, and were well-suited to bear fruit in an age in which religion and superstition—the flame and the smoke—were so curiously intermingled. What he conceived to be the mission of his life is indicated in the Biblical verse with which he headed the dedication of his *Hope of Israel* (1650). The book, it is interesting to observe, was dedicated to Spinoza’s father and the other wardens of the Jewish school. At the head of the dedication is the first verse from Isaiah 41 <note>The text cites Isaiah 41, though the quote provided is from Isaiah 61:1.</note>: *To preach good tidings to the meek; he has sent me to bind up the broken-hearted.*

<summary>This page details Manasseh ben Israel's move to Amsterdam after his family escaped the Inquisition, his rapid rise to become a Rabbi and printer, and his eventual role as a teacher to Spinoza. It characterizes him as a romantic and mystical figure whose dedication to his people led to idealistic but influential projects.</summary>
<keywords>Manasseh ben Israel, Joseph ben Israel, Amsterdam, Spinoza, Rabbi Uzziel, Hebrew printing-house, Hope of Israel, Isaiah 41:1, Inquisition</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the biography of Manasseh ben Israel, following his appointment to the senior department of the Amsterdam Jewish School and his role as a likely influence on the young Spinoza.</meta>

<page-num>xxvii</page-num>
<header>THE LIFE OF SPINOZA</header>

In 1655, Manasseh came to England on a special mission to Oliver Cromwell to advocate for the readmission of the Jews into England. Two years later he returned to the Netherlands, carrying with him the body of his eldest son. His great schemes seemed shattered. Poor, prematurely aged, and full of sorrows, he died at Middelburg in 1657.

Manasseh ben Israel was a prolific writer, and his books show undeniable evidence of very wide reading and extraordinary industry. He cites not only Jewish writers like Ibn Gabriol and Maimonides, but also Euripides and Virgil, Plato and Aristotle, Duns Scotus and Albertus Magnus. Poets and legalists, mystics and rationalists—he had an appreciation for them all, even if it was not always a very sophisticated appreciation. He rather prided himself on his secular knowledge and felt flattered when he was described not simply as a "theologian," but also as a "philosopher" and "Doctor of Medicine." <note>The text says "Physics," which in the 17th century referred to medicine.</note> On a portrait engraved in 1642, he is described as "*Theologicus et Philosophus Hebræus.*" <note>Hebrew Theologian and Philosopher</note> Moreover, he had numerous Christian acquaintances and friends, and corresponded with learned men and women in all parts of Europe—even with Queen Christina of Sweden and Hugo Grotius, the famous statesman, jurist, and historian. In various letters to Vossius, Grotius expressed his great and sincere esteem for Manasseh. Gerhard Vossius, "the greatest polyhistor <note>A person of great and varied learning; a universal scholar.</note> of the Netherlands," was on intimate terms with Manasseh and visited him often. Nor was Manasseh at all intolerant. He was very friendly with Caspar Barlæus, the Amsterdam Professor of Philosophy and Rhetoric, who was somewhat suspected of being a free-thinker. Barlæus was a noted Latin scholar and poet, and he added as a preface to one of Manasseh's books (*De Creatione*) a Latin poem which was

scarcely orthodox. We also hear of Manasseh’s presence at a merry gathering in the house of Episcopius in honor of Sobierre, a noted French wit. On occasion, Manasseh would also introduce some of his Jewish friends to his Christian acquaintances. In one of his letters to a professor at Leyden, Vossius mentions that Manasseh had just paid him a visit and brought with him a Portuguese Jew whom he desired to recommend for a medical degree. It does not seem unreasonable to suppose that Manasseh ben Israel exercised a potent personal influence over Spinoza, who must have studied under him for a number of years. This is not to say that Manasseh was competent to make any direct contribution toward the development of Spinoza’s philosophy. But his indirect influence must have been considerable. After all, the greatest service that even the best teacher can render does not consist so much in the actual information that he imparts as in the stimulus that he gives and the love of truth that he instills. And Manasseh, as we have seen, was a man of wide culture, of broad sympathy, and truly devoted to scholarship. What is more likely than that he should use his influence with Spinoza’s father so that Baruch might be taught Latin and other secular subjects? And what is more natural than that Manasseh—who encouraged and helped his young Christian friend, a son of Gerhard Vossius, to study and translate Maimonides—should have been even more eager to urge his Jewish students to study their own Hispano-Jewish literature, of which they were so justly proud?

At the house of his rabbi, Spinoza would occasionally meet Christians who were interested in Judaism or in the Jewish interpretation of the Old Testament. Here also he may have met Rembrandt, who, between 1640 and 1656, lived in the very heart of the Jewish quarter and was probably on friendly terms with “the Amsterdam Rabbi,” as Manasseh was called. For Rembrandt etched a portrait of

Moreover, in the Hermitage at St. Petersburg, there is a Rembrandt painting of a Rabbi, aged and worn, which is believed to be Rabbi Manasseh ben Israel. If so, we must suppose that Rembrandt, hearing of the return and illness of his old friend of twenty years or more, hurried to him in Middelburg. Deeply impressed by the tragic change that had come over the once-handsome but now prematurely aged and broken-down Rabbi, he embodied his impression in that portrait. Perhaps it was the art of Rembrandt that inspired young Spinoza to try his hand at drawing. For we are told that Spinoza was an amateur draftsman, and his early biographer, Colerus, actually possessed a number of ink and charcoal sketches which Spinoza had made of his friends, including one of Spinoza himself in the costume of Masaniello (Thomas Aniello), who in 1647 led the Neapolitan revolt against Spain and was murdered soon afterward. In any case, it is known that Spinoza had a number of Christian acquaintances and friends at a very early stage in his career, and that he helped some of them in the study of the Hebrew Bible; it is not improbable that he was first introduced to some of them by Manasseh ben Israel, the courteous and easily accessible Rabbi, whom they first consulted when they took up the study of Hebrew. And it is probably more than a mere accident that Spinoza knew and corresponded with Isaac, the son of Gerhard Vossius, and possessed copies of some of the works of both, as well as those of Grotius and even Delmedigo—all of them friends of Manasseh, whose own book, *The Hope of Israel*, Spinoza also possessed.

Last, though by no means least, there was the moral earnestness of Manasseh. He was a dedicated disciple of a dedicated master. His teacher and predecessor in office, Rabbi Uzziel, was known for his moral courage. It was

<header>INTRODUCTION</header>

...his outspoken condemnation of the moral laxity of a part of the Amsterdam Jewish community led to a schism in the young congregation and the formation of a third congregation in 1618. For reasons already explained, some members of the community had been Roman Catholics for several generations and had grown dangerously accustomed to the habit of obtaining priestly absolution for moral failings. Rabbi Uzziel would have none of it. Like the prophets of old, he would make no truce with immorality and denounced it without regard for status. Manasseh ben Israel also had the reputation of being a sincere and eloquent preacher, and he probably passed on some of his master's moral earnestness to his pupil Spinoza. No doubt young Spinoza could and did draw from the wells of living water; no doubt he could and did draw moral inspiration from the prophetic books themselves. Nevertheless, a living example of their moral tone could not fail to intensify his sensitivity to that spirit of the prophets which Spinoza's own writings still breathe.*

The school curriculum, though fairly encyclopedic in its range of subjects, was entirely in Hebrew. Other languages and the more modern sciences, or the more modern treatment of them, had to be studied outside the school. He learned Spanish and Portuguese from his parents; Dutch, from his environment. Morteira, who was Venetian by birth, may have taught him some Italian; and Manasseh ben Israel, some French. We are informed that he learned Latin from a German scholar, possibly a certain Jeremiah Felbinger—a man of a rather unorthodox reputation who may also have taught him German. The study of Latin was not popular among the Jews at that time. It was too closely associated with Roman Catholicism and the Inquisition. In fact, it was common

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Spinoza’s education, transitioning from his Hebrew studies to his pursuit of secular knowledge and the study of Latin, which was controversial in the Jewish community at the time.</meta>

<page-num>xxxi</page-num>
<header>THE LIFE OF SPINOZA</header>

Among the Jews, it was common to speak of Latin as “the priests’ language.” Therefore, a knowledge of Latin was not a common accomplishment for Jews then. A certain Mochinger, writing to Manasseh ben Israel in 1632, complained that in Bohemia and Germany he had not come across any Jew who had learned even the basics of Latin; he went on to encourage Manasseh to persevere with his Latin and to teach it to others as well. Even in Amsterdam, where—as the same writer states—there were a number of Jews who knew Latin well, it was viewed with suspicion as the medium of a worldly wisdom. This "worldly wisdom," much like the “Greek wisdom” of ancient times, was suspected (not without reason) of leading people away from Judaism. Spinoza’s schoolmate, Moses Zacuto—to whom reference has already been made above, and who began as a poet and ended as a mystic—actually fasted for forty days as an act of penance for his early devotion to Latin.

If, therefore, Spinoza studied Latin, it may be taken for granted that he also pursued other secular studies, especially mathematics (which he is reported to have studied under an Italian) and physics. He soon required both of these for his work in optics, and they may actually have led him to learn the art of polishing lenses. He probably also studied the later scholastic philosophy as it was explained around that time in the works of Burgersdijck, a Professor of Philosophy at Leiden (who died in 1632), and by his successor, Heereboord (who died in 1659).

In 1652, Francis van den Enden—a former Jesuit, former diplomat, former bookseller, doctor, and classical scholar—opened a school in Amsterdam, and Spinoza went there to complete his secular studies. Van den Enden was certainly unconventional in his beliefs and was strongly suspected of atheism. Colerus relates that some of Van den Enden's former students “blessed the memory of their parents every day for taking care to remove them in time from the school of such a harmful and ungodly master.” But he was admittedly a capable teacher, and Spinoza, no doubt, owed to him his mastery of Latin, also

<header>INTRODUCTION</header>

...what little knowledge he had of Greek, the advancement of his medical and physical knowledge, and most likely also his first introduction to the philosophy of Descartes, whose recent death in 1650 must have attracted renewed attention to his writings. Van den Enden, as we shall see, was also kind to Spinoza in other ways and certainly deserved something better than the tragic fate that befell him.

In March 1654, Spinoza's father died. Spinoza now had to provide for his own livelihood. His "schooling" was finished. A new period began for him.

### § 4. SPINOZA'S ALIENATION FROM THE SYNAGOGUE—1654-1656

Spinoza had an innate passion for clear and consistent thinking. The great intellectual gifts with which fortune had generously endowed him were thoroughly exercised and sharpened through the long study of Hebrew legal and religious codes. These are full of subtle problems and even subtler solutions. Whatever Spinoza may have later thought of their inherent merits, their value as mental training was undeniable. However, this power of insight was slowly but inevitably bringing him into conflict with the very sources from which it had drawn its strength. Furthermore, quite apart from the sharpening of his reasoning skills, his Hebrew studies also provided him with plenty of material and motivation for the exercise of his critical insight. The spirit of rationalism permeates the entire literature of the Jews during the Spanish period,<note>See the author's Aristotle in Medieval Jewish Thought.</note> and the masterpieces of that literature were the pride of the Jewish refugees from the Iberian Peninsula—indeed, of all Jews. In the commentary of Abraham Ibn Ezra (1092–1167), he found many bold and suggestive hints. In the Preface,

<summary>Following the death of his father in 1654, Spinoza began his independent life; however, his rigorous study of Hebrew law and Jewish rationalist philosophy began to distance him intellectually from the Synagogue.</summary>
<keywords>Spinoza, Descartes, Francis van den Enden, Hebrew codes, Jewish rationalism, Abraham Ibn Ezra, Spanish period</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Jewish intellectual influences on Spinoza, specifically focusing on how medieval commentators like Ibn Ezra, Maimonides, and Gersonides provided the foundations for his rationalist critique of Scripture.</meta>

<page-num>xxxiii</page-num>
<header>THE LIFE OF SPINOZA</header>

Ibn Ezra states that he “will show no partiality in the exposition of the Law,” and although the promise seems bolder than the fulfillment, yet every now and then one meets with “a word to the wise” which is just sufficient to direct attention to some inconsistency in Scripture, to the post-Mosaic authorship of certain passages in the so-called Five Books of Moses, or to the different authorship of the first and second parts of Isaiah. These hints, obscure as they may seem, justify Ibn Ezra’s claim to be called “the father of the <term>Higher Criticism</term> of the Bible,” and they certainly led to Spinoza’s subsequent important contributions to this kind of Biblical criticism. In the *Guide of the Perplexed* by Moses Maimonides (1135–1204), his attention was drawn to certain crudities and inconsistencies in Biblical theology, which Maimonides, indeed, tried to explain away or to reconcile with the requirements of reason, though apparently, in the judgment of Spinoza, with little success. And Maimonides’ treatment of the institution of sacrifices as merely a temporary concession or device to wean Israel from idolatry could not help but suggest to Spinoza that other religious customs, too, were only temporary in character and validity. In the writings of Gersonides (1288–1344), he saw rationalism encroaching on miracles and on prophecy, so as to explain away their supposed supernatural character. Maimonides had already boldly asserted that any passage in the Bible which appeared to conflict with reason must be reinterpreted so as to be in harmony with it. This method of “interpreting” Scripture into conformity with reason still seemed to save the priority of the Bible over human reason—though only in appearance. Gersonides went further than that. Frankly admitting the possibility of a real conflict between Reason and Revelation, he openly declared that the Bible “cannot prevent us from holding that to be true which our reason prompts us to believe.” Moreover, the tendency toward free thought was very much in the air

Their early critiques of scriptural consistency and their attempts to reconcile faith with reason laid the groundwork for Spinoza's own radical biblical criticism.</summary>

ever since the Renaissance, and it affected young Jews as it affected others. For example, in 1628, a Jewish scholar named Joseph Delmedigo arrived in Amsterdam; he had studied at the University of Padua. He was well-versed in philosophy, medicine, physics, and mathematics, as well as in Hebrew literature, and he had also studied astronomy under Galileo. He seems to have stayed several years in Amsterdam, where Manasseh ben Israel published a selection of his works for him. He was a remarkable product of that age of conflict between the old and the new. Unsettled by the new spirit of the age yet faithful to the old, his mind inclined at times toward skepticism and at others toward mysticism; his nomadic life was both typical and expressive of a restless, vacillating mind seeking in vain to regain its equilibrium. And, judging by contemporary complaints, Amsterdam's Jewish community had many such religious malcontents, and the leaders had to cope with the trouble as best they could. Already in 1623, Samuel da Silva, a Jewish physician in Amsterdam, was called upon to write a defense of the immortality of the soul and the inspiration of the Bible against the skeptical views aired by Uriel da Costa. In 1632, Manasseh ben Israel published the first part of his *Conciliator*, in which he sought to reconcile the apparent inconsistencies of Scripture. The <term>Marano</term> <note>Also spelled Marrano; Spanish or Portuguese Jews who were forced to convert to Christianity but often practiced Judaism in secret, many of whom later emigrated to places like Amsterdam to return to their faith.</note> refugees, like others who threw off the yoke of Roman Catholicism, turned back to the Bible, and the difficulties which some of them encountered there may have been one of the causes that prompted Manasseh’s enterprise. Spinoza undoubtedly knew this book. But he probably appreciated the problems which it attacked much more than the solutions which it offered. And if the Bible already presented difficulties, how extravagant and unwarranted must have appeared that elaborate superstructure which the Rabbis had built upon it "line upon line and precept upon precept"! At all events, Spinoza's difficul-

<meta>This page continues the discussion of intellectual influences on Spinoza, specifically how his early challenges to religious tradition were preceded by other Jewish thinkers and how he differed from them in his refusal to separate theory from practice.</meta>

<header>THE LIFE OF SPINOZA</header>

ties, insofar as they turned on the narrower problems of the Hebrew Scriptures and Jewish ceremonial, were by no means new. They had been clearly realized and partly addressed by others long before him.

As regards the wider philosophical questions, it is difficult to say what Spinoza’s philosophy was like at that stage of his life. One can scarcely suppose that his thought was already systematized into a definite philosophical theory. Most likely his views were as of yet only loosely connected and, in the main, negative rather than positive in tendency. And these views also were, in very large measure, if not exclusively, suggested to him by Jewish writers. These more philosophical problems, too, were not altogether new; they had been realized and grappled with by other Jews before him. The popular conception of Creation (*creatio ex nihilo* <note>Creation out of nothing.</note>) had been denied by both Ibn Ezra and Gersonides, who maintained the eternity of matter. Crescas (1340–1410) had maintained that God had extension, and the Jewish Mystics taught that Nature was animated. Maimonides had denied that man was the center of creation, maintaining that each thing exists for its own sake, and Crescas denied the validity of final causes. Maimonides also had suggested the relativity of good and evil, and Ibn Ezra and Crescas had maintained a thoroughgoing determinism.

Spinoza, however, felt the accumulated burden of all these problems, and he may already have been sufficiently influenced by <term>Cartesian</term> thought to refuse to accept any unproved assertions. Moreover, Spinoza lacked the power (one is almost inclined to call it a gift) which his Jewish predecessors possessed, namely, the power of detaching their theories from their practical everyday life. However advanced or heterodox their views may have been, they were still conservative in feeling and conservative in practice, and they observed religious customs just like the most orthodox. Such an attitude may easily be accused of duplicity; but

<summary>This page argues that Spinoza's early philosophical problems were largely inherited from a long tradition of Jewish thinkers who had already questioned creation, anthropocentrism, and free will. It highlights that Spinoza differed from these predecessors by insisting on aligning his private theories with his public practice, rather than maintaining a conservative lifestyle while holding radical views.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Spinoza's intellectual development, moving from the specific Jewish thinkers who influenced him to the practical tension between his evolving beliefs and his participation in the Jewish community's rituals and social life.</meta>

<page-num>xxxvi</page-num>
# INTRODUCTION

We do not really explain it by giving it negative labels. It is often perfectly honest, and it is found in all creeds, at present no less than in the past. And, after all, the difference is mostly one of degree rather than of kind. Even Spinoza’s feelings remained more conservative than his thoughts until the end. That was why he could not help using the language of religion long after his thought seemed to have emptied it of its religious meaning. At all events, he made no secret of his views, and he grew lax regarding ceremonial rituals, whose theoretical basis no longer appealed to him. The elaborate dietary laws of orthodox Judaism must have been something of an obstacle in his social interactions with Christian friends; and although he, no doubt, observed these laws for a time from pure force of habit, even when their *raison d’être* had already lost its hold on him, still he probably grew weary of excusing his apparent lack of sociability on the grounds of a custom in which he no longer believed.

Moreover, the comparatively liberal religion of his Mennonite and Collegiant* friends—their Quaker-like simplicity, their brotherly equality, their humanitarian rejection of strife and war, and the plain decorum of their prayer meetings—all this must have tended to make him increasingly dissatisfied with the over-elaborated rituals of his own community and the relative lack of decorum in their Synagogue services. On the other hand, his Jewish neighbors were beginning to feel scandalized by this breach of ritual practices, his frequent absence from the Synagogue, and the reports of his attendance at Christian prayer meetings. This was especially so considering that his father and grandfather had held office in the Synagogue, and Baruch himself had been looked upon as a promising “light of the Exile.” Mutual distrust developed into mutual antipathy. The conservatives could not understand how anyone could, merely on account of personal inconvenience, deliberately ignore divinely ordained

precepts—except out of sheer perverseness. They failed to realize that anyone who did not accept the divine origin of such customs, and did not see any obvious moral purpose in them, would simply not think it worthwhile to sacrifice time or anything else for their sake. And Spinoza himself was almost equally unsympathetic when he failed to realize that customs which seemed like a burden to him were nevertheless felt to be a blessing and a privilege by those who sincerely regarded them as divine ordinances—as opportunities to serve God. Meanwhile, the apparent lack of decorum in the Synagogue was largely the result of Israel's feeling of familiarity with God. Such mutual misunderstandings neither began nor ended in the days of Spinoza. In any case, trouble was brewing. After his father's death, Spinoza probably became less cautious than before. He did not entirely sever his connection with the Synagogue, for the Synagogue accounts show that he was present in the Synagogue on the Sabbath, December 5, 1655, and made an offering. It was the Sabbath of the Feast of Lights, in memory of the Maccabean uprising against Antiochus Epiphanes, and Spinoza had a warm admiration for all enemies of tyranny—did he not actually picture himself in the guise of Aniellos, the Neapolitan rebel against the tyranny of Spain? It stands to reason that Spinoza would have maintained his connection with the Synagogue. He could hardly resist the call of filial piety to recite the mourner's prayer for his father, just as he had done for his mother during his childhood. The prayer was innocent enough. Though a "mourner's prayer," it was not a prayer for the dead; in fact, it contained no reference whatever to the dead. It was a prayer for peace, and its fundamental tone was one of praise for God, which, coming at the moment of profoundest sorrow, was regarded as the finest expression of resignation and faith. Spinoza could scarcely have taken any serious objection to it at that time, and on such

an occasion, and he would thus remain attached to the Synagogue during his year of mourning. In the months of September, October, and November fell the anniversaries of the deaths of his sister Miriam, his stepmother, and his mother respectively. He would be expected to attend the Synagogue on these occasions and would hardly be disinclined. We need not, therefore, be surprised to find him in the Synagogue again on the 5th of December. In all probability, that was not the last occasion either on which he was seen in the Synagogue—the anniversary of his father’s death, in March 1656, most likely saw him there again. What exactly happened in the interval between March and July 1656 is not certain, though it may not be difficult to guess. Possibly some of his young Jewish friends spoke to him on the subject of death—a subject natural enough under the circumstances—and may have been surprised and shocked to hear from him that, in his view, the Bible did not teach the doctrine of the immortality of the soul, and that, in the Bible, “soul” was simply synonymous with “life.” This might have led to the more general question of the existence of disembodied spirits or angels, which Spinoza then described as unreal and mere phantoms of the imagination. But what about God? would be the natural reply. God, said Spinoza, was also not incorporeal, but extended. At all events, it was these heretical views which were soon afterward made the grounds for his excommunication; but they were not really the only grounds—there were other reasons.

Reference has already been made to the fact that, upon the death of their father, Rebekah endeavored to keep her half-brother from his share of the inheritance. Her idea, no doubt, was that Spinoza might earn his livelihood, whereas she had nothing with which to support herself and ought therefore to be provided for. Possibly her brother-in-law, de Casseres, a prospective Rabbi, learned in the

During this time, a family conflict arose as his sister Rebekah attempted to deny him his inheritance.</summary>

Law, and being uncommonly shocked by Spinoza’s religious lapses—of which Rebekah probably knew much and told him even more—advised her that according to strict Jewish law, Spinoza’s misconduct disqualified him from inheriting his father’s property. Spinoza naturally resented such high-handed methods and appealed to the law of the land, which of course took no notice of the subtleties of Rabbinic legislation. Spinoza won his lawsuit, but, realizing the moral claims of his sister’s position, he refrained from taking anything beyond a bedstead; he likely took that as a memento as much as an item of value or necessity. This appeal to the secular authorities against his sister hardly made him more popular with his people, however little some of them may have sympathized with her particular methods. Moreover, the report of his heresies, on which Rebekah had based her exclusive claims, became public and was predictably exaggerated as it spread from person to person.

Meanwhile, Spinoza had to earn a living. He could hardly consider staying with his sister, or with any other relative, after this family quarrel, and he had no certain means of support to fall back upon. Fortunately, Van den Enden, realizing his pupil’s plight, came to his rescue. Spinoza assisted him in his school, and in return, Van den Enden provided him with a home and all his needs at his own house. This, of course, led to a complete break with Jewish dietary laws. But this was not all. Van den Enden, as previously mentioned, had a bad reputation, and his school was strongly suspected of being a center for teaching atheism. Whether Van den Enden really deserved his bad reputation is not at all certain. It may be granted that he was not particularly orthodox in his views; he knew too much to satisfy the requirements of the zealots. On the other hand, it must be remembered that when Dirck Kerckrinck courted Clara Maria Van den Enden, he had to convert to Roman Catholicism before her father consented to

the marriage (1671). Be that as it may, the school had a bad reputation, and Spinoza’s reputation did not improve by his close connection with it. Possibly some of the fathers—who subsequently earned the daily gratitude of their sons for taking care in due time “to remove them from the school of such a harmful and godless master” as Van den Enden was reputed to be—were not slow in pinning some of the blame on his Jewish assistant. Spinoza, who was as yet too inexperienced to appreciate the wisdom of discretion, may have given voice to many a heterodox thought. If so, the scandalized fathers who repeatedly tried to persuade the city magistrates to close Van den Enden’s school, and who actually did succeed in driving him out of Amsterdam eventually, would not keep very quiet about Spinoza, and the Jewish authorities would have good reason to take alarm.

Except by the select few, religious tolerance was scarcely understood in those days, even in the Netherlands. That the persecuted turn persecutors has become a truism; it is sad, but it is true. In practice, the cry for religious tolerance has all too often amounted to this: you have persecuted me long enough now, let me persecute you for a change. At the very beginning of their long struggle against the tyranny of the Inquisition, the mutual intolerance of the various religious sects in the Netherlands caused infinite trouble to William the Silent, and very nearly wrecked their enterprise. As their fortunes improved and the need for union became somewhat less urgent, intolerance became increasingly manifest. The Calvinists, who were in the majority, regarded their Church more or less as the established Church, to which the Reformed clergy tried their utmost to compel all others to conform. When Philip III made a twelve-year truce with the United Netherlands in 1609, he did so, it is said, in the sinister hope that mutual religious persecutions among the different religious sects

<meta>This page continues the historical context of religious intolerance in the Netherlands, moving from general observations about the Calvinist majority to the specific theological and political conflicts between the Arminians and the Contra-Remonstrants in the early 17th century.</meta>

<page-num>xli</page-num>

would bring about the fall of the Netherlands that the Spanish troops had failed to achieve. To tell the truth, there was significant justification for that sinister hope.

In 1610, the followers of Arminius (Professor of Theology at Leiden, died 1609) presented their Remonstrance <note>The "five points" of the Remonstrance were (i) conditional election; (ii) universal redemption through Christ; (iii) salvation by grace; (iv) the ability to resist grace; and (v) the possibility of falling from a state of grace.</note> against extreme Calvinism to the provincial parliament of Holland and West Friesland, and the struggle between the Arminians (or Remonstrants) and the extreme Calvinists (or Contra-Remonstrants) reached its peak in 1619, when the Synod of Dordrecht excommunicated the Arminians, closed their places of worship, and caused the expulsion of Remonstrant preachers from most of the States. Barneveldt, the political leader of the Remonstrants and considered the greatest statesman of the Netherlands, was executed; Hugo Grotius, one of their most distinguished scholars, was imprisoned and only escaped through the bold ingenuity of his wife. One interesting result of the banishment of Arminian pastors was the formation of the Collegiant sect, which decided to do away with the clerical office entirely and held relatively informal gatherings (*collegia*) for prayers and religious discussions conducted completely by laymen. (The Mennonites, with whom Spinoza also had friendly relations, had emerged under very similar circumstances during the sixteenth century).

The events of 1619 clearly show the temperament of the dominant religious sect in the United Provinces. Fortunately, enlightened statesmen and magistrates generally managed to resist the persecuting zeal of the Reformed or Calvinist clergy. However, this was not always the case, and the zealots did not lessen their efforts despite repeated discouragement.

In 1653, the clerical Synods forced the States-General to issue a strict decree against the Socinians

or Unitarians, many of whom consequently joined the Collegiants.

After all, then, the decree of toleration embodied in the Union of Utrecht did not secure very much in the way of real toleration. Non-Calvinist Christians were allowed to live in the Netherlands without suffering in person or property on account of their nonconformity. For those days, even that was a great deal; but the right of public worship was quite another matter. And if the Union of Utrecht did not secure real toleration for all Christian sects, much less did it guarantee anything to the Jews, who had not been considered in it at all. They had not even been formally admitted into the Netherlands, but their presence had been more or less tolerated. Even in 1619, when the Jewish question was definitively raised in the Netherlands, it was decided to allow each city to decide for itself whether it would permit Jews to live there or not. Their position was precarious indeed. They had to take care not to offend the religious sensitivities of their neighbors. And their troubles began soon enough.

About the year 1618, a Marrano <note>A "Marrano" was a Sephardic Jew in Spain or Portugal who converted to Christianity to avoid persecution but often continued to practice Judaism in secret.</note> refugee from Portugal named Gabriel da Costa arrived in Amsterdam. Both he and his late father had held office in the Catholic Church, but seized by a sudden longing to return to the religion of his ancestors, Gabriel fled to Amsterdam, where he embraced Judaism and changed his name from Gabriel to Uriel. His ideas about Judaism had been derived chiefly from reading the Old Testament, and his contact with actual Rabbinic Judaism somewhat disappointed him. He then began to speak contemptuously of the Jews as Pharisees, and expressed his views very freely against the belief in the immortality of the soul and the inspiration of the Bible. These views were, of course, as much opposed to Christianity as to Judaism. The Jewish physician, de Silva, as already stated, tried to refute these heretical
