views in a book published in 1623. Da Costa replied, in 1624, with a treatise which was very confused, and which, while accusing de Silva of slander against the author, actually reiterated those heresies. Partly from fear that an outcry might be raised against the Jews as promoters of heresy, the Jewish authorities excommunicated Uriel da Costa, and as a kind of official repudiation of all responsibility for him, they communicated the facts to the civil authorities, who thereupon imprisoned him, fined him, and ordered his book to be burned. Shunned by Jews and Christians alike, da Costa found his existence very lonely and intolerable, and in 1633 he made up his mind, as he said, “to become an ape among apes,” and made his peace with the Synagogue. But he soon became quite reckless again and was excommunicated a second time. Again he grew weary of his isolation, and once more he approached the Synagogue authorities to have the ban removed. Determined not to be duped again, yet reluctant to reject him completely, they imposed harsh conditions on him. He submitted to the conditions—he recanted his sins publicly in the Synagogue, received thirty-nine lashes, and lay prostrate on the threshold of the Synagogue while the congregation stepped over him as they passed out. It was a cruel degradation. And his humiliation weighed so heavily on his mind that he committed suicide soon afterward. This happened in 1640, and Spinoza must have remembered the scandal.

If the Jewish community in Amsterdam felt it necessary to repudiate, in such a drastic manner, their responsibility for Uriel da Costa’s heresies, so as to avoid giving offense to their Christian neighbors, there was every reason why they should feel even greater discomfort on account of Spinoza’s heresies in 1656. It was a critical period in the annals of Jewish history. During the Muscovite and Cossack invasion of Poland (1654–1656) entire Jewish communities were

There was consequently a considerable influx of Polish Jews into Amsterdam. Now, even in the twentieth century, when countless missionaries are sent to spread the Gospel from China to Peru, Jewish refugees have been shown but scant Christian charity under similar circumstances, so we have every reason to suppose that the condition of the Amsterdam Jewish community did not gain in security through this influx of destitute refugees. Then more than ever was it necessary to be cautious, and avoid giving offense to the people of the land, especially in the matter of the most delicate of all things—*religion.*<note>That their fears were not unfounded is clear from the fact that even some twenty years afterward, various Synods of the Reformed Church tried to persuade the civil authorities to pass strong measures for the forcible conversion of the Jews.</note> They did not want another scandal. One da Costa affair was enough, and more than enough. Yet they must not incur the responsibility for Spinoza's heresies. So at first they tried to bribe Spinoza. They promised him a considerable annuity if he would only keep quiet, and show some amount of outward conformity to his religion. They must have known well enough that silence and partial outward conformity do not alter a man's views; they were surely shrewd enough to realize that a heretic does not cease to be a heretic by becoming also a hypocrite. If their sole object had been to suppress heresy in their midst, that was not the way to gain their end. Heresy would not wither by becoming profitable. The real motive that prompted them must have been that just indicated—though it is very likely that they did not realize it so explicitly. If they had done so, and if they had urged these points on Spinoza, he would, undoubtedly, have appreciated the need for caution and silence. But they evidently did not understand him, they evidently misconceived his character entirely, and the

The attempt to gag him with a bribe was precisely the method best calculated to defeat their goal. The only person who might have understood him, and whose intervention might have been successful, was Manasseh ben Israel. But he was in England at that time, on a mission to Cromwell. Consequently, threats were tried next; but the threat of excommunication had no effect on Spinoza. They had reached the end of their rope. The only course open to them, as they saw it, was to place him under the ban. The feeling against him was undoubtedly so strong that a fanatic might have tried to do him physical harm. It may be that such an attack gave rise to the story of an attempt to assassinate Spinoza with a dagger as he was leaving the synagogue or the theater. However, there is no evidence for this, and the probability is strongly against it.

Sometime in June 1656, Spinoza was summoned before the court of Rabbis. Witnesses provided evidence of his heresies. Spinoza did not deny them—he attempted to defend them. Thereupon, he was excommunicated for a period of only thirty days, in the hope that he might still relent. But he did not. Accordingly, on July 27, 1656, the final ban was publicly pronounced against him in the Synagogue at Amsterdam. It was phrased in the following terms:

> “The members of the council let it be known to you that they have long known of the evil opinions and actions of Baruch de Espinoza, and have tried by various methods and promises to make him turn from his evil ways. As they have not succeeded in improving his conduct, but, on the contrary, have received more information every day about the horrible heresies which he practiced and taught, and other outrages which he has committed, and as they had many trustworthy witnesses to this, who have testified and given evidence in the presence of the said Spinoza, and have convicted him; and as all this has been investigated in the presence of the Rabbis, it has been resolved with their consent that the said Espinoza should be anathematized and cut off from the people of Israel, and now he is anathematized with the following anathema:
> “ ‘With the judgment of the angels and with that of the saints, with

> “We ordain that no one may communicate with him verbally or in writing, nor show him any favor, nor stay under the same roof with him, nor be within four cubits of him, nor read anything composed or written by him.”

This amiable document of the “holy congregation” is nothing less than a blasphemy. It must be remembered, however, that the actual anathema was a traditional formula, and (unlike the preamble and conclusion) was not specially written for the occasion. No doubt it shows a greater familiarity with the wording of the Bible than with its best teaching. But the Jews who excommunicated Spinoza were no worse than their neighbors in this respect. These awful curses were but the common farewells with which the churches took leave of their rebellious friends. Nor were these the worst forms of leave-taking, by any means. After all, swearing breaks no bones and burns no one alive, as did the rack and the stake which were so common in those days. The Catholic Church excommunicated only when it could not torture and kill; and then its anathemas, though they may have been more polished in wording,

--- Page 53 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's historical analysis of Baruch Spinoza's excommunication, comparing it to other historical bans and exploring the political pressures that led the Jewish community in Amsterdam to take such drastic action.</meta>

were incomparably more brutal in effect. The ban pronounced upon William the Silent, for instance, contained nothing less than an urgent invitation to assassins to murder him, in return for which pious deed they would receive absolution for all their crimes, no matter how heinous, and would be raised to noble rank; and that ban actually accomplished its sinister object! It is, therefore, unjust to single out this ban against Spinoza and judge it by present-day standards. Nor should it be forgotten that if Judaism alone had been concerned, more leniency would have been shown; the whole thing might even have been ignored. Elisha ben Abuyah, the Faust of the Talmud, was not persecuted by the Jews, despite his heresies. The ban against Spinoza was the tribute paid to Caesar, rather than to the God of Israel.

As in the case of da Costa, and for the same reasons, the Jewish authorities officially communicated the news of Spinoza’s excommunication to the civil authorities, who, in order to appease the wrath of the Jewish Rabbinate and the Calvinist clergy, banished Spinoza from Amsterdam, though only for a short period.

On the whole, there is some reason to suppose that the anathema was not a curse, but a blessing in disguise. It freed him entirely from sectarian and tribal considerations; it helped to make him a thinker of no particular sect and of no particular age, but for all people and for all time.

However reprehensible some of his fellow Jews may have considered his heretical utterances and unorthodox actions, there can be no doubt that Spinoza did not really desire to sever his connection with them entirely. This is evident from the fact that he did not ignore, as he might have done, the summons to appear before the court of Rabbis to defend himself against the charge of heresy. It is true that when informed of his final excommunication, he is reported to have said: “Very well, this

<page-num>xlviii</page-num>
<header>INTRODUCTION</header>

"...does not force me to do anything which I would not have done of my own accord, had I not been afraid of a scandal." But the last words of this expression of his natural resentment only seem to confirm the suggestion about his previous anxiety to avoid a complete rupture, if he could do so honestly. It was partly perhaps also for this reason that even after his excommunication he addressed an *Apology* (written in Spanish) to the Synagogue authorities. In this document, he probably sought to defend his heretical views by showing that they had the support of some of the most eminent Rabbis, and to condemn the injustice of attributing to him "horrible practices and other enormities" because of his neglect of mere ceremonial observances. Unfortunately, this document has not yet been recovered, though some of its contents are said to have been subsequently incorporated in his *Tractatus Theologico-Politicus*. It would shed a flood of light on Spinoza's intellectual history. However, the *Apology* did not improve matters. Cut off from his community, without family or friends, he stood alone, but firm and unshaken. Unlike da Costa, he never wavered. He seems to have made contact with Jews again afterward; but it was they who had to seek him out.

## § 5. THE LAST YEARS OF SPINOZA'S STAY IN AND NEAR AMSTERDAM—1656–1660

Banished from Amsterdam, Spinoza went to live in Ouwerkerk, a little village to the south of Amsterdam. He possibly had some Christian friends there who had influence with the civil authorities; and apparently he intended to return to Amsterdam at the earliest opportunity. Perhaps also he was not altogether uninfluenced by the thought that the Jewish cemetery was there, and that his mother, his sister, his father,

<summary>Following his excommunication, Spinoza wrote a lost *Apology* to the Synagogue defending his views and protesting the attacks on his character before moving to the village of Ouwerkerk. Unlike other excommunicated figures like da Costa, Spinoza remained firm in his convictions despite being isolated from his community.</summary>

<keywords>Spinoza, Amsterdam, Synagogue, excommunication, Apology, Tractatus Theologico-Politicus, Uriel da Costa, Ouwerkerk, Spanish, Rabbis, Jewish cemetery</keywords>

--- Page 55 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Spinoza following his excommunication, detailing his move to the village of Ouwerkerk, his return to Amsterdam, and his work as an optician to support his studies.</meta>

<page-num>xlix</page-num>
<header>THE LIFE OF SPINOZA</header>

and others once dear to him, had found their last resting place in it.

To support himself, he had to rely on the lenses he made—a craft he had mastered during the years immediately preceding his exile. He made lenses for spectacles, microscopes, and telescopes, and his friends sold them for him. The work suited his tastes well enough because it kept him in touch with his scientific studies. He evidently excelled at it, for later on, his fame as an optician attracted the notice of Christiaan Huygens and Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz, among others. But it was an unfortunate occupation in other ways. The fine glass dust he inhaled during his work must have been very harmful to his health, especially when we keep in mind that he eventually died of <term>consumption</term> <note>tuberculosis</note>, and that he probably inherited the disease from his mother, who died so young. For the time being, however, it was a pleasant occupation, and with his frugal habits, it left him enough time to pursue his scientific and philosophical studies.

As already suggested, Spinoza did not stay long in Ouwerkerk but returned after a few months to Amsterdam, where he remained until 1660. We possess only the most meager information about the events that happened during this period (1656–1660). Apparently, he gave some private lessons in philosophy and pursued his studies tirelessly. By the end of this period, he had already moved beyond Descartes and had thought out the essentials of his own philosophy.

From Spinoza’s subsequent correspondence, we obtain a glimpse of his friends and associates during this period, while the opening pages of his *Improvement of the Understanding* both enlighten and mystify us about his life during those last years in Amsterdam.

After leaving Amsterdam in 1660, Spinoza continued a friendly correspondence with several residents in Amsterdam, whom he also visited for a short time in 1663. These

<summary>Spinoza supported himself by grinding lenses, a trade that aligned with his scientific interests but likely damaged his health. During his final years in Amsterdam, he moved past Cartesian thought to develop the foundations of his own philosophical system.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the biography of Spinoza, moving from his early years in Amsterdam and Ouwerkerk to a discussion of the circle of friends and correspondents he established during his time in Amsterdam.</meta>

<page-num>1</page-num>
<header>INTRODUCTION</header>

His correspondents must therefore have been known to him during his stay in Amsterdam, and what is known about them helps to throw some light on this obscure period in Spinoza's life history. They were Pieter Balling, Jarig Jelles, Dirck Kerckrinck, Lodewijk Meyer, Simon Joosten de Vries, and Jan Rieuwertsz.

Pieter Balling had acted for some time as the representative or agent for various Spanish merchants. It is quite possible that Spinoza's knowledge of Spanish first brought him into contact with Balling. Balling was a Mennonite and a declared enemy of dogmatism. In 1662, he published a book entitled *The Light on the Candlestick*, in which he attacked religion based on stereotyped dogmas and advocated a religion, partly rationalistic and partly mystical, based on the inner light of the soul. The whole spirit of the book might be summed up in these familiar lines by Matthew Arnold:

> "These hundred doctors try
> &nbsp;&nbsp;To preach you to their school.
> We have the truth, they cry.
> &nbsp;&nbsp;And yet their oracle,
> Trumpet it as they will, is but the same as yours.
>
> "Once read your own breast right,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;And you have done with fears.
> Man gets no other light,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;Even if he searches a thousand years.
> Sink into yourself: there ask what ails you, at that shrine."

In 1664, he translated Spinoza's version of Descartes' *Principia* into Dutch. In a letter written in the same year, we see Spinoza trying to console Balling on the loss of his child, dealing tenderly with Balling's "premonitions" of his impending loss.

Jarig Jelles was at one time a spice merchant in Amsterdam, but feeling that "knowledge is better than choice gold, that wisdom is better than rubies, and all the things..."

He wrote a book to show that Cartesianism did not lead to atheism but was, on the contrary, quite compatible with the Christian religion. Spinoza seems to have helped him in the composition of this book. Jelles was one of the friends who persuaded Spinoza to publish his version of Descartes' *Principia*, and even paid the cost of its publication. He also took an active part in the publication of Spinoza's posthumous works, the preface of which is so similar in tone to the book by Jelles that he is regarded as its author by some very competent authorities.

Dirck Kerckrinck was seven years younger than Spinoza, whom he first met at Van den Enden's school (around 1652–1656). He studied medicine and became the author of various medical treatises. Colerus relates some gossip to the effect that Spinoza and Kerckrinck were rivals for the hand of Clara Maria, the gifted daughter of Van den Enden, and that she accepted Kerckrinck because he was rich, while Spinoza was poor. But as Clara Maria was born in 1644, this very natural attempt to introduce a touch of romance into Spinoza's life as a bachelor is an utter failure. Clara Maria was barely sixteen when Spinoza left Amsterdam for good in 1660, and he had ceased to be her father's pupil in 1654 or, at the latest, in 1656. As a resident in her father's house, he may have been fond of her as a mere child, and some expression of endearment uttered in that sense probably gave rise to this pretty tale. It is true, however, that Kerckrinck did marry her in 1671, as already mentioned. Spinoza possessed several of Kerckrinck’s medical works, which he had, no doubt, sent to him as an old friend.

Lodewijk Meyer was a medical practitioner in Amsterdam. He was about two years older than Spinoza and a man of versatile talents. He had studied not only medicine but

It was he who wrote the interesting preface to Spinoza’s version of Descartes’ *Principia*.

Simon Joosten de Vries was an Amsterdam merchant. He was only about a year younger than Spinoza, though his attitude toward Spinoza was always that of a humble disciple. He studied medicine under the direction of Spinoza, and his attachment to Spinoza is evident from a letter he wrote in 1663, after Spinoza had left Amsterdam. 
> “For a long time,” he writes, “I have been longing to be with you; but the weather and the harsh winter have not been favorable to me. Sometimes I complain about my lot in being separated from you by such a great distance. Happy, most happy, is your companion Casearius, who lives with you under the same roof, and who can converse with you on the most excellent topics during dinner, or supper, or on your walks. But although we are so far apart in body, yet you have constantly been present in my mind, especially when I take your writings in my hand and apply myself to them.”

In the same letter, he reports on a philosophical society for the study of Spinoza’s philosophy, which was shared with de Vries and others in manuscript form, and he asks for further explanation of some difficult points. The sincerity and depth of his devotion was further shown by his offer of a gift of 2,000 florins to Spinoza, which was, however, declined. Later on, Simon de Vries, whose health was even worse than Spinoza’s, feeling that his end was drawing near, wished to make Spinoza his heir. Again the philosopher dissuaded him, urging the prior claims of the testator’s own relatives. Upon the death of Simon de Vries, his brother

Jan Rieuwertsz was a bookseller in Amsterdam and was about fifteen years older than Spinoza. He was a Collegiant and very liberal in his views. His shop had the bad reputation of being a gathering place for scoffers. He published and stocked the works of many authors with unorthodox reputations, including those of Descartes, Balling, Jelles, and Spinoza. His son was also a devoted admirer of Spinoza.

Such were some of the men with whom Spinoza shared a friendly relationship during his last years in Amsterdam. Further details are missing. It is possible he had given private lessons to Simon de Vries (who speaks of him as "master"), Balling, and others; or he may have held some kind of seminar or class for the informal discussion of religious and philosophical questions. If so, the substance of his *Metaphysical Thoughts* (which were subsequently added to his version of Descartes' *Principia*) and of his *Short Treatise on God, Man and his Well-being* must have been developed during these years and for these purposes. This would also account for the continuation or revival of similar meetings for the discussion of Spinoza's views, as reported in the letter of Simon de Vries.

Though little is known of these years, there can be no doubt that they were years of storm and stress in Spinoza’s mental history. This much may be gathered from the impressive pages with which he opens his *Treatise on the Improvement of the Understanding*.

> "After experience had taught me [so he writes] that all things which are ordinarily encountered in common life are vain and futile, and when I saw that all things which caused me any anxiety or fear had in themselves nothing of good or evil, except in so far as the mind was moved by them; I finally determined to inquire if there were anything which was a true good capable of communicating itself, and by which alone the mind could be affected to the

<page-num>liv</page-num>
# INTRODUCTION

exclusion of all else; whether, indeed, anything existed by the discovery and acquisition of which I might have continuous and supreme joy for all eternity. I say that *I at last determined*: for at first glance it seemed unwise to be willing to give up something certain for something that was still uncertain. I saw, to be sure, the advantages derived from honor and riches, and that I would be obliged to abstain from the pursuit of these if I wished to apply myself seriously to something different and new. And if, by chance, supreme happiness should lie in them, I saw clearly that I would have to do without it; but if, on the other hand, it did not lie in them, and I applied myself only to them, then I would also have to go without the highest happiness. I, therefore, pondered in my mind whether, perhaps, it would be possible to arrive at the new plan of life, or at least some certainty about it, without any change in the order and usual plan of my life—something which I have often attempted in vain. Now the things which one mostly encounters in life, and which—so far as one may gather from their actions—men esteem as the highest good, can be reduced to these three: namely, riches, honor, and pleasure. The mind is so distracted by these three that it can scarcely think of any other good. . . . When, therefore, I saw that all these things stood in the way of my applying myself to any new plan of life; in fact, that they were so opposed to it that one must necessarily abstain either from the one or from the other, I was forced to inquire which would be more useful to me; for, as I have already said, I seemed to be willing to give up a certain good for something uncertain. But after brooding a little over this subject, I found, in the first place, that if I gave up those things and devoted myself to the new plan of life, I would be letting go of a good that was uncertain by its very nature . . . for one which was uncertain, not in its nature . . . but only regarding its attainment. After unremitting reflection, I came to see that, if I could only make up my mind thoroughly, then I would give up certain evils for a certain good. . . . Not without reason did I use the words, *if I could only make up my mind thoroughly*. For although I saw this so clearly in my mind, yet I could not, in that way, put aside all avarice, sensuous pleasure, and the desire for fame. This one thing I saw: that so long as my mind pondered these thoughts, it turned away from those other things and considered the new plan of life seriously. This was a great comfort

<summary>Spinoza recounts his internal struggle to abandon worldly pursuits like wealth, fame, and pleasure in favor of a higher, eternal good. He concludes that while worldly goods are inherently uncertain and distracting, the pursuit of a new way of life offers a certain good, even if its attainment remains difficult.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the biography of Spinoza, specifically detailing his transition from a life of worldly conflict in Amsterdam to a life of philosophical peace in Rijnsburg.</meta>

<page-num>lv</page-num>
<header>THE LIFE OF SPINOZA</header>

to me. . . . And although at first these periods were rare and only of very brief duration, yet as the true good gradually became better known to me, these periods grew more frequent and longer.”

The above “confession” was written by Spinoza in 1661. The inner struggle between worldly attractions and the call of the spirit was over then. Indeed, his earlier work, the *Short Treatise*, which was completed in 1660, already bears unmistakable evidence of the peace that crowned that internal conflict. This conflict must, therefore, be attributed to the years immediately preceding 1660. His last years in Amsterdam, when he first became acquainted with real life and the struggle for existence, must have reminded him often enough of the desirability of worldly goods and the hardships of poverty and obscurity. After all, he was human, and he could hardly escape the common lot of mortals—the conflict between the two souls that dwell in the human breast. But Spinoza was not inclined to speak about himself. He lifts only a corner of the veil, behind which we may well imagine scenes of storm and stress during the period between his excommunication in 1656 and his departure from Amsterdam in 1660. Early in that year, weary of the bustle and worldliness of that commercial center, he went to live among unworldly people with old-fashioned virtues in a remote village—Rijnsburg. He withdrew from the frantic crowd, but not out of disgust or a hatred of humanity. He had caught a glimpse of the highest good for mankind, and he wanted to strengthen his hold on it under more favorable conditions. He had discovered that the sorrows of man “arise from the love of the transient,” while “love for an object eternal and infinite feeds the mind with unmixed joy, free from all sorrow.”

<summary>This page recounts Spinoza’s transition from the spiritual and worldly turmoil of his final years in Amsterdam to a life of quiet reflection in Rijnsburg. It emphasizes his realization that lasting happiness is found in the love of the infinite rather than transient worldly goods.</summary>

<page-num>lvi</page-num>
<header>INTRODUCTION</header>

## **§ 6. SPINOZA’S STAY IN RIJNSBURG—1660–1663**

Rijnsburg is a village about six or seven miles northwest of Leyden <note>Modern Leiden</note>. Its humble cottages, narrow lanes, quiet waterways, and charming medieval church still maintain an old-world appearance much as they did in Spinoza’s day—except, of course, for the clumsy but convenient steam-trams which pass by on their way to and from Leyden and Katwijk—or Noordwijk-aan-Zee. Within easy walking distance, on the road to Leyden, is Endgeest, a pleasant little rural place where Descartes once lived for several years, but which is now known chiefly for its mental asylum.

During the seventeenth century, Rijnsburg was the headquarters of the Collegiants. This sect, whose origin has already been explained above, rejected infant baptism and insisted on adult baptism by immersion. And Rijnsburg, on the old Rhine, was their place of baptism. That was the reason why the Collegiants were also commonly called the “Rijnsburgers.” Now Spinoza, as we have seen, numbered several Collegiants among his friends, and it was probably at the suggestion of one of his Collegiant friends that he went to live there. In any case, early in the year 1660, he seems to have taken up residence there, probably with a surgeon named Hermann Homan, in a newly built little cottage standing in a narrow lane, which has since come to be known as Spinoza Lane. Sometime afterward, apparently, the landlord’s religious humanitarianism led him to engrave, or to have engraved, on a stone in the cottage wall the well-intentioned message expressed in the concluding stanza of Kamphuyzen’s *May Morning*:

> “Alas! if all men were wise,
> And were good as well,
> The Earth would be a Paradise,
> Now it is mostly Hell.”

<summary>In 1660, Spinoza moved to Rijnsburg, a village serving as the headquarters for the Collegiant sect, where he lived in a small cottage owned by Hermann Homan. The page describes the town's geography, its connection to Descartes, and the religious environment that welcomed Spinoza.</summary>
<keywords>Spinoza, Rijnsburg, Leyden, Collegiants, Descartes, Endgeest, Hermann Homan, Kamphuyzen, Spinoza Lane, baptism, Dutch history</keywords>

--- Page 63 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Spinoza during his residence in Rijnsburg, identifying his cottage and discussing the philosophical influences that shaped his work during this period.</meta>

<page-num>lvii</page-num>
<header>THE LIFE OF SPINOZA</header>
<sig>e</sig>

And it was by this inscription that, based on the authority of an old tradition, the cottage has been identified. It still exists and is still surrounded by open fields rich in garden produce and flower bulbs. Restored and equipped with everything that diligent searching could find and money could buy that would interest students of Spinoza, the cottage is now known as the *Spinoza-huis* or Spinoza Museum. It serves as a kind of shrine sacred to the memory of the philosopher, where many pilgrims travel to pay homage to a profound mind and lofty character, feeling something of his peace of mind in that sanctuary of ancient tranquility.

One reason that prompted Spinoza to seek a quiet retreat was probably the desire to record his thoughts in a systematic form. Dissatisfied with the Scholastic philosophy still popular at the time, he and his friends had turned eagerly to the writings of Descartes. The opposition of the strict Calvinists to Cartesian philosophy actually tended to make it more appealing to the Remonstrants (including the Collegiants) and, indeed, to all who had suffered from or were opposed to the religious intolerance of the dominant Reformed Church. The call for impartiality and an open mind in the interpretation of Scripture was felt to have a certain kinship with the Cartesian method of philosophizing—specifically, his preliminary doubt of whatever could be reasonably disputed. Consequently, there was a gradual alliance between liberal religion and Cartesian philosophy. Spinoza's friends were mostly Cartesians and remained so until the end. Whether Spinoza himself was ever a thorough Cartesian is not known. There is no doubt whatever that Descartes' writings exercised a very powerful influence on Spinoza. By 1660, however, Spinoza had already moved beyond the fundamentals of Cartesian Metaphysics, though he still continued to follow Descartes in his Physics. Now, as we have already noted, during his last years in Amsterdam,

<keywords>Spinoza-huis, Rijnsburg, Spinoza Museum, Descartes, Cartesianism, Scholasticism, Calvinists, Remonstrants, Collegiants, Metaphysics</keywords>

--- Page 64 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Spinoza's life at Rijnsburg, focusing on the formation of his philosophical circle and the writing of his first major work, the "Short Treatise."</meta>

<page-num>lviii</page-num>
<header>INTRODUCTION</header>

Spinoza seems to have acted as a teacher or leader of a small philosophical circle. Its members, including Spinoza himself, were primarily interested in religious questions at first. They approached philosophy from the side of religion, and only to the extent that religious problems led up to philosophical considerations. God and His attributes, Nature and Creation, Man and his Well-being, the nature of the Human Mind, and the Immortality of the Soul—these were the topics which chiefly interested them, and on which, we may assume, Spinoza had accumulated various notes for those informal talks with them. He wanted to elaborate and to systematize these notes. This was the first task which occupied him at Rijnsburg, and it resulted in the *Short Treatise on God, Man and his Well-being*. But he continued to keep in touch with his Amsterdam friends and sent them the parts of the manuscript as he completed them. Though Cartesian in appearance, and partly also in substance, the *Short Treatise*—Spinoza’s very first philosophical essay—already marks a definite departure from the philosophy of Descartes in its identification of God with Nature, and its consequent determinism and naturalism. Spinoza himself fully realized the extent of his deviation from Descartes, and the novelty of his views even as compared with the novelties of Cartesianism, which was at that time “the new philosophy” *par excellence*. So he begged his friends not to be impatient with his novel views, but to consider them carefully, remembering that “a thing does not therefore cease to be true because it is not accepted by many.” He also realized that some of these views were liable to prove rather dangerous to minds more eager for novelty than for truth. He was therefore careful about the kind of people to whom he communicated his views, and also begged his trusted friends to be careful likewise. Caution was also necessary on account of the unremitting vigilance of heretic-hunters.

<summary>Spinoza led a philosophical circle at Rijnsburg and wrote his first work, the Short Treatise, which marked his move away from Cartesianism toward his own theories of naturalism and determinism. He cautioned his friends to share his radical views carefully to avoid the attention of religious authorities.</summary>
<keywords>Spinoza, Rijnsburg, Short Treatise, Descartes, Cartesianism, determinism, naturalism, heretic-hunters</keywords>

--- Page 66 ---
<meta>This page contains the translated conclusion of Spinoza’s letter to Henry Oldenburg, dated October 1661. It follows the discussion of Spinoza’s early philosophical developments and his transition into writing more formal treatises.</meta>

As for your new question—namely, how things began to be and by what connection they depend on the first cause—I have composed an entire short work on this subject and also on the improvement of the intellect, and I am currently occupied with transcribing and polishing it. But I sometimes stop working because I have no definite plan yet regarding <gloss>its</gloss> publication; I fear, indeed, that the theologians of our time might take offense and, with their usual hatred, lash out at me, as I utterly dread quarrels. I will look for your advice on this matter. 

And so that you may know what is contained in this work of mine that might be a stumbling block to preachers, I say that many attributes which are assigned to God by them, and by everyone else known to me, I consider to be created things. Conversely, other things which they consider to be created things due to their prejudices, I contend are attributes of God and have been misunderstood by them. Furthermore, I do not separate God from nature in the way that all others known to me have done. I therefore await your advice, for I look upon you as a most faithful friend whose loyalty it would be wrong to doubt. <gloss>Farewell meanwhile</gloss>, and continue to favor me with your friendship as you have begun. I am

->entirely yours<-
->**Benedict Spinoza**<-

<page-num>lxi</page-num>
### THE LIFE OF SPINOZA

->"As the character of the age in which we live [Spinoza adds] is not unknown to you, I would most earnestly beg you to be very careful about sharing these things with others. I do not mean to say that you should keep them absolutely to yourselves, but only that if you ever wish to share them with others, you shall have no other goal in view except the happiness of your neighbor; being at the same time clearly assured that the reward for your labor will not disappoint you in this."<-

Having finished the first draft of his *Short Treatise*, Spinoza felt that he had tackled all the major problems of religion and philosophy without any preliminary explanation of the requirements of philosophical method, and without any adequate justification for his own approach. Consequently, he turned his attention to this problem next and began his *Treatise on the Improvement of the Understanding*. In a letter dated October 1661, responding to some questions from Henry Oldenburg, Spinoza states that he had written a complete little treatise on the origin of things and their relationship to the <term>first cause</term>, as well as on the improvement of the understanding, and that he was busy just then copying and correcting it. It would appear from this that Spinoza's intention at that time may have been to combine the *Short Treatise* and the *Treatise on the Improvement of the Understanding*. What actually happened, however, is not entirely certain. The editors of Spinoza's posthumous works only had a fragment of the *Treatise on the Improvement of the Understanding*, and apparently nothing of the *Short Treatise*, of which we currently possess only two Dutch versions, discovered around 1860. The editors of the *Opera Posthuma* state that the *Treatise on the Improvement of the Understanding* was one of Spinoza's earliest works and that he had never finished it, but they appear to be uncertain whether it was only a lack of time or the inherent difficulties of the subject that prevented him from finishing it.

<summary>Spinoza expresses a need for caution when sharing his radical ideas, prioritizing the welfare of others. The text details the origins and fragmented preservation of his early works, the Short Treatise and the Treatise on the Improvement of the Understanding.</summary>

<keywords>Benedict Spinoza, Short Treatise, Treatise on the Improvement of the Understanding, Henry Oldenburg, Opera Posthuma, philosophical method, first cause, Dutch versions, 17th-century philosophy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the introduction to Spinoza's life, describing his growing reputation among the scholarly community at Leyden and the circumstances leading to his meeting with Henry Oldenburg in 1661.</meta>

<page-num>lxii</page-num>
# INTRODUCTION

In the meantime, Spinoza seems to have acquired some reputation not only with the Rijnsburgers but even among some of the professors at Leyden. This may have been due to his participation in the Collegiant Conferences held at Rijnsburg. These conferences for the discussion of religious questions were open to all who cared to come. And some of the students from the neighboring University at Leyden made a practice of attending these meetings and taking part in the debates. Some of them very likely came there for fun, though others, no doubt, had worthier motives. It was in this way that Spinoza came into contact with, among others, Johannes Casearius and the brothers Adriaan and Johannes Koerbagh, of whom more will be said shortly. And in this way also, Spinoza’s name and history may have become known to some of the Leyden professors, among them Johannes Coccejus, professor of theology, famous as the author of the first standard Hebrew dictionary, and even more so as the author of the dictum that an interpreter of the Scriptures should approach his task with a mind free from all dogmatic prejudices—the dictum which helped to bring about a kind of alliance between the Remonstrants and the Cartesians, to which reference has already been made. Now Coccejus was a native of Bremen, and when his countryman Henry Oldenburg visited Leyden in 1661—eager as usual to make the acquaintance of everybody who was remarkable in any way—Coccejus may have suggested to him a visit to Spinoza. Possibly Oldenburg had also heard something about Spinoza from Huygens, who was in correspondence with the English scientists among whom Oldenburg had moved, had actually visited London that very year, and may have met Oldenburg at one of the meetings of the “Gresham College,” which was soon to blossom into the “Royal Society.” At all events, in July 1661, Oldenburg visited Spinoza in Rijnsburg.

<summary>This page explains how Spinoza's involvement in the Collegiant Conferences at Rijnsburg brought him to the attention of University of Leyden scholars and likely led to his initial 1661 meeting with Henry Oldenburg.</summary>
<keywords>Spinoza, Rijnsburg, Leyden, Collegiant Conferences, Johannes Coccejus, Henry Oldenburg, Christiaan Huygens, Royal Society, Gresham College</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the biography of Spinoza, specifically detailing the background and career of Henry Oldenburg leading up to his first meeting with Spinoza in 1661.</meta>

<page-num>lxiii</page-num>
# THE LIFE OF SPINOZA

Henry Oldenburg, as already remarked, was a native of Bremen, where he was born around 1620. During the war between England and Holland that followed Cromwell's enforcement of the Navigation Act in 1651, the shipowners of Bremen seem to have suffered. It was therefore decided to send an envoy to make representations to Cromwell concerning the neutrality of Bremen. Accordingly, in 1653, Henry Oldenburg was entrusted with this diplomatic mission, which brought him into contact with Milton, who was then Latin Secretary to the Council, and other eminent Englishmen of the time. For some reason, he remained in England after the conclusion of his mission, staying in Oxford in 1656 and acting as a private tutor to various young gentlemen, including Boyle's nephew, Richard Jones. He traveled with Jones in France, Germany, and Italy during the years 1657–1660, attending the most important academies of science and becoming acquainted with the leading figures of the scientific world. During his stay in Oxford, Oldenburg had been associated with the leading spirits of the "Invisible College," a society for the discussion of scientific problems. There was a similar society in London, the "Gresham College." With the Restoration of Charles II in 1660, it was decided to apply for a charter for the formation of a "Royal Society" to carry on the work of these two societies, and an acting secretary was required to undertake the work of organization, etc. Just then, Oldenburg returned from his continental tour, and his wide reading and extensive knowledge of people and affairs marked him out as the ideal man for the post, for which he was accordingly nominated. In the following year, 1661, Oldenburg had occasion to visit his native town, Bremen, and on his return journey *via* Holland, he visited Leyden (among other places), and then Rijnsburg, where, as already mentioned, he had a long interview with Spinoza.

The subject discussed on that occasion and the impres-

the impression which Spinoza made on Oldenburg may be gathered from the following letter which Oldenburg wrote to Spinoza immediately after his return to London, in August 1661.
> "It was with such reluctance," [he writes] "that I tore myself away from your side, when I recently visited you in your retreat at Rijnsburg, that no sooner am I back in England than I already try to join you again, at least as far as this can be achieved by means of correspondence. Solid learning combined with kindness and refinement (with which Nature and study have most richly endowed you) have such an attraction that they win the love of all noble and liberally educated men. Let us, therefore, most excellent sir, give each other the right hand of unfeigned friendship, and cultivate it diligently by every kind of attention and service. Whatever service my humble powers can render, consider as yours. And permit me to claim a part of those intellectual gifts which you possess, if I may do so without detriment to you. Our conversation at Rijnsburg turned on God, infinite Extension and Thought, on the difference and the agreement between these attributes, on the nature of the union of the human soul with the body; and further, on the principles of the Cartesian and the Baconian philosophy. But as we then discussed themes of such importance only at a distance, as it were, and cursorily, and as all those things have since then been lying heavily on my mind, I now venture to claim the right of our new friendship to ask you affectionately to explain to me somewhat more fully your views on the subjects mentioned above, and not to mind enlightening me, more especially on these two points: namely, first, what do you consider to be the true distinction between Extension and Thought; secondly, what defects do you observe in the philosophy of Descartes and of Bacon, and how, do you think, might they be eliminated, and replaced by something more sound? The more freely you write to me about these and similar matters, the more closely..."

> "...you will bind me to yourself, and the greater will be my obligation to render similar services, if at all possible."

The letter concludes with a promise to send Spinoza a volume of scientific essays by Robert Boyle, between whom and Spinoza, Oldenburg subsequently acted as a kind of intermediary.

It is not at all clear what kind of an introduction Oldenburg had to Spinoza, or, indeed, whether he had any introduction at all. And Spinoza was neither so talkative nor so indiscreet as to reveal his whole mind to a stranger. But he had evidently treated Oldenburg generously and with his usual courtesy, and Oldenburg's letter is certainly very remarkable for its tone of generous appreciation—all the more remarkable because he was considerably older than Spinoza and had been befriended by so many of the intellectual giants of that period, while Spinoza was apparently an obscure outcast.

It is noteworthy that Spinoza's conversation with Oldenburg turned on Bacon and Descartes. This is not surprising, for Spinoza was at that time (1661) very much occupied with the question of philosophical method, and the two alternatives which he must have been carefully weighing against each other were the empirical, inductive method of Bacon, and the deductive, geometric method of Descartes. This was the very problem with which he was then struggling in his *Treatise on the Improvement of the Understanding*, as we gather from his subsequent reply to Oldenburg, which has already been cited above. Spinoza ultimately sided with Descartes in favor of the geometric method. He felt that the deductive method was the right one in philosophy, and that the best form of exposition was that exemplified in Euclid's geometry. This had already been urged and, to some extent, also illustrated by Descartes; and Spinoza also now tried a similar experiment by arranging one of the chapters of his *Short Treatise* into geometric form, consti-

...constituting what is now its First Appendix. Soon afterwards, he was occupied even more with Descartes and tried a much more extensive experiment in the application of the geometric method.

In 1662, possibly in the winter of 1661–1662, Johannes Casearius, a student of Theology at the University of Leiden, came to stay in Rijnsburg and lived in the same house with Spinoza, who agreed to help him with the study of philosophy. Casearius was only about nineteen then, apparently rather immature and fickle-minded, more devoted to novelty than to truth. He proved to be very trying to Spinoza and caused him some anxiety. Still, Spinoza had faith in the youth's good qualities, which only required a little time to mature and assert themselves. And the subsequent history of Casearius confirmed Spinoza's expectations. In the meantime, however, Spinoza had to be cautious in the treatment of his pupil. What Casearius no doubt wanted from Spinoza was that he should explain the newest philosophy to him. At that time, this generally meant Cartesianism. Spinoza had something newer than that, and Casearius may have caught some hint of this and came to him for that reason. But Spinoza did not think it good for one of his youth and temperament. He therefore decided to teach him the essentials of scholastic metaphysics as they were then taught at most universities, but to combine with it a good deal of his own criticism, and also to substitute Cartesian physics for the older physics entirely. He had probably followed a very similar course with his previous pupils in Amsterdam. But being convinced by this time that the geometric method was the most persuasive way of imparting knowledge, he turned the Second Part and a portion of the Third Part of Descartes' *Principia* into geometric form.

In the meanwhile, Spinoza had been growing discontented with his *Short Treatise*. For a time, he probably tried to

Finding, however, that he could wield the geometric method of exposition so well, he seems to have decided to start afresh, and to do for his own philosophy what he had already done, to some extent, for the philosophy of Descartes. In short, he began his *Ethics*, and early in the following year, 1663, a part, if not the whole, of the First Book of the *Ethics* was already in the hands of his Amsterdam friends.

By that time, however, Spinoza was already preparing to leave Rijnsburg. He had been there about three years then. Most likely they were his happiest years. They were certainly among his most fruitful years. But one of the reasons which had brought him there also drove him away now. He had come there so as to be able to work quietly, undisturbed by friend or foe. And for the first two years or so his hopes were realized. But gradually, as his circle of friends and acquaintances extended, more and more of his time was taken up by them, and taken away from his work. He therefore decided to move from there to Voorburg, near the Hague. He left Rijnsburg in April 1663, but, before going to Voorburg, he wanted to see his old friends again, and went accordingly to Amsterdam, where he stayed about two months. While on this visit to Amsterdam he showed his friends his Euclidean version of Descartes’ *Principia*, Part II. Jarig Jelles, Lodewijk Meyer, and other Cartesian friends of his then persuaded him to do the same with the first part of the *Principia*. He did so in two weeks, and consented to their publication, together with his own *Metaphysical Thoughts*, on condition that Meyer revised the whole work, improving its phraseology where necessary, and adding a preface to explain that Spinoza was far from being in entire agreement with the Cartesian philosophy, even as thus molded in the Euclidean

Spinoza (it should be noted here immediately) had no delusions about the absolute logical force of the geometric method. For in his very first publication, he explained and defended *more geometrico* a system of philosophy with which he did not agree.

### § 7. SPINOZA’S STAY IN VOORBURG—1663–1670

In June 1663, Spinoza arrived in Voorburg and took up his lodgings in the *Kerklaan*, at the house of a painter named Daniel Tydemann. Although it was little more than a half-hour walk from The Hague, the village of Voorburg was at that time almost as isolated as Rijnsburg, and there were times when it took Spinoza a week or more to send or receive a letter from The Hague. During the next two years or so, he was busy working on his *Ethics*. But he also found time to maintain a fairly extensive correspondence with old friends, to make new friends, and to pay occasional visits to other towns. In the winter of 1663–1664, he returned to Rijnsburg for about two months; in the following winter (1664–1665), he seems to have visited either the sister or the brother of Simon de Vries at Schiedam; in the following April (1665), he visited his old friends in Amsterdam. He also made frequent excursions to The Hague, where he used to stay with a certain Mesach Tydemann, possibly a brother of his Voorburg landlord.

If Spinoza found Voorburg rather lonely at first, conditions changed soon enough, so that he complained that he was hardly his own master because so much of his time was taken up

Among the people with whom he associated more or less during his stay in Voorburg, the most interesting were Vossius the philologist, who later became the Canon of Windsor (who probably consulted Spinoza on subjects relating to the Hebrew language and literature, much as his father, Gerhard Vossius, used to consult Manasseh ben Israel), Christian Huygens, Hudde, van Beuningen, and Jan de Witt.

Christian Huygens—the discoverer of Saturn’s rings, inventor of the pendulum clock, and originator of the undulatory theory of light—was living within easy walking distance of Spinoza during the years 1664–1666, and the two saw a great deal of one another during that period. Both of them were keenly interested in the making and improvement of lenses, and this common interest formed their chief or only bond. In character, the two men were very different. Spinoza was generous and open in sharing whatever knowledge he possessed that might be of service to others; Huygens, on the other hand, was stingy and always on his guard lest his trade secrets should leak out. In his letters to his brothers, Huygens refers to Spinoza as *l’Israélite*, *le Juif de Voorburg*, or *notre Juif*; he asks his brother to inform him of Spinoza’s activities, but urges him to keep a certain optical secret from him, fearing that Hudde and others might hear of it through him. To strangers, he undoubtedly spoke of Spinoza with greater respect. For instance, some years later (1675), he remarked to Tschirnhaus that he had a great regard for Spinoza.

It was probably through Huygens that Spinoza became acquainted with Johan Hudde. Hudde was the Burgomaster of Amsterdam and a member of the States of Holland, in which capacity he frequently visited The Hague, which was the seat of government. He was, moreover, a man with a scientific mind, which prompted him to take up the art of grinding lenses, which in those days seems to have been a fashionable hobby, not unlike present-day photo-

We have just seen how anxious Christian Huygens was lest Hudde should learn more from Spinoza than Huygens wanted him to know. Moreover, unlike Huygens, Hudde was also keenly interested in problems of religious philosophy, and we still have three letters that Spinoza addressed to him on the subject of God’s unity. Hudde very likely introduced Spinoza to some of his friends in the political sphere, and was, undoubtedly, instrumental in obtaining for Spinoza the protection and patronage that was possibly one of the main reasons why Spinoza had moved near The Hague.

When Spinoza consented to the publication of his version of Descartes’ *Principia*, he had a specific goal in mind. He explained this goal clearly in his letter to Oldenburg in late July 1663. “It may be <note>he writes</note> that on this occasion some of those who hold the highest positions in my country may be found wishing to see my other writings, which I acknowledge as expressing my views; they will then enable me to publish them without any risk of violating the civil law. If this happens, I will certainly publish something immediately; but if not, I would rather be silent than impose my opinions on people against the wishes of my country, and so incur their hostility.” What exactly Spinoza intended to publish immediately is not quite certain—possibly the *Short Treatise*, or more likely the first book of his *Ethics*, or the whole work, which he may have hoped to complete in the near future.

At all events, it is clear that Spinoza was anxious to win the sympathy of some of those who held the reins of government, and Hudde was just the man to help him. He probably introduced him to Coenraad van Beuningen, a former Burgomaster of Amsterdam and at one time a diplomatic envoy of the Netherlands to the courts of France and

Van Beuningen was friendly toward the Jews, and when Louis XIV remarked to him that it was scandalous that the Dutch should tolerate the Jews, he replied: “Is not the fact that God himself has not destroyed them a proof that He wants them to be tolerated in the world? And since all other countries expel them, and yet they must live somewhere, it cannot be ungodly that Amsterdam at least should receive them.” But most important of all was Spinoza’s introduction to Jan de Witt, the Grand Pensionary of Holland, of whom more will be said presently.

Spinoza was gradually being drawn into the turbulent current of contemporary politics. In the meantime, however, he was making progress with his *Ethics*, receiving calls from old friends and distinguished strangers, and corresponding with all sorts and conditions of people.

Oldenburg’s first letter to Spinoza, which was cited above, was followed by a cordial and regular correspondence. The Royal Society, of which Oldenburg was the acting secretary, had (as Spinoza was duly informed) received its royal charter from Charles II in 1662, and was going full sail on its course of scientific exploration. Its ambition was nothing less than (to use Oldenburg’s bold phrase) “to take the whole universe to task,” and its versatile cosmopolitan secretary spared no pains to publish its doings to the world, and to gather all the latest scientific news and gossip from the four corners of the earth. Spinoza thus heard from Oldenburg all that was done in England for the advancement of science, as well as frequent kind messages from Robert Boyle, who, however, never condescended to write himself to the “odd philosopher,” though he sent him his writings, invited his criticisms, and replied to them through Oldenburg. Spinoza also sent what news he could, especially about Huygens. Occasionally we hear echoes of contemporary events in spheres other than the purely scientific. Oldenburg complains about the Plague which was raging in London

And he wants to know what Spinoza and also the Jews of Amsterdam think of the “rumor which is on everyone’s lips here that the Jews are about to return to Palestine.” This referred to the escapades of the impostor *Sabbatai Zevi*, who began as a pseudo-Messiah and ended as an apostate, but whose pretenses, aided by the incessant sufferings of the Jews, deceived for a time even the Amsterdam Jews, whose opinion Oldenburg was curious to know—prayers being offered up in the Amsterdam Synagogue for “the King Messiah,” and some new prayer-books being dated “the year one of the Messiah”! It would be interesting to know what Spinoza thought about this tragi-comedy. But just at this point the correspondence between Spinoza and Oldenburg comes to an abrupt end. The next letter between them, at least of those which still survive, was written some ten years later. Possibly there were other letters, or it may be that the Great Fire of London in 1666 and the continued war between England and Holland (in which Bremen, Oldenburg’s native city, sided with England) made further correspondence impractical for a time; while in 1667 Oldenburg was actually imprisoned in the Tower of London, charged with “dangerous plans and practices,” the vagueness of which suggests that it was simply his vast foreign correspondence that had made him an object of suspicion to a king who was too much of an adept at intrigue not to suspect everyone, and to a government which had no appreciation of a man who had “taken to task the whole universe.” Oldenburg was eventually released; but his sad experiences had made him nervous and circumspect, as we shall see.

Evidently Spinoza had informed him that he was already at work on what was to be the *Tractatus Theologico-Politicus*. And in his reply to Oldenburg's letter, Spinoza writes (September or October 1665) quite explicitly that he is writing a treatise on the Scriptures. The *Ethica*, then, must have been put aside suddenly, just as it was nearing completion, and for the next four years or so we find Spinoza hard at work on his *Tractatus Theologico-Politicus*. This certainly seems strange. What was the cause of this sudden change in the direction of his thoughts?

In his letter to Oldenburg, Spinoza states three reasons that prompted him to take up the new treatise. In the first place, he wanted to deal with the theologians, whose prejudices were the main obstacle preventing people from becoming philosophical. Spinoza intended to expose these prejudices and even hoped to convert some of the more intelligent clergymen. In the second place, he wanted to refute the charge of atheism that was constantly brought against him. In the third place, he wanted to defend by every means in his power freedom of thought and speech from the tyranny and arrogance of the clergy, who were doing their utmost to suppress it. To appreciate these reasons adequately, it is necessary to make a brief survey of the historical circumstances that seemed to call for such a book as the *Tractatus Theologico-Politicus*, the need for which must evidently have appeared very urgent to Spinoza to have made him put aside his great work, just as it was nearing completion, in order to tackle these mixed problems of theology and politics.

Spinoza, as we have seen, was anxious to win the favor of the men who were in power, so that he might publish his philosophy without interference. Such patronage was indispensable in those days, for the sake of both the thinker

<summary>Spinoza halted his work on the Ethica to write the Tractatus Theologico-Politicus, a decision driven by his desire to challenge theological prejudice, refute charges of atheism, and defend freedom of speech.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the historical and political environment in the Netherlands that influenced Spinoza's work, specifically focusing on the religious opposition to new philosophy and the political tension between the Republican and Orange factions.</meta>

<page-num>lxxv</page-num>
<header>THE LIFE OF SPINOZA</header>

and his thoughts. Descartes, for instance, did not feel safe, despite his most formal gestures of respect to the Church; and even in the Netherlands, where there was neither reason nor desire to worry about the feelings of the holy Roman Church, Cartesian philosophy met with considerable resistance from the clergy and was repeatedly forbidden from being taught at the universities. Although the civil authorities were generally inclined to be liberal, the Calvinist or Reformed clergy often had enough power to cause the confiscation and destruction of books to which they took exception, and the authors of such books were occasionally made to suffer both financially and physically. Spinoza's desire to win the favor and protection of those in power was therefore natural enough. And he succeeded almost better than he could have expected. For he gained the sympathy of no less a person than the Grand Pensionary himself—Jan de Witt. However, his very success in a way defeated his primary goal by diverting his attention from purely philosophical problems. How this happened will soon be clear.

Reference has already been made to the struggle between the Remonstrants and the contra-Remonstrants, and the tragic fate of the Remonstrant leader, Barneveldt, in 1619. That conflict was by no means a purely religious one. Church and State, religion and politics—if not quite as closely united as elsewhere—were far from being completely separated, even in the Netherlands. Politically, that conflict was between the principle of autonomy for each of the United Provinces (and especially for Holland) and the principle of the dominance of the House of Orange. In that early conflict, Barneveldt stood for the former principle, while Maurice, the *Stadtholder* (or the so-called "Lieutenant," but actually a virtual or aspiring monarch), stood for the latter. Although Barneveldt's life ended in 1619, the conflict did not; it only required a suitable opportunity to break out again. In 1650, the *Stadtholder*, William II, chagrined

with the motto, *Liberty forever!* The years which followed
were years of great anxiety for the Netherlands. Cromwell,
prompted by the idealistic idea of a European Protestant
Coalition, proposed to the States-General of the Netherlands
that they should allow themselves to be absorbed by Eng-
land. When this was declined, he brought the “Naviga-
tion Act” into operation with a view to crippling the Dutch
shipping trade. War followed. But negotiations were
soon reopened, and peace was concluded in 1654. It was
during these troubles that Jan de Witt, the brilliant son of
Jacob de Witt, found and used his opportunity. In 1653 he
had been elected Grand Pensionary of Holland, and it was
largely through his skill that the peace negotiations with
England reached a successful conclusion in the following year.
Unfortunately for de Witt, Cromwell, in his anxiety to keep
Charles II at a safe distance, stipulated as one of the con-
ditions of peace that the young Prince of Orange (son of
William II and nephew of Charles II) should be made in-
eligible for the posts of Stadtholder and Captain-General of
the Netherlands forces. And, knowing that most of the
United Provinces would strongly resent the very sugges-
tion of such a condition, de Witt had to persuade the
Hollanders to bind themselves at least to such a secret
“Act of Seclusion.” This, of course, was bound to inten-
sify the opposition between the de Witts and the House of
Orange, and to lead to a fresh conflict between the Repub-
lican and the Monarchist parties in the Netherlands. The
House of Orange, largely owing to its early alliance (in
the days of Barneveldt) with the orthodox majority, eventu-

eventually realized their monarchical ambitions, and the de Witts, whose broad tolerance and republican zeal made them more like William the Silent than were his own descendants, were destined to meet with a tragic end. But all that was still to come. At the time with which we are at present concerned, Jan de Witt was still the Grand Pensionary of Holland, and virtually the head of the United Provinces. Still, he had his enemies. His very tolerance gained for him the secret opposition of the Reformed Clergy, who were bent on Calvinizing everybody and everything. And the Orange party was diligent in cultivating the friendship of the Calvinists. The one radical safeguard for the maintenance of the Republic, as de Witt must have seen, lay in widening the outlook of its citizens, so that politics might be purged of religious animosities, and people might live at peace with each other and cooperate in all national enterprises without regard to their private views on matters which did not affect their conduct as citizens. In 1665, during the wars with England and Sweden, when the Dutch were so hard pressed that they had to employ French troops, the voice of discontent made itself heard in various quarters; Calvinist prophets capitalized on these temporary trials by proclaiming them to be visitations sent from heaven in punishment for the godlessness of the country's rulers, and clamored that the young Prince of Orange should be set in supreme authority to make the country more godly.

> "Moses and Aaron, the Sword and the Word,"

they cried, must always go hand in hand.

Already before this, Jan de Witt seems to have urged or encouraged various writers, who shared his views, to use their pens in support of his policy of tolerance—in short, in support of the separation between Church and State. One such book was written by his own nephew and namesake, others were written by Dr. Lodewijk Meyer and other members of the Spinoza circle, and Jan de Witt himself is

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the political environment in the Dutch Republic, specifically regarding the efforts of republican leaders like Jan de Witt to combat religious intolerance, and introduces Spinoza's shift from his philosophical work to political engagement.</meta>

<page-num>lxxviii</page-num>
<header>INTRODUCTION</header>

said to have written or contributed some chapters to such a political pamphlet. It seems natural enough, therefore, that at such a critical period Spinoza, the “good republican,” should lay aside his more speculative *Ethica* in order to play his part in the struggle against bigotry and intolerance. He intended to expose the prejudices, the arrogance, and the lust for power of the clerical party. But it was useless to simply add one more political pamphlet to the many in which the principle of freedom of thought and speech had already been ably defended on general philosophical and humanitarian grounds. The zealots were deaf and blind to such arguments. To them, philosophy meant heresy, and humanism meant atheism. The stronghold of the clerics was the Bible. From it they drew all the arguments with which they so often silenced people, even when they failed to convince them. Spinoza resolved to turn his attention to the stronghold itself, leaving mere skirmishes to others. He would show that the very Bible on which these arrogant theologians based their whole case did not support them at all, that they were simply ignorant of these very Scriptures, and that they used religion and the Bible merely as a cloak for their own impudent lust for power over others. Such a work required vast and varied learning and insight—but Spinoza (and at that time perhaps he alone) possessed them to an eminent degree. And it required time—perhaps more than Spinoza anticipated. But Spinoza begrudged neither time nor effort, and for the next four years he was deeply engrossed in theological and political studies, which resulted in the *Tractatus Theologico-Politicus*.

Intermingled with this wider, grander motive there was yet another, a private or personal motive. He desired to show (as he wrote to Oldenburg in the autumn of 1665) that he was not an atheist, as was commonly supposed. By the time Spinoza finished his treatise he had probably forgotten all about this private aim. If he was really still anxious to change public opinion about himself, he could hardly hope

<summary>Spinoza paused his work on the Ethica to write the Tractatus Theologico-Politicus, seeking to challenge the clerical party’s authority by using a scholarly analysis of the Bible to defend freedom of thought. He also hoped to use the work to disprove the common accusation that he was an atheist.</summary>

to do so by publishing his treatise anonymously, as he did in 1670. The fact is that although his personal experiences added zest to his enterprise in 1665, they gradually faded into the background as he proceeded with his work. But in any case, it is interesting to ask what these personal experiences were. One naturally thinks, at first, of his excommunication in 1656. But that was an old story by then, and at that time Spinoza was hardly concerned much, if at all, about the good opinion of the Amsterdam Jews. It is better to turn to Voorburg, and to what happened there in 1665, to shed light on this subject. The event we are referring to was not significant, but it is interesting as an incident in Spinoza's life and as typical of the religious climate of the time. The pastorate of the Voorburg Church happened to be vacant that year. There were two candidates: one liberal and the other orthodox. Spinoza's landlord and others petitioned the authorities on behalf of the more liberal candidate. Consequently, the orthodox party sent a counter-petition accusing the Tydemann party of sheer wickedness; they stated at the same time that the Tydemann petition had been "concocted by a certain Spinoza, an Amsterdam Jew by birth, who is an atheist, scoffs at all religion, and is inflicting harm on the Republic, as many learned persons and ministers can attest." Evidently Spinoza had a bad reputation among the champions of orthodoxy in the village, though it is pleasant to think that the more liberal section showed enough faith in him to enlist his sympathy and help even in their religious concerns.

In the course of the same year, Spinoza had a distinguished visitor: Field-Marshal Charles de St. Denis, Seigneur de St. Evremont, who left us a pleasant record of his impression. "Spinoza [he wrote] was of medium height and had pleasant features. His knowledge, his modesty, and his unselfishness made all the intellectual people in The Hague esteem him and seek his acquaintance."

# INTRODUCTION

<page-num>lxxx</page-num>
<header>INTRODUCTION</header>

Spinoza remained in Voorburg until 1670, but not many details have reached us about him even during this period. He kept in touch with his Amsterdam friends, to whom he sent his manuscript of the *Ethics* for reading and discussion at their philosophical society’s gatherings. Some of them, notably Simon de Vries, also visited him at Voorburg. That Spinoza’s health was not robust is evident from his letter to one of his medical friends in Amsterdam (Adriaan Koerbagh), to whom he incidentally mentions that he had been suffering repeatedly from tertian ague <note>a fever, often malarial, that recurs every third day</note>, and asks him for some conserve of roses. It was about this time, apparently, that Simon de Vries wanted Spinoza to accept a gift from him of two thousand florins. Simon de Vries died in 1667, and his death must have been felt very deeply by Spinoza. 

The following year, 1668, brought bad news about another of his friends. Adriaan Koerbagh, whom Spinoza got to know at Rijnsburg, had studied law and medicine at Leiden, and possessed considerable mental gifts. Spinoza liked him and encouraged him in the study of philosophy; in the above-mentioned letter, he actually offered to send him the manuscript of the *Ethics*. But, though clever, Koerbagh seems to have had little or no character. In any case, early in 1668, he published two works titled *A Garden of Flowers* and *Light in Dark Places*, in which he attacked medicine, morals, and religion in a most reckless and shameless manner. He was promptly arrested, and though he expressed regret and recanted, yet (as this was not his first offense) he was fined 6,000 florins and condemned to ten years’ imprisonment with hard labor, to be followed by exile. It should be mentioned to his honor that he entirely exonerated his brother, who had also been arrested; and when Spinoza’s name was mentioned during the trial, he took the entire responsibility upon himself, emphatically denying that Spinoza or anyone else was in any way responsible for what he had written. However

<summary>This page describes Spinoza's life in Voorburg, his sharing of the Ethics manuscript with friends, and the tragic imprisonment of his associate Adriaan Koerbagh for publishing controversial works. Despite his own health struggles with tertian ague, Spinoza remained central to a circle of loyal, though sometimes persecuted, intellectuals.</summary>

<keywords>Spinoza, Ethics, Simon de Vries, Adriaan Koerbagh, Voorburg, tertian ague, A Garden of Flowers, Light in Dark Places, Leiden, philosophical society</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the biography of Spinoza, following the legal condemnation of his friend Adriaan Koerbagh in 1668.</meta>

<page-num>lxxxi</page-num>
<header>THE LIFE OF SPINOZA</header>

Though there may be little to say in Koerbagh's favor, the punishment was certainly savage. One of the officers of the court had actually urged a much more severe sentence: that his fortune should be confiscated, his right thumb cut off, his tongue bored through with a red-hot iron, and that he should be imprisoned for thirty years! Koerbagh died in prison the following year. The affair must have made a deep impression on Spinoza, who had expected much from him and whose views Koerbagh had certainly assimilated and spread—though Spinoza would have been the last person to condone immorality.

In the meantime, Spinoza had been busy with his *Tractatus Theologico-Politicus*, and it was published in 1670. He had now been seven years at Voorburg and may have needed a change, or perhaps his friends in The Hague had urged him to come and live among them. In any case, Spinoza left the village and went to live in The Hague.

### § 8. SPINOZA'S STAY IN THE HAGUE—1670-1677

Spinoza's first lodgings in The Hague were situated on the *Stille Veerkade*, a quiet wharf not far from the Great Church of St. James. He lodged and boarded with a widow named Van Velen. A single room on the second floor served him as bedroom, workroom, and study, all in one. Interestingly enough, it was in that same room that Colerus subsequently wrote one of the earliest biographies of Spinoza. The house has been identified (it bears the number 32), but it has, no doubt, been very much altered since those days; and the *Stille Veerkade* is no longer a wharf but an ordinary street, the waterway having been filled in with earth long ago.

Probably one of the attractions which The Hague had

His room on the Stille Veerkade was notably the same location where his biographer Colerus later lived and worked.</summary>
<keywords>Adriaan Koerbagh, Tractatus Theologico-Politicus, Voorburg, The Hague, Stille Veerkade, Van Velen, Colerus, biography</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Spinoza's life in The Hague and his relationship with the political leader Jan de Witt following the publication of the *Tractatus Theologico-Politicus*.</meta>

for Spinoza was that it brought him into closer touch with Jan de Witt. It seems certain that he had already known him for some time. The political views of the *Tractatus Theologico-Politicus* are very similar to those of the Grand Pensionary, and it was under his protection that this treatise had been published. When the opportunity arose, de Witt's enemies spoke quite openly of the treatise as a wicked instrument "forged in hell by a renegade Jew and the devil, and issued with the knowledge of Mr. Jan de Witt." It was probably also during his stay in Voorburg, and while giving his time and energy to the composition of the *Tractatus Theologico-Politicus*, that Spinoza accepted an annual pension of 200 florins from de Witt, which was paid even after de Witt's death. Once in The Hague, Spinoza must have received many visits from the Grand Pensionary; and local gossip, indeed, still refers to such private visits from him, and his usual entrance by the garden door at the back of the house.

The need for protection from high places showed itself soon enough. As early as the June following the publication of the *Tractatus Theologico-Politicus*, the Church Council of Amsterdam had condemned it, and the condemnation of other Church Councils followed in rapid succession. The book had caused a great stir in the learned world and went through five editions within a relatively short time. But it had stirred up a hornet's nest, and for many years to come, theologians and other respectable people showed their orthodoxy by constant denunciations of that godless treatise. The civil authorities were repeatedly approached and pressured to exercise the power of the law. But as long as de Witt was in power, the persistent zealots were successfully resisted. Even after de Witt's death there were men, like Burgomaster Hudde, who could, for a time, defeat the efforts of the clergy. But when William III found it desirable to ingratiate himself with the clergy and the mob,
