and to play to the gallery for a crown, the *Tractatus Theologico-Politicus* was strictly prohibited (1674), and other measures were also contemplated against the known author of the anonymous treatise.

In May 1671, Spinoza found it necessary to change his lodgings. He was receiving 300 florins a year from the brother of Simon de Vries and 200 florins a year from de Witt—that is, about £40 a year—besides what little he may have still been earning by making lenses. He found that he could not afford to continue paying Mrs. Van Velen’s charges for board and lodging, and therefore looked for rooms where he might provide his own food and economize in that way. Accordingly, he moved into the adjoining *Paviljoensgragt*, where he rented two small rooms in the house of a painter named Hendrik van der Spyck. This house has also been identified and may now be recognized by the tablet affixed to the front wall just below the window on the second story, where Spinoza’s rooms were located. Here also, the “gragt,” or waterway, has long since made room for an ordinary road. Spinoza lived with the Van der Spycks until the end of his life.

When Spinoza settled in The Hague after the publication of his *Tractatus Theologico-Politicus*, he once more turned his attention to his neglected *Ethics*, which had already seemed near completion in 1665. The comparatively long interval that had passed since he had put it aside to take up more urgent work had probably brought with it the need or the desire for significant modifications or elaborations of details; consequently, the *Ethics* only reached its final form in 1675. In the meantime, however, Spinoza must have also devoted his attention to other things besides the *Ethics*. While at work on his *Tractatus Theologico-Politicus*, he had again taken up his Hebrew and Biblical studies and had mastered a massive amount of political literature. In that treatise, he was chiefly concerned with the

<summary>Due to financial constraints, Spinoza moved to more affordable rooms in the house of Hendrik van der Spyck in 1671, where he eventually completed his final version of the Ethics in 1675.</summary>

final results of these studies and reflections, and the different departments of thought were necessarily all intermingled. It would naturally occur to him, or some of his friends would suggest to him, that it was desirable to work out each of these subjects independently, and more fully than was possible in the above treatise. Spinoza, while completing and perfecting his *Ethics*, would accordingly also be preparing for a scientific treatise on the Hebrew language, for a translation of the Old Testament based on such an exposition of the character of Hebrew, and, lastly, for a separate treatise on political theories. By way of a change from theology and politics he would also turn again sometimes to mathematics and physical science, with a view to supplementing his *Ethics*, some day, by a treatise on natural philosophy. That Spinoza wished to write such a work on natural philosophy, and also to give a new exposition of the principles of algebra, we know; but he did not live to realize these wishes. His other intentions fared rather better. Spinoza did begin a *Hebrew Grammar*, a Dutch translation of the Bible, and a *Political Treatise*. But he seems to have been dissatisfied with his translation, and destroyed what he had done. The *Hebrew Grammar* remained unfinished, so did the *Political Treatise*, which, however, was much nearer completion. He has also left an essay *On the Rainbow* and another *On the Calculation of Chances*. Very likely he did not begin to write all or any of these while he was still occupied with his *Ethics*. But he must have been preparing for them, and we are told that at times he was so hard at work that he did not leave his room for days, nor go out of the house for three months at a stretch.

In the meantime black clouds were gathering in the political atmosphere, and a storm was preparing to burst upon the heads of the de Witts and their friends.

Reference has already been made to the war between England and Holland in 1665. That war was concluded in

1667, when England was persuaded to reach an agreement partly due to de Ruyter’s daring and successful expedition to Chatham (where the sound of Dutch guns was heard in London), but even more so by the intervention of Louis XIV, who took sides with the Netherlands. Shortly afterward, however, Louis XIV revived his claims to the Spanish Netherlands (Belgium) and led an army there. The Dutch grew alarmed. It was good to have Louis XIV as a friend, but it was dangerous to have him as a neighbor. Jan de Witt consequently sought a means of checking French ambitions and succeeded in doing so through the Triple Alliance between the Netherlands, England, and Sweden. This was in 1668. Louis XIV intended to take revenge on de Witt. First, he started a tariff war with the Netherlands; next, he bribed Charles II (by the Secret Treaty of Dover, 1671); and in 1672, England and France declared war against the Netherlands, and a French army of 120,000 men invaded the totally unprepared United Provinces. For some time, there had been a growing conspiracy in favor of the young Prince of Orange and against Jan de Witt, who had done his best to keep him from power, especially by engineering the “Perpetual Edict” of 1667, which decreed that no Captain-General or Admiral-General of the United Provinces could simultaneously be the *Stadtholder* of a province. The conspiracy now reached a sudden climax. There was a cry for the Prince of Orange to take command in the field and deliver the country as his father had done. The “Perpetual Edict” was overturned, and its author was not forgotten on the day of reckoning. With the country unprepared and the enemy sweeping everything before them, the populace was easily stirred to uncontrollable fury, which had to find an outlet upon a scapegoat. After failed attempts to arrange their legal execution, the mob broke into the prison at The Hague while Jan de Witt was visiting his brother Cornelis there and murdered the two brothers in

<meta>This page continues the account of the political upheaval in the Netherlands, detailing the 1672 murder of the de Witt brothers and its profound impact on Spinoza, as well as the later execution of his teacher, Van den Enden.</meta>

<page-num>lxxxvi</page-num>

in the most brutal fashion. This happened on the 20th of August 1672. More than twenty years of the most devoted and able service to the Republic were forgotten in a moment of wrath, and the Prince of Orange, William III (the future King of England), was not altogether guiltless of the crime.

When Spinoza heard of the horrible tragedy, he was quite beside himself. His usual philosophic calm entirely deserted him. He burst into tears, and, distracted with grief and anger, he wrote on a placard his utter abhorrence of "the very lowest of barbarians" who had committed the iniquitous murder. He wanted to go out and post his denunciation near the scene of the crime. Fortunately, Van der Spyck was more discreet. He locked the door, so that Spinoza could not get out to share the fate of the de Witts.

Some time after these terrible events, the heirs of Jan de Witt showed some hesitation about continuing Spinoza's pension. Some of the philosopher's friends, when they heard of it, urged him to enforce his legal claims on the strength of the written promise which he possessed. But Spinoza simply returned that document to de Witt's heirs, without any comment. Impressed by his conduct, they continued his pension without any further trouble.

The war between France and Holland proved fatal to yet another friend of Spinoza. His old teacher, Van den Enden, had been compelled to leave Amsterdam some years before these events. For a time he stayed in Antwerp, and then settled in Paris. Here his desire to help his own country at that critical period led him to join in a conspiracy to betray Quillebœuf to the Dutch, and to raise a rebellion in Normandy. All this would, of course, have greatly helped the Netherlands in their struggle with Louis XIV. But the conspiracy was discovered, and Van den Enden was beheaded in front of the Bastille in November 1674. Such was the tragic end of the man who had befriended Spinoza.

in the early days of his struggle, and who had contributed not a little towards the early development of his scientific thinking.

The war with France had yet further consequences in store for Spinoza. In 1673, the French army under Prince Condé was encamped at Utrecht, and among the officers there was a Colonel Stoupe, who was in charge of a Swiss regiment. Stoupe was a former parson, well-read, but an adventurer. Condé was a man of liberal views, and interested in art, science, and philosophy. And during their enforced idleness at Utrecht, Stoupe suggested that as Spinoza (already famous as the author of the geometric version of Descartes' *Principia*, and much more so as the author of the *Tractatus Theologico-Politicus*) lived quite near, at the Hague, it would be interesting to get to know him. Condé accordingly sent, through Stoupe, an invitation to Spinoza to visit him at Utrecht. What induced Stoupe to seek the acquaintance of Spinoza seems fairly clear. Although a Calvinist, and at one time a minister of his religion, he had brought a regiment of Swiss soldiers into the service of Catholic France against the Calvinist Netherlands. The fact is that he was just an unscrupulous adventurer; at heart (as Bishop Burnet has said of him) he was neither a Protestant nor a Christian, but a man of intrigue and of no virtue. But he was anxious to keep up appearances, and when a countryman of his took him severely to task for helping the Catholics against his own fellow-Calvinists, he tried to defend himself by suggesting that the majority of the Dutch were not Calvinists at all, but heretics of the blackest dye. In a pamphlet which he published about September 1673, he refers to Spinoza as a bad Jew and a worse Christian, who had written a treatise with the aim of destroying all religion and establishing atheism. This book (he added) was, nevertheless, openly sold and widely read, and no Dutchman has taken the trouble to refute it, while

<summary>During the French occupation of Utrecht in 1673, Colonel Stoupe invited the famous Spinoza to meet Prince Condé, though Stoupe later publicly attacked Spinoza's character and work to shield his own lack of religious integrity.</summary>
<keywords>Spinoza, Prince Condé, Colonel Stoupe, Utrecht, Descartes' Principia, Tractatus Theologico-Politicus, Bishop Burnet, Calvinism, Catholicism, atheism</keywords>

--- Page 94 ---
<meta>Continuing from the account of Colonel Stoupe's motives, this page details Spinoza's journey to the French camp at Utrecht and the hostile reception he received upon his return to the Hague.</meta>

<page-num>lxxxviii</page-num>
<header>INTRODUCTION</header>

its author was, in fact, much sought after by learned men and fashionable ladies, and so on. The object of the invitation to Spinoza, so far as Stoupe was concerned, was therefore simply to get what information he could that might be used for his self-defense. And such were the terms in which he described Spinoza, apparently at the very time when he professed the greatest regard for him!

Spinoza, on the other hand, a dreamer by nature, probably saw in this invitation from Prince Condé a possible opening for peace negotiations, and was anxious to do his duty. He seems to have consulted some people in authority, and whatever they may have thought about it privately, they could certainly see no harm in Spinoza’s errand. And so, armed with the necessary safe-conducts, Spinoza made his way to Utrecht in May 1673. He was well received by Count Luxembourg, on behalf of Prince Condé, who had in the meantime been called away, and he was invited to stay there and await the Prince’s return. Spinoza’s interactions with the Count, with Stoupe, and others there, seem to have been of the friendliest kind, and it is known that he made a very good impression. But when, after waiting several weeks, the news arrived that Condé could not return, Spinoza took his departure. He had been offered a pension if he would dedicate a book to Louis XIV; but Spinoza was not Stoupe—he was not ready to serve any master for hire. He declined the request and returned to the Hague.

The people at the Hague had, in the meantime, caught wind of Spinoza’s visit to the enemy’s camp. With the charity of a mob, they could give only one meaning to this—Spinoza was a spy. When, therefore, he arrived at the Hague, scowls and stones greeted his return, and Van der Spyck was afraid that the mob would break into the house. Spinoza, however, begged him not to be afraid. “I am innocent,” he said,

<summary>Spinoza travels to Utrecht in 1673 to meet Prince Condé, hoping to aid peace efforts, but he is met with suspicion and accusations of being a spy upon returning to the Hague. Despite being offered a royal pension to dedicate a work to Louis XIV, Spinoza maintains his independence and refuses the offer.</summary>

<meta>Continuing from the account of Colonel Stoupe’s motives, this page describes Spinoza’s brave return to the Hague and his subsequent refusal of a prestigious academic position at the University of Heidelberg.</meta>

> “and some of our leading statesmen know why I went to Utrecht. As soon as the people make any commotion, I shall go out to them, even if they should do to me what they did to the good de Witts. I am a good Republican, and my desire is the good of the Republic.”

Apparently Spinoza’s frank and fearless bearing in the moment of danger reassured the suspicious people, and he escaped without harm.

The invitation from Prince Condé was not the only compliment paid to Spinoza that year. A more important invitation had reached him in February. It came from the Elector Palatine, Karl Ludwig, the brother of the Princess Elizabeth, who had befriended Descartes. The Elector offered him the Professorship of Philosophy at the University of Heidelberg. The invitation certainly had considerable attractions, and Spinoza considered it for about six weeks. But, in the first place, he could not make up his mind to become a public teacher after all these years of habitual quietness and retirement. In the second place, he had misgivings about the statement made in the invitation concerning the Prince’s confidence that Spinoza would not misuse his freedom in philosophical teaching to disturb the public religion.

> “I do not know [Spinoza wrote] the limits within which the freedom of my philosophical teaching would be confined, if I am to avoid all appearance of disturbing the publicly established religion. Religious quarrels do not arise so much from ardent zeal for religion as from men’s various dispositions, and the love of contradiction which makes them habitually distort and condemn everything. . . . I have experienced these things in my private and secluded life; how much more should I have to fear them after my promotion to this post of honor.”

So he gracefully acknowledged the Prince’s generosity in offering him the Professorship and declined it with thanks. There can be no doubt that it was the wisest course, for, besides the reasons stated by Spinoza himself, it must be

It should be remembered that he could scarcely tear himself away from his many friends in Holland, and that the course of events in his fatherland (as his political writings show) affected him too deeply to allow him to go abroad during that critical period. Moreover, although he may not have anticipated such an early end as befell him (he died four years later), yet with his condition of consumption, he could scarcely expect to live to an old age.

There can be no doubt that Spinoza had a large circle of friends and acquaintances, although the dominance of the orthodox and the bad reputation of his views compelled people, out of sheer prudence, to keep quiet about their knowledge and admiration of him. One of his most devoted friends at the Hague was a Dr. J. M. Lucas, a medical practitioner, who later wrote the oldest surviving biography of Spinoza, which expresses the most passionate devotion to the philosopher. Another of his medical friends was Dr. G. H. Schuller, who practiced medicine in Amsterdam, but also devoted much time to alchemy and philosophy. It was Schuller who brought Spinoza into contact with one of the most promising of the younger scientists, Tschirnhaus, and, through him, also with the most eminent philosopher of the next generation—Leibniz. Tschirnhaus was a young German Count who had studied at Leyden. In 1674, he made the acquaintance of Schuller in Amsterdam. Having studied Descartes, he was interested to hear all about Spinoza, with whom he soon started a correspondence, and he also came into personal contact with him toward the end of the same year. In the following summer, 1675, he visited London, where he met Oldenburg and Boyle. He also visited Paris in the same year, and, on the advice of Spinoza, called on Christian Huygens, who had settled in Paris since 1667, and (it is interesting to note by comparison) had continued to enjoy the profitable patronage of Louis XIV even during the years of disaster which that King had

inflicted on the Netherlands, while Spinoza had declined even to dedicate a book to him for the sake of a pension. The still interesting correspondence between Spinoza and Tschirnhaus lasted about two years. In 1683, Tschirnhaus published his *De Medicina Mentis* (*On the Medicine of the Mind*), dealing with the same problem as Spinoza's *Treatise on the Improvement of the Understanding*, and borrowing some of its ideas. But prudence prevented him from mentioning Spinoza, to whom he simply referred as *quidam* (somebody).

Incidentally, Tschirnhaus's visit to London led to a resumption of the correspondence between Oldenburg and Spinoza, which seems to have been dropped since 1665. Spinoza had sent a copy of his *Tractatus Theologico-Politicus* to Oldenburg, who felt rather shocked by its unorthodox views and expressed himself accordingly in a letter that may not have reached Spinoza, but which, in any case, would probably not have brought about a renewal of their correspondence. The account, however, which Tschirnhaus gave of Spinoza and his views seems to have produced a conciliatory effect on Oldenburg, who thereupon wrote another letter to Spinoza, saying that "a closer consideration of the whole subject had convinced him that he (Spinoza) was far from attempting any injury to true religion and sound philosophy." Spinoza, who had taken no notice of the various "refutations" of his treatise published by various people, was nevertheless anxious to know, and to discuss carefully, the objections which Oldenburg—or, indeed, any reasonable people—had to bring against his views. In the course of his increasingly stiff letters, it turns out that Oldenburg objected to the entire system of Spinoza's philosophy, and that what he wished Spinoza to do was nothing less than to write a kind of philosophical defense of orthodox Christianity! Spinoza may well have wondered whether Oldenburg was guilty of stupidity or of hypocrisy.

<header>INTRODUCTION</header>

In the meantime, Spinoza had finished his *Ethics* and was contemplating its immediate publication. He mentioned this to Oldenburg in a letter written at the end of June 1675. Oldenburg replied that he "would not object to receiving a few copies of the said treatise" to distribute among his friends, but asked him to send them in such a way that no one might know of it, and begged him "not to insert any passages which may seem to discourage the practice of religion and virtue."

About the end of July 1676 Spinoza went to Amsterdam to arrange for the publication of the *Ethica*. What happened there is best told in Spinoza’s own words. "While I was negotiating <note>he writes to Oldenburg</note> a rumor started circulating that I had a book in press concerning God, in which I endeavored to show that there is no God. Many people believed this report. As a result, certain theologians—perhaps the authors of the rumor—took the opportunity to complain about me before the Prince and the Magistrates. Moreover, the foolish Cartesians, being suspected of favoring me, endeavored to remove the slur by attacking my opinions and writings everywhere, a course of action which they still pursue. When I became aware of this through trustworthy men, who also assured me that the theologians were everywhere lying in wait for me, I decided to put off publishing until I saw how things were going. . . . But matters seem to get worse and worse, and I am still uncertain what to do."

Oldenburg must have felt intensely relieved by the news that the publication of the *Ethica* had been indefinitely postponed. The poor man had changed indeed. In his early days, hearing of Spinoza’s hesitation to publish the equally unorthodox *Short Treatise*, he had begged Spinoza to ignore the "petty theologians" and to publish. "Come, good sir <note>he then said</note>, cast away all fear of stirring up the pygmies of our time against you. Long enough have we sacri-

...sacrificed to ignorance and pedantry. Let us spread the sails of true knowledge, and explore the recesses of nature more thoroughly than before." He had grown nervous, almost stupidly nervous, since then. It must be remembered, however, that he had learned an unpleasant lesson in the Tower of London, in 1667, that he was never really a profound thinker, and that his environment, though scientific, was not at all enlightened. Robert Boyle, for instance, regarded his escape from a certain thunderstorm as due to miraculous intervention, and one may well believe that he had strange opinions about the author of the *Tractatus Theologico-Politicus*, as Tschirnhaus relates. Perhaps it was this very treatise (coupled with "the shades of doubt which," as he confessed, "did sometimes cross his mind") that first suggested to him the idea of founding the Boyle Lectures for the defense of Christianity. And Oldenburg was sufficiently under the influence of Boyle not only to suspect Spinoza's philosophy, which was defensible enough, but even to suspect his motives, which was quite indefensible, and which Spinoza certainly resented.

The *Ethics*, then, had to be set aside, and it was not destined to be published during the author's lifetime. Spinoza now applied himself to the other writings, which have already been listed above. The *Political Treatise* must have engaged most of his attention and interest. From one point of view it was a fine tribute to the memory of that eminent statesman Jan de Witt, whose conduct of affairs received here its fullest philosophical justification. Moreover, that liberal regime was rapidly passing away, as Spinoza had good reason to know. The Dutch had arrived at the parting of the ways, and showed a marked tendency to leave the republican highway for the path of monarchy. Like Samuel of old, he was determined solemnly to warn his countrymen. But, above all, he wanted to set before them a vivid exposition of the great principles of all true statesmanship—

In response to the shifting Dutch political climate, Spinoza turned his focus to writing the *Political Treatise* as a defense of republicanism and a tribute to Jan de Witt.</summary>

<page-type>text</page-type>

statesmanship, the supreme ideal of all statecraft. That ideal was the perfection of the individual citizen. This was only attainable where there was security and freedom. And the supreme duty of the State was to secure these two conditions. Democracy was the best form of government. The ideal, however, may also be approached under other forms of government. But whatever the external form may be (and Spinoza must have realized his country's almost irrevocable drift towards monarchy), do not let the true ideal be forgotten. The *Political Treatise* was the "Ethical Will and Testament" which Spinoza left for his country; and it was a dying hand that wrote it, too late to finish it.

Four months before his death, Spinoza made the personal acquaintance of Leibniz. About eight years before that, Leibniz had already read Spinoza's version of Descartes' *Principia*, and in 1671 he had sent him a copy of his "Notice of the Progress of Optics." In return, Spinoza sent him a copy of his *Tractatus Theologico-Politicus*. This book was already known to Leibniz and had been described by him as "an unbearably free-thinking book." But he did not know until then who its author was, nor did his teacher, Professor Thomas, who had written a "refutation" of it. Leibniz wanted to communicate his discovery to his teacher without, however, disclosing more than his diplomacy dictated. "The author of the book," he wrote, "is Benedict Spinoza, a Jew (my Dutch friends write to me) expelled from the Synagogue for his monstrous opinions, but a man of universal learning, and especially eminent in Optics and in the construction of very fine telescopes." In 1675, Leibniz was in Paris, and there he met Tschirnhaus, who had read a manuscript copy of Spinoza's *Ethics* and now communicated some of Spinoza's views to Leibniz. Leibniz grew eager to read the *Ethics* for himself, and Tschirnhaus wrote to Dr. Schuller to obtain Spinoza's permission to show Leibniz a copy of the *Ethics*. But

Spinoza declined. He had no faith in Leibniz, and his distrust was not unfounded. "What," asked Spinoza, "takes Leibniz away from Frankfurt, and what is he doing in Paris?" Spinoza had reason to suspect that Leibniz was on a mission to reunite Protestants and Catholics, which would lead to a joint effort to repress all liberal tendencies and to suppress freedom of thought and speech, which were so dear to his heart. Leibniz's attitude toward these things was certainly unlike Spinoza's, and his subsequent behavior toward Spinoza rather justified the instinctive distrust with which Spinoza first met him. But when Leibniz came to The Hague in the autumn of 1676, Spinoza's distrust and reserve vanished. Leibniz frequently visited Spinoza in his humble lodgings, and there (as he himself recorded) "conversed with him often and at great length." He also obtained a firsthand knowledge of Spinoza's *Ethics* then. During the following years, Leibniz devoted close attention to the philosophy of Spinoza and even assimilated some of his ideas, but there was a remarkable lack of common generosity—not to say common honesty—both in the way he generally avoided all reference to Spinoza and in the tone of his remarks when he did refer to him on rare occasions.

Spinoza's days were ending fast. Dr. Schuller, writing to Leibniz on February 6, 1677, expressed his fear that Spinoza would not remain among them much longer, as his consumption was growing worse from day to day. He was only forty-four years of age, but his constitution was weakened through hereditary consumption, aggravated by the glass dust from the lenses and the sedentary habits of the student. And he had lived strenuous days. To the very last, he was active. On Saturday afternoon, February 20, 1677, he was still downstairs chatting with the Van der Spycks. But he had already sent for Dr. Schuller and retired to bed early. On the Sunday morning Dr.

<header>INTRODUCTION</header>

Schuller arrived. Spinoza was up, and at midday had some chicken broth which the doctor had ordered for him. There seemed to be no immediate danger, and the Van der Spycks went to church in the afternoon. On their way home, they were informed that Spinoza was no more. He had passed away at three o’clock, in the presence of Dr. Schuller.

Four days later, Spinoza was buried in the New Church on the Spuy, which is quite near to the *Paviljoensgragt*. Six coaches followed the funeral procession, and many prominent people followed him to his last resting place, which was close to that of Jan de Witt. Of worldly possessions, he left very little behind him, and those were chiefly in the way of books. Dr. Schuller took possession of some of the most valuable of these, and even then there still remained about 160 works (some of them quite costly), the list of which has fortunately been preserved; and copies of nearly all of them are now in the Spinoza Museum at Rijnsburg. The proceeds from these, and from some lenses which he also left behind, were just enough to defray all his debts and the cost of burial—though his grave was only a hired grave, and was used again some years after his death.

In accordance with Spinoza’s instructions, his desk, containing the manuscripts of his unpublished works, was entrusted to the care of Jan Rieuwertsz, the Amsterdam bookseller. Immediate publication seemed to be dangerous for the publisher and editors; and when they had the courage, they did not have the money to proceed with the printing. For a time, they thought of selling the manuscript of the *Ethica* to Leibniz, intending no doubt to apply the proceeds toward the cost of printing it from one of their own copies of that work. Schuller had already communicated with Leibniz about it, but at the last moment, someone at The Hague came to the rescue, and as early as November 1677, Spinoza’s *Opera Posthuma* appeared in print. It consisted of one quarto volume and contained the *Ethics*, the *Political Treatise*, the *Treatise on the Improvement of the Understanding*, the *Letters*,

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Spinoza's legacy, following the details of his death and the inventory of his estate. It describes the clandestine publication of his posthumous works and the eventual worldwide recognition he received centuries later.</meta>

and the *Hebrew Grammar*. All names and other means of identification had been carefully removed from the correspondence; the editors' names, as well as the name of the publisher and the place of publication, were not given; and only Spinoza’s initials (B. D. S.) appeared on the title page. The editors were Jelles (who appears to have written the Preface), Meyer, and Schuller; and the editorial work seems to have been carried out secretly in one of the rooms of the Orphan Asylum, which had just been established in Amsterdam by some of Spinoza's Collegiant friends. It was at this Orphan Asylum (which is still in existence) that some of the originals of Spinoza's letters were later discovered, with editorial pencil notes on them.

Two hundred years later, a remarkable contrast to this secrecy was witnessed when the entire learned world joined in celebrating Spinoza’s memory. In 1880, his statue was erected in The Hague, within view of both houses where he had lived during his final years. And a new, complete edition of his works was published in 1882, containing a portrait specially engraved from the painting in the library at Wolfenbüttel, where Lessing—poet, philosopher, and champion of the mistreated—had, nearly a century before that, taken the first steps toward the proper recognition of Spinoza. The tribute paid to his memory was worldwide; and it was well-deserved. For there is considerable truth in Heine's witty saying that "all our modern philosophers, though often perhaps unconsciously, see through the glasses <note>This is a play on words regarding Spinoza's trade as a lens-grinder.</note> that Baruch Spinoza ground."

### § 9. THE CHARACTER OF SPINOZA

In attempting to form an estimate of Spinoza’s character, one should be guided by what is actually known about him from the direct evidence of those who knew him personally. There is a natural temptation to judge his

<meta>This page continues the introduction to the character of Spinoza, moving from the difficulty of assessing his personality based solely on his philosophical views to an analysis of his life and the testimonies of those who knew him.</meta>

# INTRODUCTION

personality by deductions from his views as seen through one's own spectacles. But it is not too much to say that, of the two alternative courses, it is far safer to interpret the philosophy of Spinoza in the light of what is independently known about his life and character than to estimate his character in the light of certain deductions from an independent interpretation of his views. During his lifetime, Spinoza was often condemned and vilified because of his opinions, and on account of defects which, it was tacitly assumed, these revealed in his character. There is reason to believe that, had it not been for his death, Spinoza's fate might have been very much like that of Koerbagh. After his death, it was considered a crime to say anything good about Spinoza, and for more than a century afterward his name was *anathema maranatha* <note>A biblical expression used as a formula for extreme curse or excommunication.</note>. Even people who were not too sensitive to his criticism of the Bible felt that a man who maintained the relativity of good and evil, and believed in universal necessity, had no incentive to be good, and, therefore, was very likely bad. Such an interpretation and deduction were, to say the least, very one-sided; and, toward the end of the eighteenth century, its absurdity was exposed by the no less one-sided view which, by laying exclusive stress on "the intellectual love of God" and kindred doctrines of Spinoza, transformed him into a "God-intoxicated" saint.

If we turn to the main facts of Spinoza's life, and to the recorded judgments of the people who knew him personally, there can be no doubt that Spinoza, though not a saint in the accepted sense of the expression, was certainly one of the finest characters that the history of philosophy can boast. The dominant feature in his character was his devotion to the pursuit of truth. For it, he was ever ready to make all sacrifices. Neither bribes nor threats could in any way lead him away from that pursuit. And he readily sacrificed his personal comfort in order that he might have money for books and time for study. To him the pursuit

<summary>The author argues that Spinoza's character should be judged by his life rather than by biased deductions from his philosophy. Despite historical vilification, Spinoza is presented as a man of exceptional character dedicated entirely to the pursuit of truth.</summary>
<keywords>Spinoza, character, Koerbagh, anathema maranatha, relativity of good and evil, universal necessity, intellectual love of God, God-intoxicated, pursuit of truth</keywords>

--- Page 107 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Spinoza’s character, shifting from the general perceptions of his morality to his personal commitment to truth as a way of life and his respectful attitude toward the religious beliefs of others.</meta>

<page-num>xcix</page-num>
<header>THE LIFE OF SPINOZA</header>

The pursuit of truth was no mere pastime or trade—it was the true life of man. One might almost say that it constituted the religion of Spinoza. Yet he was no mere intellectualist. If his devotion to knowledge reminds one of the striking utterances of his great medieval kinsman, Maimonides (whose *Guide of the Perplexed* Spinoza read and possessed), his moral earnestness echoes something of the voice of the Prophets. Nothing offended him more than the suggestion that his views tended to discourage the practice of virtue; nothing outraged him more than reading *Homo Politicus*, a book in which, from apparently Spinozistic principles, maxims were deduced for the most selfish and immoral conduct. Again and again he insisted on absolute purity of motive even in the communication of views which he regarded as absolutely true. When sending his *Short Treatise* to his Amsterdam friends, he begged them to be sure that nothing but the good of their neighbors would ever induce them to communicate its doctrines to others. And it was out of consideration for his fellow men that he tried, as far as possible, not to unsettle their religious beliefs. He assured the Van der Spycks that their religion was quite good, and that they need have no misgivings whatever so long as their conduct was good and upright. Good conduct and pure motives—these were the most essential things; and, devoted as he was to truth, he maintained that Turks and heathens <note>Non-Christians or those traditionally categorized as pagans.</note> who did their duty and loved their fellow men were filled with the spirit of Christ, whom Spinoza regarded as the highest type of manhood. Even in the explicit polemic of the *Tractatus Theologico-Politicus*, he bypassed without criticism the less harmful orthodox doctrines, although he disagreed with them. But there was no duplicity about him; when educated men sought his views on some of these very doctrines (such as the divinity of Christ), he did not mince words but expressed his views without any equivocation. His means for active benevo-

<summary>Spinoza viewed the pursuit of truth as a religious devotion and emphasized that moral conduct and pure motives are more important than dogma. He was careful not to unsettle the faith of others, provided they lived uprightly, while remaining completely honest about his own views when asked by educated peers.</summary>

<page-num>c</page-num>
# INTRODUCTION

...fluence were not great. Still, what little he possessed was at the service of his friends. When informed that a considerable sum of money, which he had lent in this way, was lost, he merely remarked quietly and with a smile that he would have to reduce his expenses for the future. Wealth and position had no undue attraction for him. He would not, for their sake, bind or blind his judgment by accepting the Heidelberg professorship, or even appear to do so by paying a formal compliment to a monarch whose aims and methods he condemned. In this respect, he stood head and shoulders above some of his most eminent contemporaries in the world of science. But though he was of an independent spirit, he was neither proud nor cold and reserved. He met more than halfway all people who offered him their friendship. He showed wonderful patience with the most mediocre people who turned to him with their difficulties, and he was kind to the humblest. Amid all the accusations brought against Spinoza, no specific charge was ever made against his moral character. It was always his heretical views—and his character as deduced *a priori* from these views by the ingenuity of "learned parsons"—that were flung at his head. This is remarkable in itself and is amply confirmed by Colerus, the Lutheran pastor, who, though he considered Spinoza's heresies to be abominable and most outrageous, has nevertheless made it perfectly clear that Spinoza's morals were unassailable. The peasants at Rijnsburg and Voorburg, we are expressly told, agreed that he was "a man whom it was good to know, kind, upright, obliging, and of good morals." People of culture felt a peculiar charm in his presence, and men of his own age, and even older men, looked upon him with the respect of disciples. We have seen already what impression he made on Oldenburg and the Seigneur de St. Evremont when they came into personal touch with him. The account which we have from Dr. Lucas, who knew Spinoza intimately in the Hague, breathes

<summary>This page describes Spinoza's modest lifestyle, his rejection of prestigious positions like the Heidelberg professorship to maintain his independence, and his universally respected moral character. Despite being labeled a heretic by the clergy, he was beloved by common peasants and intellectual peers alike for his kindness and integrity.</summary>

--- Page 109 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Benedict de Spinoza, focusing on his personal character, his emotional nature, and his rejection of extreme asceticism in favor of a philosophy of joy and reason.</meta>

<page-num>ci</page-num>
<header>THE LIFE OF SPINOZA</header>

a spirit of the utmost veneration. And many who have only read his writings have felt themselves in the presence of an uncommon moral atmosphere of utter unselfishness and impartiality, and a boundless faith in human goodness.

Spinoza was not a saint. He did not believe in turning the other cheek to the one who strikes him. Nor was he so focused on the next world that he despised the physical world and the flesh. He could say harsh things against insolent, ignorant people and hunters of heretics; he never quite forgot the wrongs done to him by his relatives and his community; and, in the heat of conflict, he even forgot to pause for a moment to acknowledge some of the merits of the <term>Law and the Prophets</term> <note>The Hebrew Bible or Old Testament</note>. He was human, and was influenced by emotions to a far greater extent than is supposed by those who exaggerate his intellectualism because they judge his character from certain aspects of his philosophy. He could be angry with immorality and intolerance, and he felt hurt by unmerited suspicion. He laughed to see theologians outsmart the devil with their tricks; and he wept over the tragic fate of the <term>de Witts</term> <note>Johan and Cornelis de Witt, Dutch statesmen murdered by a mob in 1672</note>. He was not even an ascetic. Although he was extraordinarily moderate in his way of life—living on a few pence a day and with a pipe as his only luxury—this was mainly due to his circumstances. His desire for independence and his devotion to books made it impossible for him to earn enough to indulge in the ordinary comforts of life, and so moderation gradually became a habit with him. But he had no contempt for the reasonable pleasures or joys of life.
> "I enjoy life [he wrote] and try to live it, not in sorrow and sighing, but in peace, joy, and cheerfulness."
And those who knew him have confirmed the truth of this. He could not understand how anyone could find—or imagine that God would find—any virtue in sighs, tears, and the like.
> "Nothing [he insists] but a gloomy and sad superstition forbids enjoyment."
Indeed, what he had originally sought in philosophy was guidance in the attainment of

--- Page 110 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Spinoza's personal motivations and character, moving from his rejection of asceticism to his pursuit of philosophy as a means to achieve lasting happiness and his unique blend of scientific reason and mysticism.</meta>

<page-num>cii</page-num>

# INTRODUCTION

happiness. It was not, as in the case of Descartes, a dissatisfaction with the state of knowledge at the time that drove him to philosophy, but rather a dissatisfaction with the ordinary pursuits and pleasures of life because they failed to bring lasting happiness. This is clear from the opening passage of his *Treatise on the Improvement of the Understanding*, which was already quoted above. He had turned to philosophy for guidance in the pursuit of happiness, and he found his happiness in the pursuit of philosophy itself.

On the other hand, there was certainly something of a higher mysticism about Spinoza. It would be a mistake to strip his religious terminology of *all* its religious meaning. We are bordering here on a difficult question of interpretation, and we do not wish to be dogmatic. Still, it should not be forgotten that, although he was convinced of the truth of his philosophy, Spinoza was far from assuming that it was the whole truth. There were only a few things, even in the world of <term>extension</term> <note>The physical realm of matter and space</note> and <term>thought</term> <note>The mental realm of ideas and consciousness</note>, of which he claimed to have the highest kind of knowledge; meanwhile, besides extension and thought, there were infinite aspects of the universe (or <term>attributes of substance</term>) of which he admittedly had no knowledge whatever. He felt more than he saw. And although he loved to live in the clear, common light of day and hated the bigotry and superstition that lurk in the shadows of the twilight, he still felt the glow of the presence that dwells in the setting sun, even if he was not lost in visions of a light that never shone on land or sea. It was something of this mystic feeling that inspired his religious language and gave his personality that charm which won over everyone who met him. It also won him the sympathy of poets like Goethe and Lessing, Coleridge and Wordsworth, just as his calm scientific outlook has made him a favorite with scientists. His moral passion seems almost aglow with this mystic fire; and if we may not call him a priest of the most high God, he was certainly a prophet of the power that works toward righteousness.

<summary>This page explains that Spinoza pursued philosophy to find lasting happiness and argues that his work contains a deep mysticism despite his scientific clarity. It highlights how his personality and world-view influenced both Enlightenment thinkers and Romantic poets.</summary>
<keywords>Spinoza, Descartes, Treatise on the Improvement of the Understanding, mysticism, extension, thought, attributes of substance, Goethe, Lessing, Coleridge, Wordsworth, ethics, happiness</keywords>

<page-num>ciii</page-num>

# HISTORY OF
# THE SHORT TREATISE

## § 1. THE DISCOVERY OF THE MANUSCRIPTS

THE *Short Treatise* was not published during Spinoza’s lifetime, nor was it included in the *Opera Posthuma* published in November 1677, shortly after Spinoza's death. The author of the Preface to the *Opera Posthuma* does not even refer to it specifically. He mentions the essay *On the Rainbow*, for which he seems to have been unable to get a copy, and which he believed had been burned by Spinoza. But, for the rest, he simply remarks in a general way that “although it is plausible that some work of our philosopher <note>Spinoza</note> may still be in someone's possession without the editor's knowledge, it may nonetheless be assumed that nothing will be found in them that is not already presented repeatedly in these writings,” that is, in the *Ethics*, the *Political Treatise*, the *Treatise on the Improvement of the Understanding*, the *Correspondence*, and the *Hebrew Grammar*, which together constituted the *Opera Posthuma*. Thus, no reference is made to the *Short Treatise* even as a possibly lost work of Spinoza. On the other hand, it should be remembered that to the editors of the *Opera Posthuma*, as indeed to Spinoza himself, the *Short Treatise* appeared to have been superseded by the *Ethics*. Therefore, the silence regarding the *Short Treatise* may not be so strange after all, and one should not attach too much importance to it. A report from 1703, the truth of which there is no reason to doubt, suggests that J. Rieuwertsz (junior), the publisher of the *Opera Posthuma*,

<summary>This page discusses the historical absence of the Short Treatise from Spinoza's posthumous works, explaining that early editors likely omitted it because they believed its content was already covered by his more mature writings like the Ethics.</summary>
<keywords>Spinoza, Short Treatise, Opera Posthuma, Ethics, J. Rieuwertsz, On the Rainbow, manuscripts, bibliography</keywords>

--- Page 112 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the history of the Short Treatise, specifically focusing on the 1703 account by Stolle and Hallmann regarding a manuscript held by the publisher Rieuwertsz.</meta>

<page-num>civ</page-num>
<header>INTRODUCTION</header>

actually possessed a manuscript copy of what is now called the *Short Treatise*, but which was then naturally regarded simply as an early draft of the *Ethics*.

In 1703, Gottlieb Stolle (1673–1744)—a Silesian who was appointed Professor of Political Science at Jena in 1717—and a Dr. Hallmann traveled through Holland, where they interviewed various people who had known Spinoza. Among others, they saw Rieuwertsz in Amsterdam. Rieuwertsz gave them some personal reminiscences of Spinoza, for whom (so they relate) he showed uncommon affection and, with tears in his eyes, wished that Spinoza were still alive. Rieuwertsz also showed them several manuscripts of Spinoza’s works, and among them was one apparently written in Spinoza’s own handwriting. This (according to Hallmann) was none other than Spinoza’s first Dutch version of the *Ethica*; it was quite different from the published *Ethica*—not worked out in the geometric method but in the ordinary way, and divided into chapters like the *Tractatus Theologico-Politicus*. Rieuwertsz assured them that the printed *Ethica* was much better than this manuscript version, though the latter contained some things that were omitted from the former, notably the chapter on the Devil. Rieuwertsz said that several friends of Spinoza had copies of that manuscript, which had never been printed because the Latin version, which had been published, was altogether superior and had been well edited. The story is not entirely free from difficulties. But it undoubtedly gives us an explicit reference to the so-called *Short Treatise*. Stolle and Hallmann’s account of their travels, written in 1704, was not published until 1847,* but Stolle repeated his information about the *Short Treatise* in his *Brief Introduction to the History of Learning*, which was published in 1718. The story about the Dutch *Ethics* and the chapter on the Devil was repeated by

Although different in structure and style from the final published version, this manuscript provides a clear historical link to Spinoza's early philosophical development.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the history of the bibliographic references to and the eventual rediscovery of Spinoza's Short Treatise, moving from early 18th-century catalogs to 19th-century research by Edward Boehmer.</meta>

<page-num>cv</page-num>
<header>HISTORY OF THE SHORT TREATISE</header>

J. F. Reimmann (1668–1743) mentioned it in his *Catalogue of Theological Books*, which was published in 1731, as did J. C. Mylius in his *Library of Anonymous and Pseudonymous Authors*, which was published in 1740. These notices, however, do not seem to have attracted any attention. Spinoza had such a bad reputation among respectable scholars (including Stolle and Reimmann) that there was no desire to discover or recover any of his unpublished works; the published ones were considered more than enough. In the second half of the eighteenth century, we indeed see some signs of an active interest in Spinoza’s remains. C. T. de Murr of Nürnberg visited Holland in search of Spinoza relics. He brought back with him a Latin manuscript copy of Spinoza’s notes on the *Tractatus Theologico-Politicus* and a report that Spinoza’s *Ethica* was originally written in Dutch and contained a chapter on the Devil; he reported that Spinoza then translated it into Latin, arranging it at the same time into a geometric form, and that, because of this and other changes, it was retranslated from Latin back into Dutch by Jarig Jelles. For about a century, the matter rested there.

In 1851, Edward Boehmer, Professor of Philosophy at Halle, went to Holland, also in search of Spinoza rarities. In Amsterdam, he bought a copy of the *Life of Spinoza* by Colerus from F. Müller, a well-known bookseller there. Section 12 of Colerus’s *Life of Spinoza* treats the philosopher’s unpublished writings very briefly, and Boehmer’s copy had a manuscript note (in Dutch) added to this section. It stated that among certain followers of philosophy there was still a surviving manuscript of a treatise by Spinoza which deals with the same subjects as the printed *Ethica*, though not using the geometric method. It also noted that its style and general direction show it to be one of Spinoza’s earliest writings—in fact, the first draft of the *Ethica*—and that for some people it is less obscure than the final version, precisely because it is not cast in the geometric form, except to a very small extent in the

<page-num>cvi</page-num>
<header>INTRODUCTION</header>

Appendix to the treatise. And at the end of the same copy of Colerus's *Life of Spinoza*, there actually followed a fairly complete analysis of the *Short Treatise*, chapter by chapter, written in the same handwriting as the note to section 12. Boehmer published his *Benedicti de Spinoza Tractatus de Deo et Homine ejusque Felicitate Lineamenta* (Outlines of Benedict de Spinoza's Treatise on God, Man, and His Happiness) in 1852, and a new momentum was given to the search for the *Short Treatise*. Not long afterward, a manuscript copy of the *Short Treatise* itself came to light. F. Müller, the same bookseller from whom Boehmer had obtained his copy of Colerus, bought this manuscript of the *Short Treatise* at an auction. And while Dr. J. van Vloten was preparing to publish it along with some Spinoza letters, which had been discovered at the Collegiant Orphan Asylum in Amsterdam, a second (and older) manuscript of the *Short Treatise* was discovered. The poet Adrian Bogaers of Rotterdam found it among his books. This (the older) manuscript is generally referred to as Codex A, and the other as Codex B. The first edition of the *Short Treatise* was published in 1862 by Dr. J. van Vloten in his *Ad Benedicti de Spinoza Opera quæ Supersunt Omnia Supplementum* (Supplement to all the Extant Works of Benedict de Spinoza). It was based on both manuscripts and was accompanied by a Latin translation. A more careful edition of Codex A was published in 1869 by Professor C. Schaarschmidt of Bonn, and also by Van Vloten and Land in their editions of the complete works of Spinoza (1882, 1895). Both manuscripts are now in the Royal Library at The Hague.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Johannes Monnikhoff’s role in preserving Spinoza's legacy, moving from the poem he wrote in the previous entry to the scholarly identification of his handwriting in key Spinoza manuscripts.</meta>

# HISTORY OF THE SHORT TREATISE

Dr. Antonius van der Linde had already shown that the handwriting in Boehmer’s *Colerus* was exactly the same as that of various manuscripts which were known to have been copied by Johannes Monnikhoff, an Amsterdam doctor who was born in 1707 and died in 1787. Before the *Short Treatise* in Codex B, there is a long introduction in which reference is made to the year 1743, so this copy could not have been written before then. The same codex also contains, at the end, *Notes to the Tractatus Theologico-Politicus*, all of them in the same handwriting. The introduction seems to be the composition of Monnikhoff, while the *Short Treatise* and the *Notes* were evidently copied by him. That the handwriting is that of Monnikhoff is certain from the fact that several manuscripts in the Hague Library, written in exactly the same hand, have introductions which are signed by him. We reproduce from one of these manuscripts a facsimile of some verses signed by Johannes Monnikhoff, for comparison with the facsimiles of several pages from Codex B. According to F. Müller, the bookseller who discovered it, Codex B of the *Short Treatise* accompanied a Dutch manuscript translation of Spinoza’s version of Descartes’ *Principia*. But there is no sign of this in the parchment-bound <term>quarto</term> <note>A book size resulting from folding a sheet of paper twice to form eight pages.</note> volume, which contains simply an *Introduction on the life and writings of Spinoza*, the *Short Treatise*, and the *Notes to the Tractatus Theologico-Politicus*—nothing more. On the back of the volume, however, the title is obviously incomplete. It says:

->Benedictus<-
->Posthumous<-

And there is evidently a second volume missing that would have had the rest of the full title on its back, namely:

->Benedictus | De Spinoza<-
->Posthumous | Works.<-

<summary>Scholar Antonius van der Linde identified Johannes Monnikhoff as the scribe of Codex B, which contains Spinoza's Short Treatise and other notes. The physical condition and labeling of the manuscript suggest it was originally the first of a two-volume set of Spinoza's posthumous works.</summary>
<keywords>Antonius van der Linde, Johannes Monnikhoff, Codex B, Spinoza, Short Treatise, Tractatus Theologico-Politicus, F. Müller, manuscript history, Descartes' Principia</keywords>

# INTRODUCTION

This is highly probable because in another two-volume manuscript also copied by Monnikhoff, the title of the work is similarly spread over the backs of the two volumes. It is also possible that the missing volume may have contained the *Principia*, or perhaps some other work, since the *Principia* was already published in Dutch as well as in Latin. It is interesting to note that the *Introduction* in Codex B also gives a summary of the *Short Treatise*, which is practically identical to the “Outline” in Boehmer’s copy of Colerus.

Codex A is a much thicker <term>quarto</term> volume and contains the *Short Treatise*, the *Tractatus Theologico-Politicus*, and the *Notes* to it, all in Dutch; however, the *Notes* are not in the same handwriting as the rest of the volume. Codex A is evidently older than B, as may be seen from the handwriting itself, which belongs to the seventeenth century and is much more faded. Moreover, even a cursory inspection reveals the fact that the scribe who had copied B had also been busy with A, which contains numerous, though mostly unimportant, additions in the same handwriting as B. For instance, at the beginning of the entire volume, there is the following title page in Monnikhoff’s handwriting:

> ->“The Writings of Benedict de Spinoza, comprising<-
>
> ->“I. A Treatise on God, Man, and his Well-being.<-
> ->“II. A Theologico-Political Treatise.<-
>
> ->“Both of them with the Notes of the Author, and translated from the Latin.”<-

Separate title pages in the same handwriting also precede the *Tractatus Theologico-Politicus* and the *Notes*. Again, following the Table of Contents, there is a portrait of Spinoza apparently inserted there by Monnikhoff, who may have taken it from a copy of the 1677 edition of the *Opera Posthuma*, and facing it (on the left) are some well-meaning

## What God is.

<margin>
Secondly, that no parts in extension are finite by its own nature; it is completely infinite and cannot be finite.
</margin>
Extension being a substance, one cannot say of it that it has parts, since it can become neither smaller nor larger, and no parts of it could be understood separately, because in its nature it <gloss>must</gloss> be infinite. And that it must be so follows from this: namely, that if it were not so, but consisted of parts, it would in no way be infinite by its nature as stated. However, that <gloss>there</gloss> could be an infinite nature conceived [as being composed of parts] is impossible, for by their nature all parts are finite. Add to this: if it were to consist of various parts, it could then be understood that if some of its parts were destroyed, the extension would nevertheless remain and not be destroyed along with those destroyed parts; a matter <gloss>thoroughly</gloss> which is clearly contradictory for something that is infinite by its own nature and can never be determined or finite, nor be understood as such. Furthermore, regarding the division in Nature: to that we say that such division, as has also been said before, never occurs in the substance, but always and only in the modes of the substance. If I then wish to divide water, I only [divide] the...

---

2. Second: for then there would be a mode, which cannot be; for extension is without and before all modes. Therefore, the third: and so there is no part, but extension as a whole.

There are also numerous page-headings, chapter-headings, and cross-references in Monnikhoff’s writing. Occasionally he also inserted a word in the text, or re-copied an illegible note, as may be seen from the accompanying facsimile, where the illegible marginal note in the original handwriting is seen crossed out and rewritten by Monnikhoff as a footnote. It is clear, therefore, that codex A is older than B, and that the copyist who wrote out B also knew and used A. But when and by whom was A written? The writing, as already remarked, belongs to the seventeenth century. But it was certainly not written out by Spinoza himself. This is obvious already from the title-page, where we are distinctly told (in the same writing as the bulk of the manuscript) that the *Short Treatise* was originally composed in Latin, and that it was translated for some of Spinoza’s disciples; and the whole tone of this title-page (or preface, as it might be called) is very unlike what we should expect from Spinoza. Moreover, a reference to Spinoza’s autograph † is quite conclusive on this point. It has been suggested that codex A was copied by William Deurhoff (? 1650-1717), a Dutch theologian and a Cartesian. This suggestion derived considerable plausibility from the fact that the fairly numerous other manuscripts copied by Monnikhoff were all of them the works of Deurhoff—Monnikhoff’s signed verses, already given above, actually occur in one such manuscript, and face a portrait of Deurhoff. It seems, therefore, not unnatural to suppose that Monnikhoff copied codex B from

The only authentic autograph of Deurhoff that has been discovered so far consists of his signature, written in 1685, and this seems to be insufficient to rely upon with certainty. Dr. W. And the tone of the Preface on the title page of A is also unfavorable to it, because Deurhoff had no such admiration for Spinoza. On the other hand, it may be reasonably supposed that codex A was the property of Deurhoff, and that Monnikhoff obtained it from him.

Dr. W. Meyer has made the interesting suggestion that codex A was originally the property of Jarig Jelles—perhaps the very copy of the translations which he himself had obtained of the <term>Short Treatise</term> and the <term>Tractatus Theologico-Politicus</term>. Jarig Jelles was one of the oldest and warmest friends of Spinoza, and had paid the cost of publishing Spinoza’s version of Descartes’ <term>Principia</term>, both the Latin and the Dutch versions. Jelles, who was a spice merchant, did not know Latin, and it may have been he who persuaded Pieter Balling to translate Spinoza’s <term>Principia</term> into Dutch for that reason. It would appear that he also had the <term>Tractatus Theologico-Politicus</term> translated into Dutch, and that he was about to have it published in 1671. For, in a letter addressed to Jelles in that year, Spinoza begs him to prevent the publication of the Dutch translation of the <term>Tractatus Theologico-Politicus</term>, as it might lead to the prohibition of even the Latin edition. Accordingly, no Dutch translation of this treatise appeared until 1693, and then another followed in 1694. Now the Dutch version of the <term>Theologico-Political Treatise</term> which is contained in codex A is not identical with either of these two other translations, and it is most probably earlier than 1694, because a new translation would hardly

Meyer, therefore, suggests that the Dutch version contained in A is the very same one that was about to be published in 1671, but was held back at Spinoza’s request. And since the *Short Treatise* is in the same handwriting, and judging by the preface also seems to have been intended for publication, Dr. Meyer supposes that Jelles had this also translated into Dutch, and that he intended to publish it together with the *Tractatus Theologico-Politicus*. He even conjectures that the translations were made by Dr. Lodewijk Meyer; but there is no real evidence of this.

One is inclined to ask whether Codex A might not be identical to the manuscript that Rieuwertsz is reported to have shown to Stolle and Hallmann in 1703. But the terms of the report make it uncertain whether that manuscript was claimed to be in Spinoza’s own handwriting or in that of the bookseller’s father. And, in any case, the statement in the preface on the title page—that the *Short Treatise* was originally written in Latin—could scarcely have escaped their notice; and, since they undoubtedly report that the manuscript was in Dutch just as Spinoza had at first composed it, the probability is that they saw a different copy. There is no doubt, however, that manuscript copies of the *Short Treatise* were extant among various friends and readers of Spinoza at the end of the seventeenth century, and Codex A is most likely one of these manuscripts.

Both A and B, however, claim to be only translations, or copies of a translation, from the Latin, and not copies of a Dutch original. This is also confirmed by an examination of the text of the manuscripts, which contains various mistakes that can only be explained on the assumption that they are mistranslations from the Latin. Some of these will be indicated in the notes.

<header>INTRODUCTION</header>

Furthermore, codex A cannot be the original copy even of a translation because it contains several mistakes that can only be explained by the assumption that they are misreadings of Dutch words; for example, writing *alderwijste* (wisest) where the context requires *aldervrijste* (freest). Additionally, codex B has far too much in common with A to be plausibly regarded as providing an independent translation of the *Short Treatise*. *Prima facie* the most plausible assumption is that A is itself a copy of an older manuscript, and that B is more or less a copy of A; this suggestion is also largely confirmed by further internal evidence.

### § 3. THE TWO MANUSCRIPTS COMPARED

Overall, both manuscripts provide essentially the same translation of the *Short Treatise*, although there are many minor differences, most of which are noted in this translation. In terms of neat appearance and smooth expression, B is far superior to A. In A, notes and additions to the text are sometimes found all around the page—at the top and bottom, and to the left and right of the text. It is sometimes difficult to distinguish between the text and the notes. In B, however, the arrangement is perfectly clear and neat. Similar differences are evident in the composition of the two manuscripts. In A, the punctuation is sometimes absolutely chaotic—there are entire strings of colons and semicolons, bringing together ideas that have no real connection, while at other times periods disconnect what should have been connected. Occasionally, the task of translating technical expressions also seems to have been avoided, and they are simply provided in their Latin form. All, or nearly all, such crude errors are absent from

<summary>This page compares Codex A and Codex B of Spinoza's Short Treatise, arguing that A is a copy of an older manuscript and B is a more polished version derived from A. It notes that while A is cluttered and poorly punctuated, B is clear, neatly arranged, and more linguistically refined.</summary>
<keywords>Codex A, Codex B, Short Treatise, manuscript comparison, Spinoza, Dutch translation, textual criticism, paleography</keywords>

B—the punctuation is quite normal, and it generally translates into Dutch—rather than simply reproducing—such expressions as *a priori*, *a posteriori*, *attributum*, *essentia*, *idea*, etc. Again, in A, the second part of the *Short Treatise* has numerous marginal summaries of the text in addition to the explanatory notes; B omits nearly all these marginal summaries and also some of the notes. Apart from these relatively external differences between the two codices, there are also more important differences between them. A often has a sentence or an expression which B omits; on the other hand, there are only a comparatively few cases in which B has any important sentence or expression which A lacks. Again, A has numerous mistakes which are not found in B; on the other hand, there are extremely few instances in which a passage is given correctly in A and wrongly in B. Illustrations of all this will be found in the accompanying translation and notes, though the punctuation had to be somewhat improved occasionally. But such, in general terms, is the relationship between the two manuscripts of the *Short Treatise*.

What may reasonably be deduced from the above facts? Some (Schaarschmidt, for instance) are inclined to minimize the differences between A and B and suggest that the improvements in B over A were made more or less arbitrarily by Monnikhoff, who had no other manuscript before him except A, and that he was guided simply by his own common sense or fastidious taste, as the case may be, in making the numerous alterations in his own copy. A great many of the differences between A and B might certainly be accounted for in this way. Sigwart, however, maintains that it is scarcely possible to account for all the differences that way; and he inclines to the belief (rightly, we think) that Monnikhoff had some other manuscript<note>This hypothetical third manuscript is generally called C.</note>, besides A, which enabled him to make so many improve-

It seems clear, however, that the suggested other manuscript (if Monnikhoff really had another to consult) could not have been the original Latin manuscript or a copy of it, because some of his mistakes would have been impossible in that case. Nor, in all probability, was it even an independent Dutch translation of the original, because in that case B would most likely not have had so very much in common with A as it actually has. That Monnikhoff might have consulted another Dutch manuscript of the *Short Treatise* (besides A) seems likely from the fact that Rieuwertsz, for instance, had such another Dutch manuscript (as Hallmann reports), and there may have also been others in Amsterdam, where Monnikhoff lived. At the same time, it is just possible that Monnikhoff had only codex A before him, and that his own critical insight enabled him to make the various corrections and improvements.

### § 4. THE COMPONENT PARTS OF THE SHORT TREATISE

Even a cursory examination of the *Short Treatise* shows that it is not a homogeneous whole, but a complex of parts in which a closer scrutiny reveals different strata of thought representing different stages of development. Comparatively external differences suffice to enable us to distinguish four separate parts in the *Short Treatise*, namely:

(i) the bulk of the text of the treatise (both parts);
(ii) the so-called footnotes or marginal additions;
(iii) the two dialogues at the end of Part I, chapter ii; and
(iv) the so-called Appendices at the end of the treatise.

It may be remarked at once that no one seriously doubts that the *Short Treatise* as a whole is the work of

Spinoza. The only portions whose authenticity might be doubted are some of the notes. Many of the notes to Part II in A are evidently mere marginal summaries that were not made by Spinoza, and nearly all of them were omitted by Monnikhoff, no doubt for this very reason. They have also been omitted from all the published editions and translations of the *Short Treatise*. Some of the remaining notes (or additions) are also probably by someone other than Spinoza, as is the preface on the title page of A. Most of the long notes, however, are certainly Spinoza’s own, and Monnikhoff says so explicitly on the extra title page he wrote in codex A (which was already cited above), while the “Outline” in Boehmer’s Colerus states explicitly that Spinoza had added notes for further explanation and elaboration of his views. And the rest of the *Short Treatise* is Spinoza's beyond a doubt. The previously mentioned traditions about his Dutch *Ethics* with a chapter on the Devil, along with passages in his letters—which we will refer to when we try to determine the date of its composition—sufficiently confirm Spinoza's authorship as claimed on the title pages of both manuscripts.

But, although Spinoza wrote the whole of the *Short Treatise* (excepting the suspicious notes) as we now have it, he evidently did not write it all at the same time. What we have before us is a first draft along with successive attempts to correct, supplement, or reconcile various parts of it. The bulk of the text represents that first draft. The chapters are strung together somewhat loosely; inconsistencies of thought or expression have not yet been removed. Some of the so-called notes or marginal additions are really new versions of the corresponding text, which Spinoza apparently intended to rewrite. They often represent a distinct advance in thought, bridging the gap between the text of the *Treatise* and the *Ethics*. The Dialogues

<summary>This page confirms Spinoza's authorship of the *Short Treatise*, while identifying certain marginal notes as later additions by others. It characterizes the work as a collection of drafts and revisions that show the development of Spinoza's philosophy toward his later *Ethics*.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the analysis of the *Short Treatise*, moving from the authenticity of the notes to the experimental structure of the work and the likely chronological order of its various sections.</meta>

<page-num>cxxii</page-num>
<header>INTRODUCTION</header>

elaborate special points, while assuming what has already been explained in other parts of the *Treatise*. Like the first Appendix, they also represent an experiment in the form of exposition. Spinoza evidently realized very quickly that his was not the art of writing Platonic dialogues. The second Appendix is concerned with the elaboration of a special point. The first, as already stated, is an experiment in the geometric form of exposition, and is intimately related to the *Ethics*. The *Treatise* shows us Spinoza in his workshop gradually shaping the material for his great edifice. It is, of course, all the more interesting for that. But it is practically impossible to determine precisely the chronological sequence of its parts. At one time it was supposed that the Dialogues were the oldest parts of the *Treatise*. Freudenthal, however, has shown that they must have been written *after* the main text of the *Treatise* because they assume a knowledge of various views already explained in other parts of the work. Thus all that may be asserted with confidence is that the notes, the Dialogues, and the Appendices are later than the rest of the *Treatise*. It is also possible to determine which parts of the work were the last additions. Detailed information relating to these questions will be found in the Commentary. But it is important to note immediately that we are dealing with a book which was never properly prepared for publication, Spinoza having finally determined to recast the exposition of his philosophy in the geometric form, as we have it in the *Ethics*. The present arrangement of the *Treatise* is probably due in part to one of his disciples, whose insight was not sufficient to guard him against misplacing some parts, omitting others, and retaining passages which were meant to be discarded. Occasionally also readers' comments seem to have found their way into the text through the copyist's lack of discrimination.

It also suggests the current disorganized state of the manuscript is due to a disciple's oversight and the inclusion of external reader comments by copyists.</summary>

## § 5. THE ORIGIN AND CHARACTER OF THE SHORT TREATISE

It is difficult to determine with any precision when the *Short Treatise* was begun, but it is comparatively easy to determine when it was already completed. About the end of 1661, Spinoza wrote to Oldenburg, saying:

> “Regarding your new question—namely, how things began to exist, and what is the bond of dependence between them and the first cause—on this subject, and also on the improvement of the understanding, I have written a complete little treatise, and am at present engaged in copying and improving it. Sometimes, however, I put the work aside, for I am not yet sure about publishing it. I fear that the theologians of our day might take offense and, with their usual rancor, attack me, as I have an absolute horror of quarrels.”

It is clear from this that the *Treatise on the Improvement of the Understanding* was already sufficiently advanced for Spinoza to think of its early publication. But this cannot be the only treatise to which Spinoza refers here, because it contains nothing about the origin of things and their dependence on the first cause—which is the primary concern of the little treatise Spinoza mentions—nor does it contain anything to warrant Spinoza’s evident apprehension that it would provoke the rancor of the theologians. Spinoza can only be referring, in this letter, to our *Short Treatise*, the style and contents of which prove it to be an earlier work than the *Treatise on the Improvement of the Understanding*. The *Short Treatise* must have been already finished when Spinoza wrote the letter to Oldenburg quoted above; but owing to his recent work with Bacon and the question of philosophic method, which he had also discussed with Oldenburg, he seems to have begun the *Treatise on the Improvement of the Understanding* with the

<summary>This page argues that Spinoza's 1661 letter to Oldenburg refers to the Short Treatise, rather than just the Treatise on the Improvement of the Understanding, based on its specific theological and metaphysical content. It establishes the Short Treatise as the earlier of the two works.</summary>
<keywords>Spinoza, Oldenburg, Short Treatise, Treatise on the Improvement of the Understanding, first cause, theologians, Bacon, philosophic method</keywords>
