<meta>This page continues the discussion on the dating and purpose of the *Short Treatise*, arguing that it was completed between 1660 and 1661, and explains why Spinoza eventually moved toward the geometric method used in the *Ethica*.</meta>

<page-num>cxxiv</page-num>
<header>INTRODUCTION</header>

intention of using it as a general introduction to his entire philosophy as contained for the most part in the *Short Treatise*. The opening passages of the former treatise, already quoted above,<note>See pp. liii. ff.</note> are hardly appropriate as an introduction to a mere theory of knowledge; they refer instead to philosophy as a whole. Spinoza’s growing preference for the geometric method, and his successful experiment in applying it to Descartes’ *Principia*, as well as the gradual modification of some of his views, soon led him to begin a new exposition of his philosophy, such as he eventually gave in the *Ethica*. And thus, the *Short Treatise* fell into neglect. But there can be no doubt that it was already completed in 1661—possibly even the year before—if we allow for the *Treatise on the Improvement of the Understanding*, which, though a fragment now and probably even more fragmentary then, must nevertheless have taken him some time to write.

The main text of the *Short Treatise*, then, must have been written no later than 1661. It seems equally clear that it could not have been finished before 1660—that is to say, before Spinoza’s move to Rijnsburg. The reason for this suggestion is found in the concluding paragraph of the Second Part of the *Treatise.*<note>See pp. 149 f.</note> It is actually an epistle addressed to his friends, to whom he was sending the entire manuscript of the *Short Treatise* (before the Appendices were written). Its tone and contents strongly suggest that it was written to friends at a distance. It is not difficult to guess who these friends were. They were Balling, Jelles, Meyer, and the other members of the philosophical circle to whom Spinoza also subsequently sent the completed portions of the *Ethica* in manuscript. His friends, then, were in Amsterdam. If Spinoza had still been living in or near Amsterdam, it would hardly have been necessary for him to write that exhortation—

<page-num>CXXV</page-num>

tation. It must, therefore, have been written when Spinoza had already left Amsterdam and its neighborhood, and had gone to Rijnsburg. And this happened early in 1660.

We maintain, accordingly, that the *Short Treatise* was not *finished* before 1660. But, as already suggested, it was probably begun much earlier than that. Many or most of its chapters very likely contain the substance of the notes which Spinoza dictated to his disciples while teaching at Amsterdam. This seems to be supported, to some extent, by a marginal summary at the side of the above-mentioned concluding paragraph of the *Treatise*.<note>See the first note on p. 149.</note> This note seems to have been placed there by a disciple of Spinoza, and speaks of the *Treatise* as having been dictated, while the text says that it was *written*. Very likely a good portion of the *Treatise* had actually been dictated to his friends while Spinoza was at Amsterdam, but the completed *Treatise* must have been sent to them in manuscript from Rijnsburg.

Avenarius has suggested that the *Short Treatise* was quite a youthful work; that the Dialogues were written about 1651, and the main text in 1654 or 1655. The suggestion was largely based on the assumption that the *Tractatus Theologico-Politicus* was finished in 1661 or earlier. But it is now known that Spinoza did not complete it until 1669 or 1670. The comparative immaturity of the *Short Treatise* as compared with it does not, therefore, compel us to assume that the *Treatise* was written long before 1661. And the internal evidence is against such an early date as 1655. The tone of the concluding paragraph of Part II shows that, when writing it, Spinoza had already acquired a certain authority in a circle of philosophical friends. He could not have written in that style at the age of 22 or 23. Again, his reference to the "character of the age" seems to

<meta>This page continues the discussion regarding the dating of Spinoza’s *Short Treatise*, specifically examining internal evidence such as his references to Christian doctrine and his excommunication to narrow down the period of composition.</meta>

<header>INTRODUCTION</header>

point to his own excommunication as an event in the past. Moreover, the *Treatise* shows an interest in specific Christian doctrines and their reinterpretation (the son of God, Regeneration, Sin in relation to the Law, and Grace). Spinoza must have been moving for some time in a Christian environment to feel such an interest in problems of Christian theology. The characters he introduces as illustrations bear New Testament names, and he even devotes a chapter to Devils, in whom the Jews took very little interest. All this argues in favor of the supposition that the *Short Treatise* was not written until some years after Spinoza's severance from the Jewish community (1656). Freudenthal maintains, accordingly, that it must have been composed between 1658 and 1660. With this view we concur, allowing, however, that some of the additions may be later than 1660, while some parts of the *Treatise* or some of its views may date from *before* Spinoza's excommunication, because one of the charges already brought against him then was that he had asserted that extension was an attribute of God.

It is interesting to note, in conclusion, that when Spinoza made his literary *debut* he was already a pantheist. His pantheism was not in any sense a development of Cartesianism; he started from it, and at once criticized the Cartesian dualism from that point of view. He probably owed his introduction to pantheistic views partly to Jewish mysticism, with which he must have been made acquainted by Rabbis Morteira and Ben Israel, who were both of them strongly inclined towards mysticism, and partly to Bruno, to whose writings, as already suggested, Van den Enden may have directed his attention. The *Short Treatise* shows also considerable familiarity with, and indebtedness to, the writings of Descartes, as will be shown in the Commentary. But Spinoza is never merely a follower of the Jewish Mystics, or of Bruno, or of Descartes. From the first he

<meta>This page provides a guide to the symbols and abbreviations used in the translation of Spinoza's Short Treatise, specifically distinguishing between the two primary manuscripts, A and B.</meta>

## EXPLANATIONS OF SIGNS, etc.

A stands for the older manuscript of the *Short Treatise*.

B stands for the later manuscript of the *Short Treatise*.

Where nothing is stated to the contrary, the translation follows A.

\* . . \* Words, etc., between asterisks are in B, but not in A.

[ ] Words in square brackets are those of the translator; but

\*[ ]\* When there are asterisks outside the brackets, then B contains the words in those brackets.

† is used as a reference mark for notes which are given in A.

‡ is used as a reference mark for notes indicating different readings, etc.

“ . . .” Occasionally words are put in quotation marks to draw attention to the fact that they are used in a peculiar sense.

Explanations of difficult words and passages will be found at the end of the volume (p. 165 *ff*) arranged according to chapter and page.

Beginners may omit most of the footnotes in the translation during a first reading.

<summary>This page explains the editorial symbols and abbreviations used to distinguish between the two primary manuscripts (A and B) of the Short Treatise. It also provides guidance on how to interpret translator notes, brackets, and quotation marks throughout the text.</summary>
<keywords>manuscript A, manuscript B, editorial signs, translation conventions, reference marks, Spinoza, Short Treatise</keywords>

# SHORT TREATISE
### ON
## GOD,
## MAN,
### AND HIS
# WELL-BEING.

->Previously written in the Latin language by B. D. S.<-
->for the benefit of his students who wished<-
->to devote themselves to the practice of<-
->Ethics and True Philosophy.<-

->And now translated into the Dutch language<-
->for the benefit of the lovers of Truth and Virtue:<-
->so that those who have thought so broadly of it,<-
->and show its usefulness to simple-minded critics,<-
->may have their mouths stopped; and although<-
->they are slanderers of what they do not yet<-
->understand, they may help take heed of<-
->God, themselves, and each other's<-
->well-being. And that those who<-
->are sick in mind might be led by the<-
->spirit of gentleness and tolerance,<-
->following the example of the Lord<-
->Christ, our best Teacher.<-

<meta>This page begins Chapter 1 of the treatise, following the introductory sections. It transitions into the formal philosophical arguments for the existence of God, utilizing both *a priori* and *a posteriori* reasoning characteristic of the period's metaphysical discourse.</meta>

# That God exists. Chapter 1.

## Chapter 1.

Concerning then the first point: namely, whether there is a God? We say that this can be proven, first (*a priori*) or from what comes before, as follows.

<margin>* Understand "defined nature" as that through which the thing is what it is, and from which it cannot be separated without also destroying that thing; just as it belongs to the essence of a mountain that it has a valley, or to have the essence of the mountain in the valley. This is truly eternal and unchangeable, and must always be in the concept of the mountain, even if it never existed.</margin>
1. Everything that we clearly and distinctly understand to belong to the * Nature of a thing, we can also affirm with truth about that thing:

But that existence belongs to the Nature of God, we can clearly and distinctly understand. Therefore.

Otherwise, also as follows:

2. The essences of things are from all eternity, and shall also remain unchangeable for all eternity:

The existence of God is essence. Therefore. *A Posteriori*, or from what follows, as follows.

<margin>* God</margin>
1. If man has an Idea of God, then * God must formally exist:

2. But man has an Idea of God. Therefore.

The first we prove as follows:

If there is an Idea of God, then the cause of it must be formal: <unclear>waer de</unclear> and containing within itself everything that the Idea has objectively: but there is an Idea of God: Therefore. To maintain the reasoning of the first proof, we establish these following ground rules, namely:

1. That knowable things are infinite.
2. That a finite understanding cannot grasp the infinite.
3. That a finite understanding cannot understand anything by itself unless it is determined by something from outside, because just as

---

<margin>* In the description, see Chapter 2, regarding how God has indivisible attributes, we can [understand] his essence. All that we clearly and distinctly [perceive] to belong to the nature of a thing can also be affirmed with truth of the thing; neither is the thing established, nor is it [denied] that God is proved when the true thing is affirmed.</margin>
truth of the thing can be affirmed: but to the Nature of one, [specifically] that if it has those attributes, the attribute which is Being belongs to it. Therefore. The appeal to say that this is indeed affirmed of the Idea, but not of the Thing itself, is false: for the Idea does not consist materially of the attribute that belongs to this essence, so that the God, which [is]...

<summary>This page presents philosophical proofs for the existence of God using both a priori (from essence) and a posteriori (from the idea of God in man) arguments. It defines the "nature" of a thing as its inseparable essence and establishes ground rules for understanding the infinite.</summary>

<summary>This page introduces the first chapter of the treatise, beginning an *a priori* argument for the existence of God based on the "defined nature" or essence of things. # CHAPTER I
## THAT GOD EXISTS

As regards the first, <note>B: this.</note> namely, whether there is a God, this, we say, can be proved.

*\*I.\** In the first place, *a priori* thus:

1. Whatever we clearly and distinctly know to belong to the nature <note>Understand the definite nature through which a thing is what it is, and which can by no means be removed from it without at the same time destroying that thing: thus, for instance, it belongs to the essence of a mountain that it should have a valley, or the essence of a mountain is that it has a valley; <note>B simply: to the essence of a mountain belongs a valley.</note> this is truly eternal and immutable, and must always be included in the concept of a mountain, even if it never existed, or did not exist now.</note> of a thing, we can also truly affirm of that thing. Now we can know clearly and distinctly that existence belongs to the nature of God;

Therefore . . .

2. The essences of things are from all eternity, and unto all eternity shall remain immutable;
The existence of God is essence;
Therefore . . .

<summary>This page outlines two logical arguments for the existence of God, focusing on the idea that existence is an inseparable part of God's eternal and immutable essence. --- Page 158 ---
<meta>This page continues the first chapter of the treatise, transitioning from a priori arguments to a posteriori proofs for the existence of God based on the presence of the idea of God in the human mind.</meta>

<page-num>16</page-num>
<header>GOD, MAN, AND HIS WELL-BEING</header>

<margin>1</margin> **\*II.\*** *A posteriori*, as follows:

->If man has an idea of God, then God † must exist<-
->*formally*;<-
->Now, man has an idea of God;<-
->Therefore . . .<-

The first part we prove as follows:

->If there is an idea of God, then its cause<-
->must exist *formally*, and contain in itself all<-
->that the idea possesses *objectively*;<-
<margin>10</margin> ->Now there is an idea of God;<-
->Therefore . . .<-

In order to prove the first part of this argument, we state the following principles, namely:

1. That the number of knowable things is infinite;
2. That a finite understanding cannot grasp the infinite;
<margin>20</margin> 3. That a finite understanding, unless it is determined by something external, cannot through itself know anything; because, just as it has no power to know all things equally, so little [does it have power to know one thing more than another] <note>The sentence is incomplete at the bottom of the page.</note>

---

† From the definition which follows in chapter 2, namely, that God has infinite attributes, we can prove his existence as follows: Whatever we clearly and distinctly see to belong to the nature of a thing, we can also truly affirm of that thing; now existence, which is an attribute, belongs to the nature of a being that has infinite attributes; therefore . . . To assert that this may well be affirmed of the idea, but not of the thing itself, is false: for the Idea does not really consist of the attribute which belongs to this <margin>30</margin> being, so that what is affirmed is <note>affirmed</note> neither of the thing, nor of that which is affirmed of the thing; so that there is a great difference between the Idea and the *Ideatum* <note>The object represented by an idea.</note>: therefore what is affirmed of the thing is not affirmed of the Idea, and *vice versa*. <note>Text corrupt. See Commentary.</note>

<summary>This page presents an a posteriori argument for God's existence, asserting that the human idea of an infinite God must be caused by a being that actually possesses those infinite attributes. It also outlines the limitations of human understanding, noting that a finite mind cannot grasp the infinite or initiate knowledge without external determination.</summary>

<margin>1</margin>From all this, the second point is proven: that the cause of a man's ideas is *not* his imagination but some external cause. This cause compels him to perceive one thing rather than another, and it is nothing other than this: the things whose <term>essentia objectiva</term> is in his understanding exist <term>formaliter</term>, and are closer to him than other things. If, then, man has the idea of God, it is clear that God must exist <term>formaliter</term>, though not <term>eminenter</term>, as there is

ideas that we have must also be fictions. If this is so, *how*
<margin>10</margin>does it happen that we find such a great difference among them? For regarding *some*, we see that it is impossible for them to exist; *for example*, all monsters supposed to be composed of two natures, such as an animal that would be both a bird and a horse, and the like. It is impossible for these to have a place in Nature, which we find to be structured differently; ‡ other ideas may exist, but do not have to; however, whether they exist or do not exist, their essence is always necessary. Such is the idea of a triangle, and that of the love within the soul apart from the body, etc.; so that even if I at first thought that I had imagined these, I am nevertheless compelled afterward
<margin>20</margin>to say that they are, and would be, the same regardless, even if neither I nor anyone else had ever thought about them. Consequently, they are not merely imagined by me, and must also have a <term>subjectum</term> outside of me other than myself, without which <term>subjectum</term> they cannot be. In addition to these, there is yet a *third idea*, and it is unique; this one carries with it necessary existence, and not, like the previous ones, the mere possibility of existence. For in the case of those, their essence was indeed necessary, but not their existence, while in this case, both its existence and its essence are necessary, and it is nothing without them. I therefore see now
<margin>30</margin>that the truth, essence, or existence of anything never depends on me; for, as was shown with reference to the second kind of ideas, they are what they are independently of me, whether regarding their essence alone, or regarding both essence and existence. We also know that it cannot change into anything better,† because it is perfect—which it would not be if it could improve—nor can such a being be subjected to anything outside of it, since it is omnipotent, and so forth.

From all this, then, it follows clearly that we can prove that God exists both *a priori* and *a posteriori*. It is better, indeed, to prove it *a priori*. For things which are proved in the latter <margin>10</margin> way [*a posteriori*] must be proved through their external causes. This is an obvious imperfection ‡ in them, since they cannot make themselves known ‡‡ through themselves, but only through external causes. God, however, who is the first cause of all things, and also the cause of himself [*causa sui*], makes himself known through himself. Therefore, one should not give much weight to the saying of Thomas Aquinas—namely, that God could not be proved *a priori* because he, supposedly, has no cause.

# CHAPTER II

## WHAT GOD IS

Now that we have proved above *that God exists*, it is time to show *what he is*. Specifically, we say that he is *a being of whom all or infinite attributes are predicated*,† and of these attributes, every one is infinitely perfect in its kind. Now, in order to express our views clearly, we shall set forth the following four propositions:

<margin>10</margin>
1. That there is no finite substance,†† but that every substance must be infinitely perfect in its kind; that is to say, that in the infinite understanding of God, no substance can be more perfect than that which already exists in Nature.

<margin>20</margin>
† The reason is this: since *Nothing* can have no attributes, the *All* must have all attributes; and just as *Nothing* has no attribute because it is *Nothing*, so that which is *Something* has attributes because it is *Something*. Hence, the more it is *Something*, the more attributes it must have; and consequently, since God is the most perfect, and is all that is *Anything*, he must also have infinite, perfect, and all attributes.

<margin>30</margin>
†† Once we can prove that there can be no *Finite Substance*, then all substance must, without limitation, belong to the divine being. We prove it thus: 1. It must either have limited itself, or ‡ some *other* must have limited it. It could not have done so itself because, having been infinite, it would have had to change its whole essence. Nor can it be limited by another: for this again must be either finite or infinite; the former is impossible, therefore the latter; therefore it <note>that is, the other thing</note> is God. He must, then, have made it finite because he lacked either the power or the will <note>to make it infinite</note>: but the first <note>supposition</note> is contrary to his

That *there can be no finite substance* is clear from this: if it were so, it would necessarily possess something derived from Nothing, which is impossible. For from where can it derive that in which it differs from God? Certainly not from God, for he possesses nothing imperfect or finite, *etc.* So, from where then, if not from Nothing? Therefore, there is no substance other than infinite. From this it follows that *there cannot be two identical infinite sub-*
<margin>20</margin> *stances*; for to postulate such would necessitate limitation. And from this, again, it follows that *one substance cannot produce another*; specifically: the cause we might suppose to produce this substance must have the same attribute †† as the one produced, and also either just as much perfection ‡‡‡, or more, or less. The first case is not possible, because there would then be two identical [substances]. The second is also not possible, because in that case there would be a finite [substance]. Nor the third, because something cannot come from nothing. Moreover, if the finite †††† came from the infinite, then the infinite ‡‡‡‡‡ would also be finite, *etc.*
<margin>30</margin> Therefore, one substance cannot produce another. And from this, again, it follows that all substance must exist “*formaliter*,” for if it did not exist, there would be no possibility for it to ever come into existence.

The first [alternative] is not true, because it is impossible that a substance should have wanted to make itself finite, especially a substance which had come into existence through itself. Therefore, I say, it is made finite by its cause, which is necessarily God. ---

† To say to this *that the nature of the thing required such* [limitation] *and that it could not therefore be otherwise—that is no reply*: for the nature of a thing can require nothing while it does not exist. Should you say that one may, nevertheless, see what <margin>20</margin>belongs to the nature of a thing which does not exist: that is true as regards its existence, but by no means as regards its essence. And herein lies the difference between *creating* and *generating*. To create is to posit a thing *quo ad essentiam et existentiam simul* [<gloss>i.e., to give a thing both essence and existence</gloss>]; while in the case of generation a thing comes forth *quo ad existentiam solam* [<gloss>i.e., it only receives existence</gloss>]. And therefore there is now in Nature no creation but only generation. So that when God creates he creates at once the nature of the thing with the thing itself. He would therefore show ill will if (from lack of <margin>30</margin>will, and not of power) he created the thing in such a way that it should not agree with its cause in essence and existence. However, what we here call creation can really not be said ever to have taken place, and it is only mentioned to indicate what we can say about it, if we distinguish between creating and generating.

<margin>1</margin>Nature. Or thus: All substance is real, and when a substance is considered by itself its existence does not follow from its essence; therefore, no existing substance can be known through itself, but it must belong to something else. That is, when with our understanding we consider "substantial" Thought and ["substantial"] Extension, then we consider them only in their essence and not as existing; that is, [we do not consider] that their existence necessarily belongs to their essence. When, however, we prove [of each] that it is an attribute of God, we thereby prove *a priori* that it exists, and *a posteriori* (as regards extension alone) [we prove its existence] from the modes which must necessarily have it for their <term>subjectum</term>.</note> So it must necessarily
<margin>10</margin>follow that Nature, which results from no causes, and which we nonetheless know to exist, must necessarily be a perfect being to which existence belongs.

<margin>1</margin> He is a simple being. Moreover, when extension is divided it is passive; and with God (who is never passive, and cannot be affected by any other being, because he is the first efficient cause of all) this can by no means be the case. To this we reply: (1) that “part” and “whole” are not true or real entities, but only “things of reason,” and consequently there is in Nature † neither whole nor parts. (2) A thing composed of different parts must be such that its parts, taken separately, can be conceived and <margin>10</margin> understood without one another. Take, for instance, a clock which is composed of many different wheels, cords, and other things; in it, I say, each wheel, cord, etc., can be

† In Nature, that is, in “substantial” Extension; for if this were divided, its nature and being would be at once destroyed, as it exists only as infinite extension, or—which comes to the same thing—it exists only as a whole.

But should you say: is there no part in extension prior to all its modes? I say, certainly not. But you may say, since there is motion in matter, it must be in some part of matter, for it cannot <margin>20</margin> be in the whole, because the whole is infinite; and where shall it be moved, when there is nothing outside it? Therefore it must be in a part.‡ My answer is: Motion alone does not exist, but only motion and rest together; and this is in the whole, and must be in it, because there is no part in extension. Should you, however, say that there is, then tell me: if you divide the whole of extension, then—as regards any part which you cut off from it in thought—can you also separate it in nature from all [other] parts? And supposing this has been done, I ask: what is there between the part cut off ‡‡ and the rest? You must say: a vacuum, or another body, or something <margin>30</margin> of extension itself; there is no fourth possibility. The first will not do, because there is no vacuum (something positive and yet not a body); nor the second, because then there would exist a mode, which cannot be, since ‡‡‡ extension as extension is without and prior to all modes. Therefore, it must be the third; and then there is no part, but only the whole of extension.‡‡‡‡

<margin>1</margin>conceived and understood separately, without the com-

can exist without the whole; but because extension is a sub-

neither diminish nor increase, and no parts of it can be
understood separately, because by its nature it must be infinite.
And that it must be so follows from this: namely, because
if it were not so, but consisted of parts, then it would not <margin>10</margin>
be infinite by its nature, as it is said to be; and it is
impossible to conceive of parts in an infinite nature, since by
their nature all parts are finite.‡ Add to this: if it
consisted of different parts, then it should be understandable
that, supposing some parts of it were annihilated, exten-
sion might remain all the same and not be annihilated
together with the annihilation of some of its parts; this is
clearly contradictory for something that is infinite by its own nature
and can never be, or be conceived as, limited or finite.
Further, as regards the parts in Nature, we maintain that <margin>20</margin>
division—as has also been said already before—never takes place
in substance, but always and only in the mode of substance.
Thus, if I want to divide water, I only divide the mode of
substance, and not the substance itself. And whether this mode
is that of water or something else, the substance is always the same.‡‡

Division, then, or passivity, always takes place in the
mode; thus when we say that man passes away or is
annihilated, then this is understood to apply to man only in
so far as he is such a composite being, and a mode of sub-
stance, and not the substance on which he depends. <margin>30</margin>

<margin>1</margin> Moreover, we have already stated, and we shall repeat it later, that outside God there is nothing at all, and that he is an *Immanent Cause*. Now, passivity—whenever the agent and the patient <note>In this context, the "agent" is the one who acts, and the "patient" is the one who is acted upon or receives the action.</note> are different entities—is a clear imperfection, because the patient must necessarily be dependent on that which caused the passivity from the outside; therefore, it has no place in God, who is perfect. Furthermore, concerning such an agent who acts within himself, it can never be said that he has the imperfection of a patient, because he is not
<margin>10</margin> affected by another; this is the case, for instance, with the understanding, which, as the philosophers also assert, is the cause of its own ideas. Since, however, it is an immanent cause, what right does one have to say that it is imperfect, no matter how frequently it is affected by itself? ‡ Lastly, since substance is the cause and the origin of all its modes, it may with far greater right be called an agent than a patient. And with these remarks, we consider all objections adequately answered.

It is further objected that there must necessarily be a first cause which sets body in motion, because when it is at rest
<margin>20</margin> it is impossible for it to set itself in motion. And since it is clearly evident that rest and motion exist in Nature, they think these must necessarily result from an external cause. But it is easy for us to reply to this; for we admit that if body were a thing existing by itself, and had no other attributes than length, breadth, and depth, then, if it were actually at rest, there would be no cause within it to begin moving itself. However, we have already stated before *that Nature is a being of which all attributes are predicated*, and this being the case, it can lack nothing necessary to
<margin>30</margin> produce everything that is to be produced.

Having so far discussed what God is, we shall say only a word, as it were, about his attributes: namely, that those known to us consist of only two, which are *Thought and*

# FIRST DIALOGUE
## BETWEEN UNDERSTANDING, LOVE, REASON, AND DESIRE

**LOVE.** I see, Brother, that both my essence and perfection depend on your perfection; and since the perfection of the object you have conceived is your perfection, while mine proceeds from yours, tell me now, I pray you, whether you have conceived such a being as is supremely perfect, incapable of being limited by any other, and in <margin>10</margin> which I also am included.

**UNDERSTANDING.** For my part, I consider Nature only in its totality as infinite and supremely perfect; but if you have any doubts about it, ask Reason, and she will tell you.

**REASON.** To me, the truth of the matter is undeniable; for if we were to limit Nature, then we should, absurdly enough, have to limit it with mere Nothing. ‡ We avoid this absurdity by stating that it is *one eternal unity, infinite, omnipotent, etc.*—that is, that Nature is infinite and that all is contained <margin>20</margin> within it; and the opposite of this we call Nothing.

# SECOND DIALOGUE
### BETWEEN
## ERASMUS AND THEOPHILUS
### RELATING PARTLY TO THE PRECEDING, PARTLY TO THE FOLLOWING SECOND PART

ERASMUS. I have heard you say, Theophilus, that God is a cause of *all things*, and, at the same time, that he can be *nothing other than an immanent cause*. Now, if he is an *immanent cause* of all things, how then can you call him a *remote* ‡ cause? For that is impossible in the case of an immanent cause.

THEOPHILUS. When I said that God is a remote ‡ cause, I only said it with reference to the things <insert>which God has produced mediately, and not with reference to those</insert> which God (without any other conditions beyond his mere existence) has produced immediately; but I certainly did not mean to call him an absolutely remote ‡ cause, as you might also have clearly gathered from my remarks. For I also said that in some respects we can call him a remote cause.

ERASMUS. I now adequately understand what you mean to say; but I also note that you have said *that the effect of the* ‡‡ *immanent cause remains united with its cause in such a way that together they constitute a whole*. Now, if this is so, then I think God cannot be an immanent cause. For if he and that which is produced by him together form a whole, then you ascribe more essence to God at one time than at another. I ask you to resolve these doubts for me.

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Erasmus and Theophilus, where Theophilus addresses Erasmus's confusion regarding whether joining an effect to an immanent cause increases that cause's essence.</meta>

# ERASMUS AND THEOPHILUS

<margin>1</margin> THEOPHILUS. If, Erasmus, you want to free yourself from this confusion, then pay close attention to what I am going to tell you now. The essence of a thing does not increase through its union with another thing with which it forms a whole; on the contrary, the first remains unchanged. I will give you an illustration so that you may understand me better. A sculptor has made various forms from wood in the likeness of the parts of the human body. He takes one of these, which has the form of a human chest, and joins it to another, which has the form of a human head, and from <margin>10</margin> these two he makes a whole which represents the upper part of a human body. Would you then say that the essence of the head has increased because it has been joined to the chest? That would be wrong, because it is the same as it was before. For the sake of greater clarity, let me give you another illustration: namely, the idea I have of a triangle, and another idea resulting from extending one of the angles, which extended angle is necessarily equal to the two interior opposite angles, and so forth. These, I say, have produced a new idea—namely, that the <margin>20</margin> three angles of the triangle are equal to two right angles. This idea is so connected with the first that it can neither exist nor be conceived without it.‡ Pay close attention now: although the new idea is joined to the previous one, the essence of the previous idea does not undergo any change as a result; on the contrary, it remains without the slightest change. You may also observe the same thing in every idea that produces love in itself: this love in no way adds to the essence of the idea. But why provide more examples? You can see it clearly in the subject that I <margin>30</margin> have been illustrating and which we are discussing now. I have distinctly stated that all attributes, which depend on no

---

For example, I want to have light in a certain room; I kindle a light, and this lights up the room by itself; or I open a window <note>shutter</note>. # CHAPTER III

## THAT GOD IS A CAUSE OF ALL THINGS

We shall now begin to consider those attributes [of God] which we called *Propria.*† And, first of all, how God is a cause of all things.

<margin>10</margin>
Now, we have already said above *that one substance cannot produce another; and that God is a being of whom all attributes are predicated;* from which it clearly follows that all other things can by no means be, or be understood, apart from or outside him. Therefore we may say with all reason *that God is a cause of all things.*

As it is usual to divide the efficient cause into eight divisions, let me, then, inquire how and in what sense God is a cause.

First, then, we say that he is an *emanative or productive cause of his works;* and, insofar as there is activity, *an active or operating cause,* which we regard as one and the same, because they involve each other.

<margin>20</margin>
Secondly, he is an *immanent,* and not a *transeunt cause,* since all that he produces is within himself, and not outside him, because there is nothing outside him.

Thirdly, God is a *free cause,* and not a *natural cause,* as we shall make clear and manifest when we come to consider whether God can omit doing what he does, and then it will also be explained what *true freedom* consists of.

---

<margin>30</margin>
† The [attributes] following are called *Propria,* because they are only adjectives, which cannot be understood without their substantives. That is to say, without them God would indeed be no God, but still it is not they that constitute God; for they reveal nothing of the character of a substance, through which alone God exists.

<summary>This chapter defines God as the cause of all things, explaining that because God is the substance of all attributes, nothing can exist outside Him. The author categorizes God's causality as emanative, immanent, and free rather than transeunt or purely natural.</summary>
<keywords>God, attributes, Propria, efficient cause, immanent cause, transeunt cause, substance, divine freedom, causality</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Chapter III, expanding on the eight divisions of the efficient cause as they apply to God, following the discussion of God as a productive, immanent, and free cause.</meta>

<header>GOD, MAN, AND HIS WELL-BEING</header>

<margin>1</margin> Fourthly, God is a *cause through himself*, and not *by accident*; this will become more evident from the discussion on Predestination.

Fifthly, God is a *principal cause of the works which he has created immediately*, such as motion in matter, etc.; in these works, there is no place for a subsidiary <note>instrumental</note> cause, since such causes are limited to particular things; for example, when he dries the sea by means of a strong wind, and so forth in the case of all particular things ‡ in Nature.

<margin>10</margin> The *subsidiary provoking cause* is not <note>found</note> in God, because there is nothing outside of him to incite him. The *predisposing* ‡‡ *cause*, on the other hand, is his perfection itself; through it, he is a cause of himself and, consequently, of all other things.

Sixthly, God alone is the *first* or *Initial cause*, as is evident from our previous proof.

Seventhly, God is also a *Universal cause*, but only insofar as he produces various things; otherwise, this can never be attributed to him, as he needs no one else in order <margin>20</margin> to produce any results.

Eighthly, God is the *proximate cause* of the things that are infinite and immutable, and which we assert to have been created immediately by him; but, in one sense, he is the remote cause of all particular things.

# CHAPTER IV

## ON GOD’S NECESSARY ACTIVITY

<margin>1</margin> We deny that God can refrain from doing what he does, and we shall also prove this when we discuss Predestination; there we will show that all things necessarily depend on their causes. But, in the second place, this conclusion also follows from the perfection of God; for it is true, beyond a doubt, that *God can make everything just as perfect as it is conceived in his Idea*; and just as things that are conceived by him cannot be conceived by him more perfectly than he conceives them, <margin>10</margin> so all things can be made by him so perfect that they cannot come from him in a more perfect condition. Again, ‡ when we conclude that God could not have refrained from doing what he has done, we derive this from his perfection; because, in God, it would be an imperfection to be able to refrain from doing what he does; we do not, however, suppose that there is a subsidiary external cause in God that might have moved him to action, for then he would not be God.

But now, again, there is the controversy whether, specifically, of all that is in his Idea and which he can realize so <margin>20</margin> perfectly—whether, I say, he could refrain from realizing anything, and whether such an omission would be a perfection in him. Now, we maintain that, since all that happens is done by God, it must therefore necessarily be predetermined by him; otherwise he would be subject to change, which would be a great imperfection in him. And as this predetermination by him must be from eternity—in which eternity there is no before or after—it follows irresistibly that God could never have predetermined things in any other way than that in which they are now. <margin>30</margin>

Because God is eternal and immutable, all things are necessarily predetermined, and he could not have acted otherwise without being imperfect.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument from Chapter IV regarding the necessity of God's actions. # GOD, MAN, AND HIS WELL-BEING

<margin>1</margin> they are determined now, and have been from eternity, and that God could not have existed either before or without these determinations. Further, if God were to omit doing anything, then he must either have some cause for it or not; if he has, then it is necessary that he should omit doing it; if he does not, then it is necessary that he should not omit doing it; this is self-evident. Moreover, in a created thing, it is a perfection to exist and to have been produced by God, for, of all imperfections, non-existence is the greatest <margin>10</margin> imperfection; and since God desires the welfare and perfection of all things, it would follow that if God desired that a certain thing should not exist, then the welfare and perfection of this thing must be supposed to consist in its non-existence, which is self-contradictory. That is why we deny that God can omit doing what he does. Some regard this as blasphemy and as a belittling of God; but such an assertion results from a misunderstanding of what constitutes *true freedom*; this is by no means what they think it is, namely, the ability to do or to refrain from doing something good or evil; <margin>20</margin> but *true freedom is only, or nothing other than* [the status of being] *the first cause, which is in no way constrained or coerced by anything else, and which through its perfection alone is the cause of all perfection*; ‡ consequently, if God could omit doing this, he would not be perfect: for the ability to omit doing some good, or accomplishing some perfection in what he does, can have no place in him except through defect. ‡‡

That God alone is the only free cause is, therefore, clear not only from what has just been said, but also from this: <margin>30</margin> namely, that there is no external cause outside him to force or constrain him; none of this is the case with created things. Against this, it is argued as follows: The good is only good

Since we say that *Freedom* does not consist in <note>having the choice of</note> doing or <margin>20</margin> not doing something, and since we have also shown that what makes him <note>God</note> do anything can be nothing else than his own perfection, we conclude *that, had it not been that his perfection made him do all this, then the things would not exist, and could not come into existence, in order to be what they are now. This is just like saying: if God were imperfect then things would be different from what they are now.* ‡‡

So much regarding the first <note>attribute</note>; we shall now pass on to the second attribute, which we call a *proprium* of God, and see what we have to say about it, and so on to <margin>30</margin> the end.

# CHAPTER V

## ON DIVINE PROVIDENCE

THE second attribute, which we call a *proprium* <note>of God</note>, is his Providence, which to us is nothing else than the *striving* that we find in the whole of Nature and in individual things to maintain and preserve their own existence. For it is manifest that no thing could, through its own nature, seek its own annihilation, but, on the contrary, that every thing has in itself a striving to preserve its condition and to improve itself. Following these definitions of ours, we <margin>10</margin> therefore posit a *general* and a *special providence*. The *general* <note>*providence*</note> is that through which all things are produced and sustained insofar as they are parts of the whole of Nature. The *special providence* is the striving of each thing separately to preserve its existence <note>that is to say, each thing</note>, considered not as a part of Nature, but as a whole <note>by itself</note>. This is explained by the following example: All the limbs of a man are provided for and cared for insofar as they are parts of the man; this is *general providence*. <margin>20</margin> While *special* <note>*providence*</note> is the striving of each separate limb (as a whole in itself, and not as a part of the man) to preserve and maintain its own well-being.

<vocab>Divine Providence, proprium, striving, Nature, general providence, special providence</vocab>

<summary>This chapter defines Divine Providence as the natural striving for self-preservation found in all things. It distinguishes between general providence, which governs things as parts of the natural whole, and special providence, which is the individual drive of a thing to maintain its own existence.</summary>

# CHAPTER VI
## ON DIVINE PREDESTINATION

<margin>1</margin>The third attribute, we say, is divine predestination.
1. We proved before that God cannot fail to do what he does; namely, that he has made everything so perfect that it cannot be more perfect.
2. And, at the same time, that without him nothing can exist, or be conceived.
It remains to be seen now whether there are in Nature <margin>10</margin>any accidental things—that is to say, whether there are any things which may happen and may also not happen. Secondly, whether there is anything concerning which we cannot ask why it is.
Now, that there are no accidental things we prove thus: That which has no cause to exist cannot possibly exist; that which is accidental has no cause: therefore . . .
The first is beyond all dispute; the second we prove thus: If anything that is accidental has a definite and certain cause why it should exist, then it must necessarily <margin>20</margin>exist; but for it to be both accidental and necessary at the same time is self-contradictory; Therefore . . .
Perhaps someone will say that *an accidental thing* has indeed no definite and certain cause, but rather an accidental one. If this were so, it must be so either *in sensu diviso* or *in sensu composito* <note>In a divided sense or in a composite sense</note>; that is to say, either the existence of the cause is accidental, and not its being a cause; or it is accidental that a certain thing (which indeed must necessarily exist in Nature) should be the cause of the occurrence of that accidental thing. However, both the one and the <margin>30</margin>other are false.

<summary>This page argues against the existence of "accidental" things in Nature, asserting that everything has a definite cause and therefore exists by necessity. The author posits that a thing cannot be both accidental and have a cause, as a cause makes its existence necessary.</summary>
<keywords>divine predestination, causality, necessity, accidental things, Nature, in sensu diviso, in sensu composito, God</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the logical proof against the existence of accidental things in Nature, transitioning from the refutation of accidental causes to the necessity of the human will having an external cause, based on the principle that God is the sole first cause.</meta>

<header>ON DIVINE PREDESTINATION</header>

For, regarding the first point, if something accidental is <margin>1</margin>
accidental because the existence of its cause is accidental,
then that cause must also be accidental, because the cause
which has produced it is also accidental, *and so on to infinity.*
And since it has already been proved, *that all things*
*depend on one single cause*, this cause would therefore also
have to be accidental: which is manifestly false.
As regards the second point: if the cause were no more com-
pelled to produce one thing than another, that is, if the
cause were no more compelled to produce this something <margin>10</margin>
than not to produce it, then it would be impossible at once
both that it should produce it and that it should not produce
it, which is quite contradictory.
Concerning the second question raised above, *whether*
*there is no thing in Nature about which one cannot ask why it*
*is*, this remark of ours shows that we have to inquire through
what cause a thing is real; for if this cause did not exist
it would be impossible that the thing should exist. Now, we
must look for this cause either in the thing or outside the
thing. If, however, anyone should ask for a rule whereby <margin>20</margin>
to conduct this inquiry, we say that none whatever seems
necessary. For if existence pertains to the nature of a thing,
then it is certain that we must not look outside it for its cause;
but if such is not the case, then we must always look outside
the thing for its cause. Since, however, the first pertains
to God alone, it is thereby proved (as we have already also
proved before) that God alone is the first cause of all things.
From this it is also evident that this or that will of man (since
the existence of the will does not pertain to its essence) must
also have an external cause, by which it is necessarily <margin>30</margin>
caused; that this is so is also evident from all that we have
said in this chapter; and it will be still more evident when,
in the second part, we come to consider and discuss the
freedom of man.
Against all this others object: how is it possible that

<page-num>50</page-num>
# GOD, MAN, AND HIS WELL-BEING

<margin>1</margin> God, who is said to be supremely perfect, and the sole cause, disposer, and provider of all, nevertheless permits such *confusion* to be seen everywhere in Nature? Also, why has he not *made man so that he is unable to sin?*

Now, in the first place, it cannot be rightly said that there is *confusion* in Nature, since nobody knows all the causes of things so as to be able to judge accordingly. This objection, however, originates from this kind of ignorance: namely, that they have established general *Ideas* with which,
<margin>10</margin> they think, particular things must agree if they are to be perfect. These *Ideas*, they state, are in the understanding of God, as many of *Plato's* followers have said: namely, that these general *Ideas* (such as *Rational, Animal,*‡ and the like) *have been created by God; and although those who follow* Aristotle *say, indeed, that these things are not real things, but only things of Reason, they nevertheless frequently regard them as <note>real</note> things, since they have clearly said that his providence does not extend to particular things, but only to kinds;* for example, God has never exercised his
<margin>20</margin> providence over Bucephalus, etc., but only over the whole genus Horse. They say also that God has no knowledge of particular and transient things, but only of the general, which, in their opinion, are imperishable. We have, however, rightly considered ‡‡ this to be due to their ignorance. For it is precisely the particular things, and they alone, that have a cause, and not the general, because they are nothing.

God then is the cause of, and providence over, particular things only. If particular things had to conform to some
<margin>30</margin> other Nature, then they could not conform to their own, and consequently could not be what they truly are. For example, if God had made all human beings like Adam before the fall, then indeed he would only have created Adam, and not Paul or Peter; but no, it is just perfection

at wrong times.

necessary, conform to the Idea of Peter, and not to the
Idea of Man; good and evil, or sin, these are only modes
of thought, and by no means things, or anything that has
reality, as we shall very likely show yet more fully in what <margin>20</margin>
follows. For all things and works which are in Nature
are perfect.

# CHAPTER VII

## ON THE ATTRIBUTES WHICH DO NOT PERTAIN TO GOD

Here we shall take up the consideration of those attributes <note>As regards the attributes of which God consists, they are only infinite substances, each of which must of itself be infinitely perfect. That this must necessarily be so, we are convinced by clear and distinct reasons. It is true, however, that up to the present only two of all these infinite beings are known to us through their own essence: these are thought and extension. Everything else that is commonly ascribed to God is not an attribute of his, but only certain modes which may be attributed to him either in consideration of all—that is, *all* his attributes—or in consideration of *one* attribute. In consideration of *all* [it is said], for instance, that he is eternal, self-subsisting, infinite, the cause of all things, and immutable. 
<margin>20</margin>
In consideration of *one* [it is said], for instance, that he is omniscient, wise, etc., which pertains to thought; and, again, that he is omnipresent, fills everything, etc., 
<margin>30</margin>
which pertains to extension.</note> which are commonly attributed to God, but which, nevertheless, do not belong to him; as also of those through which people seek to prove the existence of God, though in vain; and also the rules of accurate definition.

<margin>10</margin>
For this purpose, we shall not trouble ourselves very much about the ideas that people commonly have of God, but we shall only inquire briefly into what the philosophers can tell us about it. Now, these have defined God as *a being existing through or of himself, the cause of all things, omniscient, almighty, eternal, simple, infinite, the highest good, of infinite compassion, etc.* But before we approach this inquiry, let us just see what admissions they make to us.

<summary>This chapter examines the common but incorrect attributes assigned to God by philosophers, distinguishing between God's actual attributes—infinite substances like thought and extension—and the mere modes often mistaken for attributes.</summary>
<keywords>God, attributes, philosophers, infinite substances, essence, thought, extension, modes, omniscient, omnipresent</keywords>

--- Page 195 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of traditional philosophical definitions of God, arguing that they rely on external properties (propria) rather than essential attributes and fail to provide a true definition a priori.</meta>

<header>ATTRIBUTES NOT PERTAINING TO GOD</header>

<margin>1</margin>In the first place, they say that it is impossible to give a true or correct definition of God because, according to their opinion, there can be no definition except *per genus et differentiam* (by genus and difference); and as God is not a species of any genus, he cannot be defined correctly, or according to the rules.

In the second place, they say that God cannot be defined because a definition must describe the thing itself positively; while, according to their standpoint, our <margin>10</margin>knowledge of God cannot be of a positive but only of a negative kind; therefore, no proper definition can be given of God.

They also say, furthermore, that God can never be proved *a priori* because he has no cause, but only by way of probability or from his effects.

Since by these assertions of theirs they admit sufficiently that their knowledge of God is very limited and slight, let us now proceed to examine their definition.

In the first place, we do not see that they provide in it any <margin>20</margin>*attribute* or attributes through which it can be known what the thing (God) is,‡ but only some *propria* or properties which do, indeed, belong to a thing, but never explain what the thing is. For although *self-subsisting, being the cause of all things, highest good, eternal and immutable*, etc., are unique to God alone, we nevertheless cannot know from those properties what that being is to whom these properties pertain, or what attributes he has.

It is now also time for us to consider the things which they ascribe to God, but which do not, however, pertain to <margin>30</margin>him,† such as being omniscient, merciful, wise, and so forth. These things, since they are only certain modes of the thinking thing, can by no means exist or be understood without

---

† That is to say, when he is considered as all that he is, or with regard to all his attributes; *see page 52, note, on this point.*

‡ B: through which the thing (namely God) can be known.

He asserts that characteristics like "merciful" or "wise" are merely modes of a thinking thing rather than fundamental divine attributes.</summary>
<keywords>definition of God, per genus et differentiam, a priori, divine attributes, propria, thinking thing, Spinoza, essence vs properties</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the critique of traditional theological definitions of God, specifically challenging the idea that God can be defined through the logical categories of genus and difference.</meta>

<header>GOD, MAN, AND HIS WELL-BEING</header>

<margin>1</margin>Consequently, the substances ‡ whose modes ‡‡ they are also cannot be attributed to him, who is a *Being existing without the help of anything, and solely through himself*.

Lastly, they call him the highest good; but if they understand by this something different from what they have already said—namely, that God is immutable and a cause of all things—then they have become entangled in their own thought or are unable to understand themselves. This is the outcome <margin>10</margin>of their misconception of good and evil, for they believe that man himself, and not God, is the cause of his sins and wickedness—which, according to what we have already proved, cannot be the case; otherwise, we would be compelled to assert that man is also the cause of himself. However, this will appear even more evident when we come to consider the human will.

It is necessary that we should now unravel their specious arguments with which they seek to excuse their ignorance in Theology.

<margin>20</margin>First of all, then, they say that a correct definition must consist of a “*genus*” and “*differentia*.” Now, although all the Logicians admit this, I do not know where they get it from. And, to be sure, if this must be true, then we can know nothing whatever. For if it is through a definition consisting of *genus* and *differentia* that we can first come to know a thing perfectly, then we can never know perfectly the highest *genus*, which has no *genus* above it. Now then: If the highest *genus*, which is the cause of our knowledge of <margin>30</margin>all other things, is not known, then much less can the other things be understood or known which are explained by that *genus*. However, since we are free and do not consider ourselves in any way tied to their assertions, we shall, in accordance with true logic, propose other rules.

->‡ B: substance.<-
->‡‡ A: essences (*beings*); B: modes (*manners*).<-

<summary>The author argues that God, as a self-subsisting being, cannot be defined by substances or modes, and critiques the traditional logical requirement of defining things by genus and difference. He points out that if such a requirement were true, the "highest genus" would be unknowable, rendering all subsequent knowledge impossible.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical critique of traditional theological definitions, focusing on the distinction between attributes (substances) and modes, and defending the possibility of knowing and proving God's existence.</meta>

<page-num>55</page-num>
<header>ATTRIBUTES NOT PERTAINING TO GOD</header>

<margin>1</margin>of definition—namely, on the lines of our division of Nature.

Now we have already seen that the attributes (or, as others call them, substances) are things, or—to express ourselves better and more aptly—<note>constitute</note> a being which subsists through itself, and therefore makes itself known and reveals itself through itself.

As to the other things, we see that they are but modes of the attributes, without which they can neither be nor <margin>10</margin>be understood. Consequently, definitions must be of two kinds (or sorts):

1. The first, namely, are those of attributes, which pertain to a self-subsisting being; these need no <term>genus</term>, or anything else through which they might be better understood or explained. For, since they exist as attributes of a self-subsisting being, they also become known through themselves.

2. The second <note>kind of definitions</note> are those <note>of things</note> which do not exist through themselves, but only through <margin>20</margin>the attributes whose modes they are, and through which—as their <term>genus</term>—they must be understood.

And this is <note>all that need be said</note> concerning their statement about definitions. As regards the other <note>assertion</note>—namely, that God cannot be known by us adequately—this has been sufficiently answered by <note>Descartes</note> in his answers to the objections relating to these things, page 18.

And the third <note>assertion</note>—namely, that God cannot be proved <term>a priori</term>—has also already been answered by us. Since God is the cause of himself, it is enough that we prove <margin>30</margin>him through himself, and such a proof is also much more conclusive than the <term>a posteriori</term> proof, which generally rests only on external causes.

<summary>The author distinguishes between self-subsisting attributes, which are known through themselves, and modes, which must be defined through attributes. He also defends the adequacy of our knowledge of God and the validity of proving God's existence a priori.</summary>

# CHAPTER VIII

## ON NATURA NATURANS

Before we proceed to anything else, we shall briefly divide the whole of Nature—namely, into *Natura naturans* and *Natura naturata*. By *Natura naturans*, we understand a being that we conceive clearly and distinctly through itself, and without needing anything besides itself (like all the attributes which we have so far described), that is, God. The Thomists likewise understand God by it, but their <margin>10</margin> *Natura naturans* was a being (so they called it) beyond all substances.

We shall divide *Natura naturata* into two: a general and a particular. The *general* consists of all the modes which depend immediately on God, which we shall treat in the following chapter; the *particular* consists of all the particular things which are produced by the general mode. Thus, *Natura naturata* requires some substance ‡ in order to be well understood.

---

# CHAPTER IX
## ON NATURA NATURATA

<margin>1</margin>
Now, regarding the general *Natura naturata*, or the modes or creations which depend on or have been created by God immediately, we know of no more than two: namely, motion in matter,$\dagger$ and the *understanding* in the thinking thing. These, then, we say, have existed from all eternity, and for all eternity will remain immutable. This is truly a work as great as befits the greatness of the master-craftsman. <margin>10</margin>

Everything that specifically concerns *Motion*—such as the fact *that it has existed from all eternity, and for all eternity will remain immutable; that it is infinite in its kind; and that it can neither exist nor be understood through itself, but only by means of Extension*—all this, I say, because it [Motion] more properly belongs to a treatise on Natural Science rather than here,$\ddagger$ we shall not consider in this place. We shall only say this about it: that it is *a Son, Product, or Effect* created immediately by God.

# CHAPTER X

## WHAT GOOD AND EVIL ARE

In order to explain briefly what good and evil are in themselves, we shall begin this way:
Some things are in our understanding and not <note>B: not such.</note> in Nature, and so they are also only our own creation; their purpose is to understand things distinctly. Among these, we include all relations which refer to different things, and these we call *Entia Rationis* [beings of reason]. Now the question is whether good and evil belong to the *Entia* <margin>10</margin> *Rationis* or to the *Entia Realia* [real beings]. But since good and evil are only relations, it is beyond doubt that they must be placed among the *Entia Rationis*; for we never say that something is good except with reference to something else which is not as good, or is not as useful to us as some other thing. Thus, we say that a man is bad only in comparison with one who is better, or also that an apple is bad in comparison with another which is good or better.
All this could not possibly be said if that which is better <margin>20</margin> or good (in comparison with which the "bad" is named) did not exist.
Therefore, when we say that something is good, we only mean that it conforms well to the general Idea which we have of such things. But, <note>A: "And therefore"; B: "Nevertheless."</note> as we have already said before, the things must agree with their particular Ideas—whose essence must be a perfect essence—and not with general <insert>Ideas</insert>, since in that case they would not exist.
As for confirming what we have just said, the matter is clear

<summary>The author defines good and evil as "beings of reason" (*Entia Rationis*) rather than real entities in nature, existing only as comparative relations in the human mind. Things are labeled good or bad based on how they conform to general ideas or how they compare to other existing things.</summary>
<keywords>Good and Evil, Entia Rationis, Beings of Reason, Entia Realia, Ideas, Relations, Comparison, Understanding, Nature</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the argument from the previous chapter, concluding the demonstration that good and evil are mental constructs rather than inherent properties of natural objects or actions.</meta>

<header>GOD, MAN, AND HIS WELL-BEING</header>

<margin>1</margin> to us; but still, to conclude our remarks, we will add the following proofs:

All things that are in Nature are either things or actions. Now, good and evil are neither things nor actions. Therefore, good and evil do not exist in Nature.

For if good and evil were things or actions, then they would have to have their own definitions. But good and evil (as, for example, the goodness of Peter and the wickedness of Judas) have no definitions apart from the essence of Judas <margin>10</margin> or Peter, because this essence alone exists in Nature, and they cannot be defined without their essence. Therefore, as stated above, it follows that good and evil are not things or actions that exist in Nature.

## * PREFACE *

HAVING discussed God and universal and infinite things in the first part, we shall now proceed in the second part to the treatment of particular and finite things; though not of all, since they are innumerable, but we shall only treat those which concern man; and, in the first place, we shall consider here what man is, insofar as he consists of certain modes (contained in the two attributes which we have noted in God). I say "certain modes," <margin>10</margin>for I by no means think that man, insofar as he consists of spirit, soul,† or body, is a substance. Because, already at the

† 1. Our soul is either a *substance* or a *mode*; it is not a substance, because we have already shown that there can be no finite substance; it is therefore a *mode*.

2. Being a mode, then, it must be such either of "substantial" extension or of "substantial" *thought*; not of extension, because, etc.; therefore of *thought*.

3. "*Substantial*" *Thought*, since it cannot be finite, is infinitely perfect in its kind, and an attribute of God.

4. Perfect thought must have a *Knowledge*, *Idea*, or mode of <margin>20</margin>thought of all and everything that is *real*, of substances as well as of modes, without exception.

5. 6. This Knowledge, Idea, etc., of each particular thing which happens to be real is, we say, the soul of this particular thing.

7. All and various particular things that are real have become <margin>30</margin>so through motion and rest, and this is true of all the modes of "substantial" extension which we call bodies.

<margin>1</margin> At the beginning of this book, we proved (1) that no substance can have a beginning; (2) that one substance cannot produce another; and lastly (3), that there cannot be two like substances.

As man has not existed from eternity, is finite, and is like many other men, he can be no substance; so that all that he has of thought are only modes of the attribute of thought which we have attributed to God. And, again, all that he has of form, motion, and other things, are likewise [modes] of the <margin>10</margin> *other attribute which is attributed *by us* to God.*

And although from this—[namely,] that the nature of man can neither be, nor be understood without the attributes which we ourselves admit to constitute substance—some try to prove that man is a substance, yet this has no other ground than false suppositions. 10. # CHAPTER I

## ON OPINION, BELIEF, AND KNOWLEDGE

<margin>1</margin>
To begin our consideration of the modes † of which man consists, we shall state: (1) what they are, (2) their effects, and (3) their cause.

Regarding the first, let us begin with those that are first known to us: namely, *certain ideas or the consciousness of the knowledge of ourselves, and of the things that are outside of us.*

<margin>10</margin>
Now we acquire these ideas †† (1) either merely through belief (which belief arises either from experience or from hearsay), (2) or, in the second place, we acquire them by way of a true belief, (3) or, thirdly, we have them as the result of clear and distinct conception.

The first is commonly subject to error.

The second and third, however, although they differ from one another, cannot err.

<margin>20</margin>
To make all this somewhat clearer and more intelligible, we shall give the following illustration taken from the Rule of Three.

Someone ††† has just heard it said that if, in the Rule of Three, the second number is multiplied by the third, and then divided by the first, a fourth number will then be obtained which has the same relation to the third as the

---

<margin>30</margin>
††† This person merely forms an opinion or, as is commonly said, believes through hearsay only. <note>B omits this note.</note>

<margin>1</margin>second has to the first. And despite the possibility that the person who presented this to him might have been lying, he still made his calculations accordingly. He did so without having acquired any more knowledge of the Rule of Three than a blind man has of color, so that whatever he may have said about it, he simply repeated it as a parrot repeats what it has been taught.

Another,† having a more active intelligence, is not so easily satisfied with mere hearsay, but tests it by some <margin>10</margin>actual calculations; and when he finds that they agree with it, then he gives credence to it. But we have rightly said that this one is also subject to error; for how can he possibly be sure that his experience of a few particulars can serve him as a rule for all?

A third,†† who is not satisfied with hearsay, because it may deceive, nor with experience of a few particulars, because this cannot possibly serve as a rule, examines it in the light of true Reason, which, when properly applied, has never deceived. This then tells him that on account of <margin>20</margin>the nature of the proportion in these numbers it had to be so, and could not happen otherwise.

A fourth,††† however, having the clearest knowledge of all, has no need of hearsay, or experience, or the art of reasoning, because by his penetration he sees the proportion in ‡ all such calculations immediately.‡‡

# CHAPTER II

## WHAT OPINION, BELIEF, AND CLEAR KNOWLEDGE ARE

WE come now to the consideration of the effects of the
different levels of knowledge, of which we spoke in the
preceding chapter, and, in passing as it were, we shall
explain <insert>Pencil circle around: what Opinion, Belief, and clear Knowledge are.</insert>

The first type, then, we call *Opinion*,
the second *Belief*, but the third is what we call clear
*Knowledge*.‡ <margin>10</margin>

<margin>Pencil bracket extends from line 11 to line 20</margin>
We call it *Opinion* because it is subject to error and has
no place when we are sure of anything, but only in those
cases when we are said to guess and to surmise. The second
we call *Belief*, because the things we grasp only with
our reason are not seen by us, but are only known to us
through the conviction of our understanding that it must
be so and not otherwise. But we call that *clear Knowledge*
which comes, not from our being convinced by reasons,
but from our feeling and enjoying the thing itself, and it
surpasses the others by far. <margin>20</margin>

After these preliminary remarks let us now turn to their
effects. Of these we say this: namely, that from the first
proceed all the “passions” which are opposed to good
reason; from the second, the good desires; and from the
third, true and sincere Love, with all its offshoots.

We thus maintain that Knowledge is the direct cause
of all the “passions” in the soul. For we consider it once
for all impossible that anyone, who neither thinks nor knows
in any of the preceding ways and modes, should be
capable of being incited to Love or Desire or any other <margin>30</margin>
mode of Will.

->‡ B omits this sentence.<-

<summary>This chapter defines the three grades of knowledge—Opinion, Belief, and clear Knowledge—and establishes them as the direct causes of human passions, desires, and love. Clear knowledge is distinguished as the highest form because it arises from direct experience and enjoyment of a thing rather than mere reason or surmise.</summary>

# CHAPTER III

## THE ORIGIN OF PASSION. PASSION DUE TO OPINION

<margin>5</margin>HERE, then, let us see how, as we have said, the passions derive their origin from opinion. To do this well and intelligently, we shall take some specific ones and prove what we say by using these as illustrations.

Let *Surprise*, then, be the first. This is found in one who knows a thing according to the first manner <note>of Knowledge</note>; ‡ for, <margin>10</margin>since from a few particulars he draws a conclusion which is general, he is surprised whenever he sees anything that goes against his conclusion; † like one who, having never seen any sheep except with short tails, is surprised at the sheep from Morocco which have long ones. So it is

‡ A refers to the following note already here; B refers to it at the next semicolon.

<margin>15</margin>† This should on no account be taken to mean that a formal inference must always precede astonishment; on the contrary, it exists also without that, namely, when we tacitly believe that a thing <margin>20</margin>is <note>always</note> so, and not different from what we are accustomed to see, hear, or think about it, etc. For example, Aristotle says, *a dog is a barking animal*, therefore he concludes, whatever barks is a dog; but when a peasant says *a dog*, he means tacitly just the same as Aristotle did with his definition. So that when the peasant <margin>25</margin>hears the barking he says, *a dog*; and so, if they had heard some other kind of animal bark, the peasant, who had drawn no <note>explicit</note> inference, would be just as astonished as Aristotle, who had drawn an inference. Furthermore, when we become aware of something about which we had never thought before, it is not really something the <margin>30</margin>like of which, whether as a whole or in part, we have not known before; only it is not structured in the same way in all respects, or we have never been affected by it in the same way, etc.

<margin>1</margin>It is told of a peasant that he had convinced himself that no other fields existed beyond his own; but when he happened to lose a cow and was forced to go and search for her far away, he was amazed at the vast number of fields that existed beyond his few acres. And, certainly, this must also be the case with many philosophers who have convinced themselves that no more worlds exist beyond this field or little globe on which they live (simply because they have seen no others). But surprise is never felt by one <margin>10</margin>who draws correct conclusions. This is the first passion.

The second is *Love*.‡ Since this arises either from true ideas, from opinion, or, finally, from hearsay alone, we shall first see how it arises from opinion, and then how it arises from true ideas; for the former leads to our ruin, while the latter leads to our supreme happiness; and then we shall see how it arises from hearsay.

‡ The content of the next three paragraphs is presented in the following simpler order in Manuscript *B*:
The second is Love. This arises either, 1, from hearsay, or <margin>20</margin>2, from opinion, or 3, from true ideas.

Regarding the first, we generally observe it in the attitude of children toward their father; because their father tells them that this or that is good, they are inclined toward it without knowing anything more about it. We see it also in those who, out of love, give their lives for their country, and also in those who fall in love with something based only on what they have heard about it.

Regarding the second, it is certain that whenever anyone sees, or thinks they see, something good, they are always inclined to unite themselves with it; and, for the sake of the good they perceive <margin>30</margin>therein, they choose it as the best, beyond which they then know nothing better or more pleasant. Yet if it ever happens (as it usually does in these matters) that they discover something better than the good currently known to them, then their love immediately shifts from the first thing to the second. We shall demonstrate all of this more clearly when we discuss the freedom of man.

Regarding love from true ideas, as this is not the place to discuss it, we shall skip it for the present. <note>See note † on page 72.</note>
