We generally observe this in the attitude of children toward their father: because their father tells them that this or that is good, they are inclined toward it without knowing anything more about it. We see it also in those who, out of love, give their lives for their homeland, and also in those who fall in love with something based only on what they have heard about it.

<margin>20, 30</margin>
Next, Hatred—the exact opposite of love—arises from error which results from opinion. For when someone has concluded that a certain thing is good, and another person happens to do something to the detriment of that thing, then a hatred arises in them against the one who did it; and this, as we shall explain afterward, could never happen if the true good were known. For, in comparison with the true good, everything that exists or is conceived is nothing but wretchedness itself; and is not such a lover of what is wretched much more deserving of pity than of hatred?

Finally, hatred also comes from mere hearsay, as we see in the Turks against Jews and Christians, and in the Jews

## CHAPTER IV

### WHAT COMES FROM BELIEF; AND ON THE GOOD AND EVIL OF MAN

<margin>1</margin> Since we have shown in the previous chapter how the Passions arise from the error of Opinion, let us now see here the effects of the two other modes of Knowing. And first of all, [the effect] of what we have called True Belief.†

<margin>10</margin> This shows us, indeed, what a thing ought to be, but not what it really is. And this is the reason why it can never unite us with the object of our belief. I say, then, that it only teaches us what the thing ought to be, and not what it is; between these two there is a great difference. For, as we remarked regarding the example taken from the rule of three, when anyone can, by the aid of proportion, find a fourth number that shall be related to the third as the second is to the first, then (having used division and multiplication) they can say that the four numbers must be proportional;

---

<note>
† Belief is a strong proof based on Reasons, by which I am con-
<margin>20</margin> vinced in my mind that the thing is really, and exactly as it is, outside my understanding, just as I am convinced in my mind that it is. I say, *a strong proof based on Reasons*, in order to distinguish it both from Opinion, which is always doubtful and liable to error, and from Knowledge, which does not consist in being convinced by Reasons, but in an immediate union with the thing itself. I say, *that the thing is really and exactly as it is outside my understanding—really*, because reasons cannot deceive me in this, for otherwise they would not be different from opinion. *Exactly as it is*, for it can only tell me what the thing ought to be, and not what it really is, otherwise
<margin>30</margin> it would not be different from Knowing. *Outside*, for it makes us enjoy intellectually not what is in us, but what is outside us.
</note>

<summary>This page defines True Belief as a conviction based on reason that identifies how things should be, distinguishing it from both uncertain Opinion and the direct, intuitive union of Knowledge. It uses a mathematical analogy to show that while Belief provides logical certainty about external objects, it does not achieve the same unity with the object as true Knowledge does.</summary>

But when he comes to see the proportion in the way that we have shown in the fourth ‡ example, then he says with truth that the thing is so, because then it is within him and not beyond him. *Let* *this* *suffice* as regards the first [effect].

The second effect of true belief is that it leads us to a clearer understanding, through which we love God, and thus it makes us intellectually aware of the things which are not <margin>10</margin>within us, but outside us.

The third effect is that it gives us the knowledge of good and evil, and shows us all the passions which should be suppressed. And as we have already said that the passions which come from opinion are liable to great evil, it is worth the effort to see how these also are sifted out by this second kind of knowledge, so that we may see what is good and what is bad in them.

To do so conveniently, let us, using the same method as before, look at them closely, so that we may know through <margin>20</margin>it which of them should be chosen and which rejected.

But, before proceeding to this, let us first state briefly what constitutes the good and evil of man.

We have already said before that all things are necessarily what they are, and that *in Nature there is no good and no evil*. So that whatever we want man to be * [in this respect] * must refer to his kind, which is nothing else than a thing of *Reason*. And when we have conceived in our mind an Idea of a perfect man, it should make us look (when we examine <margin>30</margin>ourselves) to see whether we have any means of attaining such perfection.

Hence, then, whatever advances us towards perfection, we call good, and, on the contrary, what hinders, or also what does not advance us toward it, we call bad.

I must therefore, I say, conceive a perfect man, if I want

<margin>1</margin> to assert anything concerning the good and evil of man, because if I were to consider the good and evil *of some individual man,* say, *for example*, of Adam, I would be confusing a real thing (*ens reale*) with a thing of Reason (*ens Rationis*), which must be most scrupulously avoided by an upright *Philosopher*, for reasons which we shall state in what follows, or on another occasion. Furthermore, since the destiny of Adam, or of any other individual creature, is not known to us except through the outcome, so *it follows* that what we <margin>10</margin> can say even of the destiny of man must be based on the idea which our understanding forms of a perfect man,† which destiny, since it is a thing of Reason, we may well know; so also, as already noted, are good and evil, which are only modes of thinking.

To come gradually to the point: We have already pointed out before how the movement, passions, and activities of the soul arise from ideas, and these ideas we have divided into four kinds, namely: [according to whether they are based on] mere hearsay, experience, belief, or clear knowledge. And from <margin>20</margin> what we have now seen of the effects of all these, it is evident that the fourth, namely, clear knowledge, is the most perfect of all. For opinion often leads us into error. True belief is good only because it is the way to true knowledge, and awakens us to things which are really lovable. So that the final end that we seek, and the highest that we know, is true knowledge. But even this true knowledge varies with the objects that come before it: the better the object is with which it happens to unite itself, the better also is this knowledge. And, for this reason, he is the most perfect <margin>30</margin> man who is united with God (who is the most perfect being of all), and so enjoys Him.

† For from no individual creature can one derive an Idea that is perfect; for the perfection of this object itself—[that is,] whether it is really perfect or not—cannot be deduced except from a general perfect Idea, or *Ens Rationis*.

<margin>1</margin>## CHAPTER V
### ON LOVE

LOVE, which is nothing more than the enjoyment of a thing and union with it, we shall divide according to the qualities of its object; that is, the object which man seeks to enjoy and to unite himself with.

Now some objects are transient in themselves; others, indeed, are not transient because of their cause. There is yet a third that is eternal and imperishable through its own <margin>10</margin>power and might.

The transient are all the particular things which did not exist from all time, or <note>B: but</note> have had a beginning.

The others are all those modes <note>B: the general modes</note> which we have stated to be the cause of the particular modes.

But the third is God, or, what we regard as one and the same, *Truth*.

Love, then, arises from the idea and knowledge that we have of a thing; and according as the thing shows itself to be greater and more glorious, so also is our love greater.

<margin>20</margin>There are two ways it is possible to free ourselves from love: either by getting to know something better, or by discovering that the loved object, which we hold to be something great and glorious, brings much woe and disaster in its train.

It is also characteristic of love that we never think of freeing ourselves from it (as we do from surprise and other passions); and this for the following two reasons: (1) because it is impossible, (2) because it is necessary that we should not be released from it.

---

<margin>30</margin><note>B: but</note> <note>B: the general modes</note>

<summary>The author defines love as the enjoyment of and union with an object, categorizing these objects as transient things, causal modes, or the eternal God. He explains that love stems from knowledge and notes that, unlike other passions, we do not naturally seek to be freed from it.</summary>
<keywords>Love, God, Truth, modes, transient objects, union, knowledge, passions</keywords>

<page-num>79</page-num>

### ON LOVE

It is impossible because it does not depend on us, but <margin>1</margin>
only on the good and useful qualities that we discern in the object;
it would be necessary for these never to have become known
to us if we were not to—*or should not*—love it; and this is
not a matter of our free choice, or dependent on us, for
if we knew nothing, it is certain that we would also be
nothing.

It is necessary that we are not released from it,
because, owing to the weakness of our nature, we could not
exist without enjoying something with which we become <margin>10</margin>
united, and from which we draw strength.

Now, which of these three kinds of objects should we
choose or reject?

As regards the *transient* (since, as noted, we must,
owing to the weakness of our nature, necessarily love
something and become united with it in order to exist), it
is certain that our nature is in no way strengthened
through our loving and becoming united with them, ‡
for they are weak themselves, and one cripple cannot
carry another. And not only do they not help us progress, but <margin>20</margin>
they are even harmful to us. For we have said that *love is
a union with the object which our understanding judges to be
good and glorious*; and by this we mean such a union
whereby both the lover ‡‡ and what is loved become one
and the same thing, or together constitute a single whole. He,
therefore, is indeed always wretched who is united to
transient things. For, since these are beyond his power
and subject to many accidents, it is impossible that, when
they are affected, he should be free from these <term>affects</term>. And,
consequently, we conclude: If those who love transient <margin>30</margin>
things that have some measure of reality are so wretched,
how wretched must they be who love honor, riches, and
pleasures, which have no reality at all!

<margin>1</margin>
Let this be enough to show us how Reason teaches us to avoid such fleeting things. For what we have just said shows us clearly the poison and the evil that lurk concealed in the love of these things. But we see this even more clearly when we observe what a glorious and excellent good we are kept away from through the enjoyment of these things.

<margin>10</margin>
We said before that the things which are transient are beyond our power. *But* let us be clearly understood; we do not mean to say that we are a free cause depending upon nothing else. We only mean that when we say some things are in our power and others are beyond it, we mean by those in our power such things as we can produce through the order of, or together with, Nature, of which we are a part. By those which are not in our power, we mean such things as—being outside us—are not subject to any change through us, because they are very far removed from our real essence as it is fashioned by Nature.

<margin>20</margin>
To proceed, we come now to the second kind of objects, which though eternal and imperishable, are not so through their own power.‡ However, if we conduct a brief inquiry here, we become immediately aware that these are only mere modes which depend immediately on God. And since their nature is such, they cannot be conceived by us unless we, at the same time, have a conception of God. In Him, since He is perfect, our Love must necessarily rest. And, to express it in a word, if we use our understanding rightly, it will be impossible for us not to love God.

<margin>30</margin>
The reasons why are clear. *First of all*, because we find that God alone possesses essence, and all other things are not essences but modes. And since the modes cannot be rightly understood without the entity on which they immediately depend; and [as] we have already shown before

<header>ON LOVE</header>

<margin>1</margin>That if, while loving something, we come to know a better thing than what we then love, we always prefer it immediately and forsake the first; it follows, therefore, incontrovertibly that when we come to know God, who has all perfection in himself, we must necessarily love him.

Secondly, if we use our understanding well in acquiring a knowledge of things, then we must know them in <note>relation to</note> their causes. Now then, since God is a first cause of all other things, therefore, from the nature of the case 
<margin>10</margin>(<term>*ex rerum natura*</term>), the knowledge of God is, and remains, prior to the knowledge of all other things: because the knowledge of all other things must follow from the knowledge of the first cause. And true love always results from the knowledge that a thing is glorious and good. What else, then, can follow but that it can be lavished upon no one more ardently than upon the Lord our God? For he alone is glorious and a perfect good.

So we see now how we can make love strong, and also how it must rest only in God.

<margin>20</margin>Whatever more we still have to say about love, we shall remember to say ‡ it when we consider the last kind of knowledge. In what follows here, we shall inquire, as we promised before, into which of the passions we are to entertain and which we are to reject.

<page-num>82</page-num>

<margin>I</margin>
# CHAPTER VI
## ON HATRED

HATRED is an inclination to keep away from us that which has caused us some harm.‡ Now it is to be noted that we perform our actions in two ways, namely, either with or without passion. We act with passion, as is commonly seen in the conduct of masters toward their servants who have done something wrong. We act without passion, as is told of Socrates, who, when he was compelled to punish his slave for the <margin>10</margin> slave’s own good, never did so when he felt that he was enraged against his slave.

Now that we see that our actions are performed by us either with or without passion, we think it is clear that those things which hinder or have hindered us can be removed, when necessary, without any disturbance on our part. And so, which is better: that we should flee from these things with aversion and hatred, or that, with the strength of reason, we should (for we think it possible) endure them without losing our temper? First of all, it is <margin>20</margin> certain that when we do what we have to do without passion, then no evil can result from it. And, since there is no middle ground between good and evil, we see that, just as it is bad to do anything in a passion, so it must be good to act without it.

But let us examine whether there is any harm in fleeing from things with hatred and aversion.

As for the hatred which comes from opinion, it is certain that it should have no place in us, because we know that one and the same thing is good for us at one time, and bad 

---

<margin>30</margin> <note>B: obstacle or hindrance.</note>

Using Socrates as an example, the author suggests that acting without passion is inherently good and avoids the harms associated with hatred.</summary>

<keywords>hatred, passion, Socrates, reason, ethics, good and evil, aversion, emotional control</keywords>

<header>ON HATRED</header>

<margin>1</margin>for us at another time, as is always the case with medicinal herbs.

Ultimately, it depends on whether the hatred arises in us only through opinion, and not also through true reasoning. But to ascertain this properly, we think it right to explain clearly what hatred is, and to distinguish it from aversion.

Now I say that Hatred is a disturbance of the soul against someone who has done some harm to us willingly and <margin>10</margin>knowingly. But aversion is the disturbance which arises in us against a thing on account of some weakness or injury which we either know or think is in it by nature. I say, by nature; for when we do not suppose *or think* that it is so, then, even if we have suffered some hindrance or injury from it, we have no aversion for it, because we may, on the contrary, expect something useful from it. Thus, when someone is hurt by a stone or a knife, he does not on that account feel any aversion for it.

After these observations, let us now briefly consider the consequences of both of them. From hatred, sorrow <margin>20</margin>follows; and when the hatred is great, it produces anger, which not only, like hatred, seeks to flee from what is hated, but also to annihilate it, when that is possible; from this great hatred also comes envy. But from aversion there comes a certain sorrow, because we consider ourselves to be deprived of something which, since it is real, must always have its essence and perfection.

From what has just been said, it may be easily understood that, if we use our Reason correctly, we can feel no hatred or aversion for anything, because, if we do, we deprive our-<margin>30</margin>selves of that perfection which is to be found in everything.‡

---

‡ <note>B continues: “while, on the contrary, if we want anything we must contrive to improve whatever we want from nature, whether for our own sake, or for the sake of the thing itself”—and omits the next sentence.</note>

<vocab>Hatred, aversion, disturbance, soul, Reason, perfection, anger, envy, medicinal herbs</vocab>

<summary>The author distinguishes between hatred, caused by intentional harm, and aversion, caused by perceived natural defects, noting that both lead to sorrow and anger. He concludes that true reason prevents both passions by recognizing the inherent perfection in all things.</summary>

<header>GOD, MAN, AND HIS WELL-BEING</header>

<margin>1</margin> We also see with our Reason that we can never <note>reasonably</note> feel any hatred whatever toward anyone, because for whatever exists in Nature, if we have any desire concerning it, then we must always seek to improve it, whether for our own sake or for the sake of the thing itself. And since a perfect man is the best thing *for us* that we know of among everything we have around us or before our eyes, it is by far the best—both for us and for all people individually—that we should at all times seek to educate them toward this perfect state. For only then can we
<margin>10</margin> reap the greatest benefit from them, and they from us. The way to achieve this is to always show regard for them in the manner in which we are constantly taught and urged to do by our good Conscience; for this never leads us to our ruin, but always toward our happiness *and well-being.*

In conclusion, we say that Hatred and Aversion contain as many imperfections as Love, by contrast, contains perfections. For Love always produces improvement, strengthening, and expansion, which constitute
<margin>20</margin> perfection; while Hatred, on the contrary, always leads to desolation, weakening, and annihilation, which constitute imperfection itself.

## CHAPTER VII

### ON JOY AND SORROW ‡

<margin>1</margin>HAVING seen that Hatred and Surprise ‡‡ are such that we may freely say they can have no place in those who use their understanding as they should, we shall now proceed in the same manner to speak of the other passions. To begin with, Desire and Joy shall come first. Since these arise from the same causes from which love follows, we shall only say regarding them that we must remember and call to mind what we said then; and with <margin>10</margin> this, we leave the subject.

We turn next to Sorrow, of which we may say that it arises only from opinion and the imagination *which follows* from it: for it comes from the loss of some good.

Now we have already remarked above, that whatever we do should tend toward progress and improvement. But it is certain that as long as we are sorrowing, we make ourselves unfit to act in this way; for this reason, it is necessary that we should free ourselves from it. This we can do by thinking of the means by which we may recover what we <margin>20</margin> have lost, if it is in our power to do so. If not, [we must reflect] that it is just as necessary to put an end to it, ‡‡‡ lest we fall prey to all the miseries *and disasters* which sorrow necessarily brings in its train. And either course *must be adopted* with joy; for it is foolish to try to restore and make up for a lost good by means of a self-sought and provoked evil.

# CHAPTER VIII

## ON ESTEEM AND CONTEMPT, etc. <note>B enumerates all the topics in the heading of this and the following chapters.</note>

WE shall now proceed to speak of Esteem and Contempt, of Self-respect and Humility, of Conceit and Culpable Humility. We shall take them in the order mentioned above, and try to distinguish accurately what is good and what is bad in them.

Esteem and Contempt are felt insofar as we know a thing to be something great or small, whether this great or little thing is within us or outside us. <note>B begins this chapter as follows: In order to distinguish thoroughly the good and evil in these Passions, we shall take them up in turn, beginning with Esteem and Contempt, which refer to something known that is in or outside us; the first relating to something great, the last to something small.</note>

Self-respect does not extend to anything outside us,
<margin>10</margin>and is only attributed to one who knows the real worth of his perfection, dispassionately and without seeking esteem for himself.

Humility is felt when anyone knows his own imperfection, without regard to the contempt of others for himself; <note>B: without any self-contempt.</note> so that Humility does not refer to anything outside the humble man.

Conceit is this: when someone attributes to himself a perfection which is not to be found in him.

Culpable humility is this: when someone attributes to
<margin>20</margin>himself an imperfection which he does not have. I am not speaking of those hypocrites who, without meaning it,

# CHAPTER IX

->ON HOPE AND FEAR, &c.<-

We will now begin to speak of Hope and Fear, of Confidence, Despair, and Vacillation, of Courage, Boldness and Emulation, of Pusillanimity and Timidity, *and lastly of Jealousy,* and, as is our habit, we will take them one by one and then indicate which of these can hinder us and which can benefit us. We will be able to do all this very easily if only we attend closely to the thoughts that we can <margin>10</margin> have about a thing that is yet to come, whether it is good or bad.
*The ideas which we have about things refer either:*
        *1. To the things themselves; or,*
        *2. To the person who has the ideas.*
The ideas that we have regarding the thing itself are these: either the thing is regarded by us as accidental—that is, as something which may or may not happen—or [we think] that it necessarily must happen. So much as regards <margin>20</margin> the thing itself.
Next, as regards the person who thinks about the thing, the case is this: he must do something either to promote the thing or to prevent it. Now, from these thoughts all these passions result as follows: when we think that a certain thing which is yet to come is good and that it can happen, the soul assumes, in consequence of this, that form which we call *hope*, which is nothing else than a certain kind of joy, though mingled with some sorrow. <margin>30</margin> And, on the other hand, if we judge that that which may

But when we think that the thing is bad, and that it necessarily must come, then *despair* enters into the soul; <margin>10</margin>which is nothing else than a certain kind of sorrow.

So far we have spoken of the passions considered in this chapter and given positive definitions of them, and have thus stated what each of them is; we may now proceed in a reverse manner and define them negatively. We *hope* that the evil may not come, we *fear* lest the good should not come, we are *confident* that the evil will not come, we *despair* because the good will not come.

Having said this much about the passions insofar as they arise from our thoughts concerning the thing itself, <margin>20</margin>we have now to speak of those which arise from the thoughts relating to the person who thinks about the thing; namely:

If something must be done in order to bring the thing about, and we come to no decision concerning it, then the soul receives that form which we call *vacillation*. But when it makes a firm resolve to produce the thing, and this can be brought about, then that is called *courage*; and if the thing is difficult to achieve, then that is called *intrepidity* or *bravery*.

<margin>30</margin>When, however, someone decides to do a thing because another (who had done it first) has met with success, then we call it *emulation*. \*Lastly,\*

If anyone knows what he must decide to do in order to promote a good thing and to hinder a bad one, and yet does not do so, then we call it *pusillanimity*; and when the

## CHAPTER X

### ON REMORSE AND REPENTANCE

On the present occasion we shall speak, though briefly, about *remorse* and *repentance*. These never arise except as the result of rashness; because *remorse* comes only from this: that we do something about which we are then in doubt whether it is good or whether it is bad; and *repentance* comes from this: that we have done something which is bad.

<margin>10</margin>
And since many people (who use their understanding rightly) sometimes (because they lack that habitual readiness which is required so that the understanding may be used rightly at all times) go astray, it might perhaps be thought that such Remorse and Repentance might soon set them right again, and from this it might be concluded, as the whole world does conclude, that they are good.‡ If, however, we gain a proper insight into them, we shall find that they are not only not good, but that they are, on the contrary, harmful, and that they are consequently bad. For
<margin>20</margin>
it is obvious that we always succeed better through Reason and the love of truth than through remorse and sorrow. They are, therefore, harmful and bad, because they are a certain kind of sorrow, which <note>sorrow</note> we have already shown above to be injurious, and which, for that reason, we must try to avoid as an evil; and consequently we must likewise shun and flee from these also, which are like it.

‡ B continues: but, on the other hand, when we look into the matter thoroughly the case is quite otherwise, for we shall find that
<margin>30</margin>
they are not only not good . . .

<summary>The author argues that remorse and repentance are not virtuous but are harmful forms of sorrow that arise from rashness. He concludes that following Reason and the love of truth is a far more effective way to live rightly than relying on these negative emotions.</summary>

## CHAPTER XI

### ON DERISION AND JESTING

DERISION and jesting rest on a false opinion and reveal an imperfection in the person who derides and jests.

The opinion on which they rest is false because it is assumed that the one who is derided is the primary cause of the effects they produce, and that these effects do not necessarily (like all other things in Nature) depend on God. They reveal an imperfection in the derider; <margin>10</margin> because either the thing being derided is truly worthy of derision, or it is not. If it is not, then it shows bad manners to deride what should not be derided; if it is worthy of derision, then those <note>who deride it</note> show that they recognize an imperfection in what they deride, which they ought to remedy not through derision, but rather through sound reasoning.

Laughter does not refer to another person, but only to the man who perceives some good in himself; and since it is a certain kind of joy, there is nothing else to be said about it other than what has already been said about joy. I speak of <margin>20</margin> the kind of laughter caused by a specific idea which provokes ‡ one to it, and not at all of the laughter caused by the movement of the <note>vital</note> spirits; as for this (since it has no relation to good or evil), we did not intend to speak of it here.

As for envy, anger, and indignation, we shall say nothing about them here, except to refer back to what we have already said above concerning hatred.

‡ B continues: the laugher to it without any reference to good or evil, and not at all regarding the kind of laughter caused in him by the movement of the <note>vital</note> spirits; it was not our intention to <margin>30</margin> speak of this. Again, . . .

## CHAPTER XII

### ON GLORY, SHAME, AND SHAMELESSNESS

<margin>1</margin>WE shall now also briefly consider *glory*, *shame*, and *shamelessness*.‡ The first ‡‡ is a certain kind of joy which everyone feels in himself whenever he becomes aware that his conduct is esteemed and praised by others, regardless of any other advantage or profit they may have in view.

Shame is a certain *kind of* sorrow which arises in someone <margin>10</margin>when he happens to see that his conduct is despised by others, regardless of any other disadvantage or injury they may have in view.

Shamelessness is nothing else than a lack of, or a casting off of, shame—not through Reason, but either from an innocence of shame (as is the case with children, primitive people, etc.) or because, having been held in great contempt, one now goes to any length without regard for anything.

Now that we know these passions, we also know, at the same time, the vanity and imperfection which they have in <margin>20</margin>them. For glory and shame are not only of no advantage, because of what we have observed in their definitions, but also (inasmuch as they are based on self-love and on the opinion that man is the first cause of his own actions, and therefore deserving of praise and blame) they are harmful and must be rejected.

I will not, however, say that one ought to live among men in the same way that one would live away from them, where glory and shame have no place; quite the contrary,

<note>‡ B omits this sentence.</note>
<margin>30</margin><note>‡‡ A: *De eerste* [The first]; B: *De eere* [Glory].</note>

<summary>This chapter defines glory and shame as joy or sorrow derived from the opinions of others, and shamelessness as the lack of these feelings. The author argues that because these passions are based on self-love and the false belief in human free will, they are inherently imperfect and harmful.</summary>
<keywords>glory, shame, shamelessness, passions, self-love, praise, blame, social conduct, first cause</keywords>

--- Page 239 ---
<meta>This page concludes the discussion on how glory and shame can be ethically utilized for the benefit of others and provides a brief transition to the definition of shamelessness.</meta>

<header>ON GLORY, SHAME, AND SHAMELESSNESS</header>

<margin>1</margin>
<margin>10</margin>
I admit that we are not only free to utilize them when we apply them in the service of mankind and for their improvement, but that we may even do so at the price of limiting our own (otherwise perfect and legitimate) freedom. For example: if anyone wears expensive clothes in order to be respected, he seeks a glory which results from his self-love without any consideration for his fellow men; but when someone observes that his wisdom (with which he can be of service to his neighbors) is despised and trampled underfoot *simply* because he is dressed in shabby clothes, then he will do well if (from the motive of helping them) he provides himself with clothes to which they cannot take exception, thereby becoming like his fellow man in order that he may win over his fellow man.

Further, as regards Shamelessness, this shows itself to be such that in order to see its deformity all that we need is merely its definition, and that will be enough for us.

<vocab>Glory, Shamelessness, self-love, mankind, wisdom, deformity</vocab>

## CHAPTER XIII

### ON FAVOR, GRATITUDE, AND INGRATITUDE

<margin>I</margin>
Now follows <insert>the consideration</insert> of *favor, gratitude, and ingratitude*. Regarding the first two, they are the inclinations the soul has to wish and to do some good to one’s neighbor. I say "to wish" <insert>this happens</insert> when good is returned to someone who has done some good; I say "to do" <insert>this is the case</insert> when we ourselves have obtained or received some good.

<margin>10</margin>
I am well aware that almost all people consider these affects to be good; but, despite this, I venture to say that they can have no place in a perfect man. For a perfect man is moved to help his fellow man by sheer necessity only, and by no other cause; therefore, he feels it all the more to be his duty to help the most godless, seeing that his misery and need are so much greater.

Ingratitude is a disregard *or shaking off* of gratitude, just as shamelessness is of shame, and that without any rational ground, but solely as the result of either greed or <margin>20</margin> immoderate self-love; and that is why it can have no place in a perfect man.

<vocab>favor, gratitude, ingratitude, perfect man, self-love, misery</vocab>

<summary>The author defines favor and gratitude as desires to return or perform good deeds, but argues that a perfect man acts out of rational necessity rather than these emotional inclinations. Ingratitude is dismissed as a product of greed and self-love that has no place in a perfected character.</summary>
<keywords>favor, gratitude, ingratitude, perfect man, necessity, affects, self-love, ethics</keywords>

## CHAPTER XIV

### ON GRIEF

GRIEF will be the last of the passions that we discuss, and with it we will conclude. Now grief is a certain kind of sorrow arising from the contemplation of some good which we have lost, and <insert>lost</insert> in such a way that there is no hope of recovering it. It makes its imperfection so manifest that as soon as we examine it, we think it bad. For we have already shown above that it is bad to bind and link ourselves to things which <margin>10</margin> may easily, or at some time, fail us, and which we cannot have when we want them. And since it is a certain kind of sorrow, we have to shun it, as we have already remarked above, when we were treating sorrow.

I think, now, that I have already shown and proven sufficiently that it is only True Belief or Reason that leads us to the knowledge of good and evil. And so when we come to prove that Knowledge is the first and principal cause <note>B omitted “cause,” but the word seems to have been inserted recently—perhaps by Van Vloten, as a marginal pencil note <margin>30</margin> suggests.</note> of all these passions, it will be clearly manifest that if we use our understanding and Reason rightly, it should <margin>20</margin> be impossible for us ever to fall prey to one of these *passions* which we ought to reject. I say our *Understanding*, because I do not think that Reason alone is competent to free us from all these: as we shall afterwards show in its proper place.

We must, however, note here as an excellent thing about the passions, that we see and find that all the passions which

<margin>10</margin> To give even greater clarity to all this, it is useful to note that the foundation of all good and evil is *Love directed toward a certain object*: for if we do not love that object which (*note well*) alone is worthy of being loved, namely, God, as we have said before, but things which through their very character and nature are transient, then (since the object is subject to so many accidents, yes, even to annihilation) there necessarily results hatred, sorrow, etc., according to the changes in the object loved. Hatred, when anyone deprives him of what he loves. Sorrow, <margin>20</margin> when he happens to lose it. Glory, when he leans on self-love. Favor and Gratitude, when he does not love his fellow man for the sake of God. 
But, in contrast with all these, when man comes to love God who always is and remains immutable, then it is impossible for him to fall into this turmoil of passions. And for this reason we state it as a fixed and immovable principle that God is the first and only cause of all our good and delivers us from all our evil. 
Hence it is also to be noted \*lastly,\* that only Love, etc., <margin>30</margin> are limitless: namely, that as it increases more and more, so also it grows more excellent, because it is directed toward an object which is infinite, and can therefore always go on increasing, which can happen in the case of no other thing except this alone. # CHAPTER XV

## ON THE TRUE AND THE FALSE

<margin>1</margin> LET us now examine the *true* and the *false*, which show us the fourth and last consequence of true belief. Now, in order to do this, we shall first state the definitions of Truth and Falsity. Truth is an affirmation (or a denial) made about a certain thing which agrees with that same thing; and Falsity is an affirmation (or a denial) about a thing which does not agree with the thing itself. But this <margin>10</margin> being so, it may appear that there is no difference between the false and the true Idea. Since the affirmation or denial of this or that are mere <note>Literally "true," but the translator probably mistook *merus* (mere) for *verus* (true).</note> modes of thought, and the true and the false Idea differ in no other way <note>In version B this sentence begins as follows: "But since the affirmation or denial of this or that are mere modes of thought, there seems to be no difference between the true and the false idea except that," etc.</note> except that one agrees with the thing and the other does not, they are therefore not really, but only logically <note>*door reeden* [through reason.]</note> different. If this should be so, one may justly ask: what advantage does one gain from Truth, and what harm does the other suffer through falsity? And how shall one know that their conception or Idea agrees <margin>20</margin> with the thing more than the other's does? Lastly, how does it happen that one person errs and the other does not?

To this it may, in the first place, serve as an answer that the clearest things of all make known both themselves and

# CHAPTER XVI

## ON THE WILL

Now that we know the nature of Good and Evil, Truth and Falsity, and also in what the well-being of a perfect man consists, it is time to begin to examine ourselves, and to see whether we attain to such well-being voluntarily or by necessity. 

To this end, it is necessary to inquire what the Will is, according to those who posit a Will,‡ and in what way it is 
<margin>10</margin>different from Desire. Desire, we have said, is the inclination which the soul has toward something which it chooses as a good; from this it follows that before our desire inclines toward something external, we have already inwardly decided that such a thing is good. This affirmation—or, stated more generally, the power to affirm and to deny—is called the Will.†

It thus turns on the question of whether our Affirmations are made voluntarily or necessarily—that is, whether we can

‡ B omits the words “according . . . Will.”
<margin>20</margin>† Now the Will, regarded as Affirmation or Decision, *is different from true Belief and from Opinion.* It *differs from True Belief* in this: that it also extends to that which is not truly good. This is so because it lacks that conviction by which it is clearly seen that it cannot be otherwise; in the case of true belief there is, and must be, this conviction, because only good desires emanate from it. 

But it also differs from Opinion in this: that it can sometimes be quite infallible and certain; this is not the case with Opinion, which consists in guessing and supposing. 
<margin>30</margin>So we can call it Belief insofar as it can proceed with certainty, and Opinion insofar as it is subject to error.

<summary>This chapter introduces the study of the Will, defining it as the soul's power to affirm or deny after a judgment of "good" has been made. It distinguishes the Will from Desire and explains how Affirmation differs from both True Belief and Opinion based on the degree of certainty and conviction.</summary>

If, therefore, anyone should say that the soul produces the volition from itself, then I ask, by what power? Not by that which has been, for it no longer exists; also not by that which it has now, for it has none at all by which it might exist or last for even a single moment, because it is continuously created anew. Thus, as there is no thing that has any power to maintain itself or to produce anything, nothing remains but to conclude that God alone, therefore, is and
<margin>30</margin> must be the efficient cause of all things, and that all acts of Volition are determined by him *alone.*

understanding is purely passive; it is an awareness, in the soul, of the essence and existence of things; so that it is never we who affirm or deny something of a thing, but it is the thing itself that affirms or denies, in us, something of itself.

Possibly some will not admit this, because it seems to them that they are well able to affirm or to deny of the thing something different from what they know about it. But this is only because they have no idea of the <margin>10</margin>
conception which the soul has of the thing apart from or without the words ‡ <note>in which it is expressed</note>. It is quite true that (when there are reasons which prompt us to do so) we can, in words or by some other means, represent the thing to others differently from what we know it to be; but we can never bring it so far, either by words or by any other means, that we should feel about things differently from how we feel about them; that is impossible, and clearly so to all who have for once attended to their understanding <margin>20</margin>
itself apart from the use of words or other significant signs.

Against this, however, some perhaps may say: If it is not we, but the thing itself, that makes the affirmation and denial about itself in us, then nothing can be affirmed or denied except what is in agreement with the thing; and consequently there is no falsity. For we have said that falsity consists in affirming (or denying) anything of a thing which does not accord with that thing; that is, what the thing does not affirm or deny about itself. This takes place mostly in weak souls, which very easily receive a mode or ‡‡‡ an idea through a slight action of the object, and make no further affirmation or denial beyond this.

Lastly, it might also be objected that there are many <margin>10</margin> things which we *sometimes* want and [sometimes also] do not want, ‡‡‡‡ as, for example, to assert something about a thing or not to assert it, to speak the truth or not to speak it, and so forth. Desire, however, is the disposition of the soul to obtain or to do something for the sake of the good or evil <margin>20</margin> that is discerned in it; so that even after we have made an affirmation or denial about the thing, Desire still remains—namely, when we have ascertained or affirmed that the thing

Thus, the Will may well exist without the Desire, but not the Desire without the Will.

All the activities, therefore, which we have discussed above (since they are carried out through Reason under the appearance of good, or are hindered by Reason under the <margin>10</margin>appearance of evil) can only be categorized under that inclination which is called Desire, and by no means under the name of Will, which is completely inappropriate.

# CHAPTER XVII

## ON THE DISTINCTION BETWEEN WILL AND DESIRE

<margin>1</margin> Now that it is known that we have no *free* will to make
an affirmation or a denial, let us just see what is the correct
and true distinction between *will* and *desire*, or what the
Will might be which was called by the Latins *voluntas*. <note>Manuscript B adds: or good will.</note>

According to Aristotle's definition, Desire appears to
be a genus containing two species. For he says that the
<margin>10</margin> Will is the longing or inclination which one feels toward
that which *is* or *seems* good. From this it appears to me
that by Desire (or *cupiditas*) he means any inclination, whether
it be toward good or toward evil; but when the inclination
is only toward what *is* or *appears to be* good, or when
the person who has such an inclination has it under the
appearance of good, then he calls it *voluntas* or good will;
while, if it is bad—that is, when we observe in another an
inclination toward something which is bad <note>Manuscript B: and if, on the contrary, it is bad, or toward evil . . .</note>—he calls that
*voluptas* or bad will. So that the inclination of the soul is
<margin>20</margin> not something by which affirmations or denials are made,
but only an inclination to obtain something which appears
to be good, and <note>Manuscript B: or.</note> to flee from what appears to be bad.

It, therefore, remains to inquire now whether Desire
is free or not free. In addition to what we have already
said—namely, that Desire depends on the idea of its objects, and
that this understanding must have an external cause, and in
addition also to what we have said about the will—it still
<margin>30</margin>

<summary>The author distinguishes Will from Desire by defining Will as an inclination toward what appears good, whereas Desire covers inclinations toward both good and evil. He argues that these inclinations are not the source of affirmations or denials but are responses to the perceived quality of objects.</summary>
<keywords>Will, Desire, Aristotle, voluntas, cupiditas, voluptas, inclination, good and evil, free will, soul</keywords>

# DISTINCTION BETWEEN WILL AND DESIRE

<margin>1</margin> It remains to prove that Desire is not free. Many people, although they see quite well that the knowledge which a person has of various things is a medium through which their longing or inclination passes from one thing to another, yet they fail to observe what it may be that thus lures the inclination from one to the other.

However, to show that this inclination of ours is not of our own free will (and in order to present vividly before our eyes what it means to pass over, and to be drawn, from one thing to another), we shall imagine a child becoming aware <margin>10</margin> of something for the first time. For example, I hold before him a little bell, which produces a pleasant sound for his ears, so that he develops a longing for it; consider now whether he could really help feeling this longing or desire. If you say "yes," then I ask: how, and by what cause *is this to happen*? Certainly not through something which he knows to be better, because this is all that he knows; nor, again, through it appearing to be bad to him, for he knows nothing else, and this pleasure is the very best that has ever come to him. But perhaps he has the freedom to banish <margin>20</margin> from himself the longing which he feels; from which it would follow that this longing may well arise in us without our free will, but that all the same we have in us the freedom to banish it from us. This freedom, however, will not bear examination; for what, <note>Manuscript B: I say that this freedom will not stand the slightest test. This will be clearly evident; for what...</note> indeed, might it be that shall be able to annihilate the longing? The longing itself? Surely no, for there is nothing that through its own nature seeks its own undoing. What then might it ultimately be that shall be able to wean him from his longing? Nothing else, indeed, except that in the natural order and course of <margin>30</margin> things he is affected by something which he finds more pleasant than the first. And, therefore, just as, when we were considering the Will, we said that the human Will is nothing

<summary>The author argues that desire is not a product of free will but is determined by external stimuli, using the example of a child's natural attraction to a pleasant sound. He concludes that an inclination can only be overcome by a stronger, more pleasant experience rather than a conscious act of annihilation.</summary>

<keywords>Desire, free will, inclination, longing, causality, pleasure, human Will, psychology</keywords>

And Desire, regarded as a universal, being nothing but an abstraction from the particular acts of desiring this or that, and, beyond this, not actually existing in Nature, can, as such, also cause nothing.</note> Desire [in the abstract] is not anything actually existing in Nature, but is only an abstraction from the particular acts of desiring this or that. *Desire*, then, since it is not really anything, also cannot really cause anything. So when we say that *Desire* is free, it is just as if we said that *this or that Desire* is its own cause—that is, that before it existed it had already arranged that it should exist; which <margin>10</margin> is absurdity itself, and cannot be.

# CHAPTER XVIII <margin>1</margin>

## ON THE USES OF THE FOREGOING

Thus we see now that man, being a *part of the whole of Nature*, on which he depends and by which he is also governed, cannot of himself do anything for his happiness and well-being; let us, then, just see what uses we can derive from these propositions of ours. And this [is] all the more [necessary] because we have no doubt that they will appear quite offensive to some.

In the first place, it follows from this that we are truly <margin>10</margin>servants, yes, even slaves, of God, and that it is our greatest perfection to be such necessarily. For, if we were left to ourselves, and thus not dependent on God, we would be able to accomplish very little, or nothing, and that would justly give us cause to lament our lot; especially so in contrast with what we now see, namely, that we are dependent on that which is the most perfect of all, in such a way that we exist also as a part of the whole, that is, of him; and we contribute, so to speak, our share to the <margin>20</margin>realization of so many skillfully ordered and perfect works, which depend on him. ‡

Secondly, this knowledge ensures that we do not grow proud when we have accomplished something excellent (which pride causes us to come to a standstill, because

---

<note>‡ B: In the first place, because we depend on that which is the most perfect of all, in such a way that, being also a part of the whole—that is, of him—we also contribute our share to the realization of so many skillfully ordered and perfect works which depend on him, it follows therefore that we are God’s servants, and that it is <margin>30</margin>our greatest perfection to be such necessarily.</note>

<summary>This page introduces the practical applications of the theory that humans are dependent parts of Nature and God. It argues that recognizing our necessary servitude to God is the highest human perfection and a remedy against pride.</summary>
<keywords>Nature, God, servants, slaves, perfection, realization, pride, Spinoza, Short Treatise</keywords>

--- Page 258 ---
<meta>This page continues the list of practical benefits begun in Chapter XVIII, detailing how the knowledge of our dependence on God leads to social harmony, emotional stability, and true religious devotion.</meta>

<margin>1</margin> we think that we are already great, and that we need do nothing further; thereby working precisely against our own perfection, which consists in this—that we must at all times endeavor to advance further and further); but that, on the contrary, we attribute all that we do to God, who is the first and only cause of all that we accomplish and succeed in effecting.

Thirdly, in addition to the fact that this knowledge inspires us with a real love of our neighbor, it shapes us <margin>10</margin> so that we never hate him, nor are we angry with him, but love to help him, and to improve his condition. All these are the actions of such men as have great perfection or essence.

Fourthly, this knowledge also serves to promote the greatest Common Good, because through it a judge can never side with one party more than with the other, and when compelled to punish the one, and to reward the other, he will do it with a view to help and to improve the one as much as the other.

<margin>20</margin> Fifthly, this knowledge frees us from Sorrow, from Despair, from Envy, from Terror, and other evil passions, which, as we shall presently say, constitute the real hell itself.

Sixthly, <note>A adds: and lastly.</note> this knowledge brings us so far that we cease to stand in awe of God, as others do of the Devil (whom they imagine), lest he should do them harm. For why indeed should we fear God, who is the highest good itself, through whom all things are what they are, and also we who live in him?

<margin>30</margin> * *Seventhly,* * this knowledge also brings us so far that we attribute all to God, love him alone because he is the most glorious and the most perfect, and thus offer ourselves up entirely to him; for these really constitute both the true service of God and our own eternal happiness and bliss.

<header>ON THE USES OF THE FOREGOING</header>

<margin>1</margin>For the sole perfection and the final end of a slave and of a 
tool is this: that they duly fulfill the task imposed on them. 
For example, if a carpenter, while doing some work, finds 
his hatchet to be of excellent service, then this hatchet has thereby 
attained its end and perfection; but if he should think: 
"this hatchet has rendered me such good service now, 
therefore I shall let it rest, and require no further service 
from it," then precisely this hatchet would fail in its purpose, 
and be a hatchet no more. Thus also is it with man: so 
<margin>10</margin>long as he is a part of Nature, he must follow the laws of 
Nature, and this is divine service; and so long as he does 
this, it is well with him. But if God should (so to speak) will 
that man should serve him no more, that would be equivalent 
to depriving him of his well-being and annihilating 
him; because all that he is consists in this: that he serves 
God.

To cease this service would be equivalent to the destruction of a person's fundamental essence.</summary>

# CHAPTER XIX / ON OUR HAPPINESS

<margin>1</margin>Now that we have seen the advantages of this True Belief, we shall endeavor to fulfill the promise we have made—namely, to inquire whether through the knowledge which we already have (concerning what is good, what is evil, what truth is, and what falsehood is, and what, in general, the uses of all these are), whether, I say, we can thereby attain our well-being—namely, the Love of God (which we have noted to 
<margin>10</margin>be our supreme happiness)—and also in what way we can free ourselves from the passions which we have judged to be bad.

To begin with the consideration of the last, namely, the liberation from the passions,† I say that, if we suppose that they have no other causes than those which we have assigned to them, then, provided only we use our understanding rightly, as we can do very easily †† (now that we have a

<note>† All passions which come into conflict with good Reason (as is shown above) arise from Opinion. All that is good or bad in them is shown to us by True Belief; these, however—both, or either of 
<margin>20</margin>the two—are not able to free us from them. It is only the third kind, namely, True Knowledge, that emancipates us from them. And without this, it is impossible that we should ever be set free from them, as will be shown subsequently (page 133). Might this not be what others say and write so much about, though under different names? For who does not see how easily we can interpret opinion as sin; belief as the law which makes sin known; and true knowledge as grace which redeems us from sin?</note>

<note>†† Can do very easily; that is to say, when we have a thorough knowledge of good and evil: for then it is impossible to be subject

<margin>1</margin>criterion of truth and falsity), we shall never fall into them.

But what we must now prove is that they have no other causes; for this, I think, it is necessary that we should study ourselves in our entirety, having regard to the body as well as to the spirit.

<margin>10</margin>
And first, we must show that in Nature there is a body through whose form and activities we are affected, and thus become aware of it. And the reason why we do this is because when we gain insight into the activities of the body and the effects which they produce, then we shall also discover the first and foremost cause of all those passions; and, at the same time, also the means through which all those passions might be eliminated. From this we shall then also be able to see whether it is possible to do such a thing by the aid of Reason. And then we shall also proceed to speak about our Love of God.

<margin>20</margin>
Now, to prove that there is a body in Nature can be no difficult task for us, now that we already know *that* God is, and *what* God is; whom we have defined as a being of infinite attributes, each of which is infinite and perfect. And since extension is an attribute which we have shown to be infinite in its kind, it must therefore also necessarily be an attribute of that infinite being. And as we have also already demonstrated that this infinite being exists, it follows at once that this attribute also exists.

<margin>30</margin>
Moreover, since we have also proved that outside Nature, which is infinite, there is and can be no being, it is clearly manifest that this effect of the body through which we become aware of it can proceed from nothing else than from extension itself, and by no means from something else which (as some would have it) has extension in an eminent degree [*eminenter*] ‡: for (as we have already shown in the first chapter) there is no such thing.

<margin>1</margin> We must observe, therefore, that all the effects which are clearly seen to depend necessarily on extension must be attributed to this attribute, such as Motion and Rest. For if the power to produce these did not exist in Nature, then (even though Nature might have many other attributes) it would be impossible for these to exist. For if a thing is to produce something, then there must be something within it through which it—rather than another thing—can produce that something.

What we have just said here about extension, we also wish to be understood as though it had been said about thought, and *furthermore* about all that exists.

<margin>10</margin> It is to be observed further that there is nothing whatever in us that we do not have the power to become aware of. So, if we find that there is nothing else in us except the effects of the thinking thing and those of extension, then we may say with certainty that there is nothing else in us.

In order that the workings of both of these may be clearly understood, we shall first consider each by itself, and afterward both together, as well as the effects of both one and the other.

<margin>20</margin> Now, when we consider extension alone, then we become aware of nothing else in it except Motion and Rest, from which we then discover all the effects that result from them. And these two † modes of body are such that it is impossible for any other thing to change them, except only themselves. Thus, for example, when a stone lies still, then it is impossible for it to be moved by the power of thought or anything else; it can, however, be moved by motion, ‡ as when another stone, having greater motion than this stone has rest, makes it move. <margin>30</margin> Likewise, the moving stone will not be brought to rest except through something else which has less motion. It follows, accordingly, that no mode of thought can bring motion or rest into a body. In

even if it existed—could likewise affect the soul neither
more nor differently than the body does in fact now. For

soul is more active in this case, so is the feeling more perfect.

<margin>1</margin>Now, regarding what we have said in the preceding chapter, the following difficulties might be raised as objections. <note>Manuscript B inserts here a preliminary statement of the three objections which follow, and then repeats each of them in its place, as in the text.</note>

<meta>This page continues the response to the first objection regarding the physical relief of sorrow and introduces the second objection concerning the soul's influence on the motion of the body.</meta>

<page-num>127</page-num>
<header>CONFIRMATION OF THE FOREGOING</header>

<margin>1</margin>change that the vital spirits undergo. This sorrow results from the soul's love for, and union with, the body.†

That this is so may be easily deduced from the fact that this sorrow can be relieved in one of two ways: either by restoring the vital spirits to their original form—that is, by relieving the person of the pain—or by being persuaded by good reasons to make no fuss about this body. The first is temporary, and the sorrow is liable to return; but the second is eternal, permanent, and unchangeable.

<margin>10</margin>The second objection may be this: as we see that the soul, although it has nothing in common with the body, can yet cause the vital spirits (even if they were about to move in one direction) to move in another direction instead, why should it not also be able to bring it about that a body which is perfectly still and at rest should begin to move itself? †† Likewise, why should it not also be able to move all other bodies which are already in motion in whatever direction it pleases?

<note>† Man's sorrow is caused by the thought that some evil is <margin>20</margin>befalling him—namely, through the loss of some good. When such a thought is entertained, the result is that the vital spirits gather about the heart and, with the help of other parts, compress and enclose it—the exact opposite of what happens in the case of joy. Then the soul becomes aware of this pressure and is pained. Now, what effect do medicines or wine have? This: through their action, they drive the vital spirits away from the heart and create space again; and when the soul becomes aware of this, it receives new animation. This consists in the thought of evil being diverted by the change in the proportion of motion and rest caused by the wine, and it turns to something else in which the <margin>30</margin>understanding finds more satisfaction. But this cannot be the immediate effect of the wine on the soul, but only the effect of the wine on the vital spirits.</note>

<note>†† Now, there is no difficulty here as to how one mode, which is infinitely different from the other, still acts upon the other; for it is a part of the whole, since the soul never existed without the body.</note>

# CHAPTER XXI

## ON REASON

At present, we have to inquire why it happens that sometimes, although we see that a certain thing is good or bad, we nevertheless do not find in ourselves the power either to do the good or to abstain from the bad, and yet sometimes we do indeed [find this power in us]. This we can easily understand if we consider the causes that we assigned to opinions, which we stated to be the causes of all <term>affects</term>. These, we then said, [arise] either from hearsay or from experience. 
<margin>10</margin>
And since all that we find in ourselves has greater power over us than that which comes to us from outside, it certainly follows that Reason can be the cause of the extinction of opinions <note>It is all the same whether we use here the word opinion or passion; and so it is clear why we cannot conquer by means of Reason those that have come to us through experience; for these are nothing else than an enjoyment of, or immediate union with, something that we judge to be good, and Reason, though it teaches <margin>20</margin> us what is better, does not make us enjoy it. Now that which we enjoy in us cannot be conquered by that which we do not enjoy, and is outside us, as that is which Reason suggests. But if these are to be overcome then there must be something that is more powerful; in this way there will be an enjoyment or immediate union with something that is better known and enjoyed than this first; and when this exists victory is always assured; or, indeed, *this victory comes* also through tasting an evil which is recognized to be greater than the good that was enjoyed, and upon which it follows immediately. Still, experience teaches us that this evil does <margin>30</margin> not necessarily always follow thus, for, etc. *See pages 78, 118.*</note> which we have got from hearsay only (and this is so because reason has not *like these*

# CHAPTER XXII

## ON TRUE KNOWLEDGE, REGENERATION, &c.

<margin>1</margin> Since, then, Reason has no power to lead us to the attainment of our well-being, it remains for us to inquire whether we can attain it through the fourth and last kind of knowledge. Now, we have said that this kind of knowledge does not result from something else, but from a direct revelation of the object itself to the understanding. And if that object is glorious and good, then the soul necessarily becomes united with it, as we have also remarked with <margin>10</margin> reference to our body. Hence, it follows incontrovertibly that it is this knowledge which evokes love. So, when we get to know God in this manner (as he cannot reveal himself, nor become known to us otherwise than as the most glorious and best of all), we must necessarily become united with him. And only in this *union,* as we have already remarked, does our blessedness consist.

I do not say that we must know him exactly as he is, *or adequately,* for it is sufficient for us to know him to some extent in order to be united with him. For even the <margin>20</margin> knowledge that we have of the body is not such that we know it exactly as it is, or perfectly; and yet, what a union! what a love!

That this fourth <note>kind of</note> knowledge, which is the knowledge of God, is not the consequence of something else but is immediate, is evident from what we have proved before: <note>namely</note> that he is the cause of all knowledge that is acquired through itself alone and through no other thing. Moreover, it is also evident from this: that we are so united with him by nature that without him we can neither exist, nor be <margin>30</margin>

<summary>The author argues that true blessedness is achieved through a fourth type of knowledge, which is a direct and immediate revelation of God to the understanding. This direct knowledge creates a necessary union between the soul and God, which is the source of human well-being.</summary>
<keywords>Reason, well-being, fourth kind of knowledge, revelation, understanding, soul, God, union, blessedness, body, immediate knowledge</keywords>

We shall endeavour to explain, next, this union of ours with him through nature and love.

<margin>10</margin>
We said before that in Nature there can be nothing of which there is not an Idea in the soul of that same thing.† And according as the thing is either more or less perfect, so also is the union and the influence of the Idea with the thing, or with God himself, less or more perfect.

For as the whole of Nature is but one single substance, and one whose essence is infinite, all things are united through Nature, and they are united into one [being], namely, God. And now, as the body is the very first thing of which our soul becomes aware (because, as already remarked, no thing can exist in Nature of which the Idea is not in the thinking thing, this Idea being the soul of that thing), so that thing must necessarily be the first cause of the Idea.††

<margin>20</margin>

## CHAPTER XXIII
### ON THE IMMORTALITY OF THE SOUL

<margin>1</margin> If we only consider attentively what the Soul is, and from where its change and duration originate, then we shall easily see whether it is mortal or immortal.

Now we have said that the Soul is an Idea which is in the thinking thing, arising from the reality of a thing which exists in Nature. From this it follows that according to the duration and change of the thing, so must also be the <margin>10</margin> duration and change of the Soul. We remarked, at the same time, that the Soul can become united either *with the body* of which it is the Idea, or *with God*, without whom it can neither be, nor be known.

From this, then, it can easily be seen, (1) that, if it is united with the body alone, and that body happens to perish, then it must perish also; for when it is deprived of the body, which is the foundation of its love, it must perish with it. But (2) if it becomes united with some other thing which is and remains unchangeable, then, on the <margin>20</margin> contrary, it must also remain unchangeable *and lasting.* For, in that case, by what would it be possible for it to perish? ‡ Not through itself; for just as it could not begin to exist through itself when it did not yet exist, so

---

<note>B concludes this chapter as follows: For that which alone is the cause of the existence of a thing, must also, when it is about to pass away, be the cause of its non-existence, simply because it is itself changing or passing away; or that of which it is the cause must be able to annihilate itself; but just as a thing cannot begin to exist through itself when it does not yet exist, so little also can it <margin>30</margin> change or perish through itself, now that it does exist.</note>

<summary>The author explains that the soul's mortality or immortality depends on its object of union: if united only with the perishable body, it perishes, but if united with the unchangeable God, it shares in that eternal duration. He argues that a thing cannot cause its own destruction any more than it could cause its own initial existence.</summary>
<keywords>Soul, Immortality, Idea, thinking thing, Nature, God, unchangeable, cause of existence, non-existence</keywords>

--- Page 279 ---
<meta>This page completes the logical argument from the previous page, asserting that the soul cannot destroy itself and that its continued existence depends entirely on the nature of the cause to which it is united.</meta>

<page-num>137</page-num>
<header>ON THE IMMORTALITY OF THE SOUL</header>

just as little can it change or perish *through itself*, now that it exists.

Consequently, that thing which alone is the cause of its *existence* must also (when it is about to perish) be the cause of its *non-existence*, because it happens to change itself or to perish.

<summary>This page concludes the argument that the soul is incapable of perishing by its own power, as its existence and non-existence are entirely dependent on the external cause that created it. If the soul is united with an eternal cause, it shares in that immortality.</summary>

<page-num>138</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER XXIV ON GOD’S LOVE OF MAN</header>

<margin>I</margin>
Thus far, we think we have sufficiently shown what our love of God is, as well as its consequences—namely, our eternal duration. Therefore, we do not think it is necessary to say anything here about other matters, such as joy in God, peace of mind, etc., as it may easily be seen from what has been said what should be said about them. 
Thus (as we have, so far, only considered our love of God), <margin>10</margin> it still remains to be seen whether there is also a divine love of us—that is, whether God also loves mankind, specifically when they love him. Now, in the first place, we have said that no modes of thought can be attributed to God except those which are in his creatures; therefore, it cannot be said that God loves mankind, much less that he should love them because they love him, or hate them because they hate him. For in that case, we would have to suppose that people act of their own free will and that they do not depend on a first cause, which <margin>20</margin> we have already proved to be false. Besides, this would necessarily involve nothing less than a great changeability on the part of God, who, though he neither loved nor hated before, would now have to begin to love and to hate, and would be *induced* or *made* to do so by something supposed to be outside him; but this is absurdity itself.
Still, when we say that God does not love man, this must not be taken to mean that he (so to speak) leaves man to pursue his course all alone, but only that <margin>30</margin> because man, together with all that exists, is in God in such...

<summary>This chapter explores whether God reciprocates human love, concluding that God cannot love or hate in a human sense because he is immutable and all-encompassing. To suggest God reacts to human actions would falsely imply that humans have free will independent of a first cause.</summary>
<keywords>God's love, mankind, modes of thought, first cause, mutability, divine nature, free will, Spinoza</keywords>

are divine laws; the reason is that whatsoever
happens is not contrary to, but in accordance with, his own
decision. All laws that can be transgressed are human laws; <margin>20</margin>
the reason is that all that people decide upon for
their own well-being does not necessarily, on that account,
tend also to the well-being of the whole of Nature, but may, on
the contrary, tend to the annihilation of many other things.

<margin>1</margin> men make laws for their own well-being, and have no other

end <note>which</note> coincides with the eternal <note>B: beginningless</note> laws established
by God from eternity, and so, together with all others, help
to accomplish everything. For example, although the
bees, in all their work and the orderly discipline which they
<margin>10</margin> maintain among themselves, have no other end in view than
to make certain provisions *for themselves* for the winter,
still, the man who is above them has an entirely different end
in view when he maintains and tends them, namely, to
obtain honey for himself. So also <note>is it with</note> man, insofar
as he is an individual thing and looks no further than
his finite character can reach; but, insofar as he is also
a part and tool of the whole of Nature, this end of man
cannot be the final end of Nature, because she is infinite,
and must make use of him, together with also all other
<margin>20</margin> things, as an instrument.

### GOD, MAN, AND HIS WELL-BEING

1 God and the understanding of man; for, as the under-
standing is that in us which must know God, and as it stands
in such immediate union with him that it can neither exist, nor
be understood without him, it is incontrovertibly evident
from this that no thing can ever come into such close contact
with the understanding as God himself can. It is also im-
possible to get to know God through something else. 1. Be-
cause, in that case, such a thing would have to be better
known to us than God himself, which is in open conflict
with all that we have previously clearly shown, namely, that
God is a cause both of our knowledge and of all essence,
and that without him all individual things not only cannot
exist, but cannot even be understood. 2. Because we can
never attain to the knowledge of God through any other
thing, the nature of which is necessarily finite, even if it
were far better known to us; for how is it possible that we
should infer an infinite and limitless thing from a *finite*
and *limited* thing? For even if we did observe some
effects or work in nature the cause of which was unknown
to us, still it would be impossible for us to conclude from
this that there must be in nature an infinite and limitless
thing in order to produce this result. For how can we
know whether many causes have combined in order to
produce this, or whether there was only one? Who is to
tell us?

We therefore conclude, finally, that, in order to make
himself known to men, God can and need use neither
words, nor miracles, nor any other created thing, but only
himself.

<summary>The author argues that God is known most immediately by the human understanding because of their close union, and that it is impossible to know the infinite nature of God through finite, created things like words or miracles.</summary>

<page-num>143</page-num>

## CHAPTER XXV

### ON DEVILS

WE shall now briefly say something about devils, whether they exist or do not exist, and it is this:
If the Devil is a thing that is once and for all opposed to God, and has absolutely nothing from God, then he is precisely identical with Nothingness, which we have already discussed before.

<margin>10</margin>
If, as some do, we represent him as a thinking thing that absolutely neither wills nor does any good, and so sets himself, once and for all, in opposition to God, then surely he is very wretched; and, if prayers could help, then one should pray for his conversion.

<margin>20</margin>
But let us just see whether such a wretched thing could even exist for a single moment. And, if we do so, we shall immediately find out that it cannot; for whatever duration a thing has results entirely from the perfection of the thing, and the more essence and godliness things possess, the more lasting they are. Therefore, as the Devil has not the least perfection in him, how then, I think to myself, would he be able to exist? Add to this, that the persistence or duration of a mode of the thinking thing only results from the union in which such a mode is, through love, joined to God. As the precise opposite of this union is supposed in the case of the Devils, they cannot possibly exist.‡

<margin>30</margin>
As, however, there is no necessity whatever why we should assume the existence of Devils, why then should they be assumed? For we need not, like others, assume Devils in order to find in them the cause of Hatred, Envy, Wrath, and similar passions, since we have found these causes sufficiently without such fictions.

->‡ A: not exist.<-

<summary>The author argues that devils cannot exist because existence is derived from perfection and union with God, both of which devils are defined as lacking. Furthermore, he asserts that devils are unnecessary fictions, as human passions like hatred and envy can be explained without them.</summary>

<keywords>Devils, God, Nothingness, thinking thing, perfection, godliness, duration, existence, passions, Hatred, Envy, Wrath</keywords>

# CHAPTER XXVI

<margin>1</margin>By the assertion of what preceded ‡ we not only wanted to make it known that there are no Devils, but also, indeed, that the causes (or, to express it better, what we call *Sins*) which hinder us in the attainment of our perfection are within ourselves.
