Similarly, it may also be clearly gathered from the above that without Virtue, <margin>20</margin>or (to express it better) without the guidance of the Understanding, everything tends toward ruin, so that we can enjoy no rest, and we live, as it were, outside our element. and peace, we require no other principles except only this:

reflections and considerations) we have discovered

to stop and to rest here. For we have seen that, outside
of Him, there is nothing that can give us any happiness. And
it is true freedom to be, and to remain, bound by the
loving chains of His love.

<meta>This page transitions from the soul's union with God as the source of supreme happiness to a formal definition of human freedom, structured around four metaphysical propositions regarding essence, activity, and causality.</meta>

<page-num>146</page-num>
<header>GOD, MAN, AND HIS WELL-BEING</header>

<margin>1</margin>up to the desired place, or like a good genius that, without any falsity or deception, brings us news of the highest good in order thereby to encourage us to pursue it and to become united with it; this union is our supreme happiness and bliss.

So, to bring this work to a conclusion, it remains to indicate briefly what human freedom is and what it consists of. For this purpose, I shall make use of the following propositions as things that are certain and <margin>10</margin>demonstrated.

1. The more essence a thing has, the more activity it also has, and the less passivity. For it is certain that what is active acts through what it possesses, and that the thing which is passive is affected through what it lacks.

2. All passivity that passes from non-being to being, or from being to non-being, must result from some external agent and not from an inner one: because no thing, considered by itself, contains within itself the conditions that will <margin>20</margin>enable it to destroy itself when it exists, or to create itself when it does not exist.

3. Whatever is not produced by external causes can have nothing in common with them and can, consequently, be neither changed nor transformed by them.

And from these last two <note>propositions</note>, I infer the following fourth proposition:

4. The effect of an immanent or inner cause (which are the same to me) cannot possibly pass away or change so long as <margin>30</margin>this cause of it remains. For such an effect, just as it is not produced by external causes, so also it cannot be changed <note>by external causes</note>; according to the third proposition. And since no thing whatever can come to nothing except through external causes, it is not possible that this effect should be liable to perish so long as its cause endures; according to the second proposition.

<summary>The author concludes by defining human freedom through four metaphysical propositions, arguing that things with greater essence possess more activity and that internal causes produce enduring effects that cannot be altered by external forces.</summary>
<keywords>human freedom, essence, activity, passivity, external agent, immanent cause, causality, metaphysics</keywords>

The freest cause of all, and that which is most appropriate to God, is the immanent cause: for the effect of this cause depends on it in such a way that it can neither exist, nor be understood without it, nor is it subjected to any other cause; it is, moreover, united with it in such a way that together they form one whole.

Now let us just see what we must conclude from the above propositions. In the first place, then:

1. Since the essence of God is infinite, it therefore has an infinite activity and an infinite negation of passivity, <margin>10</margin>following the first proposition. In consequence of this, the more that things are united with God through their greater essence, the more activity they also have and the less passivity; and so much more also *are they* free from change and corruption.

2. True Understanding can never perish; for in itself it can have no cause to destroy itself, following the second proposition. And as it did not emanate from external causes, but from God, so it is not susceptible to any change through them, following the third proposition. And since <margin>20</margin>God has produced it immediately and He is only <note>Manuscript A: is not only</note> an inner cause, it follows necessarily that it cannot perish so long as this cause of it remains, following the fourth proposition. Now this cause of it is eternal, therefore the Understanding is too.

3. All the effects of *true* understanding which are united with it are the most excellent and must be valued above all others; for as they are inner effects, they must be the most excellent, following the fifth proposition; and, besides this, they are also necessarily eternal, because their cause is such. <margin>30</margin>

4. All the effects which we produce outside ourselves are more perfect the more they are capable of becoming united with us, so as to constitute one and the same nature with us; for in this way they come nearest to inner

<meta>This page begins Appendix I, titled "On God," following the general title page for the Appendices. It introduces the fundamental axioms and the first proposition regarding substance and attributes.</meta>

# [APPENDIX I]

## * ON GOD *

### AXIOMS

1. Substance is, by its nature, prior to all its modifications.
2. Things which are different are distinguished either <term>realiter</term> or <term>modaliter</term>.
3. Things which are distinguished <term>realiter</term> either have different attributes, such as Thought and Extension, or are referred to different attributes, as in the case of Understanding and Motion; one of which belongs to Thought, and the other to Extension. <margin>10</margin>
4. Things which have different attributes, as well as things which belong to different attributes, do not have anything in common with one another.
5. That which does not have within itself something of another thing also cannot be a cause of the existence of such another thing.
6. It is impossible that that which is a cause of itself should have limited itself.
7. That by which things are sustained is by its nature prior to ‡ such things. <margin>20</margin>

<note>‡ A: the first (prior) in; B: prior to.</note>

### PROPOSITION I

To no substance that exists can the same attribute be ascribed that is ascribed to another substance; or (which is the same) in Nature there cannot be two substances, unless they are distinguished <term>realiter</term>. ‡‡

<note>‡‡ B: . . . in Nature there cannot be posited two substances of one and the same nature.</note>

<summary>This page outlines seven foundational axioms regarding the nature of substance, attributes, and causality, followed by a proposition stating that no two substances can share the same attribute unless they are fundamentally distinct.</summary>
<keywords>substance, axioms, attributes, Thought, Extension, causality, realiter, modaliter, Nature</keywords>

--- Page 296 ---
<meta>This page continues Appendix I, providing the proofs for the first proposition and introducing the second and third propositions concerning the nature of substance and its attributes.</meta>

<margin>1</margin>### PROOF

If there are two substances, then they are distinct; and consequently (Axiom 2) ‡ they are distinguished either <term>realiter</term> or <term>modaliter</term>. Not <term>modaliter</term>, for in that case the modes would by their nature be prior to the substance, which is contrary to the first axiom; therefore, they are distinguished <term>realiter</term>; and consequently, what is predicated of one cannot be predicated of the other, which is what we intended to prove.

<note>‡ A gives the references to Axioms and Propositions in the margin; B gives them in the text.</note>

<margin>10</margin>## PROPOSITION II

One substance cannot be the cause of the existence of another substance.

### PROOF

Such a cause cannot contain in itself anything of such an effect (Proposition 1); because the difference between them is real, and therefore it cannot (Axiom 5) produce it. ‡‡

<note>‡‡ A adds: (existence); B: . . . and therefore the one cannot produce the other.</note>

## PROPOSITION III

Every attribute or substance ‡‡‡ is by nature infinite, and supremely perfect in its kind.

<margin>20</margin>### PROOF

No substance is produced by another (Proposition 2) and consequently, if it exists, it is either an attribute of God, or it has been its own cause outside of God. If it is the first, then it is necessarily infinite and supremely perfect in its kind, such

<note>‡‡‡ A: all attributes or substance; B: all substance or its attributes.</note>
<margin>30</margin>

<summary>This page provides proofs for three propositions: that substances are distinguished really rather than modally, that one substance cannot cause another, and that every substance or attribute is inherently infinite and perfect.</summary>
<keywords>substance, realiter, modaliter, causality, existence, attributes, God, infinity, perfection</keywords>

--- Page 297 ---
<meta>This page continues Appendix I, completing the proof for Proposition III and introducing Proposition IV regarding the inherent existence of substance and its relationship to the infinite understanding.</meta>

as are all other attributes of God. If the second, then it is <margin>1</margin> also necessarily such because (Axiom 6) it could not have limited itself.

## PROPOSITION IV

To such an extent does existence pertain by nature to the essence of every substance,‡ that it is impossible to posit in an infinite understanding the Idea of the essence of a substance that does not exist in Nature.

### PROOF

The true essence of an object ‡‡ is something which is <margin>10</margin> <term>realiter</term> different from the Idea of the same object; and this something exists (Axiom 3) either <term>realiter</term>, or is contained in some other thing which exists <term>realiter</term>; from which other thing this essence cannot be distinguished <term>realiter</term>, but only <term>modaliter</term>; such are all the essences of the things ‡‡‡ which we see, which before they yet existed were already contained in extension, motion, and rest, and when they do exist are not distinguished from extension <term>realiter</term>, but only <term>modaliter</term>. Moreover, it would involve self-contradiction to suppose that the essence of a substance ‡‡‡‡ is contained thus <margin>20</margin> in some other thing; because in that case it could not be distinguished from this <term>realiter</term>, contrary to the first proposition; also, it could in that case be produced by the subject which contains it, contrary to the second proposition; and lastly, it could not by its nature be infinite and supremely perfect in its kind, contrary to the third proposition.

---

<note>‡ A: to every essence of substance; B: to the essence of a substance.</note>
<note>‡‡ B: . . . of the object of an idea.</note>
<note>‡‡‡ B: essences or things. <margin>30</margin></note>
<note>‡‡‡‡ A: that an essence of the substance; B: that an essence of substance.</note>

<summary>This page argues that existence is an essential property of any substance, meaning it is impossible for an infinite understanding to conceive of a substance's essence without its actual existence in Nature. The proof distinguishes between real and modal differences, asserting that a substance cannot be contained within or produced by another thing.</summary>
<keywords>substance, essence, existence, Nature, realiter, modaliter, extension, motion, rest, infinite understanding</keywords>

--- Page 298 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical proof of Proposition IV regarding the nature of substance and introduces a corollary identifying the unity of Nature and God.</meta>

Therefore, as its essence is not contained in any other thing, it must be a thing that exists through itself.

### COROLLARY

Nature is known through itself, and not through any other thing. It consists of infinite attributes, every one of them infinite and perfect in its kind; existence pertains to its essence, so that outside of it there is no other essence or existence, and it thus coincides exactly with the essence of God who alone is glorious and blessed.

<vocab>Nature, essence, infinite attributes, God</vocab>

<note>‡ A: to every essence of substance; B: to the essence of a substance.</note>
<note>‡‡ B: . . . of the object of an idea.</note>
<note>‡‡‡ B: essences or things. <margin>30</margin></note>
<note>‡‡‡‡ A: that an e...</note>

<summary>This page concludes the argument that substance exists through itself and defines Nature as a self-sufficient entity composed of infinite attributes. It identifies this definition of Nature as being identical to the essence of God.</summary>
<keywords>Nature, God, essence, existence, infinite attributes, substance, Proposition IV</keywords>

# [APPENDIX II]

## * ON THE HUMAN SOUL *

As man is a created, finite thing, etc., it necessarily follows that what he has of Thought, and what we call the Soul, is a mode ‡ of the attribute which we call Thought, and that nothing else except this mode belongs to his essence: so much so that when this mode comes to nothing, the soul also perishes, although the aforementioned attribute remains unchanged. Similarly regarding ‡‡ what he has of Extension: what we call the Body is nothing other than a mode of the other attribute which we call Extension. When this is destroyed, the human body also ceases to exist, although the attribute <margin>10</margin> Extension remains unchanged.

Now, in order to understand what this mode is that we call the Soul, and how it derives its origin from the body, and also how its change depends only on the body (which, for me, constitutes the union of soul and body), the following must be observed:

1. That the most immediate mode of the attribute which we call Thought contains <term>objectively</term> the formal essence of all things; so much so that if one could posit a real thing whose essence was not <term>objective</term> in the aforementioned attribute, then this would not be infinite, nor supremely perfect <margin>20</margin> in its kind, contrary to what has already been proven in the third proposition. And since, in fact, Nature or God is one being of whom infinite attributes are predicated, and who contains within himself all the essences of created things, it necessarily follows that from all this there is produced

---

<note>‡ A: an attribute; B: a mode.</note>
<note>‡‡ B omits "regarding," and inserts "and" after "Extension."</note>

<vocab>Soul, Thought, Extension, mode, attribute, essence, Nature, God, formal essence, objective essence</vocab>

---

<note>‡ A: to every essence of substance; B: to the essence of a substance.</note>
<note>‡‡ B: . . . of the object of an idea.</note>
<note>‡‡‡ B: essences or things. <margin>30</margin></note>
<note>‡‡‡‡ A: that an e...</note>

<summary>This appendix explains that the human soul and body are finite "modes" of the infinite attributes of Thought and Extension, respectively. It argues that the soul's existence depends on the body's mode, while the underlying divine attributes remain eternal.</summary>
<keywords>human soul, body, Thought, Extension, mode, attribute, Spinoza, essence, Nature, God</keywords>

--- Page 300 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the human soul as a mode of the thinking attribute, explaining how the soul is an "infinite idea" that corresponds to the body's existence and how emotions like love and joy arise from this primary mode.</meta>

... in Thought an infinite Idea, ‡ which comprehends *objectively* the whole of Nature just as it is *realiter*.

2. It is to be observed that all the other modes, such as Love, Desire, Joy, etc., derive their origin from this first immediate mode; and this occurs in such a way that if it did not come first, then there could be no love, desire, nor joy, etc. From this it clearly follows that the natural love which prompts everything to preserve its body (I mean the <margin>10</margin> mode) ‡‡ cannot have any other origin than in the Idea or the "objective" essence of such a body, which is in the thinking attribute. Furthermore, since for the real existence of an Idea (or "objective" essence) no other thing is required than the thinking attribute and the object (or "formal" essence), it is certain, as we have said, that the Idea, or the "objective" essence, is the most immediate † mode of the *thinking* attribute. And, consequently, there can be no other mode in the thinking attribute that belongs to the essence of the soul of every ‡‡‡ thing, except only the Idea, which must be in the thinking attribute when its <margin>20</margin> object exists: for such an idea brings with it the other modes of Love, Desire, Joy, etc. Now, as the Idea comes from the existence of the object, it follows that as the object changes or perishes, so its Idea must change or perish; and such being the case, it is this which is united with the object. ‡‡‡‡

---

<note>† I call that mode the most immediate mode which, in order to exist, requires no other mode in the same attribute.</note>
<note>‡ A: it necessarily follows that of all that *which* is produced in Thought there is an infinite Idea... ; B: ...that there is <margin>30</margin> produced in thought an infinite idea of it...</note>
<note>‡‡ B omits the words in brackets.</note>
<note>‡‡‡ A: *gelijken* [like]; B: *iegelijk'n* [every].</note>
<note>‡‡‡‡ B: ...so this idea of it must change or perish in the same degree or measure of change or annihilation, because it is thus united with the object.</note>

<summary>This page explains that the soul is an immediate mode of the thinking attribute, serving as an "objective essence" or idea of the body. It further asserts that all other mental states, such as love and joy, are secondary modes that depend entirely on this primary idea of the object's existence.</summary>

# APPENDIX II

<margin>1</margin>Lastly, if we should want to proceed and ascribe to the essence of the soul that through which it can be real, we will be able to find nothing else than the attribute [Thought] and the object of which we have just been speaking; and neither of these can belong to the essence of the Soul, as the object has nothing of Thought and is *realiter* different from the Soul. ‡ And with regard to the attribute, we have also already proved that it cannot pertain to the above-mentioned essence, as appears even more clearly from what we said subsequently; ‡‡ for the attribute as an attribute ‡‡‡ is not <margin>10</margin> united with the object, since it neither changes nor perishes, although the object changes or perishes.

Therefore the essence of the soul consists in this alone: namely, in the existence of an Idea or "objective" essence in the thinking attribute, arising from the essence of an object which actually exists in Nature. I say, *of an object which in fact exists, etc.*, without further particulars, so as to include under this not only the modes of extension, but also the modes of all the infinite attributes, each of which also has its own soul, just as in the case of extension. And so <margin>20</margin> that this definition may be somewhat more fully understood, it should be kept in mind what I have already said when speaking about the attributes—which, I said, are not different regarding their existence, ‡‡‡‡ for they are themselves the "subjects" of their essences; also that the essence of every one of the modes is contained in the above-named attributes, and, lastly, that all the attributes are attributes of One infinite Being. For this reason also, in the ninth chapter of the First Part, I called this Idea *a creation created immediately by God*; since it contains *objectively* the "formal" essence of all <margin>30</margin>

<note>‡ B: as the object of Thought has nothing of it, but is *realiter* different from it.</note>
<note>‡‡ B: as will be seen from what we shall say later.</note>
<note>‡‡‡ B omits "as attribute."</note>
<note>‡‡‡‡ B omits the nine words that follow.</note>

<summary>This page defines the essence of the soul as an "objective essence" or Idea within the thinking attribute that corresponds to a formally existing object in Nature. This definition applies not only to human bodies in extension but to all modes of the infinite attributes of God.</summary>

<margin>1</margin>things, ‡ without omission or addition. And this is necessarily only one, considering that all the essences of the attributes, and the essences of the modes included in these attributes, are the essence of only one infinite being. ‡‡ But it must still be remarked that these modes now under consideration, <note>even when</note> none of them exists, are nevertheless equally included in their attributes; and as there is no inequality whatever in the attributes, nor yet in the essences of the modes, there can be no particu-
<margin>10</margin>larity in the idea when there is none in Nature. But as soon as some of these modes take on their particular existence, and thereby become in some way different from their attributes (because then their particular existence, which they have in the attribute, is the "subject" of their essence), then a particularity shows itself in the essences of the modes, and consequently in the "objective" essences of these which are necessarily included in the Idea. ‡‡‡ And this is the reason why we said, in the definition, that the Idea ‡‡‡‡ arises from
<margin>20</margin>*an object*, ‡‡‡‡‡ *which really exists in Nature*. And with this we think we have sufficiently explained what kind of thing the soul is in general, understanding by this expression not only the Ideas which arise from *the existence of* corporeal modes, but also those which arise from the existence of every mode of the remaining attributes.

<header>APPENDIX II</header>

But, since we have no such knowledge of the remaining <margin>1</margin>
attributes as we have of extension, let us just see whether,
having regard to the modes of extension, we can discover a
more specific definition, and one that is more appro-
priate to express the essence of our souls, for this is the
real task before us. Now we shall presuppose here, as
something already demonstrated, that extension contains no
other modes than motion and rest, and that every particular
material thing is nothing more than a certain proportion of <margin>10</margin>
motion and rest—so much so, indeed, that even if extension
contained nothing else except motion alone or rest alone, then
no particular thing could be shown or exist in the whole of
extension. The human body, therefore, is nothing more than
a certain proportion of motion and rest. Now the “objective
essence” of this actual ratio *of motion and rest*
which is in the thinking attribute—this (we say) is the soul
of the body; so that whenever one of these two modes
changes into more or less (motion or rest) ‡ the Idea
*or the soul* also changes accordingly. For example, when
the <note>amount of</note> rest happens to increase, while the <note>quantity <margin>20</margin>
of</note> motion is diminished, then there is produced by this
that pain or sorrow which we call *cold*; but if, on the
contrary, this <note>increase</note> takes place in the <note>amount of</note>
motion, then there is produced by this that pain which we
call *heat*. ‡‡ And so when it happens that the degrees of
motion and rest are not equal in all the parts of our body,
but that some have more motion and rest than others, there

# COMMENTARY

->[The numbers in large type refer to the *pages* of the translation; those in smaller type refer to the *lines*.]<-

## TITLE-PAGES, ETC.

4. THE Preface on the title-page of A must have been written by an ardent follower of Spinoza, not by Spinoza himself. Therefore, Monnikhoff felt justified in substituting a new title-page (6) that was not offensive to the theologians. The engraved Portrait in A (which is reproduced here) is the same as that found in some copies of the *Opera Posthuma*, and was probably inserted in A by Monnikhoff, who also wrote the verses facing it. It is uncertain whether the portrait was engraved during Spinoza's lifetime. According to Rieuwertsz, as reported by Dr. Hallmann in 1704 (see *Introduction*, p. civ.), it was engraved some three or four years after the death of Spinoza, probably from the Wolfenbüttel portrait (see p. xcvii.).

The verses facing the portrait have been translated by Dr. Willis as follows:

> “ Here Art presents us with Spinoza’s face,
> In which we trace deep lines of sober thought;
> Yet the mental likeness is better shown
> To those who read and make his works their own.”

## FIRST PART

The First Part is devoted to the consideration of God, His existence, attributes, etc. The same ground was sub-

...sequently covered in the First Part of the *Ethics* (*De Deo*). This and other resemblances to the *Ethics* naturally suggested that the *Short Treatise* was an early draft of the *Ethics*. Monnikhoff actually put *Ethica* on the title page of manuscript B, and the *Short Treatise* is sometimes referred to as the "small *Ethics*."

### CHAPTER I

15. The opening is remarkably abrupt. The expression "as regards the first" suggests a preceding list of topics about to be discussed, but no such list is given, unless it is on the title page of the *Treatise*, namely, *God, Man, etc.* Monnikhoff tried to avoid this awkwardness by substituting "this" for "the first." But the abruptness remains and is even more striking because so many of the other chapters begin with a list of the topics to be discussed. Freudenthal has suggested that the original opening may have been as follows: "Man has an idea of God as a Being consisting of infinite attributes, each of which is infinitely perfect in its kind. First, we will show that such a Being exists, and then we shall give our views as to what He is. As regards the first . . ." This theory is based partly on the second sentence in chapter II, which seems to have been misplaced.

It is noteworthy that Spinoza begins with proofs *that* God exists, and only then proceeds to determine *what* He is. The reason may have been this: He was teaching people who were already fairly familiar with the fundamentals of Cartesian philosophy. He, therefore, began with the Cartesian proofs of God's existence and gradually led up to his own relatively strange conception of God. This kind of pedagogical method is not uncommon in the history of philosophy. Kant, for example, started from the then-current psychology and gradually led up to very different, almost startling results.

<summary>This page discusses the scholarly view that the Short Treatise served as an early draft of Spinoza’s Ethics and analyzes the abrupt opening of Chapter I. It also explains Spinoza's pedagogical use of Cartesian philosophy to introduce his own concepts of God.</summary>

<keywords>Spinoza, Ethics, Short Treatise, Monnikhoff, Freudenthal, Cartesian philosophy, Kant, God, attributes, pedagogical method</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the commentary on Chapter I of the treatise, specifically analyzing the Cartesian influence on Spinoza’s proofs and providing definitions for key philosophical terms used in the text.</meta>

# COMMENTARY

The proofs themselves are mainly (though not entirely) Cartesian. (See *Meditations*, III and V, and the Appendix in the translation of Descartes' *Method*, etc., by John Veitch). Unlike Descartes, however, Spinoza attaches the greatest weight to *a priori* arguments.

*15, 5. A priori.* An argument is said to be *a priori* when it proceeds from the character of a thing to its implications—from conditions to consequences, or from causes to effects. It is said to be *a posteriori* when it proceeds from consequences to conditions, or from effects to causes. These terms also have other meanings, but not in Spinoza’s work.

*15, 6 ff.* The underlying thought is expressed in Spinoza's <term>Principia Philosophiæ Cartesianæ</term> (*Principles of Cartesian Philosophy*), I. Def. ix: “When we say that something is contained in the nature or concept of a certain thing, that is the same as saying that it is true of that thing, or that it can be truly affirmed of that thing.”

*15, 7.* The word “nature” here means “character” or “essence.” More commonly it means the material world, or (in Spinoza and Bruno, for example) even the entire universe. Note † was intended to guard against this ambiguity.

*15, 13.* “Essence” is one of the most difficult terms in Spinoza's vocabulary. In the <term>Cogitata Metaphysica</term> (*Metaphysical Thoughts*) it is said to be “nothing else than that mode by which created objects are comprehended in the attributes of God.” Briefly, the essence of a thing is its share of, or participation in, ultimate reality. In the case of God, essence and existence coincide. In the case of other things, their existence as relatively independent entities is distinct from their essence.

“Eternity,” in its stricter sense, does not mean “incessant duration in time,” but reality independently of time or beyond it.

*15, 16.* “The existence of God is essence.” Compare Maimonides' *Guide of the Perplexed*, I. lvii.—“It is known that existence is an accident <note>meaning quality</note> appertaining to all

But as for a being whose existence is not due to any cause—and God alone is that being, for His existence, as we have said, is absolute—existence and essence are perfectly identical. He is not a substance to which existence is joined as an accident, or as an additional element. His existence is always absolute, and has never been a new element or an accident in Him" (Friedländer's translation, 2nd ed., p. 80).

16, 2. The reference mark † is apparently misplaced because the note it refers to actually follows up on the preceding *a priori* arguments, and therefore belongs to 15, 17.

16, 3. *Formaliter* = actually or objectively (in the modern sense). The identification of *formalis* and *actualis* in medieval philosophy was due to the influence of <term>Aristotelianism</term>. According to <term>Aristotle</term>, individual things are compounds of Matter and Form, and Form is the more important of the two. Matter is that which is still imperfect or merely potential, which requires Form to make it actual. Hence, during the supremacy of <term>Aristotelian</term> philosophy in the Middle Ages, Matter was identified with Potentiality, and Form with Actuality, so that *formalis* = *actualis*.

16, 9. "*Objective*" = in thought, or subjectively (in the modern sense). The present use of the terms "subjective" and "objective" is the reverse of former usage. The term "subject" (*subjectum* = <term>ὑποκείμενον</term>) used to mean the substrate or concrete reality supporting or "underlying" its properties, and hence also the subject of predication, because in predication these properties or qualities are

# COMMENTARY

generally predicated of their "subject." (For an illustration of the older use of the term "subject," see, for example, 18, 23). By "object" (*objectum* = *ἀντικείμενον*), on the other hand, was meant something which consisted in "lying opposite" or before the mind (*quatenus objicitur intellectui*), so that "objective" referred only to the sphere of thought. This usage is already found in the writings of Duns Scotus (died 1308), and continued, with some modifications, right into the eighteenth century—Berkeley, for example, still used "real" as an antithesis to "objective." The noun "object" (*objectum*) acquired its present meaning long before the adjective did. Descartes already used the term "objects" for "things" ("*in objectis, hoc est in rebus.*"—*Principia Phil.*). The transition to the present meaning of "subjective" was probably brought about by the application of the term *subjectum* to the soul as distinguished from (or as the bearer of) its "objective" ideas. (Leibniz, for example, used the expression: "*subjectum* or the soul itself.") Hence "subjective" came to indicate whatever had reference to the soul.

<meta>This page continues the philological and philosophical commentary on Spinoza's arguments, specifically focusing on the ontological proof and the distinction between ideas and their objects, as well as the scholastic terminology used by Spinoza and Descartes.</meta>

# GOD, MAN, AND HIS WELL-BEING

another attribute (existence, in this case). The objection to this is that the implied additional attribute may be true of the Idea, but not of the *Ideatum*. And this is met by the argument that the new attribute is inferred from the other (essential) attributes, and if it is to be predicated at all, it can only be predicated of that which has those other (essential) attributes. Now the *Idea* is not actually composed (*materialiter*) of those attributes or qualities; these really pertain to the *Ideatum*. If, therefore, the new attribute (existence) follows at all from the others, it must be predicated of the *Ideatum*, not of the *Idea*, which is *materialiter* so unlike the *Ideatum* that the same attributes cannot be affirmed of both. This argument does not prove the accuracy of the ontological proof; but it seems to have been directed only against the half-hearted acceptance of it as valid in so far as the *Idea* of God was concerned.

17, 33 *f.* In opposition to Descartes, Spinoza maintains that man could not of himself produce any idea whatever. The elementary constituent ideas, even of fictions, must have been called forth in man by external causes. Descartes only insisted that man could not produce the idea of God; Spinoza extends the denial to all ideas. Compare 16, 18 *ff.*

18, 5. *Essentia objectiva* or "objective" essence = the essence of a thing as represented in thought. The correspondence between an *idea* and its *ideatum*, or object, is described in the language of scholastic philosophy as a kind of two-fold existence of the "essence" of that object. The essence exists *formaliter* (actually) in the individual concrete thing; it exists *objective*, or has "objective" essence, in thought (as an idea).

18, 8. *Formaliter—eminenter.* This scholastic antithesis has reference to the relation of a cause to its effect. If the cause contained more reality or greater perfection than its effect, then it was said to be an eminent cause, or to produce its effect *eminenter* or *modo eminentiori*. In this way,

<meta>This page continues the philological and philosophical commentary on Spinoza's work, specifically detailing the distinctions between "formal" and "eminent" causes, the nature of attributes and properties, and the term "causa sui."</meta>

->COMMENTARY<-

For example, God (according to Descartes) is the "eminent" cause of the human mind. But if, on the other hand, the effect contained only as much perfection as its cause (it cannot, of course, contain less), it was said to have been produced *formaliter* or *secundum eandem formam* (according to the same form). Thus, for example, the pressure of a foot was said to cause a footprint *formaliter*. This use of *formaliter* is different from the one explained in the preceding note.

The words "though not *eminenter* . . . outside him" seem to be both irrelevant and inaccurate. Possibly they are only a reader's comment. It is not clear why God's supreme excellence should prevent Him from being the *eminent* cause of our idea of Him. The opposite view would seem more reasonable. Probably it is implied that the *idea of God* contains as much perfection *objective* as God has *formaliter*; its cause, therefore, can only be *formal*, not *eminent*, because nothing (not even God Himself) is *more* perfect *formaliter* than the idea of God is *objective*.

18, 13. *Pegasus*, for instance. *Cf.* Descartes, *Med. V.*

18, 23. *Subjectum*—see note to 16, 9.

19, 3. *Attributes*. The expression is used here in its more common meaning, not in the stricter sense in which it is generally employed in Spinoza's writings. Hence note †. In the stricter sense of the term "attribute," only two attributes of God or Nature are known to us, namely, Extension and Thought. Each of these is a *summum genus*, and is not derived from anything else. "Properties" (*propria* or *proprietates*) are derivative; they follow from the attributes. The "attributes" referred to in the text are, as note † explains, only "properties," because they are not *summa genera*; they are not "substantial" or self-dependent, but imply the "attributes" which constitute "substance" or the self-dependent reality (God or Nature).

20, 15. *Causa sui* = the self-existent. The expression is awkward and misleading. Spinoza did not invent it; it was

Strictly speaking, that which is *causa sui*, or "its own cause," really has no cause at all. Spinoza himself has pointed out the absurd implication of the phrase *causa sui*. It seems to imply that something which did not exist could yet operate in such a way as to bring itself into existence (see 114, 6 *ff.*, and 146, 18 *ff.*).

### CHAPTER II

21, 4 *ff.* This chapter begins immediately with a definition of God, but without any indication as to the way in which the definition has been arrived at. Note †, however, makes it clear that Spinoza really started with the traditional conception of God as *Ens perfectissimum*, or "the most perfect Being" (*see lines 18 ff.*), and developed his conception from it.

It is noteworthy that the definition of God given here does not describe Him as "Substance," as does the later definition in the *Ethics*. Here the definition of God is followed up by an independent treatment of the notion "Substance" (lines 9 *ff.*), and it is then made apparent that the two notions "God" and "Substance" converge.

21, 9. Spinoza begins his account of "substance" without defining what he means by that term. He evidently

--- Page 315 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical commentary on Spinoza's *Short Treatise*, specifically discussing his transition from Cartesian dualism to his own monistic definition of Substance, God, and Nature.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>173</page-num>
<header>COMMENTARY</header>
<sig>M</sig>

Spinoza starts from the Cartesian doctrine of two ultimate kinds of substances—namely, Extension and Thought—and then suddenly shows that there can be only one Substance. To begin with, he tacitly assumes the possibility that multiple substances could exist, all grounded in the perfection of God. Once he has shown that there is only one Substance, he identifies it with God by identifying both with Nature. Descartes had defined "substance" as "a thing which exists in such a way as to need nothing beyond itself for its existence." "In truth" (he added), "only one Substance can be conceived which is absolutely independent, and that is God." However, he applied the term "substance" (or "created substance") to minds and bodies because, except for God, nothing else is required for their existence. Pointing out that Thought and Extension were the "principal attributes" constituting the essence of minds and bodies respectively, he spoke of Extension and Thought as the ultimate and distinct kinds of substances. These substances acquired such a degree of independence that Descartes was unable to reunite them, except in a superficial way.

Spinoza approached the subject with the conviction that Nature was One and perfect, in the fullest sense of those terms. He consequently took "Substance" quite seriously. The only truly independent or self-dependent being was the complete system of Reality, or Nature. Hence, beginning with a somewhat looser (Cartesian) conception of "Substance," he gradually led to the conclusion that there was only one substance, of which all other so-called substances were either attributes or modifications.

**21, 21 ff.** Note †† presents the argument of the text up to 25, 13 in a different form.

**23, 23 ff.** This distinction between "creating" and "generating" also occurs in the *Cogitata Metaphysica*, II. x., where, however, he seems to justify the possibility of creation,

only confining it to substances (in the Cartesian sense) or attributes. "A created object" (he says there) "is one which presupposes for its existence nothing except God." Modes and accidents also presuppose the attributes or substances of Extension and Thought. They are consequently not "created," but "generated." Only that has been created "whose essence is clearly conceived even without existence, and is conceived, moreover, *per se*"—Extension is given as an instance. As soon as Spinoza identified Extension, Thought, and all other (unknown) attributes with God, there was no room for this notion of "creation," except, perhaps, in that meaningless sense in which it is still implied in the expression *causa sui* <note>Self-caused</note>. Hence the denial of "creation" in the present treatise. And the "essences" of things, though described in the *Cogitata Metaphysica* as having been "created," must here be regarded simply as eternal. Traces of the earlier belief in "creation" are, however, still observable in the *Treatise*, pp. 24, 57.

**24, 24 *ff.*** *Cf.* <note>Compare</note> Maimonides' *Guide* (II. xx. p. 190): "The series of causes ends with the First Cause, from which everything derives its existence, since it is impossible that the series should continue *in infinitum* <note>To infinity</note>." Here, then, Spinoza agrees with Maimonides, and with the Aristotelians generally, that the causal series cannot continue *in infinitum*. But his views changed later, and in his 12th Letter we find Spinoza praising Rabbi Hasdai Crescas for providing an argument for the existence of God that is independent of the supposed impossibility of such an infinite causal regression.

**24, 30.** "This only substance." Spinoza does not say what substance he means, but he clearly identifies it with God.

**24, 31.** "Substance *or* attribute." The expression is noteworthy. When writing the *Short Treatise*, Spinoza was still very much under the influence of Cartesian terminology.

<meta>This page continues the philosophical commentary on Spinoza's development of metaphysical terminology, specifically the evolving relationship between the terms "substance" and "attribute" in his early writings and letters.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>175</page-num>
<header>COMMENTARY</header>

He was still inclined to speak of Extension and Thought as substances. It seemed to him a matter of indifference whether these were described as "substances" or as "attributes"; he used either term, and sometimes both together, as seen here (*see, e.g.,* 28, 13; 29, 5; 34, 2 *ff.*; 154, 18). At this stage, in fact, he defined "attribute" in the same terms as he subsequently defined "substance." In his 2nd Letter (1661), he defined "attribute" as "whatever is conceived through itself and in itself, so that its conception does not involve the conception of anything else." He illustrated his meaning by comparing Extension with motion. Extension can be conceived through itself and in itself, and is therefore an attribute; motion, on the other hand, cannot be conceived without Extension; it is therefore not an attribute, but only a mode (or modification) of an attribute (Extension). In his 9th Letter (1663), Spinoza defined "substance" in the same terms as the preceding definition of "attribute," and explicitly identified the two. "By substance I mean that which exists in itself, and is conceived through itself; that is, the conception of which does not involve the conception of anything else. I mean the same by attribute, except that it is called attribute with respect to the intellect which ascribes a certain character to substance." This, Spinoza added, will explain what he meant by using the expression, "substance *or* attribute." Briefly, "substance" simply consists of its "attributes," but of *all* of them; while each "attribute" is only one (ultimate and real) aspect or feature of "substance." The totality of attributes is therefore identical with substance, and Spinoza accordingly felt free to speak sometimes as though he ignored the difference between "substance" and "attribute." This, however, caused some difficulty among his disciples and friends. He therefore eventually adopted the stricter distinction found in the *Ethics*. But even the *Ethics* still retains traces of the earlier and looser usage; in

25, 2 *f*. What Spinoza meant by the argument "from the simplicity of God's will" may be explained by the following passage in Maimonides' *Guide of the Perplexed* (II. xiv.): "An agent is active at one time and inactive at another, according to whether the circumstances are favorable or unfavorable. . . . As, however, God is not subject to accidents which could bring about a change in His will, and is not affected by obstacles and hindrances that might appear or disappear, it is impossible, they <note>i.e., those who maintain the eternity of the world</note> argue, to imagine that God is active at one time and inactive at another. He is, on the contrary, always active in the same manner as He is always in actual existence" (p. 175).

"*En dat meer is, zo doende zouden er oneyndelijke zelfstandigheden meer niet zijn als er zijn, het welke ongerijmt is.*" Quite literally it means, ". . . there would be no more infinite substances than there are . . ." --- Page 319 ---
<meta>This page continues the scholarly commentary on Spinoza's philosophy, specifically discussing the identification of God with Nature and contrasting Spinoza's views on divine omnipotence with those of Maimonides and Descartes.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-num>177</page-num>
<header>COMMENTARY</header>
<page-type>text</page-type>

Whether an insertion or not, the passage does not seem to me to be truly a digression. Its purpose is to confirm, through a different line of argument, the identification of God with Nature, which is the main point of the preceding paragraph. The usual conception of Nature as created by, and distinct from, God, silently assumes that there is a difference between the ideas or plans of God and His realization or actualization of these ideal possibilities. By attacking this distinction between the ideal and the real, between the possible and the actual, Spinoza evidently helps to confirm his identification of God and Nature, both of which are real, and represent the totality of all that is real. Viewed in this way, the passage forms an important part of Spinoza’s argument, and we find it repeated in the *Ethics*, I. xvii. Schol.

25, 24 *f.* Spinoza’s view that God “cannot create what is self-contradictory” is also found in Maimonides and is opposed to the view of Descartes. Descartes placed no limitations whatever on God’s omnipotence (except apparently in *Med. VI.*); even contradictory propositions might be true together if God willed it so. Maimonides, on the other hand, maintained that even God could not endow a thing with contradictory qualities (*Guide*, III. xv. p. 279).

25, 25. “As it is . . .” = for it is self-contradictory, or it is like expecting God to do what is self-contradictory when we say, etc.

25, 31. Merely verbal alternatives. See note to 15, 21.

26, 1 *ff.* The subtle conundrum of whether God can know more than He does know was actually discussed by Peter Lombard, Bishop of Paris (died 1134).

26, 22 *f.* “Although . . .” Spinoza is here alluding to Descartes’ assumption (*Princ. Phil.* I. lx.) that “it is sufficient to assure us that two substances are really mutually distinct if only we are able clearly and distinctly to conceive the one of them without the other.”

26, 32. “Infinite,” that is, in number as well as in extent.

27, 3 f. It has not yet been shown that it is impossible for substances to begin to exist; nor is it shown in this treatise as we now have it. Something seems, therefore, to be missing.

27, 6 f. “Substance” is not used here in the stricter sense, but instead of “attribute.” The “essence” of the one “substance” (in the stricter meaning) does involve “existence,” but when either Thought or Extension is “considered separately,” then we can conceive it clearly without assuming its existence (cf. <term>*Cogitata Metaphysica*</term> I. ii.). Note †† corrects the loose usage of the word “substance” in the text.

28, 1 f. Descartes, for example, argued that “God cannot be a body,” because extension involves divisibility, and this in turn implies passivity, which is an imperfection because it implies dependence on something else (<term>*Principia Philosophiae*</term> I. xxiii.).

Spinoza distinguishes as follows between a real thing (<term>*ens reale*</term>), a chimera, a thing of reason (<term>*ens rationis*</term>), and a fiction (<term>*ens fictum*</term>): A chimera is only a verbal expression denoting something which can neither exist, nor be conceived, because it involves a self-contradiction <note>for example, a square circle</note>; a thing of reason (or a merely logical entity) is a mode of thought which does not exist outside the thinking mind, though it may be an important means of representing realities outside the mind <note>for example, genera and species, time, number, and measure</note>; a fiction is “a thing of reason,” insofar as it is only a mode of thought (or of imagination) and has no corresponding reality outside the mind; but not all “things of reason” are fictions—only those which involve arbitrary or accidental imaginary

28, 7. “Nature” is here used in the narrower and more usual sense, namely, as equivalent to “the physical world.” In the wider sense peculiar to Spinoza and Bruno, “Nature” = Substance = the entire Universe. In “Nature” thus regarded, “things of reason” have reality as modes of thought. Hence the note (line 13), “In Nature, that is, in substantial extension” = in the so-called Substance Extension, or in “Substance” regarded solely under the “attribute” Extension.

30, 1 f. What is here said to have been “already stated” is first considered in the Dialogues which follow, and in chapter iii. Apparently something is missing from the preceding part of the Treatise.

30, 3. An “immanent” or “inner” cause is a cause whose effects are confined within itself, as distinguished from a “transeunt” or “transitive” cause which operates on things outside itself. God, according to Spinoza, is an “immanent” cause for the same reason that he is <term>*causa sui*</term>, namely, because “outside God there is nothing at all,” whether to affect Him or to be affected by Him. This conception involves, of course, the view that God is not outside or above the world, but in it. In other words, Spinoza’s God is not a transcendent but an immanent God. And since the time of Spinoza the doctrine of divine immanence has become a commonplace among theologians of all

31, 7. "An extraneous denomination" (<term>denominatio extrinsica</term> or <term>externa</term>) or "external designation" is contrasted with an "intrinsic denomination" (<term>denominatio interna</term> or <term>intrinsica</term>). The latter unfolds the essential attributes of a thing; the former only the non-essential properties, accidents, etc. The term is used somewhat loosely here. Usually Spinoza means by "<term>denominatio extrinsica</term>" a term that indicates the relation of one thing to another—or what one thing is or does to another, as distinguished from what it is in itself. In this more usual sense, self-existence, eternity, unity, and immutability could hardly be described as "extraneous denominations." Possibly there is a slight confusion in the text; or the division which Spinoza intended may have been as follows. Whatever is predicated of God denotes either (a) what is essential in Him, or (b) what is not essential; if non-essential (b), then it indicates either (i) a "property" of God other than, though deducible from, His "essential attributes," but still representing what God

As long as Spinoza did not use the term "attribute" in the strict sense in which he here distinguishes it from "properties," anything coming under (*a*) or (*b* i) would be designated as *denominatio intrinsica*, while (*b* ii) alone would be described as *denominatio extrinsica*. But due to his stricter usage, he had no suitable name for (*b* i) as distinguished from both (*a*) and (*b* ii). He seems, therefore, to have grouped (*b* i) and (*b* ii) together as "extraneous denominations" in a wider sense. If so, the word "either" has somehow been misplaced.

31, 11. "What he is"—that is, *essentially*.

### THE DIALOGUES

32. The *Outline* of the *Short Treatise* which was discovered and published by Boehmer does not mention the Dialogues, although it refers to the Notes and the Appendices. This seems unsettling at first. Yet no one has seriously questioned the authenticity of the Dialogues. Their contents are as intimately connected with the line of thought explained in the rest of the *Short Treatise* as the contents of the *Treatise* itself are with the trend of thought in Spinoza's *Ethics*. But although their authenticity cannot be disputed, it may be questioned whether they originally formed part of the *Treatise*, or were only added later either by Spinoza or someone else. The tendency is to regard them as more or less independent essays, which were only inserted later in their current place by a disciple or copyist. If their insertion was an afterthought, then it is quite conceivable that some of the manuscripts of the *Short Treatise* may not have contained the Dialogues;

<summary>This page analyzes Spinoza's categorization of God's properties as "extraneous denominations" and addresses the scholarly debate regarding the original placement and authenticity of the Dialogues in the Short Treatise.</summary>
<keywords>Spinoza, Short Treatise, Ethics, Boehmer, Dialogues, attribute, property, denominatio intrinsica, denominatio extrinsica</keywords>

--- Page 324 ---
<meta>This page continues the scholarly commentary on Spinoza’s work, focusing on the historical authenticity and chronological placement of the Dialogues in relation to the Short Treatise.</meta>

<page-num>182</page-num>
<header>GOD, MAN, AND HIS WELL-BEING</header>

and if Boehmer's *Outline* was based on such a manuscript, the omission of all reference to the Dialogues would thus be accounted for. The fact that they are given in both codices, A and B—of which A may have been copied already during Spinoza's lifetime—is certainly in their favor.

With remarkable agreement, most critics have treated the Dialogues as the oldest of Spinoza's known writings. The arguments for this view mostly turn on their supposed immaturity, fragmentariness, and crudeness. Freudenthal, however, has shown (*Spinozastudien*, II.) that this view is untenable, because the Dialogues are really unintelligible unless they are read in the light of various ideas explained in different parts of the *Short Treatise*. He maintains (rightly, we think) that the Dialogues were written *after* the bulk of the *Short Treatise*, as separate and fuller elucidations of certain problems already briefly dealt with in the *Treatise*, assuming a familiarity with those topics. It is this tacit reliance on the exposition of various views already given in the *Treatise* that gives the Dialogues an appearance of fragmentariness and crudeness. In reality, they are no more immature than the rest of the *Short Treatise*, while their very assumption of the various doctrines explained in the *Treatise* shows that they must have been written later.

To some extent, Sigwart anticipated Freudenthal's view by showing that the second Dialogue might very well have been written *after* the rest of the *Treatise*. But he insisted that the first Dialogue must have been written some years *before* the *Treatise*. The two Dialogues, however, can hardly be separated. The second one really takes up the theme with which the first concludes, and the closing remarks of the second Dialogue seem to revert deliberately to the opening words of the first.

<summary>This page analyzes the chronological origin of Spinoza's Dialogues, arguing against the traditional view of their immaturity and supporting Freudenthal's theory that they were written after the Short Treatise as advanced clarifications.</summary>
<keywords>Spinoza, Dialogues, Short Treatise, Boehmer, Freudenthal, Sigwart, codices, Spinozastudien, chronology, manuscript authenticity</keywords>

## FIRST DIALOGUE

The insertion of this Dialogue here was undoubtedly suggested by the references to Nature in the preceding (second) chapter (pp. 24–27). This is because this Dialogue provides a further explanation of Spinoza’s conception of Nature. The view of Nature as animated and as coinciding with the Universe in its full entirety and eternity is also found in the writings of Giordano Bruno, especially in the Dialogues *De la Causa*, etc. Avenarius and Sigwart have cited numerous passages from Bruno that are similar in intent to this and other parts of the *Short Treatise*. They even regard this Dialogue as representing an early stage in Spinoza's history when he was under the more or less dominant influence of Bruno. However, no conclusive evidence has been produced so far to show that Spinoza was even acquainted with Bruno’s writings. Even Sigwart did not feel certain about this matter. Martineau thought that most of the resemblances between Bruno and Spinoza were superficial and illusory. Neoplatonic views similar to those of Bruno were very much present in the intellectual atmosphere of the sixteenth and seventeenth centuries, and Spinoza may have become familiar with them through Jewish and other sources. In any case, the resemblance between Spinoza and Bruno is by no means fundamental. Spinoza went far beyond Bruno. Despite all his rhapsodies on the infinity of Nature, Bruno never quite gave up the idea of a God who was somehow above and beyond Nature—his God was still *transcendent*; Spinoza, on the other hand, never wavered. He took his conception of the infinity of Nature very strictly, and following its apparently logical implication, he boldly identified Nature with God and conceived of God as absolutely *immanent* in Nature. Avenarius and others, basing their views on the supposed early date of this Dialogue, have distinguished three phases of Spinoza’s Pantheism. In all of them

<summary>This page discusses the philosophical origins of Spinoza's First Dialogue, comparing his strictly immanent view of God and Nature with the more transcendent views of Giordano Bruno. Scholars debate whether Spinoza was directly influenced by Bruno or simply shared a common intellectual atmosphere shaped by Neoplatonism.</summary>
<keywords>Spinoza, Giordano Bruno, Nature, Pantheism, Avenarius, Sigwart, Martineau, immanent, transcendent, Neoplatonism, Short Treatise, De la Causa</keywords>

--- Page 326 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of the development of Spinoza’s philosophy, specifically addressing the theory that his starting point evolved from "Nature" to "God" and finally to "Substance."</meta>

<page-num>184</page-num>
<header>GOD, MAN, AND HIS WELL-BEING</header>

Spinoza identified the three terms, *God*, *Nature*, and *Substance*, by showing that the same attributes apply to each of them. But it is said that at different stages, Spinoza started with a different term as his starting point. In the first Dialogue, under the supposed influence of Bruno, he set out from the term *Nature*; this was the first phase. The *Short Treatise* was supposed to represent the second phase when, under the influence of Descartes, he took his start from the term *God* (*see* chapter 1). Lastly, the *Ethics* was said to represent the third phase, when, having attained complete independence and maturity, Spinoza commenced with the term *Substance*. But this whole conception of the development of Spinoza’s philosophy is untenable. The supposed influence of Bruno is problematic. The first Dialogue already shows a knowledge of Descartes. And Spinoza’s attitude towards Cartesianism is fundamentally antagonistic both in the *Short Treatise* and in the Dialogues. No Cartesian could think of identifying God with Nature. As far as his writings show, Spinoza identified God, Nature, and Substance from the first, and seems to have attached no peculiar significance to any of them as a starting point. It is true, of course, as Martineau and others have pointed out, that the three terms, “though identical in their application, differ somewhat in their meaning; under *Nature* we are expected to think of the continuous *Source of birth*; under *God*, of the universal *cause of things*; under *Substance*, of the permanent reality behind phenomena.” But that is another matter.

32, II, 15. “Understanding”—“Reason.” Understanding is hardly the right word for what is meant here by the Dutch *Verstand* = *Intellectus*. “Spirit” or “spiritual insight” might be better in some respects. It represents the highest form of knowledge, namely, knowledge by way of immediate intuition. Reason, on the other hand, represents the lower grade of knowledge by way of discursive

inference. It will be observed that “Understanding” does not argue, but simply delivers its “immediate apprehension” (*aanschouw*), and takes no further part in the debate. The distinction between Understanding and Reason is explained in Book II, chapters 21, 22, and 26, and a knowledge of this distinction is evidently assumed in this Dialogue. In the opening chapters of Book II, the same distinction is drawn between Belief and Clear Knowledge. However, the nomenclature in this Dialogue agrees with that in the *later* chapters.

32, 17. In omitting from the text the words given in the footnote (p. 32), we have adopted a suggestion by Freudenthal, which makes the meaning quite clear. All the words (except “namely”) which we have moved to the footnote, as well as the words “we avoid this absurdity by stating that” (lines 17–18), are written in the margin in A. All these marginal additions make the text unintelligible. Apparently, the words given in the footnote represent a reader’s attempt to overcome the obscurity caused by the accidental omission of the words “we avoid this absurdity by stating that”; but when this omission was corrected, the other additions were still retained because their origin and significance were unknown to the copyist.

32, 21 f. “Desire” here means “evil desire” (*concupiscentia*), not *cupiditas*. Freudenthal has pointed out that the expression usually employed in the *Short Treatise* for “Desire” is *Begeerte*, while here we have *Begeerlijkheid*. Moreover, Spinoza’s conception of the function of “desire” (*cupiditas*) as such is very different from the sinister role which *Begeerlijkheid* plays in this Dialogue.

“Desire” voices here the dualistic view of Descartes: that there are two kinds of substances (extended and thinking substances) which have nothing in common. Spinoza combats this view in favor of his own monistic theory.

33, 26 *and following*. The relation of substance to its attributes is here described as a causal relationship; the attributes are *supported* by substance; they depend on it not only logically, but *causally*.

33, 29 *and following*. The attributes are not actually called “modes” here; their relation to substance is simply *compared* (for the sake of argument) with that of modes to attributes.

34, 12. A “second notion” (*notio secunda*) is contrasted with a “first notion” (*notio prima*). The latter represents what things really are, while the former is some mode of conceiving things. The same antithesis was also expressed by another pair of scholastic terms, namely, *intentio prima* and *intentio secunda*. What the mind “intends” or apprehends in the first instance is some concrete reality (say, a particular tree), and this constitutes the “first intention”; but as the result of reflecting on and comparing such “first intentions or notions” (as, *for example*, when we compare various trees, and mentally classify them into genera and species,

34, 16. “The thinking power.” The attribute of Thought is also described by Spinoza as a “power” in Letter 32 (*statuo dari in Natura potentiam infinitam cogitandi* <note>"I maintain that there is in nature an infinite power of thinking"</note>) and in the *Ethics*, Book II, Proposition 1, Scholium, and Book II, Proposition 21, Scholium. On page 120 (line 4), the attribute of Extension is similarly described as a “power.” The attributes thus seem to be conceived here as various “lines of force” through which God manifests or reveals Himself.

### SECOND DIALOGUE

36. In Dr. W. Meyer’s modern Dutch version of the *Short Treatise*, the second Dialogue is appended to the next chapter. His reason will be considered in the first note to that chapter. It is noteworthy that the concluding words of chapter 2 (31, 16) do not refer to a second Dialogue—they only refer to *a* Dialogue (one, not two). This, however, may only mean that the insertion of the second Dialogue in this place was an afterthought. But it can hardly be separated from the first Dialogue. It is the reference to the distinction between immanent and transeunt <note>Transeunt (or transient) causality refers to a cause whose action produces an effect outside of itself, as opposed to immanent causality, where the cause remains within the effect.</note> causality at the end of the first Dialogue that provides the theme of the second; and the concluding remarks of the second seem to refer deliberately to the opening remarks of the first.

36, 3. “Theophilus.” This name (in the Italian forms *Teofilo* and *Filoteo*) also occurs in Bruno’s Dialogues *De la Causa*, etc.; and in Bruno’s Dialogues, as in this one, the author’s own views are put into the mouth of “Theophilus.” This may be a mere coincidence, as the name would naturally occur to a writer whose moral ideal was “the love of God.”

36, 6-8. The reference is to 35, 3, and 41, 19.

36, 9-20. See 42, 21-24. An acquaintance with this passage is clearly assumed.

36, 9. "A remote cause" is contrasted with a *proximate* cause. The latter produces its effect *immediately*, without the intervention of anything else, while the former produces its (remote) effect by means of an intervening proximate cause or a chain of proximate causes. The terms *proximate* and *remote* are relative to a given effect; every cause might be both proximate and remote, but not in relation to the same effect. A *remote* cause was supposed to be separated from, and not in contact with, its effect. Hence the difficulty raised in the text as to how an *immanent* cause could also be a *remote* cause.

final reply to the question raised there. This conception of the Understanding (or Intellect) as the supreme bond of union between Man and God is essentially Aristotelian, and was adopted by the leading Jewish philosophers of the Middle Ages, notably by Maimonides (*see* the author's *Aristotle in Medieval Jewish Thought*). In his *Guide* (III. li.), Maimonides says expressly that “Man’s love of God is identical with his knowledge of Him”; he also uses the expression “intellectual worship of God,” which is so like Spinoza’s “intellectual love of God.”

## CHAPTER III

41. The way in which this chapter is copied in Codex A is likely to raise suspicion. The second Dialogue ends near the bottom of the page, leaving just about as much space as is left at the bottom of most pages in that manuscript. The last line of the Dialogue contains only the last two words. Then, in the middle of the same line, we have “Chapter III,” and four lines of very small writing follow to the very bottom of the page. The next page shows the same small handwriting, which, however, gets larger toward the end of that page, where the usual space is left. On the following page, there are only five lines of large scrawl, with more than half the page being left blank. The concluding five lines of Chapter III are written on the next page and are immediately followed, on the same page, by “Chapter IV.” The numeral IV has also been tampered with, as have the numbers at the heads of several subsequent chapters. And since Chapter III treats divine causality generally, while the second Dialogue is devoted more particularly to God’s immanent causality, Dr. W. Meyer holds that the second Dialogue was misplaced by the copyist and should really follow Chapter III. But with due deference to Dr. Meyer, it seems doubtful whether the facts really necessitate this

construction. It seems obvious that Chapter III was copied into manuscript A after Chapter IV (and possibly some of the rest of the *Treatise*) had already been copied. But the copyist had evidently left a space for Chapter III, though he miscalculated the amount of space required. It is known that Spinoza's manuscripts circulated among his friends in parts, just as they were completed. Most likely the copyist of A had the manuscript of Chapter IV before he had that of Chapter III, so he left some blank pages for the latter and copied it when he received it afterward. Chapter III is in the same handwriting as the rest of the *Treatise* in Codex A. And regarding the alterations in the numerals, it appears certain to me that the Arabic numerals have simply been changed into Roman ones—the change being probably made by the meticulous Monnikhoff. Lastly, regarding the contents of Chapter III and the second Dialogue, Spinoza is not at all concerned in this *Treatise* about repeating himself, and the second Dialogue, as already shown, has a point of contact with the first. If we were to look very closely at the continuity of content, several of the chapters of the *Treatise* would have to be rearranged, as, indeed, Dr. Meyer himself has pointed out.

41, 12 *ff.* The elaborate classification of causes to which Spinoza refers in this chapter is to be found in Franco Burgersdijck’s *Institutionum Logicarum Libri Duo*. Burgersdijck, as already stated, was a Professor of Philosophy at Leyden in the early decades of the seventeenth century, and his book on logic, to judge by the numerous editions still in existence, must have been a very popular manual. Several editions of the book were edited by Burgersdijck's successor, Heereboord, to whom Spinoza refers in his *Metaphysical Thoughts*, II. xii. It was this reference to Heereboord that Trendelenburg used as a clue to decipher this complicated and somewhat obscure classification of causes.

Though complex, the classification was really not so far-fetched as it may first appear. Substitute "conditions" for

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Franco Burgersdijck’s influence on Spinoza, specifically focusing on the classification of causes as detailed in Burgersdijck’s Logic and how Spinoza adopts these definitions.</meta>

<page-num>191</page-num>
<header>COMMENTARY 191</header>

“causes,” and the classification still contains much that is true and valuable. If by “cause” we mean “the totality of conditions,” then there is no room for such an elaborate classification of causes. But for all practical purposes, we are satisfied to apply the term “cause” to something much less than “the totality of conditions.” Mill has shown how arbitrary popular usage is in singling out first one condition and then another as “the cause,” when, as a matter of fact, all conditions are equally necessary, even if they are not equally striking or interesting on different occasions of the same kind of occurrence. It was according to this broader and looser use of the term that “causes” were classified in such a detailed way. The accompanying table (*see next page*) is taken from Burgersdijck’s *Logic* (p. 282 of the London edition of 1651).

In the accompanying table, we see the then-standard Aristotelian division of Causes into *Material*, *Formal*, *Efficient*, and *Final*, each of these being subdivided again in various ways. It would take up too much space to deal with all of them here. We are only concerned with the eightfold division of *Efficient* causes, which Spinoza has in view in the present chapter. It will be observed that Spinoza lists them in precisely the same order as they are given in the following table from *Burgersdijck’s Logic*. The following definitions are also taken from the same book.

41, 15. An *emanative* cause is one which produces its effect by its mere existence, while an *active* (or *acting*) cause is one which produces its effect through the medium of some activity which it exercises. Fire, for instance, is the *emanative* cause of its own heat, but an *active* cause of the heat which it imparts to other things. Spinoza essentially does away with this distinction in the case of God. “Emanative” here has nothing to do with the “Emanation” theory of Neoplatonism or Mysticism. Spinoza did not use the expression in the *Ethics*, possibly in order to avoid this suggestion of “emanation.”

## BURGERSDIJCK’S CLASSIFICATION OF CAUSES

<image-desc>
A bracketed classification diagram (a branching logical tree) illustrating the various divisions of causes according to the philosopher Burgersdijck. The diagram proceeds from a single root on the left and expands into multiple nested categories on the right, using large curly braces to show group relations. 

The structure branches from "Cause is considered either" into:
1. "In general"
2. "In species; and these either as," which further divides into:
   - "Matter"
   - "Form"
   - "Efficient"
   - "End"

Each of these four types of causes is subdivided into numbered categories (I, II, III, etc.), often presented as pairs of opposites (e.g., First/Second, Natural/Artificial). One branch under "Efficient" (point V) further subdivides "Less principal" into three specific terms: "<term>Procatarctic</term>," "<term>Proegumenic</term>," and "Instrument."
</image-desc>

*Cause is considered either*
- *In general*
- *In species; and these either as*
    - *Matter; and this either*
        - I. *First*, *Second*.
        - II. *Of Natural Things*, *Of Artificial Things*.
        - III. *Of Generation*, *Of Composition*.
    - *Form; and this either*
        - I. *Material*, *Immaterial*.
        - II. *Substantial*, *Accidental*.
        - III. *Natural*, *Artificial*.
        - IV. *Principal*, *Disposing*.
    - *Efficient; and this either*
        - I. *Active*, *Emanative*.
        - II. *Immanent*, *Transient*.
        - III. *Free*, *Necessary*.
        - IV. *By itself* (*Per se*), *By accident* (*Per accidens*).
        - V. *Principal*, *Less principal*: <term>Procatarctic</term>, <term>Proegumenic</term>, *Instrument*.
        - VI. *First*, *Second*.
        - VII. *Universal*, *Particular*.
        - VIII. *Proximate*, *Remote*.
    - *End; and this either*
        - I. *For whom* (*Cui*), *Of which* (*Cujus*).
        - II. *Principal*, *Secondary*.
        - III. *Subordinate*, *Ultimate*.
