<meta>This page continues the commentary on the classification of causes, specifically defining the terms used in the previous diagram from Burgersdijck and comparing them to Spinoza's usage.</meta>

<page-num>193</page-num>
<header>COMMENTARY</header>

41, 19. The distinction between an <term>immanent</term> and a <term>transeunt</term> cause has already been explained in the note to 30, 3.

41, 22. A **free** cause (according to Burgersdijck) is one which acts from deliberate choice; a **natural** (or **necessary**) cause is one which acts from necessity (*causa libera est, quæ consulto—id est, ex judicio rationis—causat. Necessaria, quæ non consulto, sed necessitate naturæ causat*). This distinction, however, did not appeal to Spinoza. He used these antithetical terms somewhat differently. By a **free** cause (as will be seen in the next chapter) he meant one which acts without any external compulsion, or externally imposed necessity. In this sense a cause might be **free** although acting from necessity—namely, when the necessity was inherent in its own character, and not due to outside forces.

42, 1. A cause **through itself**, or <term>causa per se</term>, is one whose effects are due to its own natural character; a cause <term>per accidens</term> is one which produces a certain effect not as the result of its own character, but owing to some unusual circumstances. Heereboord gives the following illustration. When an animal gives birth to one of its own kind it is a <term>causa per se</term>, but when it gives birth to a monstrosity then it is <term>causa per accidens</term>. Burgersdijck remarks, with quiet humor, *Ad causam per accidens revocatur fortuna et casus* <note>To the accidental cause are referred fortune and chance</note>.

42, 4–14. A **principal** cause is one which produces an effect by virtue of its own powers alone, without the aid of anything else. A **subsidiary** cause (<term>causa minus principalis</term>) is merely one condition or factor which is necessary but not adequate to produce a certain effect. Three kinds of subsidiary causes were recognized. Spinoza refers to them all, but somewhat obscurely. In lines 7–9 he illustrates not the subsidiary cause in general, but one special form of it, namely, the instrumental cause (<term>instrumentum</term>). Almost any means employed in the production of an effect was called an instrumental cause. A second species of subsidiary cause is the **provoking** or **inciting** cause (<term>causa procatarctica</term> or cause

<summary>This page defines and distinguishes various types of causes—such as free vs. necessary, per se vs. per accidens, and principal vs. subsidiary—while highlighting how Spinoza’s definitions differ from traditional scholastic philosophy.</summary>
<keywords>Burgersdijck, Spinoza, Heereboord, immanent cause, transeunt cause, free cause, natural cause, causa per se, causa per accidens, principal cause, subsidiary cause, instrumental cause, causa procatarctica</keywords>

--- Page 336 ---
<meta>This page continues the commentary on the classification of causes, specifically explaining the subdivisions of subsidiary causes and comparing the logical systems known to Spinoza.</meta>

<page-num>194</page-num>
<header>GOD, MAN, AND HIS WELL-BEING</header>

*incipiens aut inchoans* [beginning or initiating])—that is, any external thing or condition which incites the *principal cause* to action. The third and last kind of subsidiary cause is the **predisposing cause** (<term>causa proegumena</term>), or some internal condition which predisposes a thing toward a certain kind of action or process. For instance, if a man with a weak chest becomes very ill as a result of a cold caught in a drafty place, then the draft would be described as the **provoking** (or **inciting**) cause, while his weak chest or feeble constitution would be the **predisposing cause**. (Bain made a somewhat similar distinction, though of wider applicability, when he analyzed a cause into a "moving power" and a "collocation of circumstances.")

Spinoza's departure from Burgersdijck's division of the <term>causa minus principalis</term> (subsidiary cause) is, I think, explained by the fluctuating views of the textbooks on this point. Clauberg (a copy of whose *Logica Vetus et Nova* Spinoza is known to have possessed) divided efficient causes into <term>causa principalis</term> and <term>causa instrumentalis</term> (principal and instrumental causes). No doubt this is the division Spinoza had in mind in lines 4–9. On the other hand, Keckermann (a copy of whose *Systema Logicæ* was also among Spinoza's books) divided them as follows:

| Primary | Secondary | Tertiary | Quaternary |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Causa efficiens** | principalis | | |
| | minus principalis | impulsiva | proegumena |
| | | | procatarctica |
| | | instrumentalis | |

This also gives the four subdivisions in practically the same order as Spinoza refers to them.

42, 15. A **first cause** is one which is not dependent on (or not the effect of) any other cause; a <term>causa secunda</term> (second cause) is dependent on a first cause.

42, 17. A **universal cause** was contrasted with a **particular** one as follows. The latter can only produce *one* kind of effect; the former can produce different kinds of effects by cooperating with various other causes. God, according

### CHAPTER IV

43. The theme of this chapter is also discussed by Spinoza in the *Ethics*, I. xvi. xvii. xxxiii., and in the *Tractatus Theologico-Politicus*, vi.

43, 23 *ff.* Compare the following passage from the *Cogitata Metaphysica* (I. iii.):
> “Since nothing exists except by divine power alone, it is easy to see that those things which come into existence do so by virtue of the decree and will of God. But since there is neither inconstancy nor change in God, He must have decreed from eternity that He would produce those things which He produces now; and as nothing more is required for a thing to exist than God’s decree that it should exist, it follows that all created things have been under an eternal necessity to exist. Nor can we say that they are contingent because God could have decreed otherwise; for, since in eternity there is no *when*, *before*, or *after*, or any other change of time, it follows that God did not exist before those things were decreed, so as to be able to decree otherwise at all.”

On the other hand, in *Cogit. Metaph.* II. vii. Spinoza says that “if God willed it so, created things would have a different essence.”

44, 19. The usual scholastic definition of Freedom. Burgersdijck (*Inst. Log.* cap. xvii.) says: *Causa libera potest agere quicquid, quantum, et quando lubet.* Heereboord (*Coll. Eth.* p. 114—quoted by Sigwart) says distinctly that most philosophers defined free will as *facultas quæ positis omnibus ad agendum requisitis potest agere et non agere, aut ita agere unum, ut contrarium agere possit.* *Cf. Descartes, Med.* IV.

48, 10. <term>Accidental</term> refers to that which is neither necessary nor impossible. In the passages referred to above, Spinoza distinguishes between the “contingent” and the “possible,” which may be regarded as the two species of the “accidental.” The main point is that according to him nothing is really “accidental”; rather, some things are only regarded as accidental because of our ignorance of their causes or the way they operate.

We, on the contrary, attribute to God the knowledge of individual things, and not of universals, except in so far as He knows human minds."

## CHAPTER VII

52. Spinoza seems to refer to this chapter in his *Tractatus de Intellectus Emendatione* (Van Vloten and Land’s edition, 1895, vol. i. p. 24, note 1).

53, 1-6. This view is found, for instance, in Heereboord (*Disput. ex Philosophia*, vol. i. p. 147, quoted by Sigwart).

53, 9-11. Due partly to the desire to maintain the absolute Unity of God (with which a multiplicity of attributes was thought to be inconsistent), and partly from the concern to avoid comparing God with man, there arose in Arabic and Jewish medieval philosophy a tendency to explain away the attributes usually ascribed to God (especially in the Bible and the Koran). These attributes were accordingly treated as having solely a negative meaning; that is, as stating what God is *not* rather than what He is, or as denying some imperfection rather than affirming any human or quasi-human characteristic of Him. (Maimonides, *for example*, sums up his inquiry into God’s attributes as follows:
> “It has thus been shown that every attribute attributed to God either denotes the quality of an action, or—when this attribute is intended to convey some idea of the Divine Being Himself, and not of His actions—the negation of the opposite.” Thus “we use *One* in reference to God to express that there is nothing similar to Him, but we do not mean to say that an attribute of Unity is added to His essence.”—*Guide*, I. lvii. lviii.)

A similar tendency appeared also in Christian Scholasticism. This kind of “negative theology” seems to have been started first by Philo Judæus of Alexandria, the founder of Neoplatonism.

53, 13 *f*. Spinoza is referring to Thomas Aquinas. See **20**, 16 *ff*.

53, 19 *ff*. Compare 30, 31 *ff*. and the notes related to them.

54, 5 *ff*. *Compare Cogitata Metaphysica*, I. vi., where Spinoza says that “good” and “evil” only indicate a certain rela-

It references key figures such as Maimonides, Thomas Aquinas, and Philo Judæus to explain why certain philosophers argued that God can only be described by what He is not.</summary>

relation of one thing to another. "A thing considered by itself is called neither good nor bad; it is so only in relation to another thing, insofar as it helps it to obtain what it requires, or not." Spinoza, however, allows the application of "supremely good" to God on the grounds that all things only exist through Him.

54, 20 *ff.* *Cf. Tractatus de Intellectus Emendatione* (Van Vloten and Land, ed. 1895, vol. i. pp. 28 *ff.*—White and Stirling's translation, pp. 51 *ff.*).

55, 15 *f.* Adopting the correction suggested by Sigwart, we should read here: "Since, as attributes of a self-subsisting being, they exist through themselves, they also become known through themselves"—*nam quia ut attributa entis per se existentis [per se] existunt, etiam per se concipiuntur.* This makes the meaning clearer.

55, 20 *f.* Although the term *genus* is here applied to attribute (because the attribute here takes the place of the *genus* in the old rule of definition), it must not be forgotten that the attribute, according to Spinoza, is not generic, but singular.

In the *Treatise on the Improvement of the Understanding*, Spinoza says that the definition of a created thing "should include the proximate cause," which he there identifies with the infinite modes of Motion and Understanding, according to whether the finite mode to be explained is a mode of Extension or of Thought (vol. i. p. 31 in the 1895 edition).

55, 26. # CHAPTER VIII

56. The distinction between *Natura naturans* and *Natura naturata* may be traced back to Aristotle's distinction between

<summary>This page concludes the commentary on Spinoza's seventh chapter, discussing the relativity of good and evil and the definition of attributes, before beginning a discussion on Chapter VIII's distinction between "naturing" and "natured" nature.</summary>
<keywords>Spinoza, Sigwart, Descartes, Gassendi, Aristotle, Natura naturans, Natura naturata, attributes, genus, proximate cause, Tractatus de Intellectus Emendatione</keywords>

--- Page 342 ---
<meta>This page continues the scholarly commentary on Spinoza's work, picking up from a discussion of adequate conceptions and transitioning into the historical distinctions between the "Mover" and the "Moved."</meta>

the Unmoved (Mover) and the Moved. In the writings of Augustine (354–430), the Aristotelian division is developed into a threefold distinction: namely, (1) a Creator who was not created; (2) the created, which also creates; and (3) that which has been created but does not create. Scotus Erigena added a fourth distinction (in order to complete the dichotomous scheme): namely, (4) that which neither creates nor has been created (which equals nothing). Scotus Erigena (ninth century) already maintained that God and the Universe are identical. Nature, when regarded as a creating totality, is the same as God; while Nature, when regarded as a multiplicity of created things, is what is called the world. This mode of thought was developed more fully by Averroes (1126–1198), the leader of the Arabian Aristotelians.

56, 3–11. *Cf. Ethics*, I. xxix. Schol.

56, 12 ff. *Cf. Ethics*, I. xxviii. Schol., where the division of <term>Natura naturata</term> into "general" and "particular" is replaced by the division into things produced by God "immediately" and "mediately."

### CHAPTER IX

57, 2–8. Probably for the reasons stated in lines 7 and 8, Understanding and Motion are referred to in the *Treatise on the Improvement of the Understanding* as <term>res fixæ et æternæ</term> (fixed and eternal things). They are also commonly referred to as the "infinite modes." *Cf.* Letter LXIV.

57, 18, 20. It seems strange that *Motion* should be described as a "Son of God." But its correspondence or parallelism with Understanding, in Spinoza's scheme, compelled him to predicate of Motion whatever he affirmed of the Understanding through epithets indicating its position in the scheme. And to describe Understanding as the "Son of God" was, of course, Biblical—1 Corinthians 1:24: *Christ the power of God, and the wisdom of God.* It was, no doubt, with reference to this Scriptural passage that Spinoza wrote in

57, 21. The expression “created . . . from all eternity” amounts to a denial of “creation” in its usual sense. Spinoza makes this quite clear in *Cogitata Metaphysica*, II. x.: “Neither was the Son of God created; He was eternal like the Father. When, therefore, we say that the Father had begotten the Son from eternity, we only mean that the Father has always shared His eternity with the Son.”

57, 23-27. It is not certain whether this note was written by Spinoza, to whom it refers in the third person as “the author”—quite a unique form in Spinoza’s writings. The information conveyed is accurate in so far as Spinoza did occupy himself with, and intended to write on, the most general problems of Physics. We gather this from Letters LIX., LX., LXXXIII. But the note seems quite irrelevant. Apparently it refers to some remark in the text which was subsequently struck out.

58, 6. “Affects.” The Dutch is *Aandoeningen*, which may be a too literal translation of *Affectus*. The usual expression is *passien* or *tochten*.

The contrast required is that given between union with *substances* and union with *modes*; that given is between union with *thought* and union with *extension*, both of which are substances in the looser sense—that is, “attributes of substance.” Probably this part of the note was not written by Spinoza in its present form.

66, 1–5. According to the reservation made here, <term>God</term> or <term>Substance</term> is not part of the nature of man, because although man could not exist, or be conceived without <term>God</term>, yet <term>God</term> could certainly exist and be conceived without man. *Compare Ethics, II., Definition ii., and Proposition x.*

### CHAPTER I

67. The opening words of this chapter, as well as the opening and concluding remarks of the Preface (p. 63, lines 6 and following, and p. 66, lines 5 and following), lead one to expect an exposition of "the <term>modes</term> of which man consists." However, what is actually considered in this chapter are the three kinds of <term>knowledge</term>, while "the <term>modes</term> of which man consists" are discussed in the long note to the Preface (pp. 63 and following). Freudenthal has therefore suggested the following explanation: Originally, Chapter I did deal with "the <term>modes</term> of which man consists." But, dissatisfied with that first account, Spinoza wrote a new exposition to replace or supplement it. Due, however, to some misunderstanding of reference signs, the copyist or translator treated the new exposition as a note to the Preface, while at the same time omitting the older account, which Spinoza had probably crossed out or marked in some way as unsatisfactory. Note † seems to be a weak attempt on the part of a reader or copyist to reconcile the opening words with the actual contents of the chapter.

67, 7–8. The meaning is clear: namely, the <term>modes</term> to be considered first are the <term>modes of thought</term>, because these are known or experienced more immediately than the <term>modes of extension</term> (that is, material objects, including human bodies). Our <term>knowledge</term> of all <term>modes of extension</term> is, of course, included among the <term>modes of cognition</term>. The language, however, is rather obscure. What is "the consciousness of the knowledge of ourselves"? Freudenthal has suggested that the original Latin may have been, "*Incipiamus ab iis qui primi nobis cogniti sunt, scilicet a quibusdam*"

"*ideis vel a cognitione nostri et deinde agamus de rebus quæ extra nos sunt,*" and that "*cognitione nostri*" was (like so many other expressions in the *Treatise*) translated twice over by "<term>medegeweten</term>" (translated "consciousness") and "<term>kennisse</term>," and the whole misconstrued. In accordance with this plausible emendation, we should read here: ". . . certain ideas or our <term>knowledge</term>, and then we shall treat of the things which are outside us."

67, 10 *ff.* Here we have a threefold <term>classification</term> of the different kinds of <term>knowledge</term>, which is developed into a fourfold scheme by subdividing the first kind of <term>knowledge</term>. In chapter II, the distinction between the two subdivisions of the first kind of <term>knowledge</term> is passed over, while it is emphasized in chapter IV (76, 17 *ff.*). In the <term>Tractatus de Intellectus Emendatione</term> (pp. 7 *ff.*) we find the fourfold scheme, while in the <term>Ethics</term>, II. xl. Schol. 2, <term>Spinoza</term> returns to the threefold scheme. The special stress laid on the fourfold scheme in the *Tr. de Int. Em.* (as <term>Gebhardt</term> has suggested) was probably due to the influence of <term>Francis Bacon</term>. Indeed, the name of the second kind of <term>knowledge</term> (or of the second subdivision of what is here the first kind), namely, *perceptio ab experientia vaga*, occurs in <term>Bacon</term>’s <term>Novum Organum</term>, I. c. In a note in the *Tr. de Int. Em.* (p. 9) <term>Spinoza</term> promises a fuller account of "experience," and of the methods of "recent empirical philosophers." The reference is most probably to <term>Bacon</term>, from whose estimate of experience <term>Spinoza</term> differed, maintaining (as against <term>Bacon</term>) that "it is something altogether uncertain, . . . by means of it only the accidents of natural things are apprehended, and they are never clearly understood without a previous knowledge of their essences" (*ibid.*).

67, 11. The first kind of <term>knowledge</term> (in the threefold scheme) is here called "belief," but in chapters II and IV (and elsewhere) "opinion." The Latin was probably the same in all cases, namely, <term>opinio</term>. In English also "belief"

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>205</page-num>
<header>COMMENTARY</header>

is sometimes used for “opinion”; *for example*, “I am not sure, but that is my belief” (or “I believe so”).

67, 13. The second kind of knowledge, here called “true belief” (on p. 69, line 14, simply “Belief”), is described on p. 74, line 19, as “a strong proof based on reasons.” The distinction between “Opinion” and “True Belief” therefore recalls the Platonic (or even pre-Platonic) distinction between δόξα and ἐπιστήμη.

“Belief” (or “true belief”) seems a strange designation for reasoned or discursive knowledge. Spinoza himself substituted “Reason” afterwards (*see, for example*, p. 99, line 16—“True Belief or Reason”). Joel, however, has pointed out that Crescas employed the term “Belief” in the same sense. The expression “true belief” may have been suggested by the following passage from Maimonides’ *Guide* (I. l.):

> “Belief... is the conviction that what is apprehended exists outside the mind exactly as it is conceived in the mind. If in addition we are convinced that the thing cannot be different in any way from what we believe it to be... then the belief is true.”

67, 14. Sigwart has pointed out that the distinction between what is here called “clear and distinct conception” (or immediate intuition) and “true belief” (or discursive reasoning) is also found in Descartes (especially in the *Regulæ ad directionem ingenii*, which, however, was only published in 1701 and was therefore unknown to Spinoza). But Descartes laid no such stress on the distinction, and also conceived it rather differently. Descartes’ “immediate intuition” was mathematical in character and referred to the apprehension of the truth of certain propositions, especially the *cogito ergo sum*; Spinoza’s “clear and distinct knowledge” is mystical in character and referred to the apprehension of *objects*, especially of God.

67, 15-17. *Compare Ethics*, II. xli.

67, 25 *ff*. See the first comment on this chapter.

The three footnotes on this page, and the first three footnotes on page 68, are most likely marginal notes or summaries made by some reader of the manuscript from which A was copied.

### CHAPTER II

69, 22 *and following.* *Compare* *Ethics*, IV. Appendix, § iii.: “Our actions, that is to say, those desires which are determined by man’s power or reason, are always good; the others may be good or evil.” *Compare* also *Ethics*, III. iii.

“Passion” (πάθος = *passio*, *affectus*, or *perturbatio*) was used in the time of Spinoza, and even later, in a much broader sense than at present. It denoted not the violent emotions only, but all feelings, sentiments, and desires, as so many ways in which the mind “suffers” or “is affected” by external things.

69, 26 *and following.* *Compare* *Ethics*, II. Axiom iii.: “Such modes of thought as love, desire . . . do not arise unless there is also, in the same individual, an idea of the thing loved, desired, etc. But the idea may be there even when no other mode of thought is present.”

The view that “knowledge is the proximate cause of all the passions” is opposed to the Cartesian view, according to which the passions “are produced, sustained, and strengthened by some movement of the animal spirits” (*De Passionibus Animæ*, I. 27). Spinoza assigns a purely mental origin to the passions, while Descartes ascribed them in large measure to physiological causes.

### CHAPTER III

70. In his treatment of the passions in this and the following chapters, Spinoza follows closely Descartes’ order of exposition in his *De Passionibus Animæ*, Parts II and III. (This was already noticed by Boehmer when he published the *Outline of the Short Treatise*.) The following tables (*see* opposite) (taken, with slight changes, from Sigwart) will make this clear.

GOD, MAN, AND HIS WELL-BEING

As regards details, there are numerous important differences between Spinoza’s and Descartes’ views on the passions.

70, 8 *ff.* Spinoza’s account of “surprise” is original. Descartes simply described it as evoked by “things rare and extraordinary,” but he did not explain it.

70, 28 *ff.* The concluding part of the note seems to be directed against the view that Surprise is evoked chiefly by what is absolutely new. But the thought is expressed imperfectly.

72, 20 *ff.* Spinoza’s account of Hatred is very different from that of Descartes (*op. cit.* II. 79).

73, 4 *f.* The account here given of Desire is reversed in *Ethics*, III. ix. Schol., where it is maintained that we do not “desire anything because we think it is good, but, on the contrary, we judge a thing to be good because we . . . desire it.”

### CHAPTER IV

74, 9 *f.* In geometry, *e.g.*, we reason that such and such a figure must have such and such properties; but we do not prove thereby that such a figure actually exists.

74, 19 *ff.* The meaning of “Belief” here (as already remarked) is peculiar. Equally peculiar is the use here made of the term “Knowledge” (= spiritual intuition), and the way in which “Belief” and “Knowledge” are contrasted. Joel has drawn attention to parallel passages in the writings of <term>Crescas</term> <note>Hasdai Crescas was a 14th-century Jewish philosopher who influenced Spinoza's critiques of Aristotelianism.</note>, two of which may be given here. In words very like lines 19–21, Crescas says that “Belief is only the conviction resulting from the necessity of the case that the thing outside the soul is such as it is represented to be in the soul.” In contrast to Belief, clear Knowledge is described by Spinoza (in lines 25, 30 *f.* and elsewhere) as an “immediate union” with and “intellectual enjoyment” of what is thus known. Crescas distinguishes between

# COMMENTARY

Belief and another form of knowledge are described as follows: "We accept some views from a feeling of (logical) necessity, others with a feeling of joy and gladness. Our Bliss depends, not on Belief, but on the joy which accompanies Knowledge. For . . . only joy can unite us with God" (*The Light of the Lord*, II. v. 5, quoted by Joel).

**75, 13 *f*.** The assertion mentioned is not found in the Treatise. It appears that some part containing such a statement has been lost.

**75, 23 *ff*.** Compare the comments on I. x. (p. 201 *f*.). Compare *Ethics*, IV, Preface, Definitions I and II, and Appendix, § V.

**76, 6 *f*.** Spinoza may be referring to the first chapter of his *Cogitata Metaphysica* (Metaphysical Thoughts), which is entitled *De Ente Reali, Ficto, et Rationis* (On Real, Fictitious, and Mental Beings). This was likely already written, though the complete work of which it forms a part was not finished and published until 1663.

**76, 26 *ff*.** The new point of view is noteworthy. Until now, the passions were judged by the *kind of knowledge* which produced them; we now observe a new criterion: namely, *the nature of the objects that are loved, etc*.

**77, 3 *ff*.** This brief and somewhat unusual treatment of "surprise" almost prepares the reader for its later exclusion from the class of <term>affects</term> (or passions) in *Ethics*, III (Definition IV of the Affects). It is possible, however, that this chapter was originally followed by one specifically on "Surprise."

### CHAPTER V

**78.** It should be noted that Love is distinguished here according to the nature of its objects, not according to the kind of <term>cognition</term> from which it results—which was the method suggested at the beginning of chapter III (p. 70). It may be noted that Descartes somewhat dismissed any such distinctions based on the character of the objects loved (*De Passions de l'Âme* II. 82).

->GOD, MAN, AND HIS WELL-BEING<-

**78, 15 *f.*** "God, or . . . Truth." *Compare* p. 103, line 16—God is Truth, Truth is God. This is because by "Truth," Spinoza means "the real essence of things as thought" (Martineau).

**78, 17 *f.*** According to this, love is always "intellectual." Descartes had distinguished between *amor intellectualis* (intellectual love) and *amor sensitivus* (sensitive love), the latter of which was supposed to be caused entirely by physiological factors.

**78, 20 *f.*** In the *Tractatus Theologico-Politicus*, chapter xvi., Spinoza speaks of it as "a universal law of human nature" that we can only give up what we think is good in one of the two ways stated here.

**79, 8 *f.*** This explanation of love appears to be original; it is not found in Descartes.

**80, 30-32.** One would expect ". . . God alone is a substance . . ." The Dutch terms used are *weezen* (essence), *weezens* (essences), and *wezen* (entity).

**81, 15 *f.*** Regarding the "intellectual love of God" (*Amor Dei intellectualis*), see *Ethics*, Book V, proposition xxxii and following.

## CHAPTER VI

**82, 3 *f.*** This definition of Hatred is restricted on the next page (lines 8 and following) so as to exclude inanimate and irresponsible things from being its objects. On p. 72 (lines 20 and following) we had yet another account, written from a different point of view. In the *Ethics*, Book III (Scholium to Proposition xiii, also Definition vii at the end of the Book), Hatred is defined even more broadly than here—namely, as "sorrow with the accompanying idea of its external cause"; while in Book IV, Scholium to Proposition xlv, it is restricted again so as to exclude all but human beings from its objects.

**83, 16 *f.*** Here, things which are the "accidental" causes of injury are excluded from among the objects of "aversion"; in the *Ethics*, Book III, Definition ix of the Affects, aversion is defined as "sorrow with the accompanying idea of some object as the accidental cause of the sorrow."

**83, 20 *f.*** Here "sorrow" is described as an effect of hatred, etc.; whereas in the definitions mentioned above (from the *Ethics*)

COMMENTARY 211

Hatred and aversion are described as *species* of sorrow. We thus seem to have here an identification of *proximate cause* with *proximate genus*. *Compare* p. 199.

83, 21. Anger is accordingly defined in *Ethics*, Book III, Definition xxxvi, as “the desire by which we are impelled, through hatred, to injure those whom we hate.”

83, 24. Envy is defined in *Ethics*, Book III, Definition xxiii, as “hatred insofar as it affects a man so that he is sad at the good fortune of another person, and is glad when some evil befalls him.”

84, 5 and following. *Compare Ethics*, Book IV, Proposition xviii, Scholium (“*Therefore, nothing is more useful to man than man*”), Book IV, Proposition xxxv, Corollary i, and Book IV, Proposition xxxvii.

84, 16 and following. *Compare Ethics*, Book IV, Proposition xlv.

### CHAPTER VII

85. “Joy and Sorrow” are used in a very wide sense, almost as the equivalents of “Pleasure and Pain.” They play a more important role in the *Ethics* than they do here.

85, 8. “The same causes”—that is, the idea that a certain thing is good.

85, 12 and 13. The definition of Sorrow given here is the same as that of Grief on page 99, lines 5 and following. In the *Ethics* (Book III, Definition iii of the Affects), Sorrow (*Tristitia*) is defined as “man’s transition from greater to lesser perfection.” Descartes had defined it as the effect of a present evil.

85, 17 and following. *Compare Ethics*, Book IV, Proposition xli, where Spinoza says that Joy is in itself good, and Sorrow evil, because Joy increases the body’s power of action, while Sorrow diminishes it.

86, 2 and 3. *Compare Tractatus de Intellectus Emendatione* (p. 5), where Spinoza says that strife, hatred, sorrow, jealousy, and other evil passions arise from the love of the transient only, “but love for an object eternal and infinite feeds the mind with unmixed joy.” *Compare Ethics*, Book V, Proposition xx.

86, 7. Reminiscent of *Psalm* 16:11:

> In your presence is fullness of joy,
> In your right hand there are pleasures forevermore.

### CHAPTER VIII

87, 7-9. In the *Ethics* (Book III, Definition of the Affects xxi, xxii), <term>Existimatio</term> and <term>Despectus</term> are conceived as containing an element of bias. <term>Existimatio</term> (over-esteem) “consists in thinking too highly of someone because of our love for him"; <term>Despectus</term> (disdain) “consists in thinking too little of someone because of our hatred for him.”

87, 10. “Self-respect.” The Dutch term is <term>Edelmoedigheid</term>, which generally means “noble bearing” or “generosity.” <term>Generositas</term>, however, is defined in *Ethics*, Book III, Proposition lix, Scholium, as “the desire by which, from the dictates of reason alone, each person endeavors to help other people and to join them to himself in friendship.” This is very different from what is described here.

87, 18. “Conceit” (<term>Verwaantheid</term>) = <term>Superbia</term> (*Ethics*, Book III, Definition of the Affects xxviii), “undue self-esteem prompted by self-love.”

87, 20. “Culpable humility” (<term>strafbare nedrigheid</term>) = ? <term>Abjectio</term>, which is defined in *Ethics*, Book III, Definition of the Affects xxix, as “thinking too little of oneself, through sorrow.”

88, 6 *ff.* In the *Ethics* (Book IV, Proposition liii), Spinoza says that “Humility is not a virtue,” because the rational man should think of what he *can* do, not of what he *cannot* do. Moreover, Humility is a species of sorrow, and sorrow is always bad. Apparently, the good side of “true humility” has been joined to “self-respect” to constitute <term>acquiescentia in se ipso</term>, the contentment resulting from a just estimate of one’s powers.

88, 32. Skepticism was somewhat fashionable in the time of Spinoza, and rationalist philosophies were often confused with it. Hence, philosophers like Bacon, Descartes, and Spinoza felt it necessary to challenge Skepticism so as to make it clear that they were no Skeptics. In the *Tractatus de Intellectus Emendatione* (p. 14), Spinoza remarks of the Skeptics: “They

90, 12 *f.* The way in which Spinoza divides the passions here appears to be original.

### CHAPTER X

94, 5 *ff*. <term>Remorse</term> (*Knaging*) is conceived somewhat differently in the *Ethics* (Book III, Definition of the Affects 17), where it (*Conscientiæ morsus*) is defined as "sorrow accompanied by the idea of something past which happened unexpectedly" <note>? contrary to expectations</note>. This is <term>Disappointment</term> rather than Remorse. *Verrassing* <note>rashness, line 5</note> usually means surprise.

<meta>This page continues the scholarly commentary on Spinoza's work, comparing the definitions and ethical assessments of human passions in his "Short Treatise" with those in his later "Ethics" and the works of Descartes.</meta>

94, 7 f. **Repentance** (*Berouw*). In *Ethics*, Part III, Definition of the Affects 27, <term>Pænitentia</term> is defined as "sorrow accompanied by the idea of something done, which we believe that we did by a free decision of the mind."

94, 18 f. The definitions of "<term>Remorse</term>" and "<term>Repentance</term>" given here (in the *Short Treatise*) are the same as those given by Descartes (*De Passions de l'Âme* III. 177, 191). However, Spinoza’s assessment of them is completely opposed to that of Descartes, who considers remorse "useful" as it tends to make people more cautious in the future, and repentance as "most useful" because it leads to an improvement in conduct. In *Ethics*, Part IV, Proposition 54, Scholium, Spinoza makes a noteworthy concession: "If men who are weak in mind . . . were ashamed of nothing, and feared nothing, how could they be united or restrained? The mob inspires fear when it feels none. No wonder, therefore, that the Prophets, who were concerned about the welfare, not of the few, but of the community, commended Humility, Repentance, and Reverence so greatly. And indeed, those who are subject to these feelings can be led much more easily than others, so as to live eventually by the guidance of Reason—that is, to be free, and live the life of the blessed."

# GOD, MAN, AND HIS WELL-BEING

Descartes (*De Pass. An.* III. 180) states that a judicious use of derision might diminish vice by making it appear ridiculous.

95, 18 *f.* *Cf. Ethics*, IV. liii. Schol.: “I see a great difference between Derision (which . . . I stated to be bad) and laughter. For laughter, and jesting (<term>jocus</term>) likewise, is sheer Joy; and is therefore good in itself, provided it be not excessive. Nothing, surely, but a gloomy and sad superstition forbids enjoyment.”

95, 22. “Spirits.” The allusion is to the <term>spiritus animales</term>, the vital or animal spirits. The doctrine of <term>spiritus animales</term> is found already in the writings of the ancient Stoics and the medieval Scholastics, but was developed more fully by Descartes. Harvey’s discovery of the circulation of the blood encouraged Descartes in the working out of his conception of the automatic character of animal organisms. His dualism—that is, his view that mind and body were entirely different substances which could not directly influence each other—made it necessary for him to explain all physiological processes by the principles of mechanics. The human body was accordingly regarded by him as a cleverly contrived machine, all the parts of which (heart, lungs, brain, nerves, muscles, etc.) cooperated, or acted on each other, through the mediation of the blood which circulated all over the body. Now in passing through the heart the blood (it was said) becomes heated, its finest particles then separate from the coarser ones, and rise to the brain, while the rest of the blood, which is too thick for the arteries leading to the brain, circulates through the other parts of the body. It was this very fine part of the blood, which alone had access to the brain, that Descartes called “spirits” (<term>spiritus</term> or *esprits animaux* = <term>spiritus animales</term>). Moreover, he regarded the “<term>pineal gland</term>” in the brain to be the “seat” of the Soul, and (deviating from the requirements of his dualistic philosophy), he maintained that the soul could influence the body, not indeed by setting in

<summary>This page provides scholarly commentary on Spinoza's and Descartes' views on derision, laughter, and the physiological concept of "animal spirits." It explains Descartes' mechanical view of the human body and the role of the pineal gland as the supposed seat of the soul.</summary>

motion, but by *directing* the motion of the "vital spirits" in the same way, for example, as a horseman directs the movements of his horse—which is not carried by him, but actually carries him.* Descartes tried to minimize this violation of his dualism by weakening the material aspect of his "spirits" as much as possible. In the *Discourse on Method*, Part V, he says that "the animal spirits are like a very subtle wind, or rather a very pure and vivid flame." They play a very important *role* in his explanation of the passions. Spinoza was opposed to this causal mingling of the mental with the physical, which he criticized severely in his *Ethics* (Preface to Part V). This same difference in attitude constitutes a fundamental difference between Spinoza's and Descartes' accounts of the "passions."

### CHAPTER XII

**96.** "Glory." The Dutch word *Eere* generally means "honor," and this will be sufficient if understood in the sense of "feeling honored"; but "honor" is too ambiguous to stand alone. Definitions of the Affects, xxx, and although "Glory" is not a very satisfactory translation, it has the merit of suggesting the Latin original.

<meta>This page continues the scholarly commentary comparing Spinoza’s *Ethics* and *Short Treatise* with the philosophy of Descartes, specifically focusing on the social utility of "Glory" and "Shame," as well as Spinoza's personal habits and his definitions of "Favor" and "Gratitude."</meta>

97, 11 ff. When Descartes refers to the positive side of Glory and Shame, he means "good for the person who has these feelings." Spinoza here makes a very different suggestion, namely, how such a person may thus be enabled to do good to *others*, who might otherwise not come under his influence.

It is interesting to compare Spinoza's "philosophy of clothes" with what his biographers tell of him. Lucas (the earliest biographer of Spinoza) says that Spinoza himself was always careful to be dressed neatly when he went out, and strongly condemned deliberate neglect, saying, "It is not a dirty and neglected appearance that makes one a scholar." Colerus, on the other hand, relates that Spinoza was dressed no better than one of the poorest citizens; that a certain prominent Councillor of State, while visiting Spinoza one day, found him in an untidy morning gown, and when criticized for it Spinoza replied that "a man is not made better by having a finer gown," and that "it is unreasonable to wrap up things of little or no value in a precious cover" (see Pollock's *Spinoza*, 2nd ed. p. 394). The two accounts are not necessarily inconsistent.

### CHAPTER XV

102, 6 *ff.* Truth and Falsity are similarly defined in *Cogitata Metaphysica* I. vi., and in *Ethics*, I. *Axiom* 6. In the *Tractatus de Intellectus Emendatione* (p. 11 *f.*), however, a different view of Truth appears, in which no reference is made to “agreement” or “correspondence” with things. To have a true idea is to have objectively the <term>essentia formalis</term> of the thing thought about (the <term>ideatum</term>). This view is developed also in *Ethics*, II. xxxiv., etc., where “true” ideas are identified with “adequate” ideas, and “false” ideas with “inadequate” ones. *Cf. Ethics*, II. xliii.

102, 10 *ff. Cf.* Descartes, *Meditation* III. (Veitch, p. 118): “With respect to ideas, if these are considered only in themselves, and are not referred to any object beyond them, they cannot, properly speaking, be false; for, whether I imagine a goat or a chimera, it is no less true that I imagine the one than the other.”

102, 15. Descartes (*Principles of Philosophy* I. lx.-lxii.—Veitch, pp. 219 *ff.*) speaks of three kinds of Distinctions, namely, *real, modal,* and *logical*. A *real* distinction is that between two substances; a *modal* distinction is “that between the mode properly so called and the substance of which it is a mode, or that between two modes of the same substance”; while a *logical* distinction, or a distinction of *reason*, “is that

on our part, but is necessary. It is true that the sentence beginning on line 26 appears to suggest a kind of sensationalist view—namely, that the things outside us produce the ideas in us; and there are similar passages in chapters 16 and 19 (see page 109, lines 2 and following, and page 123, lines 29 and following). On the other hand, the explanation of error in chapter 16 (page 110, lines 1 and following) shows a very different view of human knowledge, a view more like that explained in the *Ethics*, where he insists on the spontaneity of ideation, in opposition to the view that ideas are "dumb pictures on a tablet" (Book II, Proposition 49, Scholium). Possibly Spinoza may have been thinking of the immanent necessity in the sequence of our ideas or judgments. And in the case of immanent causality, the usual distinction between activity and passivity disappears. See what he actually says on page 30, lines 8–14. It is, of course, quite easy to suppose that Spinoza's theory of knowledge went through a complete change—that he began by conceiving knowledge to be merely passive, and ended by regarding it as eminently active. But the easier interpretation is not always the more accurate one. What Spinoza really intended to oppose was, I think, the Cartesian conception of judgment as an arbitrary act of volition (*Meditation 4*). On page 109, lines 6 and following, Spinoza seems to be dealing expressly with this view of Descartes. (For a discussion of this problem see Trendelenburg, Freudenthal's *Spinozastudien*, and Gebhardt.)

104, 1 *ff.* The sentence in brackets presents some difficulty. The Dutch is "(*als door weinige of minder toevoeginge in* [B : *toevoegingen van dien*] *'t zelve gewaar wordende*)." The word "toevoeginge" seems hardly appropriate in any case. Sigwart translates it as "Affectionen" (affections), Schaarschmidt as "Anregungen" (stimulations). This is quite plausible, inasmuch as "toevoegen" is used for "addressing someone," and it may accordingly be rendered by "stimuli." This translation, however, makes the word *in* in version A wrong, while the sentence in brackets is a mere repetition of what precedes. But as

<summary>This page analyzes Spinoza's shifting views on whether knowledge is a passive or active process, contrasting his ideas with Cartesian philosophy. It also addresses specific translational difficulties regarding how stimuli and ideas are perceived in the original Dutch text.</summary>

<keywords>Spinoza, Cartesian philosophy, theory of knowledge, immanent causality, ideation, spontaneity, sensationalism, Descartes, Trendelenburg, Freudenthal, Gebhardt</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philological and structural analysis of Spinoza's *Short Treatise*, specifically focusing on the translation of terminology from Dutch and the proposed reorganization of chapters by scholars like Freudenthal.</meta>

<page-num>223</page-num>
<header>COMMENTARY</header>

“Toevoegen” literally means “to add”; it seems quite possible that “toevoeginge” may have been a rather clumsy translation of *attributa* or *accidentia* in the broader sense of “qualities.” If so, the passage can be rendered this way: “(as becoming aware of it only through a few or the less important of the attributes in it <note>or “of its attributes”</note>).” Dr. W. Meyer has paraphrased this passage in the same way, taking *toevoeging* as equal to *toeeigening*, or attribute.

## CHAPTER XVI

105. According to Freudenthal, this chapter is misplaced. The substance of one part of it—namely, page 109, line 21, to page 110, line 8—should have been given at the end of chapter 15, as containing the answer to the question raised on page 102, lines 20 and following. But the rest of the present chapter, as well as chapters 17 and 18, should follow chapter 20. For chapter 19 deals with the question “wherein the well-being of a perfect man consists,” and chapter 16 (page 105, line 4) assumes that the question has already been dealt with. In contrast, chapter 19 seems to assume an immediately preceding discussion on the advantages of “true belief,” and such a discussion is found in chapter 15. As chapters 19 and 20 obviously go together, they should both follow chapter 15 and be followed by chapters 16–18. Rearranged in this way, the connection of ideas would be as follows: the discussion of truth and falsity (or, briefly, of knowledge) serves as an introduction to chapters 19 and 20, where it is shown that knowledge is the cause of the passions, but that these may be mastered by a knowledge of God. This raises the question discussed in chapters 16 and 17, namely, whether such a self-emancipation from the passions is the effect of voluntary effort, or the necessary result of inevitable causes. And chapter 18 (which, according to Freudenthal, originally concluded the whole Treatise) rounds off the whole discussion.

105, 10. Desire: *see* p. 73, lines 4 *ff*. In *Ethics*, III. ix. Schol., Desire is defined as "*appetitus cum ejusdem conscientia*" <note>"appetite with the consciousness of the same"</note>, and *appetitus* as "*ipsa hominis essentia, ex cujus natura ea, quæ ipsius conservationi inserviunt, necessario sequuntur*" <note>"the very essence of man, from whose nature those things which serve for his preservation necessarily follow"</note>; in short (III. 108, 1. "Idea"—that is, a general idea or abstraction derived from particular acts of volition.

108, 16 *ff*. This was a common doctrine among medieval philosophers; it is found in the writings of Thomas Aquinas, Peter Lombard, and others. 109, 6 *ff*. Spinoza is probably referring here to the Cartesian view that to have an idea is one thing, but to make an affirmation or denial about it is another and depends on our free will. Spinoza identifies volition with affirmation and denial, but denies that it is free. The ideas necessitate certain affirmations or denials. Thinking is thus identified with judging. *Cf. Ethics*, II. xlix. Schol.

109, 16. "Feel" = ? *sentimus*, apprehend.

109, 21 *ff*. *See* note to p. 102, line 20 (p. 221).

In *De Anima*, III. ix., Aristotle distinguishes within the conative faculty (τὸ ὀρεκτικόν = Spinoza’s *cupiditas*, line 12) rational desire (βούλησις) from irrational desire (ἐπιθυμία), and this distinction recurs also in III. x. and in the *Rhetoric*, I. x.

112, 19 *ff.* Spinoza’s attitude towards the Aristotelian view is not expressed clearly. Since Spinoza identifies volition with affirmation and negation and Aristotle identifies it with desire, they really mean different things, although they use the same term (will). This seems to be the meaning of the sentence in question.

## CHAPTER XVIII

115, 10 *ff. Cf. Ethics*, II. xlix. Schol.
Trendelenburg has pointed out that in Plato’s *Euthyphron* man is similarly described as the slave of God. There is a vast difference, however. In Plato’s dialogue it is only “the service called *holiness*” (that is, sacrificing and praying to the Gods, as distinguished from Justice, which is service to men) that is described as “of the same nature as that which slaves render to their master.” Spinoza is not thinking at all of such restricted “divine service,” but of the whole life and conduct of man.

116, 26 *ff.* Probably an allusion to 1 *John*, iv. 13: 
> “By this we know that we dwell in Him, and He in us, because He has given us of His spirit.” 

This verse was subsequently put by Spinoza on the title-page of his *Tractatus Theologico-Politicus*.

117, 1 *ff. Cf.* Burgersdijck (*Inst. Log.* cap. xvii.) : 
> “*Instrumentarum essentia posita est in aptitudine ad usum. . . . Sic securis eatenus securis est, quatenus materiæ qualitate et forma apta est ad secandum.*”

### CHAPTER XIX

118. According to Freudenthal, this chapter and the next should have been placed immediately after chapter XV. *See* the first note to chapter XVI (p. 223).

119, 18 *ff.* It was on account of Descartes' initial skepticism that Spinoza felt it necessary to prove the existence of material bodies. "A body" = Extension, or Matter.

119, 27 *ff.* This paragraph appears, at first sight, to be directed against Occasionalism—the view, namely, that our perception of a body is produced in our mind by the direct

<meta>This page continues the scholarly commentary on Spinoza's *Short Treatise*, specifically providing annotations for Chapter XIX regarding Descartes' skepticism and the mind-body relationship.</meta>

<page-num>227</page-num>
<header>COMMENTARY</header>

action of God on the “occasion” of the presence of such a body. But there is no other evidence of Spinoza’s acquaintance with Occasionalism. It may be that Spinoza was only thinking of the “omnipotent demon” who, as Descartes suggested (*Med.* I.), might be deluding us with fancies of apparently material bodies. The context, dealing as it does with Descartes’ skepticism, seems to me to confirm this.

119, 34. The reference to the “first chapter” seems to be inaccurate. The passage to which reference is made is intended to show that there is nothing outside God (and that, therefore, no such demon can exist). This is done, not in the first chapter, but in the first *Dialogue* (also in chapter ii. Book I.).

120, 1 *ff.* In various parts of the *Short Treatise*, but especially in this chapter and the next, Spinoza deals with the relation between mind and body. He does so only indirectly, however, or incidentally—for his main inquiry is ethical, not psychological, in character. It is regrettable that he did not discuss the problem for its own sake, because in that case he would have expressed his views more clearly and consistently than he has done in these incidental discussions which originated on different occasions and had different aims. As it stands, we seem to have several different views here on the relation between mind and body. And as we have no independent knowledge of the chronological order, or of the geological formation (so to speak) of the parts of the *Treatise*, it is impossible to speak with absolute confidence about the actual order or sequence among these views. It seems reasonable, however, to suppose that their logical order is also more or less representative of their chronological sequence. His final view, we take it, was what has since become familiar as that of psycho-physical parallelism. This view is the one adopted in the *Ethics*, though with occasional lapses. The other views may be regarded as leading up to this one.

<summary>This page discusses Spinoza's possible engagement with Descartes' skepticism and Occasionalism, as well as the development of his theories on the mind-body relationship within the Short Treatise.</summary>
<keywords>Spinoza, Occasionalism, Descartes, Meditations, Short Treatise, mind and body, psycho-physical parallelism, Ethics</keywords>

--- Page 370 ---
<meta>This page continues the commentary on Spinoza's *Short Treatise*, specifically discussing the development of his theories on the relationship between mind and body, moving from interactionism to parallelism.</meta>

<page-num>228</page-num>
<header>GOD, MAN, AND HIS WELL-BEING</header>

Now, in the first place, do the body and mind interact? In some passages, the view expressed or implied is that they do. The body acts on the mind (p. 103, lines 26 *following*; 112, 26; 119, 7 *following*; 122, 26 *following*; 129, 15 *and following*, etc.); and the mind acts on the body, or, at any rate, the soul can move its own body (129, 6 *and following*), and through it also other bodies (130, 12 *following*). In other passages, however, this apparent view of direct interaction is considerably modified, if not denied. We are told that the mind cannot affect even its own body, except through the mediation of the "vital spirits," whose movements it cannot start or end, but only control or direct (121, 2 *following*, 28 *following*; 127, 10 *following*); nor can the body act directly on the mind without the intervention of "vital spirits" (122, 4 *following*). And this, of course, is the Cartesian view (*see note to 95, 22—p. 216*).

Spinoza, however, was not satisfied with this solution. After all, the "vital spirits" were physical, and one might just as well suppose that the mind can interact with the body as with them. Accordingly, we find a new solution to the problem. Mind and body can affect each other because they are mere modes of one and the same whole, or substance (127, 34 *following*; 121, 9 *following*). This answer may have suggested yet another point of view from which the problem itself disappeared.

Up to this point, the *reality* of some sort of interaction was assumed, and the problem was to explain it. Spinoza tried to do so, first by invoking "spirits," and then by his conception of a "whole" in which mind and body were most intimately united. According to Spinoza, the ultimate "whole" is Substance, of which Extension and Thought are co-attributes. These stand in no causal relationship to each other; they are, so to speak, collateral expressions of the same reality. One does not cause the other, but simply *is* the other—that is, another or parallel aspect of the same reality. Similarly, mind and body are really one whole, merely a two-faced mode of substance; the mind does not affect the body, nor the body the mind; the one simply *is* the other—that is,

<summary>This page analyzes the development of Spinoza's thoughts on the mind-body problem, tracing his shift from believing in direct interaction to proposing that mind and body are parallel attributes of a single Substance.</summary>
<keywords>Spinoza, mind-body interaction, vital spirits, Cartesianism, Substance, Extension, Thought, parallelism, modes, attributes</keywords>

--- Page 371 ---
<meta>This page continues the commentary on Spinoza's theories regarding the relationship between mind and body, specifically focusing on the transition to psycho-physical parallelism.</meta>

<page-num>229</page-num>
<header>COMMENTARY 229</header>

a parallel aspect of the same reality. So there is really no interaction—and no problem. This view is expressed, though not adequately, in the passage now under consideration (pp. 120, 121; *cf. Ethics*, II. vii. and III. ii.).

The theory of psycho-physical parallelism, first stated by Spinoza, did not receive the attention it deserved until about two centuries later, but it has remained the favorite working hypothesis among psychologists since then. (For a fuller account, *see* Freudenthal, *Ueber die Entwicklung der Lehre vom psychophysischen Parallelismus bei Spinoza*.)

120, 4. “Power”—*cf.* the note to 34, 16 (p. 187).
120, 12 *ff. Cf. Ethics*, II. xiii.
120, 21 *ff. Cf. Ethics*, II. Lemma iii., and III. ii.
120, 22. “Rest” (ἠρεμία) was regarded by Aristotle (*De Cælo*, II.) not as the mere absence of motion, but as its positive opposite; that is to say (in more modern language), not as the mere absence of kinetic energy, but as the presence of potential energy. This positive conception of “rest” is also found in Descartes’ *Principia*, II.; in *Med.*, III., however, Descartes speaks as though “rest” were the mere absence of motion, as darkness is the absence of light. Note † (p. 120) may have been directed against this suggestion.
121, 12 *ff. Cf.* p. 69, lines 26 *ff.*, and p. 158, lines 2 *ff.*
121, 23. *See* p. 78, lines 20 *ff.*
124, 3. “Object” = object of thought. The sentence is awkwardly expressed, but the meaning is clear.

The views found in the present chapter are developed much more fully in *Ethics*, IV. ix.-xvii.

### CHAPTER XXII

133. The “fourth kind of knowledge”—see pp. 67-69, and the notes on them. Sigwart cites several passages from Heereboord’s *Logic* which appear at first to express a view very like Spinoza’s on knowledge as a bond of union between humanity and God. There is, however, a fundamental difference between the two views. The knowledge to which Heereboord refers is discursive knowledge, or what Spinoza calls “Reason,” while Spinoza refers to “intuitive” knowledge, which is almost mystical in character. The view of Heereboord, it may be remarked, is already found in Maimonides and other medieval Aristotelians.

134, 7 *f.* “That same thing” = Nature (line 6) or God (see line 10), or possibly the “thinking thing”—*i.e.*, the

<meta>This page continues the scholarly commentary on Spinoza's work, moving from the discussion of the "fourth kind of knowledge" in Chapter XXII to the discussion of the soul, immortality, and providence in Chapter XXIII.</meta>

attribute Thought (see lines 15 *f.* and p. 64, lines 20 *f.*). The meaning is ultimately the same in any case. It may be that the sentence is imperfect, and (as suggested by Dr. W. Meyer) the following words should be inserted after "in" (line 7): "the thinking thing, which idea is . . ."

**134, 18.** The expression "cause" is not quite accurate here. What is meant (as the context shows) is that, corresponding to that mode in the Attribute Extension called our body, there is a mode in the Attribute Thought called our soul; but it is not the body that "produces" the soul (the Attribute Thought does that), it is only in a certain sense the "*occasion*" of its existence. Note †† was obviously intended to correct the false suggestion of the word "cause." Possibly the note was made, not by Spinoza, but by some reader.

## CHAPTER XXIII

**136.** *Cf.* p. 65, lines 31 *ff.*; also *Ethics*, V. xxi.–xxiii., xxxiii. *f.*, xxxviii. *f.* (In the *Cogitata Metaphysica*, II. xii., the soul is said to be immortal because it is a substance, and a substance cannot destroy itself, nor be destroyed by any other created substance. But this reasoning was obviously not intended to represent Spinoza's own views.)

Joel has rightly drawn attention to a certain similarity in the views of Spinoza and Maimonides on Immortality. According to both Maimonides and Spinoza, Immortality (in the higher sense) is not something which is the common right of all, independently of the lives they actually live, but rather a gift that has to be acquired by leading a life not only of moral uprightness, but also of strenuous effort after the highest kind of knowledge. Very similar to their view on Immortality is also their view on Providence. (*See the note to 140, 21 ff.*).

## CHAPTER XXIV

**138, 8.** "What there is . . ." The Dutch is *wat daar af is en te zeggen zoude zijn* <note>B: *wat daar af is, en van het zelfde zou te zeggen zijn*</note>. The construction seems to be confused; however, the meaning is clear.

**138, 13 ff.** *Compare* *Ethics*, V. xvii. (*God is exempt from passions, and is not affected by any emotion of Joy or Sadness*) and xix. (*He who loves God cannot strive to have God love him in return*).

**138, 27 ff.** *Compare* *Ethics*, V. xxxvi. and xl. Scholium: 
> ". . . Our mind, in so far as it understands, is an eternal mode of Thought, which is determined by another mode of Thought, and this again by another, and so on to infinity; so that all taken together constitute the eternal and infinite intellect of God."

**139, 4 ff.** *Compare* *Tractatus Theologico-Politicus*, chapters iv., xvi., and xix.

**140, 21 ff.** The following passage from Maimonides <note>*Guide for the Perplexed*, III. liv. p. 395</note> throws some light on this paragraph (and also on parts of chapter xxiii.): 
> "Even this <note>moral perfection</note> is only a preparation for another perfection, and is not sought for its own sake. For all moral principles concern the relation of man to his neighbor. . . . Imagine a person being all alone, and . . . all his good moral principles . . . are not required. . . . These principles are only necessary and useful when man comes into contact with others. The fourth kind of perfection is the true perfection of man: the possession of the highest intellectual faculties; the possession of . . . true metaphysical notions concerning God. With this perfection, man attains to his final end; . . . it gives him immortality and makes him what is (properly) called Man."

**141, 5 ff.** *Compare* *Tractatus Theologico-Politicus*, i. vi. xiii.

### CHAPTER XXV

143. *Compare Tractatus Theologico-Politicus*, ii.
As already stated in the *Introduction*, this chapter on *Devils* played an important role in the recovery of the *Short Treatise*. Kindness shown even to the devil is not wasted. Devils and spirits of all sorts and conditions were very real things in those days; Spinoza's quiet humor is well ahead of his time. In an earlier draft of the *Treatise* this chapter may have had a different place, for it is referred to as chapter xxi by Hallmann.

143, 15 ff. In *Ethics*, II. xxx, Spinoza says, on the contrary, that 
> "the duration of our body does not depend upon its essence . . . but . . . upon the common order of nature and the constitution of things."

### CHAPTER XXVI

144, 8. B omits the words "through reason . . ." probably because the copyist (Monnikhoff) noticed that it had not been shown how "our blessedness" is attained "through reason." *See* note to 128, 12-17 (p. 230).

> "Blessedness is not virtue's reward, but virtue itself. . . . The more a mind delights in the love of God . . . the more does it understand, that is, the greater power it has over its feelings, and the less does it suffer from evil passions."

For most people seem to believe that they are free only in so far as they are permitted to indulge in lustfulness. . . . Piety and Religion . . . they believe to be burdens. . . ."

submit to the divine law. If they believed that minds perish with the body, they would follow their own sweet will and obey chance desires rather than themselves. But “this seems to be no less absurd than the conduct of a man who, because he does not believe that he can feed his body with good food for all eternity, decides to stuff himself with poisonous and deadly drugs; or because he sees that the mind is not eternal or immortal, therefore prefers to be mad and live without reason.”

145, 8 *f*. The parable of the fish (as Joel has pointed out) was probably suggested to Spinoza by the following Talmudic legend (Babylonian Talmud, *Berachot*, 61*b*—quoted by Joel). In the reign of Hadrian, the Romans prohibited the Jews from studying the *Law*. Rabbi Akiba, however, persisted in studying and teaching it. And when a certain Pappos warned him of the danger that threatened him, he replied with the following parable: A fox on the banks of a river saw many fish hurrying away from a certain spot. Asking them why they fled, he was told that they were afraid of the nets which had just been spread for them. “Come, then,” suggested the fox, “come out, and let us live together on land, just as our forefathers did.” “What!” exclaimed the fish, “if even in our own element we can only live in fear and dread, what shall we do on land, which to us spells death?” Even so, said Rabbi Akiba, it is with the Jews. The *Law* is our element, for it is written, “*It is your life and the length of your days*.” If danger lurks in the study of the *Law*, an even greater danger lurks in its neglect.

145, 28 *f*. *Compare Hosea*, xi. 4: “*I drew them . . . with bands of love*.”

In the *Ethics*, V. xxxvi. Scholium, Spinoza says that human Salvation, or Blessedness or Freedom, consists in “a constant and eternal love toward God.”

146, 11 *f*. *Compare Ethics*, V. xl.: “The more perfect a thing is

# APPENDICES

## APPENDIX I

153. This provides a "geometric" version of the first half of chapter ii, Part I. It is remarkable that no definitions are given, although they are really essential features of the "geometric method." Spinoza, however, made up for the omission not only in the *Ethics*, but already in a brief essay (very similar to this Appendix) which he sent to Oldenburg, whose first letter to Spinoza and the latter's reply to it have already been referred to in the *Introduction* (pp. lxiv. *f.*, cxxiii.). In the course of his reply, Spinoza remarks that

<summary>This page contains scholarly cross-references between Spinoza's "Short Treatise" and his "Ethics," focusing on the relationship between God and finite beings. It also introduces Appendix I, noting Spinoza's use of the geometric method and his correspondence with Oldenburg.</summary>
<keywords>Spinoza, Ethics, ens rationis, geometric method, Oldenburg, Short Treatise, God, finite beings, Dutch translation</keywords>

--- Page 379 ---
<meta>This page continues Spinoza's reply to Oldenburg, describing a lost essay written in geometric form which Sigwart has reconstructed.</meta>

he thought it best to state his explanations separately in geometric form as well, and that he was enclosing it for Oldenburg’s perusal and criticism. Unfortunately, the enclosed essay has been lost. The correspondence (Letters II–IV), however, leaves little doubt about the contents of that essay, which Sigwart has reconstructed as follows:

### I. DEFINITIONES

1. *Deum definio esse Ens constans infinitis attributis quorum unumquodque est infinitum sive summe perfectum in suo genere.*
2. *Per attributum intelligo omne id quod concipitur per se et in se, adeo ut ipsius conceptus non involvat conceptum alterius rei. Ut ex. gr. extensio per se et in se concipitur; at motus non item. Nam concipitur in alio, et ipsius conceptus involvit extensionem.*
3. *Ea res dicitur in suo genere infinita, quæ alia ejusdem naturæ non terminatur. Sic corpus non terminatur cogitatione, nec cogitatione corpore.*
4. *Per substantiam intelligo id, quod per se et in se concipitur, hoc est cujus conceptus non involvit conceptum alterius rei.*
5. *Per modificationem sive per accidens intelligo id, quod in alio est et per id, in quo est, concipitur.*

### II. AXIOMATA

1. *Substantia est prior natura suis accidentibus.*
2. *Præter substantias et accidentia nil datur realiter, sive extra intellectum.*
3. *Res quæ diversa habent attributa, nihil habent inter se commune.*
4. *Rerum quæ nihil habent inter se commune, una alterius causa esse non potest.*

<sig>Q</sig>
<vocab>Oldenburg, Sigwart, geometric form, Deum, Ens, attributis, substantia, axiomata, definitiones, extensio, motus, accidentibus</vocab>

<meta>This page continues the reconstruction of Spinoza's geometric propositions, specifically focusing on his early formulations regarding substance and its attributes, as well as scholarly commentary on the "Cogitata Metaphysica" and "Ethics."</meta>

->GOD, MAN, AND HIS WELL-BEING<-

### III. PROPOSITIONES

1. *In rerum natura non possunt existere duae substantiae ejusdem attributi.* <note>In the nature of things, two substances of the same attribute cannot exist.</note>
2. *Substantia non potest produci <note>neither by any other substance</note>, sed de ipsius essentia est existere.* <note>Substance cannot be produced, but existence belongs to its very essence.</note>
3. *Omnis substantia debet esse infinita sive summe perfecta in suo genere.* <note>Every substance must be infinite or supremely perfect in its kind.</note>

There was also a *Scholium* like Scholium 2 in *Ethics*, I. viii., but it is difficult to restore the text of it.
The first Appendix was probably a first draft of the essay mentioned above (1661), in which Definitions were added, while the rest was shortened.
153, 6 *f*. In the *Cogitata Metaphysica* (II. v.), Spinoza lists three kinds of "distinctions." *Rerum distinctio triplex, Realis, Modalis, Rationis* <note>The threefold distinction of things: Real, Modal, and of Reason</note>. The explanations which he adds are the same as those given by Descartes. See the note for 102, 15 (pp. 220 *f*.).
153, 19 *f*. Axiom 7 looks suspicious. It is really only a repetition of Axiom 1. Possibly it was only a reader's note on Axiom 1, but it was incorporated into the text by an uncritical copyist. This suspicion is confirmed by the fact that no use is made of it in the following text.

On page 24 (lines 31 *f*.), it was maintained that whatever is in the infinite understanding of God must actually exist; here (lines 18 *f*.), we have the opposite assertion, namely, that whatever is real must have its corresponding idea in the attribute of Thought.

<header>COMMENTARY</header>
<sig>Q 2</sig>

The subject of paragraph 1 actually continues on page 159, line 28 to page 160, line 5. Freudenthal argued that these lines were misplaced somehow, as they would fit very well if placed immediately after page 158, line 2, whereas they are irrelevant in their actual context.

157, 24 and following. In *Ethics*, II. vii. Scholium, Spinoza says: “The [so-called] thinking substance and the [so-called] extended substance are really one and the same substance, which is understood now under this attribute, now under that. Similarly, a mode of extension and the idea of that mode are one and the same thing, only expressed in two ways; a truth which certain Hebrews appear to have seen as if through a cloud, for they state that God, the intellect of God, and the things perceived by that intellect are one and the same thing.”

158, 4 and following. Compare pages 69 (lines 26 and following) and 121 and following.

158, 7 and following. Compare page 79 (lines 8 and following), and *Ethics*, III. vi. f.

158, 11 and following. Compare the notes for pages 127 and following.

159, 6. <term>Realiter</term> is not quite accurate here; it is used in a broader sense or from a Cartesian point of view, according to which “things” and “souls” are substances, which are therefore different <term>realiter</term>.

159, 9. “Essence”—that is, the essence of the soul.

159, 16–17. That is, he does not say “which exists as a material (or extended) thing.”

159, 24. “As regards their existence.” Dutch: *na haar wezentlijkheyt*. The Latin was most likely <term>realiter</term>. Attributes do not differ <term>realiter</term>, because a <term>distinctio realis</term> exists only between different substances. The attributes, however, though distinct, are not distinct substances, nor are they supported by distinct substances; they are their own “subjects” or substrates (that is to say, they need no other “subjects” for their support), and together they constitute the one and only “Substance.”

159, 28 and following. See the note for 157, 14 and following (at the top of this page).

<summary>This page provides scholarly commentary on Spinoza's texts, addressing misplaced passages, the unity of substance and attributes, and the technical definition of "real distinction" versus Cartesian views.</summary>
<keywords>Spinoza, Ethics, Freudenthal, realiter, distinctio realis, substance, attribute, Hebrews, Cartesian, metaphysics</keywords>

--- Page 382 ---
<meta>This page continues the scholarly commentary and notes on Spinoza’s work, specifically focusing on the relationship between attributes, modes, and the transition of ideas from the "Short Treatise" to the "Ethics."</meta>

<header>GOD, MAN, AND HIS WELL-BEING</header>

160, 20. “*Which really exists in Nature.*” The emphasis placed on this clause will only be understood after a careful reading of the entire paragraph, omitting page 159, line 28 to page 160, line 5. As long as Extension and the other Attributes do not develop specific modes that have duration in time (existence), there is only the Attribute of Thought as an Attribute, and there are no individual “Ideas” or “Souls.” “Souls” only emerge from the Attribute of Thought as far as specific “modes” (bodies, etc.) of the other Attributes come into existence.

160, 26 and following. Spinoza generally distinguishes between the attribute of Thought and its infinite mode or Idea, *Understanding*. B <note>Referring to Manuscript B</note> must be wrong here.

161, 6 and following. *Compare* notes 7–14 on pages 63 and following. These notes show a further development of the ideas in the present paragraph, and are most likely later additions.

162, 13 and following. The concluding sentences actually contain a very brief summary of the plan which Spinoza followed in the second part of the *Ethics*, where (as Sigwart has pointed out) propositions 11–19 are devoted to the consideration of the <term>ideae corporis</term> and <term>ideae affectionum corporis</term>, 20–23 to the <term>idea ideae</term> (= <term>idea reflexiva</term>), 24–31 to sense-experience, 32–36 to adequate and inadequate ideas, 38 and following to reasoned knowledge, etc. The conclusion of the *Short Treatise* thus directs our attention to the *Ethics*.

<summary>This page provides scholarly annotations on Spinoza's "Short Treatise," clarifying the emergence of individual souls from the attribute of Thought and noting the conceptual links between this early work and his later masterpiece, the "Ethics."</summary>

<meta>This page continues the publisher's advertisements for philosophical works, following the previous page which featured books by Rudolf Eucken and W. R. Boyce Gibson.</meta>

# NATURALISM AND AGNOSTICISM

->THE GIFFORD LECTURES DELIVERED BEFORE THE UNIVERSITY OF ABERDEEN IN THE YEARS 1896-1898<-

->By JAMES WARD, Sc.D.<-

->Hon. LL.D. Edinburgh, Professor of Mental Philosophy and Logic in the University of Cambridge.<-

->*Third Edition, Revised. In Two Volumes, demy 8vo, cloth,* **price 18s. net** *(post free 18s. 6d.)*<-

This work (consisting of five parts) seeks to show that the union of Naturalism and Agnosticism which constitutes "modern scientific thought," though it has led to a widespread prejudice against Idealism and so against Theism, has yet really promoted the interest of both.

> "It cannot be doubted that it will have a wide influence on the higher thought of the country, and may even do something to restore to philosophy the pre-eminent place it once occupied in English thought." — *Athenæum*.

# NATURAL AND SOCIAL MORALS

->By CARVETH READ, M.A.<-

->Grote Professor of Philosophy in the University of London, Author of "The Metaphysics of Nature."<-

->*Demy 8vo, cloth,* **price 7s. 6d. net** *(post free, 7s. 10d.).*<-

This is an attempt to explain the principles of morality in as close a relation as possible to human life. It takes account of the historical systems of ethics, of recent researches into the development of the human race, and of our present circumstances. The first Book shows how our ideas concerning conduct and the good are influenced by social relations, individual character, and our place in the physical and animal world, and under what conditions it is possible to treat Morality as the subject of a natural science.

The second Book shows that social institutions are necessary to the good life, but often react upon it injuriously. It discusses the effect upon morality of Customs, the Family, the State, Religion, and the Fine Arts, in order to understand the actual difficulties of conduct.

->BY THE SAME AUTHOR<-

# THE METAPHYSICS OF NATURE

->*Second Edition, demy 8vo, cloth,* **price 7s. 6d. net** *(post free, 7s. 10d.).*<-

> "A book which must be ranked among the most important of recent years." — *Nature*.

> "A singularly important contribution it is to the critical and philosophic theory of our time." — *Pall Mall Gazette*.

->PUBLISHED BY<-

->ADAM & CHARLES BLACK · SOHO SQUARE · LONDON, W.<-
